of 

DALLAS COUNTY 

IOWA By EUGENE N. HASTIE

Des Moines, Iowa

PREFACE 

In the writing of this history, the author has no apology to offer, for it is his sincere conviction that the best type of citizens should have a knowledge of their local history. Before now, two county histories have been written-one in 1879, the other in 1907. 

The first was about 30 years after the earliest settlements, the second was about 30 years later, and now 30 more years have passed; a new generation is hereit is time for another. 

That others could have done better there is but little question, but inasmuch as nobody had undertaken the task I felt impelled to meet the need of the times. 

Doubtless some things have been overlooked and omitted that are more important than some subjects that are included. While care has been taken to have everything entirely correct, yet probably some things are slightly misstated; but not intentional. People's versions of some things sometimes differ, as we frequently notice. It may seem to some that the city of 

Perry is given undue notice, but naturally there will be more history of a large town than of a small one; besides my proximity to Perry enables me to recall incidents that otherwise would not be thought of. In some cases I wished to include matters in other towns that I was unable to procure. But despite the short-comings of the book, the writer feels that he is rendering the people of Dallas County a valuable service, and believes that it will appeal to the people, not because of

its literary excellence, but because of the general information that it conveys. 

To me, it has been a great pleasure to meet the people, and see their interest in a work of this kind. 

Scores of people have been contacted and interviewed, some information has come from remote places, and much was gleaned from my personal local records, and diary, which goes back to 1911. Special mention should be made of the newspaper files of the Dallas County News and the Perry Chief, which were freely searched for many items. Furthermore, Mr. R. F. Wood, author of the 1907 history, gladly consented to making use of information in his book, and credit should be given to Blake Willis, former County Attorney and member of the State Legislature, for his loyal interest and excellent recommendation of the work. 

As this volume goes forth, as another link in the chain of history, it is hoped that is shall find a permanent place on the bookshelves of our best homes, to be freely read, and passed on to succeeding generations. 

March 16, 1938.

EUGENE N. HASTIE.

This book is respectfully dedicated to my father, Robert Hastie, whose rugged constitution is characteristic of the pioneers. Although still living, he was born near the present site of Perry nearly ten years before the town was begun, and is one of a few still remaining, whose residence within the county dates back to before Civil War times.

March 11, 1938 

To Those Who May Be Interested in a History of 

Dallas County, Iowa:

I have just recently had an opportunity to read the first draft of Hastie's "History of Dallas County," as compiled and written by Eugene N. Hastie. I have asked that I be permitted to write a letter of recommendation, relative to this book. 

As a history, it is most interesting to read. In addition to this, I can say that it is authentic. The author has lived in Dallas county, all his life, and has had an opportunity to learn and to know, even the earliest history of the county. The facts, which he has compiled, are both of a general and personal nature, all of which add to the interest of the reader. In compiling this history, the author has woven a human interest story through his compilation of facts and figures. 

I predict that this history will be in demand by those whose relatives or themselves have been connected in any way with the building and progress of Dallas county. 

Very truly yours,

BW:HG 

(Signed) BLAKE WILLIS

THE NEW COURTHOUSE

SECTION ONE THE PIONEER PERIOD

CHAPTER PRIMITIVE CONDITIONS 

1837-1846 

Primitive Conditions-Trees-Animals-Fruit-Indians— Indian Battles-Raccoon Forks-Indian Effects- County Organization. 

In our imagination let us go back 100 years, to 1837, and travel, during the summer, over the ground that is now known as Dallas County, Iowa. We would see no roads, fields, groves, bridges, railroads, houses or even people, unless it was some wandering adventurer, trapper, or Indian trader. We would see a beautiful country with its virgin prairies stretching away in the distance, over hills and slopes, as far as the eye could see.

Aside from the timberline of the streams and the surface of many a pond the whole area would be covered with grass, ranging from waist-high slough grass of the lowlands to much shorter grass of the uplands. Interspersed in the grass we would see myriads of prairie flowers-flags, cowslips, violets, sweet williams and wild roses.

Upon leaving the grassland and entering the woods that skirted all the larger streams we would see a miscellaneous collection of native trees; ash, shellbark hickory, white elm, red elm, bur oak, red oak, white oak, hackberry, linden, soft maple, walnut, box elder, honey locust, coffee tree, butternut, willow, poplar, cottonwood, a few red cedar and in some places hard maple. Should we be hungry, we need not go far, nor 11

look long, until we would find an abundance of gooseberries, choke cherries, wild plum, haws, both red and black, elderberries, raspberries, mulberries, crab apples, strawberries and wild grapes.

We would not travel very far until we would encounter streams of water-rivulets, creeks and large rivers, all quietly moving toward their destination, choosing a southeasternly course, but only the larger ones having a name. After traveling for many miles over the prairies, through the woods, and across the streams, perchance we reach the summit of an unusually high hill, and feel impressed to pause for a view of the beauties of nature. Unless we are void of real manhood and an alien to sober thinking we are apt to think of the Creator; a sense of admiration and awe will settle over us as we gaze upon the sublime scene before us. 

But vegetation is not all that abounds: the whole country is teeming with animal life as well. In the landscape we see immense flocks of wild geese, ducks, cranes, prairie chickens, turkeys and quail. On yonder hill we see a herd of buffaloes, quietly grazing, while through the brush we see some deer fleeting by. At night, in the woods, we might imagine a menagerie had been turned loose, for racoons, opposums, wolves, panthers, badgers, skunks, beavers, rabbits, and minks are busily engaged in their native haunts. Thousands of muskrats inhabit great colonies of rat houses, resembling hay shocks in many of the ponds, and in the waters of stream and lake are an abundance of choice game fish. Thus we have a mental picture of the primitive condition of one of the most fertile countries in

PRIMITIVE CONDITIONS 

13 all this fair land of ours. Fresh from the hand of God, and ready for the possession and use by man, there were no erosion problems, no fertility questions, and droughts reduced to a minimum.

But we have not yet seen all that is to be seen. We would not go far without seeing evidence of inhabitants, and ere long our eyes behold an Indian camp at the edge of the timber, by the river. A group of tepees, rising smoke, some picketed ponies, barking dogs and squaws moving about indicate a portable village of the true Americans. Perhaps at one time the Indians camped where your house now stands. Inasmuch as these aborigines once lived where we live now, their description would come within the scope of this history. 

Of the great number of Indian tribes that once tread the American continent, only two were known to be permanent residents of Dallas County-the Sacs and the Foxes. It is not the author's thought to speculate upon their origin, but only to consider known facts pertaining to the time that they roamed over our present territory. Their nature, in many respects, was different from ours. The men were natural rovers, caring mostly for hunting, fishing and trapping. With this done, they left the real work for their wives, or squaws, who secured the wood, built the fires, cooked the food, and cared for the children, who, when small, were called papooses. Besides this, the squaw crudely tilled small areas of ground in which they raised corn, beans, and other vegetables.

In hunting, alertness, endurance, and revenge perhaps no people have ever exceeded them. They were

the natural product of the times and conditions under which they lived; rugged in the extreme, fearless in battle, and exceedingly cruel. They gloried in the trophies of the chase and the one who could endure the most hardship and secure the most scalps was regarded as a great hero. Their predominant passion was for war. They had a keen eye for trinkets, beads and bright colors, and became good patrons of the fur traders, who sold them many things in exchange for furs.

Their wigwams were made by setting a circle of small poles which were tied together at the top, and over this framework were spread skins and bark. 

There were no windows, but an opening was left at the top through which the smoke of the fires must pass, consequently in cold weather their homes were usually, what to us would be, unbearable with smoke. The cooking was mostly done by suspending an earthen vessel over a fire, into which was put meat, ground corn, beans, and other vegetables. The variety of meat depended upon whatever they might have, even skunks would do; but like us, they preferred certain kinds, deer meat being one of their favorites. While some care was given regarding cleanliness, yet from our view point it would not be fit to eat. 

They worshipped no idols, but believed in the "Great 

Spirit" and hoped to eventually enter the "happy hunting grounds." They were noted for their war dances and religious festivals. Sometimes they would cut into small pieces a live dog, and while the flesh was still warm they would eat it raw. But in spite of their barbarious

PRIMITIVE CONDITIONS 

15 mode of living they had many good traits. If the white man was really a friend and would treat them right, they could depend upon the Indian's word; he, too, was a friend and one that could be relied upon. 

A few years after our imaginary glimpse into Dallas county, history relates a very interesting event. From "The Redmen of Iowa" by R. R. Fulton, published in 

1882, we quote the following: "About the year 1841 some 1,600 Sac and Fox

Indians were encamped on the east side of the Des Moines, a short distance above the mouth of the Raccoon, and within the limits of the present city of Des Moines. It was in August or September the Sacs and 

Foxes were engaged in a great war dance when a warrior of the Delaware tribe came into the camp bringing news of the murder by the Sioux of 23 Delawares on Raccoon River. They were on their way from their home west of the Missouri to visit their friends, the Sacs and Foxes, on the Des Moines. The 

Sioux had followed their trail, and came unexpectedly upon them in the morning just as they were ready to mount their ponies and pursue their journey to the 

Sac and Fox camp. The Delawares, under their chief, Neswage, defended themselves bravely, killing 26 of the Sioux, but losing 23 of their own warriors-all, indeed but one who brought in the sad tidings to the 

Sacs and Foxes. This battle, or rather massacre, occurred just north of where Adel now stands, at the edge of the timber. "Among the chiefs of the Sacs and Foxes present when the news of this massacre of their friends came

in were Keokuk, Pashepaho and Kishkchosh. The 

Sacs and Foxes immediately raised the war whoop, and determined to avenge the massacre of their friends. 

Pashepaho, then about 80 years of age, was too feeble to mount his pony, but when mounted, was still a good and faithful rider. Under his leadership five or six hundred mounted warriors started on the war path, armed with bows and arrows, tomahawks, war clubs, knives and guns. They followed the trail of the Sioux about 100 miles north of the scene of the massacre, where they overtook them. There a desperate battle was fought, and the Sacs and Foxes were victorious. 

They reported 300 Sioux warriors slain, while only seven of their own braves were killed. "When the Sacs and Foxes started in pursuit of the 

Sioux, John Evans, Thomas Connelly and James Ewing all connected with the trading post, accompanied them as far as the place where the Sioux had attacked the Delawares. They found the body of Neswage, the 

Delaware chief, lying by a tree, and by his side was his tomahawk. The tree was scarred by repeated blows of tomahawks where bodies of four Sioux warriors, who had evidently been slain by the hand of Neswage. This chief was a man over six feet in height, and of great muscular power. The Delaware who alone. escaped and carried the news to the Sac and Fox encampment, had during the fight, unobserved, concealed himself in some high grass. He reported that Neswage said, when he first saw the Sioux coming upon them; 'We are all dead men! We will fight as long as we can'."

PRIMITIVE CONDITIONS 

17 In 1841 Capt. James Allen erected a fort at the junction of Raccoon and Des Moines rivers, in order to preserve peace and order among the outlaws and thieving bands of Indians who menaced the new country. A trading post was established and the place was called "Raccoon Forks".

It is not the object of this work to relate the early history of Iowa, but it should be borne in mind that the whole state of Iowa was only a small part of the Louisiana Purchase, which our government secured from France in 1803 for $15,000,000. On October 11, 1842 the Sac and Fox Indians agreed to a treaty with the Federal government to relinquish their claim to a large territory, including Dallas county, and move westward to Kansas, but were given three years in which to effect the change; thus the way became open for settlers to safely enter this territory. 

Just as a good foundation is essential for a permanent structure so a good fertile soil and natural resources are necessary for a permanent and prosperous civilization. These basic elements here abounded to a marked degree, and became the backbone of our principal industry-agriculture.

The Indians, who are now gone, have left a lasting effect upon our civilization. The Indian is prominent in much of our literature. Our museums would be shorn of much of their glory had the Indians not contributed their part, and many of our well known names of counties, towns and rivers came from the Indians. 

Iowa counties with Indian names are: Winneshiek, Winnebago, Osceola, Sioux, Chickasaw, Pocahontas,

Cherokee, Sac, Blackhawk, Poweshiek, Keokuk, Mahaska, Pottawattamie, Wapello and Appanoose. Some of the Iowa towns named for Indians are: Camanche, Cherokee, Indianola, Osceola, Keokuk, Osage, Pocahontas, Sac City, Sioux Center, Sioux City, Ute and

Dakota City, and even Dallas county did not escape some use of Indian names, for Adel was first called Penouch, which is an Indian name meaning “far away”. 

In 1846 Iowa was admitted into the Union as one of the United States of America. This was about the time of the very first settlers in Dallas county. James 

K. Polk was president of the United States and Ansel 

Briggs became the first governor for the new state. 

In time some settlers came to Raccoon Forks to join the soldiers and traders, and the new town became known as Fort Des Moines. Our county received its name from Hon. Geo. M. Dallas of Pennsylvania, who was then vice president of the United States. 

The first county to be organized in Iowa was Des Moines county, where Burlington is situated; this was in 1833. Gradually the settlements moved westward, but not until 13 years later was Dallas county ready for organization. Dallas and adjacent counties were organized in the following years:

Polk Madison Dallas

Boone Greene Guthrie 1846 . 1846 .1846 .1849 .1851 1851

PRIMITIVE CONDITIONS 

19 The last Iowa counties to be organized were Hamilton and Humboldt, both in 1857, making a period of 

24 years that Iowa was being settled. 

Dallas county has an area of 576 square miles.

Thirty-nine other counties are of the same size. The others range in size from 400 square miles (Des 

Moines) to 984 square miles (Kossuth). 

A government estimate of Dallas county's (Perry) latitude is 41 degrees and 51 minutes while the longitude is 94 degrees and 5 minutes. Perry is 975 feet above sea level with a gentle slope to the southeast, falling to a level of 800 feet at Des Moines.

CHAPTER  

1846-1868 

Emigration-Mode of Coming-Political Divisions-First Settlers of Various Townships-Land Surveying- County Seat of Justice-Buying the Land— Penouch-Supervisors-First Court and Elections.

By early settlers we mean those who settled in the county before the advent of railroads, which was about 1870 or a little before. These were the real pioneers; those who laid the foundation for our local commonwealth. 

For years the emigrant tide of settlers had been rolling westward from the Atlantic seaboard to gradually conquer and possess the fertile plains of the West. The increasing population, both by natural increase and by foreign immigration, needed more room; young men starting in life needed better opportunities, and the spirit of the times were well expressed in Horace 

Greeley's words, "Go west young man." The miscellaneous collection of people making up the early population of the county came from most of our eastern states, as well as from numerous countries of Europe. 

With but few exceptions they were people of sterling worth and moral character; industrious, peaceful, thrifty and religious, possessing the traits of ideal citizens. 20

The first railroad to reach the Mississippi river was the Chicago & Rock Island at Rock Island, Ill., in 1854, consequently, the earliest settlers must come some other way. The best and most common way was to come down the Ohio river to the Mississippi, by steamboat, thence north to "Iowa's front door." The boat fare, including board, from Pittsburgh, Pa., was less than $40, and if a man wanted to sleep on deck with his own blankets, and furnish his own food, the fare alone was only $13. The steamboats stopped at all the river towns, but probably most people bound for Dallas county got off at Keokuk or Muscatine.

From here the journey must be resumed by stage coaches which were run regularly all through the country, but especially in a westernly direction. The tired people must have been glad to reach their destination after so long and slow a journey, and if they had crossed the Atlantic, as many did, in slow sailing vessels, several months might be required for the entire trip. Still others came bringing their families and a few possessions in covered wagons, while others walked; any way to get to the new country, acquire some of the cheap land and establish a homestead. 

To avoid confusion, before taking up the location of the first settlers we must mention the changes that took place in the political division of the county. When the county was first organized there were not sufficient people nor business to justify any minor divisions. On 

March 5, 1850, the county commissioners met and ordered the divisions made as shown on following page. 

These divisions were made for political and judicial

reasons and were called precincts. By order of the court on March 17, 1857, further changes were made as indicated below.

THE FIRST SETTLERS 

In speaking of the geographic location of the following pioneers we shall use the present township

names regardless of what the various sections were called then.

VAN METER TOWNSHIP 

Not until 23 years after the county was organized did Van Meter township have its birth, for prior to that time this territory had been parts of other town- 29R 28R

Meter family, who were early and prominent settlers. 

There is considerable variance as to who was really the first settler on Dallas county soil, but impartial investigators of this particular point 60 years ago

believed the following to be the true facts. During the fall of 1845, and not long after the Indians had relinquished their claim to the county, two brothers, Daniel and Lewis Stump, came through the southern part of the county prospecting. Being favorably impressed with the country they finally selected and staked out claims in Van Meter township along Raccoon river.

Their position that fall and following winter was anything but promising, for while the Indians had given up their land they had not yet been removed to their reservation in Kansas, and some of them were not in a very good mood over the proposed change, however the boys remained, built a cabin 16 by 18 feet, and made rai's for fencing. After safely passing through the winter they were happily joined by their brother, John, and sister Mary, in the spring.

Later in the spring John Wright and his family came and settled nearby. Prior to John's coming Levi and 

James Wright came to the Stump cabin, and finding the doors and windows (or light holes) securely barred against the Indians, in the absence of the boys, who were away working or hunting-the Wrights entered the cabin by climbing through the roof and awaited the return of their host. They, too, took claims and later Levi brought his family. 

Before the year 1846 had expired the following additional settlers came: George and Shubal Howarth, William and John Ellis, Henry Stump and family. Noah 

Staggs, Mr. Clark, Henry Garner, Henry Busick, John 

Juvenaugh, James Black, Wm. McCubbin, Richard

Golden, John Clayton, Sylvanus Night, James Moore,

Nathan Moore and Wm. Brown which made quite a colony for the first year. 

The first death of a white person to occur in the county was that of Wm. Coffin, John Wright's fatherin-law. He was an old man and a blacksmith by trade. 

His death took place in the log cabin of the Stump brothers during their second winter here. 

He was buried in a rough crude box hewn from native logs, and lovingly laid away in what became known as the "Clayton graveyard".

The first white woman to live in the county was Mary Stump who kept house for her brothers in the notable Stump cabin until her father, Henry Stump, and the rest of the family came. As might be imagined, girls and women were greatly in the minority in the frontier settlement and Mary Stump had no difficulty in finding a lover. She had the distinction of being a participant in the first marriage in the county, when on September 2, 1847, she was married to George

Haworth. The ceremony was performed by J. C. 

Corbell, Esq., who was the first justice of the peace in the county, and this case was his first experience in tying matrimonial knots.

The first school house in the vicinity was a log house built on section 15, and was taught by Wm. McCubbin or Miss Malind Night, but the narrator does not remember which was first. The first church organized was a Methodist church sometime during 1847, and the first church building erected and dedicated was probably the one in the town of Van Meter. On south

Raccoon river about three miles west of where Desota now stands Glover & McPherson built a sawmill about 1855. In 1859 they sold out to Jacob Van Meter who tore down the sawmill and erected a gristmill in its place.

The first election in this township was held at the 

Henry Stump home on April 5, 1847. At this time there was a spirited contest between the rival candidates for sheriff, and it is said that a jug of whisky won the day. Prominent in the early history of the county is the Van Meter family, the first of which, Jacob Van Meter, came in 1850. He drove from Kokomo, Indiana with a team and wagon. He acquired 640 acres of land and began farming. In 1856 he and H. G. Van 

Meter engaged in the banking business in Adel, and still later engaged in the milling business. 

During Civil War times he organized a company of soldiers for the Union Army. After the war he erected a four-story mill at Van Meter, but in 1884 it was completely wrecked and washed away by flood water.

Undaunted, he built another and continued in the milling business. Two other prominent early settlers in 

Van Meter township were Jacob Stump and John 

Brown, each coming in 1853. Additional early settlers were:

ADEL TOWNSHIP 

This township was named from the county seat which was located within its borders. The first settler in this township was Samuel Miller, who settled in what became known as the Miller settlement, being a short distance east of where Adel was later built. His arrival here was on March 12, 1846. Two weeks later, W. W. Miller and family, accompanied by his son, John, and his son-in-law, Eli Smithson, came and took claims nearby. It so happened that part of John's claim was where Adel was laid out not long afterward. 

Other Millers who soon joined the colony were Isaac, Eli, Martin and Jesse. Besides those already mentioned were Isaac Tribby, William Galway, Joseph C. 

Corbell and others. Because of the formation of the town of Adel this settlement grew faster than any in the county during the early times. During 1847 and 

1848, the following additional settlers came to Adel township: S. K. Scovell, Horation and Barney Morrision, Chelsea Shelton, Isaac Magart, James A. and 

Thomas Butler, "Cris" Fowler, "Jeff" Jones, Rud 

Lathrop, Thos. J. Drummond, Wm. C. and Daniel 

James, Anderson Kelley, E. J. Fowler, Ira Sherman, John and Valentine Cline, Benjamin Greene and others.

The first school house in Dallas county was built during the winter of 1847-48. W. W. Miller realizing the need, and being able, financed the whole enterprize, but it was open to all who cared to attend. He employed Stephen K. Scovell as teacher, who received the sum of eight dollars per month and board for his services, for a period of three months. The first church organized in the township was also the first for the entire county. It was organized by Elder John P. 

Glenn in Miller's school house in May of 1847. This was the beginning, (in a reorganized form) of the present Christian church of Adel. 

The first election in Dallas county was on April 5, 1847. There were only two polling places, one at the above mentioned school house and the other at the Henry Stump home farther south. There were only 

25 votes cast at both places, and an early historian estimates that the county's entire population then did not exceed 75 or 80.

In recent years, since the public is becoming more interested in local history, a large stone marker has been placed at the former site of the historic Miller school house, which reads: "In a school house where this stone stands DALLAS COUNTY was organized and the first county election was held April 5, 1847."

This marker is on the north side of the main paved highway about two miles east of Adel, and is a fitting tribute to the earnest endeavors of the early pioneers. 

The name of Diddy is closely associated with the early history of Adel township. The senior member of the

family to come to Dallas county was Levi Diddy who arrived in Adel in a prairie schooner on October 6, 1852. 

He located on the Judge Burns farm near the geographic center of the county. Much of his life was spent in serving the public in various ways, being assessor, coroner, school director, stage and express agent and justice of the peace. His son, John, was only eight years old when the parents came to Iowa. When only 

17, John enlisted in the army, and throughout his life was a well known citizen of the county, being elected as sheriff three terms in succession. 

The well known Brenton name in Iowa dates back to 1853 when Dr. J. B. Brenton drove from Indiana in a covered wagon. Six weeks were required for the trip over the poor roads of those times. He settled in 

Adel township and began to practice his profession, which soon extended into five counties. For a half century he was a faithful and devoted physician. On one occasion he rode from Adel to New Jerusalem when the thermometer was 39 degrees below zero and no track through the deep snow. He was the grandfather of Clyde and Charles Brenton.

An interesting account is given of Geo. Garoutte who settled in 1847, when the county was extremely new. 

Upon his arrival here, he only possessed a team and wagon and $30 in money. For the first winter's provision he bought an 80 pound shoat and some corn; this, with venison, wild turkey, coon and fish made an abundance of meat, and coon lard was used to shorten the corn dodgers. The nearest mill was at Oskaloosa, 90 miles away, and to this mill he went through the

deep snow. Upon his return, he found that his original supply of meal had been divided among the neighbors, and was soon exhausted, and they were all living on boiled corn. (How about some angel food cake, fruit salad and bon bons?)

Isaac J. Farlow is worthy of special mention in Adel township. In 1853, he and two companions made a trip to Iowa to see the West. By steamboat they went down the Ohio river and up the Mississippi to Keokuk from whence they walked to Adel. After spending the winter here they returned home the same way they came. The next year he returned, and in 1856 began farming. During the winter of 1858-59 he taught the first school in Colfax township in his own house. His first two years here were spent in carpentering, and after his marriage he and his wife began housekeeping in a part of the old log building which had been used for the county's first court house. 

All of the pioneers were familiar with the name of Hon. Cole Noel. For a half century he was one of the county's leading citizens. Coming to Adel in 1853, he had but three years to wait until he was elected clerk of the courts, a position which he held very efficiently for ten years. He served in the state legislature at the time an appropriation was made for the building of the new capitol. He had special appointments to positions of trust by both President Lincoln and Harrison. He was an outstanding character and his name was synonymous for honesty and square dealing.

BOONE TOWNSHIP 

This township presumably received its name from Mr. Wm. D. Boone, one of its prominent early settlers. 

Due to the fact that for many years Boone township included what is now the east half of Van Meter township it is difficult to keep the early township histories separate. Many of the early incidents which now properly belong to Van Meter township really occurred in what was then Boone township.

The first settler in Boone township was John Wright who settled on section 22 in 1846. He had previously settled farther west in Van Meter township, but after only a short sojourn there, he moved to the above mentioned place. The next year Wm. D. Boone, George

Gresham, J. C. Goodson and John Johnson came, and before long still more came, making a very flourishing community. The first school was taught by Mr. Goodson's wife, Priscilla. The first church to be organized was a Methodist church, and was begun by J. Q. Hammond, a circuit preacher. Other early settlers were 

Robert McConnell, 1851; James Robinson, 1856, and 

Wm. Cook, 1862.

DES MOINES TOWNSHIP 

This township derives its name from its principal river-the Des Moines. From May 18, 1846 to the following September, Mr. O. D. Smalley was the only settler in this section of the country. He located near the river on section 26. He has been styled by some as the Christopher Columbus of Dallas county for he was the monarch of all he could survey. He was a member of the first grand jury drawn in the county. 

This memorable session was held beneath the limbs of a large tree on "Coon" river near Adel, and the only business transacted was a horse trade between Mr. 

Smalley and another member. During the fall John and David Spear, Jerry Evans, Samuel Ramsey, and

Judge McCall came to be his neighbors, all setlling further up the river, and the following spring Judah Leaming joined the number. Other early settlers were: 

Messrs. Rhoads, Snyder, Harlow, Robbins and Newell.

The first conveyance of real estate, with a recorded deed. not only in the township. but in the county as well, was made on February 13. 1850. In the deal, Judah Leaming Jr. sold 160 acres of land for $100 to

Martin Tucker of Polk county. The first election was held at the house of Judah Leaming in August, 1848. 

The first post office in the township was in the home of O. D. Smalley, which was opened in September, 1858, and Mr. Smalley was the appointed postmaster. 

When Xenia was started the post office was moved to the new town and another one was opened in the southeast part of the township called Snyder Post Office, both being served by the stage route running from Des 

Moines to Boone.

The first school was held at the home of Martin Tucker in 1850, and was taught by Dr. Plumly, with ten pupils in attendance. In an early time Xenia became quite a pioneer town. There was a blacksmith shop, church, school, post office and stores were operated by Messrs. Leaming, Parks, and Craft, and Dr. 

Brookings looked after the health of the community.

In 1867, W. Ruth built a saw and gristmill near the river on section 3. For years it did a thriving business and was known as "Ruth's Mill". Mr. Taylor also had a steam sawmill on section 24. The first minister was John Johns of the Baptist denomination. 

Among the early settlers of the township was Francis 

E. Guthrie, who came in 1854. Six years later he erected one of the first frame houses in Des Moines township. A son, W. Scott Guthrie, was one of the first students at Iowa State College and helped to set the shade trees that now adorn the campus grounds. 

In 1872 his father was accidentally killed by a log sled overturning, and Scott took charge of the farm. In 

1910, he became county treasurer.

Another early settler was John Perry who settled near High Bridge in 1853. In 1879 he contracted with the government to carry the mail by stage from Des Moines to Mongonia via Xenia. He also carried passengers and baggage, and from July, 1879 to July, 1880 he never missed a single day. His remuneration was $800. Henry Thornley came to Des Moines township in 1849, but died in 1853, in the prime of life. His interment was the first in an old local cemetery. Two sons, G. H. and S. M., became well known business men, the former in Woodward and the latter in Perry. 

In 1849 Enos Rhoads settled within the township, coming with ox teams from Keokuk. He was the founder of Methodism in this part of the state and was lovingly and familiarly called "Uncle Daniel" by the earliest settlers. His home was the meeting place for the early worshippers, and "became a home for every stranger, his hospitality being extended to the learned and ignorant, the rich and the poor alike—no one being turned from his door." Other prominent early settlers were Thos. E. Newell who settled in 1852 and John. 

Breakfield who came two years later.

UNION TOWNSHIP 

This township received its name from the fact that middle Raccoon and south Raccoon flow together within its bounds, thus the union of the two rivers suggests the name.

The first settler here was David Dailey who settled on section 4 in 1847. The next spring he was joined by Humphrey Smith, and his son-in-law, Henry Owens.

These two built the first mill near the mouth of Cottonwood creek, known as Owen's mill. Unfortunately it was entirely swept away during the big flood of 1851. 

The raising and christening of this mill was made the occasion of a great frolic in pioneer style. Harvey 

Adams, George Garroutte and Zebin Babcock and all the neighbors were invited. "Henry Owens was a gentleman of the old school, and had, of course, laid in a very large supply of 'liquid fire' for the occasion." 

At nightfall, and after the work was completed a bountiful meal was served. Those who lived too far away remained all night. 

Zebin Babcock was always called "Squire Babb", and his weakness was an extreme love for liquid refreshments. This man with three of his neighbors and friends determined to return home that night. There was no road nor even pathway to lead them toward Penouch and home. The moon was shining bright and the dew falling fast. They had not gone far until 

Squire Babb fell from his horse, because of an overdose of spirits. Judge Burns and George Garroutte dismounted and put the Squire back on his horse. As soon as they ceased to steady him he went sprawling to the ground again. By now he was limber and deaf to all their appeals.

Finally Garroutte became impatient and began to beat the inebriate, each blow being accompanied by an outburst of virulent imprecations. Finally the 

Squire came to, saying, "Mr. Garroutte, I can whip you the best day you ever saw-you are my neighbor, and sir, I would as soon be found fighting my mother

as my neighbor, sir!" George vigorously replied: "Get up, you d---- old whig, or I'll kill you and throw your old carcass into the creek to feed the minnows." Again they got him astride his horse, and with a man on each side holding a leg, and another leading the horse they traveled over the prairie until they came to the house of Abner McKean on Panther creek. Here they unloaded the Squire and left him for the night, with 

Harvey Adams to keep him quiet.

Other early settlers were Leroy Lambert, Mr. Wilcox, John W. Hayes, Elish Morris, John Willis, Uriah 

Stotts, George B. Warden, Nelson Cave, L. D. Hewitt and Chas. Vermillion. 

The Cavenaugh brothers, Patrick, Thomas and

Michael, settled on the land where Redfield is located, and in 1852 started a small town which they called New Ireland. About 1856 they sold the townsite and a large tract of land to Col. James Redfield, and some of his kinfolks. In an early day, before Colonel Redfield purchased the Cavanaugh townsite of New Ireland he started a town near the junction of south Raccoon and middle Raccoon and called it Wiscotta. This move was prompted by an early survey for the Rock Island railroad through that section, but which failed to materialize.

Being only a mile from New Ireland there was quite a bit of rivalry between the two towns, Wiscotta at first prevailing. General stores were operated by 

Colonel Redfield, Mr. Wright, Noel & Lyons, Chance & Miller. Peppard & Leeper had a chair factory and 

Thomas & Smith run a blacksmith shop. There was

a school house 24 by 70 with 80 scholars in attendance. 

Miss Lucy Shepherd and Miss Dora Sales were teachers. There was also a Friends church, and Horner's mill was just east of the village. But unforeseen changes were ahead. When the great rush of emigration was pressing westward over the state road the route passed through New Ireland, which by this time was called Redfield, and in a short time practically all the business of Wiscotta was moved to the more favored place. The first cemetery of this township was the Wiscotta graveyard. Among the first ones buried here was the father of the Cavenaugh brothers. 

One of the leading pioneer doctors of the county was Dr. Macy B. Maulsby. For a third of a century his name was a household word throughout large area of the county. Although educated for the medical profession in the east he could not resist the urge to go west. Consequently he came to Dallas county and purchased a tract of land near where Linden now stands, with the intention of giving up his profession and tilling the soil, but doctors were so few and so far apart in the new country that he was almost compelled to resume his original calling. The following year he sold his land, moved to Redfield, and began a medical career. The life of a doctor in those days was sometimes a very strenuous and difficult task. The customary mode of travel was by horseback. Through all kinds of weather, fording streams and encountering snowdrifts took a strong physique and resolute courage, for sometimes their services were needed 30 or 

40 miles away.

ADAMS TOWNSHIP 

This township was named after Stephen Adams, who was instrumental in getting the township separated from Adel township in order to avoid a railroad tax that was about to be voted on in Adel township. George 

S. Hill was one of the first settlers (perhaps the first) who came on May 18, 1846, and settled on sections 

10, 11, 14 and 16. Adjoining his land were the homesteads of John Longmire and Tristram Davis, and before long John and Levi Davis, Archibald Crowl and Nathan and Abner McKean joined the ranks. George 

Hill built the first frame house in 1857. It was 24 by 48 feet, and a story and a half high. The first burying ground, on section 16, was donated by Tristram Davis, and the first deaths among the settlers were the Hill sisters, Matilda and Malinda, whose deaths occurred about two weeks apart in July, 1850. 

Owing to faulty cooperation there was no school house built until 1853; when one was built by law on section 11. This building, including equipment, cost $161. The first water mill was built by Charles Bilderback in 1856. This township has no town within its borders, and only about two miles of railroad. 

In order that our modern youth may get a fair conception of the hardships and privations that the early settlers went through we shall cite the experiences of 

Mr. and Mrs. William Coffin. Mr. Coffin came to 

Adams township in the spring of 1857. The following

September he was married in Jefferson county, where he had previously lived. The newly married couple

journeyed from the parental home, to the new home in Dallas county, in an ox-drawn wagon. "Some honeymoon" our youngsters would say, "but too slow for now; an airplane would be better." For over a year the only furniture they had was what Mr. Coffin had made himself.

Four years later their humble home was destroyed by fire. Just after their removal into a new house lightning struck, tearing out both ends of the house and killing a young man sleeping within. Having no money, Mr. Coffin was much dismayed, but he took courage, and pressed on doing the best he could. Soon after he had to leave his family and home to engage in the Civil War. Despite all these trying circumstances they triumphed in the end, and became prosperous and honored citizens. Their lot may have been harder than some, but most of the pioneers were well acquainted with toil, self-reliance, privation, reverses, frugality, sacrifices and thrift.

Other early Adam township settlers were Masson 

Bilderback, 1850; J. V. King, 1851; Frederick Sears, 1854; M. A. Chesnutwood, 1854; S. S. McKibben Jr., 1854; G. S. McKibben, 1854; Samuel Van Cleave, 1853, and Henry Hunt, 1859.

SUGAR GROVE TOWNSHIP 

According to Judge Burns the earliest settlements were as follows: L. D. Burns, George Garroutte, Harvey Adams, and Zabina Babcock took claims simultaneously in 1847. The next spring came Adam Vineage, James Pierce, James McLain and John Sullivan

Jr. with their families, besides "Dutch" Henry, a bachelor. Wm. Boyd and sons, Samuel and James, S. C. 

Taylor, Hayes Boyles, Isaac Ballard and Milton Randolph came in 1849, followed by Wm. Groves, Wm. 

Cartwright and William and Allen Boyles in 1850.

The first school was taught by Slemmons Taylor, who received $20 for a three month term. "The first religious service was held at the house of Wm. Boyd, on the evening of April 22, 1851, sermon by Rev.

Hare, of the M. E. church; text, Acts 10th chapter and 

43rd verses". Besides Mr. Boyd's family there were 

16 present and a few children. In the southwestern. corner of the township Mr. Rhinehart built a mill about. 

1850. This was along Raccoon river and just below the mill site a fish trap was located which supplied fresh fish for nearby settlers. 

As before stated Adam Vineage settled in the spring of 1848. In July his wife became sick and suddenly died. Imagine the loss and consternation for Mr. Vineage no doctor, undertaker, nor even a cemetery in which to bury his companion. They did the best they could under the circumstances, and that was to bury her in an oldwagon box, in a crude grave in a plot of ground which became known as Kimery's graveyard. 

Thus reads the story of the pioneers. Joys and anticipation were often interspersed with sorrow, hardship and privation.

Other Sugar Grove settlers in an early time were 

John Bevens, Thos. Stevens, Isaac Warford, Thos. 

Storm, Andrew Rhinehart and John Temple. Still others were Howard Taylor, 1847; Jacob Mitchell,

1850; T. Beall, 1852; John Warford, 1852; John West, 1853; Daniel West, 1853; S. Edmunson, 1854; S. E. 

Albin, 1855; L. L. Collins, 1855; Moses Slaughter, 1855, and J. O. Warrington, 1855.

LINN TOWNSHIP 

The first settler in this township seems to have been 

Samuel Carpenter, who came about 1848 or '49. About the same time James Brooks, Thomas Elliott and possibly others also came. A year later James and W. W. 

Harper, Wm. Maulsby, Judge Jamison and Isaac Fee took claims. The following group was known as the second settlement in the township. Abner Hill, George 

Bailey, George Duck, Mr. Howell, Joel and Isaac 

Davis, Seth Pattee, Squire Lumpkins, M. W. Thornburg and sons as well as a few others. 

Mr. Fee tells of excessive rains and swollen streams in the spring of 1851 which greatly hindered spring work as well as going to mill or market. He further states that much good farm land was lost to agricultural uses by being sown to cottonwood trees. One piece of 

40 acres or more, near the mouth of Mosquito creek, and not far from his place, was thus planted to trees. 

The year before, this ground had raised a bumper corn crop, averaging 75 bushels or more per acre. Twentyseven years afterward these trees were from 18 to 20 inches in diameter.

The old Harvey waterpower gristmill was built on the bank of middle Raccoon about 1857. In early times there were three post offices in the township: 

Greenvale, Linn and Harvey's Mill. The first perma-

nent church was the Berean M. E. church which was organized in 1866. At first they met in a school house, but in 1874 a church edifice was built on section 20. 

Other pioneers of this township were: Allen Moore, 1852; J. A. Bailey, 1853; L. D. Bailey, 1853; John 

Jamison, 1853; B. B. Allen, 1854; Wm. Ellis, 1854; 

John Harden, 1854; John Maulsby, 1854; S. Patty, 1854; J. Lawbach, 1855; Pleasant Parker, 1855; J. H. 

Mills, 1856; Thos. Thornburg, 1856; Robert Foy, 1857; John Joy, 1857; A. Stroud, 1858; John Jehu, Josiah Lamb, and Hiram Rosecrans.

WASHINGTON TOWNSHIP 

The eastern part of this township was settled at an early date. The first settler being John Sullivan in 

1848, but after building a cabin on his claim he traded both for a gun worth $6. During the same year James 

McLane, Jacob Minter, Samuel Mars, John Sammis, Henry Myers, John Rivers and Martin Miller took claims, followed the next year by William Rouse, Wil liam Clark and David Starbuck. The first school was taught by John Warford in 1857.

The Church of God

Buchanan school house. On August 5, 1877, this congregation dedicated a new church building. was also a Methodist congregation.

Additional pioneers were: W. H. Murphy, 1850;

BEAVER TOWNSHIP 

The first settler was Seth Dayton who settled along 

Beaver creek in 1854. Others were J. M. Townsend, C. C. Burdick, S. H. Myers, Mr. McConnell, T. H. 

Gardiner, Thos. Todd, J. H. Biggs, J. Kirby, A. J. 

Reed, Joseph Storms and the Walworth family. Mr.

Dayton was an enterprising settler and engaged extensively in the type of farming prevalent at that time. 

He was a great race horse fan and had his own private race track on his farm, and would often go away to other places for races. Conflicting stories are told of how he finally lost most of his property through betting on horse races.

When his original house burned down he conceived the idea of building a large brick house to be used as a tavern on the stage coach route that ran from Des 

Moines to Fort Dodge. Having learned brick making in the east he concluded that on his own farm was clay suitable for the purpose, and proceeded to make the bricks from which his house was built. The large two story structure stands today in a wonderful state of preservation, almost 70 years old. Inscribed on one of the upper story bricks is this inscription, "S. Dayton, 1868." This house is of special interest and is worthy of distinction as a historic relic. The basement was finished equal with the rest of the house, and contained a fullfledged bar room. Above there was an open fire place, with cranes and other customary equipment. 

On a small knoll not far from his house is said to be the place of an old Indian burial ground. In early times there was, in this vicinity, a secret dugout where stolen horses were sometimes kept by a ring of horsethieves operating in the country. By the time railroads came to Des Moines there was still an abundance of game, and Mr. Dayton used to ship the breasts of prairie chickens, by the barrel, to Chicago. 

Another outstanding citizen of Beaver township was 

Joseph Storms, who first came in 1856, driving an ox team from Wisconsin. After an abscence of a few years from the state he returned and began farming in a large way; first buying a four yoke team of oxen and a 24-inch plow at Madrid for $500. From this crude beginning he continually prospered until he accumulated 12 improved farms, totaling about 3,000 acres.

In addition to his agricultural pursuits, he was, for 40 years, active in school affairs as well as being closely associated with the Woodward State Bank. 

SPRING VALLEY TOWNSHIP 

This township was so named because of springs along the banks of Raccoon river. In speaking of the early settlers in this township we shall first mention those along Raccoon river, and then those along Beaver creek later.

The first settler was "Dutch" Henry, a Hessian, who settled west of the river opposite where Perry now stands. This was in the spring of 1848. Much mystery surrounded his circumstances. Although he often split rails and dug wells for the settlers further down the river, they could not understand why he should isolate his homestead when places equally as good were available among congenial neighbors. 

On one occasion in the settlement a crowd had gathered together for a cabin raising. Dutch Henry was there; so was a stranger from the east, who upon seeing "Dutch" thought he knew him. Approaching him with a smile he said in a pleasant, friendly way, "It strikes me, sir, that I have seen you before; is not your name 

Henry Johns?" Confounded, he stammeringly replied, "Nix, my name ish not dot, he ish von Dutch Henri, all der time, yaw." Strangely enough, "Dutch" was suddenly taken sick with violent intestinal pains. Writhing in the grass, face downward, he failed to improve, and refused any assistance from sympathetic women. 

This distressing condition continued until night fall,

and then in an unguarded moment he slipped away under cover of darkness. 

This was the last he was ever seen in Iowa. His cabin, stock and all that he possessed was abandoned, and never afterward called for. The stranger, upon reflection afterward, was sure that he was really Henry Johns, who, some years before, had stolen about $700 at Beaver, Pa., and made good his escape.

The narrator of the story states that 22 years afterward he was traveling in Kansas in a wagon, and one day in conversation with a man, he chanced to ask him his name. At once he replied, Dutch Henry. At once he recalled the past, and there before him stood the identical man of by-gone days; identified beyond question by an ugly scar above the left eye. Time had not erased his dishonest tendencies, for he cheated them on the price of a feed of corn for their horses. 

The next settler, and the first one to remain, was Nathaniel McKean, who settled along the river northwest of Perry in 1852. He was soon followed by his brother, George McKean. The next that we now have record of was John Roberts, Willis Council, W. Alexander and Mike Shively in 1854, with L. P. Wilcox, Henry Thornburg, Joseph Phillips, Patrick Gramey, George Campbell and Dr. Mowrer in 1855, John 

Crocker and Hardman Crowell in 1857 and Harvey 

Willis in 1859.

After a lapse of over 80 years the original claims of the Mowrer, Wilcox, Roberts, Phillips and Graney families are still held and occupied by the descendants, being well known residents south of Perry. An Adel

abstracter says that such cases within the county are rare.

The present well known Harry Roberts house a mile. south of Perry is no doubt the oldest house in the Perry vicinity still standing in its original design. John 

Roberts built this house of native timber, the year after the close of the Civil War, but some of the finishing material was hauled from Boone; the nearest place where such material was available. 

Dr. Peter A. Mowrer, like Dr. Maulsby and Dr.

Brenton, was one of those typical pioneer doctors, whose devotion to his practice, was an outstanding characteristic. Personal ease and comfort were secondary matters; the sick must be reached and ministered unto at all hazards. Seldom, if ever, did the weather turn these doctors aside from the pathway of duty.

The first occasion for a cemetery was on February 

11, 1865, when Mr. Robert's daughter, Mary, died.

She was buried on a plot of ground which developed into the Mowrer cemetery, near the Mowrer home. Just north of the doctor's house he had a private race track where certain settlers often had horse races. Horse racing was a common and popular pastime in those days. Seth Dayton of Beaver township often came to the Mowrer track. The first school of the settlement was just north of the Mowrer cemetery.

A magnanamous character of early times was the 

Quaker judge. Henry Thornburg, who came here from

Indiana in 1855, driving a herd of cattle. It is said he came with $50,000. Soon he acquired vast stretches

JUDGE HENRY THORNBURG JUDGE THORNBURG'S HOME

of land between Perry and Redfield. At first he settled on, what for years was known as, the John Strait place. 

Here he constructed a hay house. Having no windows it was necessary to burn a candle most of the time. 

Upon arising in the morning the family sometimes. found wild deer gathered about, trying to eat the house up.

In a few years he decided to move further down the river to another place on his holdings. This was a mile or so south of the Haney place and east of the river. 

Here he built a large 14 room house, which proved to be none too large, for during coming years he raised 22 children besides one of their own among whom were the following well known local people: Joseph Willis, Mrs. Rachel Carpenter, Fremont Beeson, Linda Beeson, Henry Beeson and Fred Baker. As soon as the house was completed he proceeded to build a gristmill on the river bank, not far from his house. For years this was known as "Thornburg's Mill," and did a flourishing business, sometimes being necessary to run day and night during rush seasons. The old historic house still stands in a good state of preservation, but the old mill is no more than a memory. 

In his characteristic way he would greet strangers passing by with, "Where do thee come from, stranger?" or sometimes, "Drive in and stay over the Sabbath."

Being an educated man for those times he became school examiner, and the one question he would put to a prospective teacher was, "Do thee know the multiplication tables?" If they did they usually got the job. For years the Quakers would have camp meeting at his

place, coming for many miles to meet together under the leafy canopy of oak leaves. Seventy years ago there were many Quakers in Dallas county, in fact it was one of the leading churches, especially in the southwestern part of the county.

The old road, over which a stage once ran from Adel to Jefferson, ran along the east side of the river, passing 

Thornburg's Mill, hitting the range of hills on the 

Phillips place, passing east of Spencer Phillip's house, thence to the diagonal road of the Mowrer place, thence to Alton post office, and on to the north. About the only stretch of road used then, that exists now, is the diagonal road just southwest of Perry.

In 1859 Harvey Willis took land within the present limits of Perry, his east line being Eighth street; a few years later his brother John secured land joining Harvey's on the south, so that the two brothers owned most of the land between Eighth street and the river. 

Harvey's residence was a short distance southeast of where the round house is located, while his brother's place was where Fifth and Otley avenue is now. They also had a brother, Lindsey Willis, who lived south of 

John's place.

When the early settlers came this township was united with Dallas township, and the whole area was called by this name. In 1858 some of the settlers wanted to form a new township and call it Spring Valley. Consequently some of the neighbors went to the John Roberts place to have a meeting, necessary for the change. Upon their arrival Mrs. Roberts told them that her husband was working in the timber. By

the sound of his maul they had no trouble finding him making rails in regular pioneer style. In few words they stated their business, and the meeting convened at once, by the choosing of Mr. Roberts as chairman and Cornelius McKean, secretary. A resolution was drawn up requesting the said change, signed by all present, and sent to County Judge Lloyd D. Burns at 

Adel. The request was granted and the first township election was held at the Jonathen Chiles home in November (1858).

In time the settlers of this community became numerous enough to justify the opening of a post office. About 

1858 a small post office was opened in the dwelling house of James Morse, who served under President 

Buchanan. What to call the post office was a question at first, but Mr. Morse, who had been a postmaster at 

Alton, Ohio before coming west, suggested the name of Alton, and this was chosen. Mr. Morse was followed by Abraham Bennett and Cornelius McKean. There was also a school built here, and at first children from the Beaver creek district came across the prairie to school, but before long the Oak Grove school was built and this was no longer necessary.

In 1866 D. J. Pattee opened a store at Alton, which he continued to run until his removal to Perry, when the town was begun. 

Just where Alton really was is a question many wonder about, but it seems to have included all the area near the round house. Alton post office moved from place to place, as it was always conducted from the home of whoever was postmaster. Pattee's store was

right close to the Harvey Willis' place, a short distance southeast of the shops.

With settlers on both sides of the river there was considerable occasion to cross the stream, which at first was done by fording, but this was not very satisfactory, especially when the river was up, so Abraham Bennett started a ferry about where the Wolf bridge now stands. Settlements along Beaver creek began about as early as along Raccoon river, with James McMichael and Robert Hastie being among the first about 1854. During the next year or so they were followed by J. B. Mills, Mr. Kellar, Decatur Potts, David Hastie and J. S. Brumfield.

In early times there was a lake about a mile and a half east of Perry, known as Pott's lake, and for years it was the home of myriads of muskrats, whose houses resembled a field of shocked hay. Up to as late as 

1865 the Indians would regularly camp in this vicinity, staying all winter, and trap the muskrats, whose pelts they sold or exchanged for supplies at a rate of about three cents apiece. They invariably were short of corn for themselves and ponies, and would beg corn from the settlers.

They never caused any trouble, but sometimes there was considerable apprehension among the scattered settlers. One might imagine the fear that would follow the news of the Spirit Lake massacre, which occurred in March of 1857. Mrs. David Hastie has told of once when her husband was away, that Indians came to her house, apparently on the war path. She thought that her time had come, but putting on a bold front and

suppressing her inward fear, she took her baby in arm and went out to meet them. Fortunately no harm came and gradually all danger passed. "The Indians are coming this way" was the breathless message brought from the north to the James Morse house in the Alton vicinity on another occasion. 

It was said that the settlers for miles around gathered together, and with barricaded doors, guns and pitchforks awaited their coming for nearly a week, but they failed to come this far south. In later times Robert 

Hastie tells how he and the boys of the neighborhood used to wrestle and run foot races with the Indian boys. 

DALLAS TOWNSHIP 

Probably Mr. Henderson was the first settler here, who came about 1854. He was soon followed by R. D. 

Corry and Wm. E. Tolle in 1855; W. H. Adams in 

1856 and A. L. Towne in 1857. Other early settlers were Jacob Rhodes, J. J. Morrain, Mose Morrain, John 

Ganoe, Adam Collins and W. Curry. In 1859 J. F.

Atkins built a gristmill on Raccoon river near the present site of Dawson.

On section 27 was beautiful Swan lake, surrounded by open prairie country. This lake of about 80 acres in area was the home of many black bass and sunfish, and a rendevous of thousands of wild ducks and geese. 

Hunters came from a great distance to bag these game birds. This lake was drained about 1905. 

COLFAX TOWNSHIP 

Among the first settlers in this township were William 

Jackson, Jehu Miller, Willis Franks, J. N. Ludington,

John Tyer, John Metz and William Loper.

There seems to be no record as to the dates of their settlements, but it was probably a little later than those of adjacent townships, due to the fact that there were no rivers within its borders. 

A German Baptist church was organized in 1869. 

Four years later they built a commodious edifice on section 4, and called it the Panther Creek church. 

Christian Long was the first pastor, followed by Robert Badger in 1877. The township's only post office was the Panther Creek post office.

WALNUT TOWNSHIP 

The settlement of this township, like Colfax, was slightly retarded, because of no large streams, except in the southwestern part, near the Raccoon river, where settlers came at quite an early date. Among the Walnut pioneers were Henry Young, John Betts, Lafe Ellis, Frank Strahl, Loren Bingham, Paul Hoff and Lyman

Ellis. The first township election was in the spring of 1857.

GRANT TOWNSHIP 

Much of this township was not settled until quite a while after other parts of the county because the land was so low and wet; however there were a few quite early settlers, Mr. Humphrey being first who came to section 22 in 1849. L. Morgan became his neighbor in 

1855 and in 1857 J. D. Whitman settled on section 30. 

Other early timers were L. W. Briggs and A. Huff. The first township election was probably not held until the

fall of 1868, which is quite late compared to other parts of the county.

LINCOLN TOWNSHIP 

This was the last township to be settled, and for many years much of the land was held by speculators and capitalists. It is believed that J. R. Powell was the first settler. His choice of land being in section 18. 

For a long time he was the only one, and not until October, 1867, was the first election held, and then there were only about the required number of voters living in the township-about seven or eight, all told. 

Wm. Mosier and some of the Thornburgs settled in the southern part. When Mr. Mosier came in 1865 there were only two other families there-James Powell and Nathen Howells. Before long they were joined by Isaac Smith, John King, Wm. and R. W. Dela, Nathaniel Allard and Jacob Pitzenberger. "Wm. 

Mosier, Jas. Powell and Isaac Smith chose the name of 'Lincoln' in honor of Abraham Lincoln."

The M. E. church at Prairie Center was organized in 1866, but the church building was not erected until 1880. An inland post office was opened at Brough, and Jacob Knapp ran a store there. Later the Brough post office was moved a mile and a half further east. 

On a part of sections 10 and 3 was Pilot lake of about 100 acres in area. Like Swan lake it abounded in fish and game. Mr. Clarke owned the lake area, and by the shore he built a wayside inn, on the old stage route that ran between Boone and Council Bluffs. Even though his tavern was only 16 by 24 feet in size yet

he sometimes accommodated as many as 40 guests for meals and lodging.

In 1876 E. M. Jones who then owned the land and lake had it drained into Panther creek. In the bottom of the lake numerous buffalo bones were found, which were eagerly secured by relic hunters. It is presumed that wounded or chased buffaloes would go into the water for refuge, and then fail to recover. This first drainage was only a partial success, being accomplished by an open ditch. There was but little attempt at farming any of this ground and it still remained a great hunting place, and it was until about 30 years later that it was tiled out and fully reclaimed. 

An interesting account is told of the naming of Panther creek, which has its source in Lincoln township. Back in 1846 John Wright and his brother-inlaw, Greenbury Coffin were strolling through the tall grass and underbrush when they suddenly came upon a large panther which showed a tendency to fight. In a combat they would not have been equal to the occasion, but fortunately Wright had a rifle and in a rapid and precise manner he sent a bullet through the animal's eye and brain. Thereafter the creek was always called 

Panther creek.

Not until the land had been surveyed by the government could the settlers secure a permanent claim. This was effected through the nearest government land office, at a price ranging from $1.25 to $5.00 per acre, depending upon the location and time of purchase. The government survey of lands is an interesting matter, and one that many people do not understand. In dif-

ferent parts of the United States are parallels of latitude, called "base lines". The one that Iowa land is surveyed from runs east and west through Arkansas. 

Then there are certain meridians of longitude called "principal meridians". The principal meridian for 

Iowa runs north and south through eastern Iowa. "From these main lines the surveyors measured off townships in each direction, but due to the fact that the earth is round and all meridians meet at the north pole, 'standard parallels' had to be established as guide base lines to keep the townships as nearly square as possible."

There are two of these correction lines across Iowa, and one of them crosses Dallas county, hence the jog between the southern tiers of townships. The surveyors used a steel chain, and a compass to get the lines straight, and the corners of each section were marked with a rock, placed a short distance beneath the surface of the ground.

Sometimes people would "step off" so much ground and settle upon it before it was properly surveyed. Such were called "squatters", and could get no deed nor title to the land. Should they acquire the land later in a legal way they probably would have to move more or less to come within the proper bounds. Upon their newly acquired land they would build their house in what seemed to be a good location, not knowing just where a road would be established. This frequently left them off the road later on; especially was this true along the rivers and timber. Of course most roads

were made on section lines, but to this day some section lines are not public highways.

One of the first things for a new county to do is to choose a location for the county seat of justice. The first session of the state legislature approved the formation of Dallas county, (February 16, 1847) and appointed a board of locating commissioners to choose a site for the county seat. The board consisted of 

Messrs. William Wear and William Canfield of Polk county and Lysander W. Babbit of Marion county. 

The following May this board met at the home of W. W. Miller, and from this point proceded to look for a suitable place. Not far to the west, and along 

Raccoon river, they decided upon the present site of 

Adel. Martin W. Miller, the first county surveyor, proceeded to lay off and plat the proposed county seat, said work being completed on May 22, 1847.

The first board of county commissioners consisted of W. W. Miller, Tristram Davis and Greenbury Coffin.

In July they held their first regular meeting, in which they sanctioned the survey and plat already made, and the deputy surveyor, A. D. Jones, suggested the name of Penouch as the name of the new town. This was an 

Indian word, meaning in English, "far away." On the same day (July 5) the county commissioners deeded the streets and alleys to the public, and reserved the square for a court house.

So far all had gone very well, but soon there arose quite a serious question—the land still belonged to the government, and there was no money with which to buy the town quarter of 160 acres. No money had

59 yet come into the treasury, and a dollar was a dollar in those days. Natural resources abounded, but they had not yet been converted into cash. How to buy the land was a real problem. Two different plans of raising the money had failed, and finally, in their extremity, W. W. Miller mortgaged his homestead to secure the necessary money.

Dr. J. R. Mills was hurriedly dispatched to the land office at Iowa City to enter the coveted town quarter, which was done on January 4, 1851. Dallas county citizens should ever appreciate the sacrificial spirit and conduct of Mr. Miller in so great a time of need. 

The name of Penouch (pronounced Pennu'ch) was soon discarded, for in the fall of 1849, a petition was granted by Judge McKay changing the name of

Penouch to Adel. Historians differ on the reason for this choice. One says that the name is in honor of "a very pretty child with a prettier mother," while another says the name was suggested by a dell in the woods, and that the name was first spelled with two l's. The reader must decide for himself which is correct. 

As has been before stated the first county board (then called commissioners) consisted of three members. In 1861 a change was made whereby a supervisor was elected from each of its ten precincts. In time there were 16 townships, instead of ten precincts, with an elected supervisor from each, which made a large and cumbersome board; but in 1870 the number was reduced to three, and has continued so ever since. 

The first three members of the board in 1870 were 

David Smart, William Ellis and J. W. Redfield.

During the first few years the office of sheriff and assessor was the same, and this place was first held by 

Jesse K. Miller. In the spring of 1847 he assessed 19 persons, who owned 10 horses, 77 cattle, 14 wagons, 46 hogs, 27 sheep and 10 guns, the total assessed value of which was $2,403.75. It took five days to complete the work, for which he received $1 a day. The first district court was held at the home of W. W. Miller on September 6, 1847. There was no court house at that time and very little business to do. Hon. James 

P. Charlton of Iowa City presided and S. K. Scovell was clerk.

It has been stated that in the first election in Dallas county there were only two voting places, and 25 votes. 

In the second election a third precinct was added to accommodate the Des Moines township voters, but in this election there was some fraud perpetrated, so for the county's first presidential election the commissioners decided to have only one polling place, and this was at the home of J. C. Corbell in Penouch in the fall of 1848. There were three parties in the field-the 

Whigs, Democrats and "Free-soilers". General Zachary Taylor, or "Old Rough and Ready" was the Whig candidate, and the one who won the election. There were 57 votes cast, 34 of which were for Gen. Taylor.
