1846-1868 

Poem-Location and Cabins-Food-Clothing-Farming-Bad Winters-Floods-Horses and Thieves-Game-Buffaloes -Elk Hunting-Religion-Education-Gristmills- Going to Market-Fort Des Moines-Eclipse of Sun-County Fairs-Newspapers. 

THE PIONEER 

In the heart of the grand old forest, A thousand miles to the west,

Where a stream gushed out from the hillside, They halted at last for rest,

And the silence of ages listened 

To the axe stroke loud and clear, Divining a kingly presence

In the tread of the pioneer. 

He formed of the prostrate beeches 

A home that was strong and good;

The roof was of reeds from the streamlet, The chimney he built of wood 

And there by the winter fireside, While the flame up the chimney roared,

He spoke of the good time coming, When plenty should crown their board. 

When the forest should fade like a vision, And over the hillside and plain

The orchard would spring in it beauty, And the fields of golden grain, And tonight he sits by the fireside

In a mansion quaint and old, With his children's children around him, Having reaped a thousand fold. 61

Now that we have, in our mind, the first settlers scattered throughout the county, we shall take a look at their domestic life, and conditions under which they lived. Their cabins were the center of activity. At first all were of log construction-it would have been impossible for it to have been otherwise, for there was no commercial lumber, and in the earliest times not even a sawmill for making native lumber. 

The early settlers invariably settled either in or near the edge of the timber that skirted the larger streams. There were good reasons for this. In the first place logs for their cabins were cut and hewn close to where they were to be used, besides this, wood for fuel and rails for fences were pioneer necessities, and must come from the timber. Then again the shelter and protection from the windswept prairies and fearful blizzards were matters not overlooked by the pioneers, as well as other advantages, such as mill sites, pleasant scenery and fishing advantages. However there were disadvantages too, such as clearing the land, erosion of hills, and subsequent depletion, however most settlers figured that the advantages far outweighed the disadvantages. 

When the father had gotten all in readiness the friendly and accommodating neighbors would meet together for a cabin raising. The women would come, preparing a dinner in common, and enjoy a social time. 

The located settlers were glad to welcome new neighbors and these occasions were made very enjoyable, as well as profitable. The logs were notched together at the corners, poles served as rafters, and boards split

PIONEER METHODS 

63 333 from trees were the customary roofing. The cracks that were certain to exist were "chinked" with clay. 

The floor was of the puncheon type, which consisted of split logs lain together with the even side up. A stick chimney, heavily plastered with clay, was run up, and a hole about two feet square served as a window, but without glass. Cloth or oiled paper was used in cold weather.

This describes the earliest cabins, but with the advent of local sawmills a much better structure could be built. 

The furniture inside was as crude as the outside. A one-legged bed was built in the corner; which was made of poles and supported on two sides by the wall. 

Upon the poles were placed thin boards and bark, and then the housewife was ready to spread the blankets, robes and bedding. The cooking was done in the fireplace, or on a small stove they might have brought with them in a covered wagon. The food, while not of great variety, was generously supplied to the hungry members of the household, and consisted mostly of pork. game birds, sorghum, cornmeal mush, cornbread, hominy, milk, butter, vegetables and wild fruit. There were no fancy dishes and salads of today-no prepared breakfast foods, no canned goods, cold packed products, jello, whipped cream nor many other things that we are familiar with today.

The men and boys wore leather boots, which were frequently oiled to make more pliable, and to preserve the leather. The clothing was mostly homemade, being heavy and warm, with very little variation through the

seven days of the week. Furs and robes were commonly used.

Of course farming and making a living for the family was about all there was to do, but that meant a lot of work in those days. Clearing the land and breaking the sod was a slow, laborious job, the latter often being done with oxen. There were no discs then, and the only harrows were poles fastened together in V shape, with spikes or pegs driven through. It would have been impossible to raise large fields of grain then because of inadequate implements, but should such have been done it would have been of little value because there was no market or demand to speak of. Horses, oxen, furs and dressed hogs were about all the farmer could convert into cash.

Corn was planted by hand and cultivated by one horse and a shovel plow. Husking was done on both sides of the wagon, requiring three men or boys, one of which must pick up the "down row." Gradually horse drawn corn planters were used with varying degrees of improvement. The grain was sown broadcast, and cut with a "cradle," i.e., an attachment on a scythe which enabled the operator to leave the cut grain in little bunches ready for tying. About the time of the Civil War the first reapers were coming into use, but these still left the grain unbound, and men must follow up binding the sheaves by hand with the straw of the bundles.

There was free range on the prairie, and cattle ran at large all summer. About four in the afternoon the boys would start out horseback to find the milk cows.

PIONEER METHODS 

65 Sometimes it took a lot of time, and it was not an uncommon thing to not find them at all that night.

Calves born in the spring, and running with their mothers all summer would make a wonderful growth in one season. A person could cut a circle around a piece of grass on the prairie, which entitled him to all the hay within the circle. 

The "old timers" never fail to tell of the severe winters when the country was new. With no modern advantages, the deep snow would almost isolate the settlers for weeks at a time. Trips for supplies, or to mill, if coming at such times, were quite hazardous. 

Spring was indeed a welcome season. Nathan Moore of Van Meter township tells of the snow coming late in the fall of 1848, and covering the ground to a depth of three feet on the level, and remaining until nearly spring. A brother of his, living in Greene county, decided to make him a visit during this winter, intending to reach George Garroutte's place by night. His progress was so slow that he came far short of his intentions. As darkness came on there was no house or person anywhere around, so all he could do was to crawl into a hollow log and spent the night, resuming his journey the next morning. Eight years later, the winter of the Spirit Lake massacre, there came another bitter cold winter. Snow fell almost continuously for weeks, and cattle died by the hundreds. The sufferings and hardships of that winter make a dark shadow over 

Iowa history. 

When the heavy snow of the winter of 1848-'49 melted in March it caused a terrible flood, and played

havoc with much of the settler's property, and cut them off from mill and market. At intervals during the spring and early summer there were also heavy rains, and flood conditions prevailed more or less until July. In their extremity some of the settlers would make a temporary raft, on which to take their wagon and grain across the rivers to get to a mill. If the wagon and contents could be gotten safely across, the horses or oxen, as the case might be, were made to swim across. 

This was a very dangerous procedure and only a few of the most daring would take such chances. Only two years later the settlers experienced another flood as bad if not worse than the former one. The rain was unprecedented and the resultant floods greatly hindered farm work. However this deluge did not extend beyond the last of May.

All through the early times horses were greatly in demand. Each settler wanted at least a team, and some more, but due to the fact that a great many immigrants came by stage and afoot there were not enough horses to meet the need, consequently many used oxen, even until quite a late date. Therefore horses were high priced in proportion to other personal property, and highly prized by those fortunate enough to own them. Because of this condition horse thievery became a common thing, and regular gangs would operate over a wide area, hence it became a common practice to speak of horse thieves, which is an expression that has but little application to present times. 

Because of the prevalence of horse stealing, and the inability of officers to cope with it vigilant com-

PIONEER METHODS 

67 mittees were formed, in which certain men banded themselves together to take the law into their own hands when they deemed it necessary. Their activities were not limited to horse thieves alone, but others, too, were sometimes made to feel the coercive hand of the vigilants. There is no doubt but what their influence contributed a great deal to the civil order of the times, for their personnel were usually reliable, law abiding citizens, only desirous of punishing guilty criminals.

During the early times when the land was being taken the settlers experienced considerable trouble from land speculators—“professional claim jumpers," and various kinds of intruders who had no intention of settling here and enduring their share of hardships and labor in developing the country. To combat these practices the settlers banded together and formed the 

Dallas County Claim Club (July 1, 1848). Judge

Burns was chosen to draft the by-laws which were to be carried out "without fear of the world, the flesh and the devil." The club checked the activities of many unscrupulous men, but sometimes well meaning and innocent men were compelled to give up their claims after improvements were started. In the absence of laws to cover all phases of civil matters, the settlers themselves crudely executed their own laws. 

An outstanding feature of pioneer days was the abundance of wild game. Wild turkeys were common at first, but disappeared long before other fowl game.

For years vast flocks of wild geese, ducks and prairie chickens abounded, and the pioneers could secure all

they wanted. Lath traps and figure 4 traps would catch them by the tubfuls. Sometimes the number on wing would be sufficient to obscure the sun. There were also quails, blackbirds and cranes.

Bur bearing animals were abundant, and much trapping was done, but pelts, like many other products, were too cheap to be very profitable. There was also some of the larger game in the earliest times such as deer and elk, and even a few buffaloes. Buffalo Grove, a pretty tract of natural timber at the intersection of Beaver Creek and the Boone county line, was so named from buffaloes seen there in an early day. 

Perhaps the last buffaloes seen in Dallas county was about the time of the close of the Civil War. At that time Mike Shively was living a mile south of Perrywhere George Mullen lives now. His son, John Henry, then only a boy, saw in the distance a string of what he thought was oxen, and called to his father, "Look at those oxen." His father, who had crossed the plains in 1850, immediately recognized them as buffaloes. 

There were about 15 and they were slowly moving along, single file, apparently tired.

Mr. Shively sent his son on a pony to call some of the neighbors and secure larger rifles. When the group overtook the buffaloes they found that others, too, had seen them, and were giving chase. John 

Crocker was blazing away at their heads, but Mr.

Shively, who knew by experience, told him that he could not kill a buffalo that way. Acting upon Mr. 

Shively's directions he again raised his big army rifle

PIONEER METHODS 

69 and sent a bullet directly through the heart, bringing down the magnificent beast of the plains. 

Christion Roop and his son followed on trying to get another, but succeeded in no more than wounding one, which safely made his escape. The buffalo was skinned and the meat divided among those who took part in the chase. As Mr. Shively recalled the incident the others who participated were Hardman Crowell, Jacob

Fessler and Mr. Fetrow. When the shooting took place the buffaloes had crossed the river and were traveling in a westernly direction.

An anonymous "old timer" tells of the chase of a lone buffalo over the ground where Woodward now stands. Captain Berry, with his hounds, is said to have pursued the animal over the said ground and into a neighboring cornfield, where it was shot. The hide was removed and the meat divided among a number of eager recipients.

Elk hunting of early times is described in the following article published in the Dallas County News, February 2, 1876, under a section entitled "Old Timers." "The capture of elk calves was, in those days, a favorite pastime, or business, rather, for there were visions of wealth to be derived from their sale. In these adventures John Wright was the natural leader and captain. The outfit consisted of teams and covered wagons sufficient to convey provisions, camp equipage, etc., and to bring home the proceeds of the chase. Cows were taken along to furnish milk for the young elk; sometimes the expedition would be gone a month or more. From Greene county to the

head waters of the 'Coon was the favorite hunting ground. "The hunting was done on horseback. First the old elk must be found, which was done by looking over the surrounding country from some high point and then with the aid of dogs to throughly search thereabout for the hidden calves. These were usually found in the tall grass surrounding the ponds that so abounded in that part of the state. When the calf found that he was observed he would spring from his hiding place and run with the speed of a race horse, and if two or three weeks old it took good "bottom" to overtake him in those bogs and ponds. Many a good farm horse was ruined in the effort; rarely a very young calf was captured by springing upon it in its bed." 

Fish traps along the rivers, and held in common by nearby neighbors, were a common thing. A very successful trap was one just below the J. H. Warrington mill in Sugar Grove township. Another was located at Rocky Ford, on Middle Raccoon, just above the Isaac Fee farm. Sometimes as many as 150 fine fish were caught at one time. Spearing fish from boats was also a common practice.

Religion had a very prominent place among the early settlers. No settlement went very far until some itinerant preacher came along preaching in somebody's house, in their grove or in a school house. Before long a local church would be organized, usually meeting in a school house. It would be, sometimes, many years before a church building was built, for there seemed to be no good reason why the school house could not serve

PIONEER METHODS 

71 the double purpose. Bible names were much more prevalent then than now. Of the 430 names of Civil 

War veterans listed in the next chapter, exactly onehalf of them are Bible names. Such names as Elisha,

Jeremiah, Ephriam and Joshua were not at all uncommon.

The Methodists, Quakers and Baptists were especially active, and possessed a type of religion that was highly beneficial in developing sterling character, which is so essential in building a stable commonwealth. The youth of today apparently do not realize how much they owe to religious forefathers and Christian influence; without which, conditions today would be much different. There was but little tendency to question the 

Bible, regeneration was considered essential, and the altars were often the scene of happy faces weeping their way to the Christ of Calvary. Forms and conventionalities were forgotten, and religion was something real. Of course, as always, there were hypocrites and wolves in sheep's clothing, but the general level was much above the worldly standard of today. 

Education, too, was something borne in mind from the start, but at first only carried out under very adverse circumstances. Settlers were few and scattered, qualified teachers were scarce, and uniformity almost impossible. Each teacher pursued a course that he was accustomed to, with but little thought as to what others were doing. But in due time our educational system began to take on some degree of uniformity, everything possible was done to encourage and help the young folks learn the common branches, but at its best it was far

behind our standard of today. Many schools were only run during the winter months, and men were usually employed as teachers, for it was generally believed that women were unable to handle the older boys. In most cases people were contented if they could read, spell, write and figure fairly well, but many never became very proficient even in these subjects. 

But despite the meager education many men made a success in life; morally, spiritually and materially. 

Those who applied themselves to special lines at home and in their daily life could rise to high positions of trust and honor. Higher education was not sought with the spirit that some have today who say, "I'm going to get an education, so I wont have to work." 

Men who made a success in life were those who were not afraid to work, and they discovered that education and industry were as two wings carrying them on to success. Today, we have, as one man has expressed it, many "educated bums." Education alone, void of other worthwhile characteristics, is worse than no education at all. Educated criminals, gangsters and political crooks constitute one of our greatest national problems. 

Most people of those times had no thought of getting rich quick, but were willing to toil for a reasonable and gradual increase. There was not that tendency to live beyond their means, but nearly all were able, by economy, hard work and good management to save and get ahead. With the exception of purchasing a home, or an investment, they secured their money first and spent it afterward.

PIONEER METHODS 

73 No early settlement cut off from civilization, could survive long without a gristmill, therefore such mills soon sprang up along the river banks. No power was so suitable or economical as water power. Usually, in locating a mill, a bend in the river was chosen and across the neck of land within the bend a narrow channel was cut, which was called a mill race. This provided a narrow stream and better current for the big mill wheel that revolved in the water, and turned the upper mill stone while the lower one remained stationary. These stones were of various sizes, but usually three feet or so in diameter, and all were indented with grooves chiseled in by hand. Into the grooves the grain would run down from a hopper above and the turning of one stone over the other would grind the wheat or corn.

For a considerable time the early Dallas county settlers had to go to the Parmelee mill on Middle river in Warren county, about ten miles southeast of Des Moines. At times this mill would be out of order and then our settlers had to go clear to Oskaloosa for grinding and foodstuffs. There were a few small mills run by horse power that would accommodate a few families. Small mills of this type were called "corn crackers." No doubt the first of this kind in Dallas county was one constructed by Samuel Miller, who began using it on December 24, 1846. Its starting was made the occasion of a great Christmas jollification the next day. Because it was fastened on a stump it was called the "Stump Mill." Two years later Mr. Miller sold

this little mill to Buel Lathrop, who moved it up to his claim on Hickory creek. 

Prairie fires were a common thing during dry times of late fall or winter, and the only guarantee of safety for a man and his household was to burn a fireguard all around his premises. This was done by plowing two furrows, some distance apart, and burning off the grass between. When conditions were right, and the wind high, these fires would travel as fast as a horse could run.

For years it was the custom of David Hastie of 

Spring Valley township, to make an annual trip to

Des Moines each fall, just before corn husking time. 

These trips were made in an open lumber wagon. 

There was but little money in circulation, and what they secured in Des Moines was mostly by exchange. 

Dressed hogs delivered there were only worth two cents a pound. Salt, boots, dishes, ammunition, cooking utensils, medicine and coffee were some of the things sought in Des Moines stores, and we can imagine the anticipation and pleasure of the children at home as the tired team turned in bearing the new things from "the city." On these trips Mr. Hastie would reach Mr. Kingman's place, at the edge of Des Moines, the first day, the second was spent in trading, returning to Kingman's by nightfall, and on the third the trip would be completed. What was true of this family was customary with many others. 

Inasmuch as Des Moines was our next door neighbor it will be of interest to mention some of the historic facts of this place in early times. In 1850 the popula-

PIONEER METHODS 

75 tion was only 502, and the town was called Fort Des Moines. Iowa City was the capital. The federal census gave Dallas county credit for 854 inhabitants. In 1857 the capital was moved from Iowa City to Fort Des Moines, and the prefix Fort was dropped from the name.

In 1860 the city had a population of 3,965, which was a wonderful increase for a ten-year period, but it was not until August 29, 1866, that the first train rolled into Des Moines. This was on the line running up from 

Keokuk, and was called the Des Moines Valley Railroad. This gave new life to the city and really gave it a chance to grow. The second decade made even greater gains than the first. With Des Moines fast becoming a city, and having rail communication with the East it is easy to see that the outlook in Dallas county was becoming more promising.

Not many people have seen a total eclipse of the sun, but Dallas county citizens living here in 1869 had this rare and unusual privilege. Drake University records show that on August 7th the eclipse began, coming on at 3:50 p. m., reaching a totality at 4:45, and entirely disappearing at 5:40, central standard time. Those who recall this time say that it really got dark, and chickens went to roost. 

No doubt most people will be surprised to know that our county fairs at Adel date back to 1856five years before the beginning of the Civil War. In those times it was called an agricultural society, and the first steps taken toward organization was held on 

December 31, 1855, with Archibald Crowl as chair-

man and Benjamin Greene, secretary. The first officers were; W. W. Payne, president; J. T. Alexander, vice president; Benjamin Greene, secretary; Jas. D.

Ferguson, treasurer, and Cole Noel, marshall. 

The first fair was held the following October, and for the next four years they continued regularly, but during the Civil War period they were discontinued. 

In 1867 the society procured and fenced the grounds, and resumed the fair in the fall. Being unable to purchase the land they leased about 24 acres of Judge Byron Rice of Des Moines, which was just north of Adel, and adjoining the mill slough. In time the land was purchased and for many years the fairs were a regular annual event. 

At an early time the matter of sheep and hogs running at large became quite a question, as well as a menace. At the time of presidential election, November 4, 1856, the county voted on a law to prohibit the continuance of the practice. Of 649 votes cast, 369 were in favor of the law and 280 against the same. 

In 1857 there came a distressing financial depression. 

So many railroads were being proposed and built that capitalists bought large areas of cheap land in view of increased values. A poor banking system, or rather a lack of any at all, was a contributing factor. It was impossible to secure enough money to carry on necessary business, real estate greatly depreciated and private families could hardy secure the necessities of life. The next year the state legislature launched a state banking system, and the speculator's lands were

PIONEER METHODS 

77 heavily taxed. These measures proved successful, and gradually conditions improved. 

Scarcely had the farmer began to recover from the panic until a new problem confronted them a crop failure in 1858. Numerous cold rains in the spring either rotted the seed corn or retarded its progress toward maturity. Small grain made a rank growth, but was ruined by excessive heat while ripening. Two thirds of the state was affected, and the courage of the people was sorely tried.

Back in the '50s the government declared that any 40-acre tract of land with more than half or its area wet and unfit for farming, should be known as swamp land, and would pass to the state for reclamation. But after the passing of the "swamp act" complaints became numerous of where the government had sold these wet tracts, when they were supposed to belong to the state of Iowa.

This practice became worse until the "indemnity act" was passed in 1855. This decreed that in cases where the government had sold these tracts after the law of 1850, that the state would be given the money, or land equivalent. In the meantime the state legislature had enacted a law transferring all their rights to the various counties in which the lands were located (1853). 

It is claimed that there were over 500 of these 40-acre tracts in Dallas county that were sold by the government and the money kept. All attempts at collection were futile and finally the county board sold their claims (1891) to J. N. Prouty for $2,300. These claims amounted to $25,000 but the chance of col-

lecting was so poor that the board felt justified in accepting this offer, although this move brought forth considerable criticism.

The first newspaper published in Dallas county was "The Ship of State" in 1856. This, a democratic newspaper, was published by Rippy & Reed in Adel. In 

1857 it bore the name "Prairie Flower" and was edited by Wm. Henry Dodge. It only lasted a few months. 

The next was the Dallas County Union, being established in the summer of 1860. It was discontinued when its editor, E. W. Fuller, went into the army. The next was the Western Journal, by Harmon Cook in 1862, followed by the Dallas County Gazette in 1866. 

This paper continued under different management until 1876, when it, too, was discontinued. During the various changes that took place there were intervals, sometimes as long as three years, when there was no paper at all. The first Fort Des Moines paper was the "Fort Des Moines Star" in 1849.

CHAPTER THE CALIFORNIA GOLD RUSH AND THE CIVIL WAR 

1849-1865 

Discovery of Gold-Dallas County's Position-The State Road -Revelry-Crossing Des Moines River-Hardships-Effect Upon County-War Declared-First Iowa Infantry- The 39th Regiment-Death of Col. Redfields— Andersonville Prison-Poem-The G. A. R. 

List of Veterans. 

A famous event in American history was the discovery of gold in California. Although the discoverers tried to keep it a secret the news got out and people began going from all parts of our country, in search of the precious metal. Not only did they go from every state of the Union but from many foreign countries as well. "Sometimes gold to the value of $1,000 was picked up by a miner in an hour's time. Gold dust was used instead of coined money, and the price of all kinds of provisions became enormously high. For a time there seemed to be no end of the discoveries." 

Within a year San Francisco, then a village of clay huts, had grown to a city of 15,000, with the people living in all kinds of improvised places of abode. 

About $4,000,000 worth of gold was obtained the first year. Is it any wonder that people got the "gold fever?"

What men did then is a striking example of what people will do for money. The hardships, privations, disappointments, sickness and death that followed in an 79

attempt to secure riches makes a dark, pathetic page of American history.

At this particular time Dallas county was on the western frontier of civilization. Between here and the Atlantic, the country was settled; between here and the Pacific was nothing but Indians, adventurers, buffaloes, mountains and vast areas of dry desolate country, and the distance was about 2,500 miles. 

The "fever" struck Dallas county in the fall of 1849 and about 30 of our people left. Their departure was at a time when they were really needed here, for the country was in need of more people rather than less. 

Some of the very best people went, including some county officers. On October 9th, Aaron L. Johns was appointed to take the place of Sheriff John S. Sammis, and later an election was called by the county commissioners to fill the numerous vacancies. 

Before this time, a state road had been established across the state of Iowa which ran from Davenport to Council Bluffs, and passed through Fort Des Moines and Adel. This road was one of the main thoroughfares for the forty-niners as they came from hundreds of eastern points enroute to California. Many departed for "the land of promise" in the fall of 1849, and up until the spring of 1850 there had no reaction begun, consequently enormous numbers of people were in readiness to leave as soon as spring came.

The spring of 1850 was a never-to-be-forgotten one in Adel. Such a scene was never seen before, nor since. The mad rush westward gained momentum the further it went, for every section through which it

CALIFORNIA GOLD RUSH AND CIVIL WAR 81 

passed added some to the swollen numbers. Haste and greed characterized the gold seekers. The old proverb, "The early bird catches the worm” had a real meaning to them, and to get there at the earliest possible time was the all important thing. Delay might cost them a fortune, so they reasoned. 

An eye witness in this vicinity gives the following description of these times: "It seems that Bedlam itself had been let loose. A continuous line of wagons stretched away to the west as far as the eye could see.

If a wagon was detained by being broken down, or by reason of a sick horse or ox, it was dropped out of line and the gap closed up immediately. If a poor mortal should sicken and die, the corpse was buried hurriedly by the wayside, without coffin or burial service. When night came on, the line of wagons was turned aside and their proprietors would go into camp.' "Very soon the sound of revelry would begin around the campfires thickly set on every hand, first to bottle and then to cards, to the echo of the most horrid oaths and imprecations that were ever conceived or uttered since the fall of man." From an issue of the Des Moines 

Gazette published April 26, 1850, we quote the following: "The rush to California still continues with but little abatement. The number of teams that have crossed the Des Moines, at this place, for the week past amount to 199-with 540 men, making a total up to this time of 690 teams and 1,797 persons. * * *

For the past two or three days we have had warm weather, which is bringing the grass up finely, just such a state of things the emigrants have been earnestly

wishing for some time back." Following this interesting item is a list, day by day, of the names of the emigrants and where they were from. 

The real hardships of the journey came when they reached the barren sections of the West. Food for man and beast was well nigh exhausted, water was extremely scarce, many things were thrown aside to lighten the load and enable the lank beasts to make better time, treacherous, blood thirsty Indians would sometimes attack them, sickness and despair seized upon many; yet they pressed on and on, a strange abnormal spirit assuring them that a fortune awaited them in California.

With the passing of such throngs and the lapse of time, people soon came to see that further hopes of securing gold would be in vain, and the tide subsided. 

By June, all had passed into history here, but not there. 

People anxiously awaited news from the West and little by little it became evident that most people had gone in vain; however, a few did secure some of the "famous" gold.

Although the gold rush worked a hardship to many people, yet the whole move had its advantages, chief of which was the public's introduction to wide areas of fertile country still unsettled. Many people from the 

East who passed through here then, being favorably impressed with the country, came a few years later and settled. Among this class was Harvey Willis and 

Mike Shively of Indiana, who went by steamboat to St. Joseph, Mo., and from thence overland to the gold regions. They walked almost the entire distance of

CALIFORNIA GOLD RUSH AND CIVIL WAR 83 

2,000 miles. After meeting with considerable success they started for home (August, 1851) going by boat to Panama, where they walked across to the Atlantic and took a ship for New York. Upon their arrival home they still had some money, but a great deal more experience.

The county's loss in population was more than doubly made up during the summer of 1850 by new settlers coming in. Their demand for corn, flour, and foodstuffs coupled with that of the passing gold seekers exhausted the supply, and before the new crop came on, corn was selling for $1.50 a bushel, and flour as high as $7.00 a hundred.

For years there had been a growing agitation over the slavery question throughout the United States. 

Statesmen had discussed the matter pro and con, but the pioneer settlers, absorbed in their new environment, knew but little about such matters. Their sympathies were with the abolishinists, but they never supposed they would ever be called upon to have any part in settling or deciding matters of this kind. As time went on, the situation grew more and more tense, until finally the climax was reached (April 13, 1861). Fort

Sumter was fired upon. "The effect of this was electrical. It unified the North, and also the South. The war spirit swept over the country like wild fire." 

Three days later, Governor Samuel J. Kirkwood received a telegraph message from the secretary of war. 

It read, "Call made on you by tonight's mail for one regiment of militia for immediate service." The governor in surprize exclaimed, "The President wants a

whole regiment of men! Do you suppose, Mr. Vandever, I can raise that many?" President Lincoln had called for 75,000 volunteers, in view of quelling the rebellion within 90 days. Iowa's quota was 970 men.

The Council Bluffs "Nonpareil" declared that "military discipline and drill were almost total strangers to the people."

Governor Kirkwood's question about being able to raise so many men was soon dispelled, for in 10 days a regiment was ready, another had been accepted, and a third was anxiously awaiting. The companies of the 

First Iowa Infantry were mobilized at Keokuk and on May 14th were inducted into the federal service. 

For a month these men remained there drilling and learning the tactics of war. On June 13th they were floating down the Mississippi ready for service. It is not at all likely that any of these men were from Dallas county because the eastern counties and cities had some militia, but Dallas county, being sparcely settled had done nothing in a military way. Iowa troops were mobilized at Iowa City, Clinton, Des Moines, Davenport, Mt. Pleasant and Council Bluffs.

It would be impossible to relate the various interesting experiences of the gallant veterans who took part in the great conflict, even of our own county, but let us notice the record of one, outstanding, from our county-Col. James Redfield, and the 39th Infantry.

This man with characteristic promptitude and energy, organized a company of volunteers, which later became a part of the 39th Regiment of Iowa Volunteers. Governor Kirkwood, recognized his ability, appointed him

CALIFORNIA GOLD RUSH AND CIVIL WAR 85 

as lieutenant colonel, and he received his commission on September 12, 1862. The companies gathered in 

Des Moines, and on October 26th, moved on to Davenport where they were speadily uniformed and equipped. On November 24th, they were mustered into the service of the United States. 

While in camp at Davenport, nearly 300 of them had the measles, and were scarcely over their sickness when they were ordered to the front. On December 

13th, they left by rail to report to Brigadier General

Tuttle at Cairo, Ill. Two days later they were transferred to a steamboat and taken down the river to Columbus. Here they had their first taste of the hardships of war. It was dark, rainy and muddy and about a week before Christmas. The shelter tents were raised, and on the cold ground they made their bed. A few days later they entrained for Corinth, Miss. 

Before they reached their destination they encountered the enemy; the railroad was cut off, and for four days, they laid behind earthworks momentarily expecting an attack. Here the men suffered much from short rations, being unable to cook what food they had. Soon they were required to make a long march with each man carrying five days' rations and 100 pounds of ammunition. Many had recently recovered from the measles, rations had been short and they had lost much sleep, consequently many of the men were worn out, and one morning over a hundred were unable to proceed further. During the day these men tried to make their way back to Trenton, but at Shady Grove were

surrounded by a regiment of Forrest's Cavalry and 

101 were taken prisoners.

Later in the winter Redfield's men came in contact with Forrest's whole command numbering over 6,000 men. They fought from 9:00 o'clock in the morning until 3:00 in the afternoon under great disadvantages.

Upon the arrival of reinforcements, Forrest fled leaving many prisoners, horses, cannon and small arms. 

On January 7, 1863, they reached Corinth, their original intended destination. Here they remained until the following November, doing garrison duty. On May 6th,

Co. H, which was guarding a corral a few miles from 

Corinth, was surrounded by 800 rebel cavalry and the captain and most of the company were made prisoners.

In November, they with others, moved into Tennessee where they had 25 miles of railroad to hold, and maintain order. In March, 1864, they marched into 

Georgia, and at Altoon. Pass, on October 5th they suffered their greatest loss and defeat. Eight companies of the 39th Regiment engaged in this conflict, and 165 men, or nearly three-fifths of their number were lost, including Colonel Redfield. At first the heroic colonel was wounded in the foot; a second shot shattered his leg, but undaunted and seated on the ground, he continued to direct the fight, but a third ball pierced his heart and he passed into eternity. In the spring of 

1865, his nephew, Ward Redfield, brought the remains to the town that bears his name and permanent burial was made. In 1868, a fine marble monument, 12 feet in height, was erected over his grave.

CALIFORNIA GOLD RUSH AND CIVIL WAR 87 

Dallas county men were much scattered, having enlisted at different times, and being assigned to different camps for training. However, a good many seemed to have been in General Sherman's army, and participated in his famous march to the sea. Before the war was over there were not enough volunteers responding, and it became necessary to draft men into the service. 

Names of qualified men were drawn by lot, and it was permissible to hire a substitute to go in one's place. 

In some cases people were given 160 acres of land for going for another, or enough money to buy it. 

No account of the Civil War would be complete without some mention of the Libby and Andersonville prisons. It hardly seems possible that humans would subject helpless fellowmen to such privation, exposure and suffering. Possibly the southern methods of dealing with slaves had so hardened their hearts that they thought nothing of treating humans worse than dogs. 

Capt. Willard Glazier in his book, "The Capture,

Prison, Pen and Escape," gives an accurate and unbiased description of these rebel prisons. Of Andersonville he says, "The poor fellows were obliged to lie night after night, week after week, on the cold damp ground, without even fire to warm themselves by." 

He further states that there could have been no good reason for not granting wood for this purpose, because there was much timber on three sides of the prison, and the men would have been glad to prepare it. 

The greater portion of this prison was a large lot surrounded by a high board fence, upon the top of which paced many guards, day and night, each carry-

ing a big army musket. Within the enclosure, some distance from the edge, was a "dead line" and if for any reason a poor inmate should reach this line he would be shot down without mercy by the vigilant guards.

Through this pen flowed a small stream of water, from which the prisoners must secure all their water for drinking, cooking, and washing bodies and clothing. 

With hundreds of men incarcerated under such conditions during the scorching heat of summer we may imagine the unsanitary conditions that prevailed. Half starved and clothed, exposed to the elements and surrounded by the most unsanitary conditions imaginable, many became sick and died. Mr. Glazier says that during the months of July and August as many as 75 would sometimes die in one day, and each morning they were hauled out by the wagon load and buried. like dead stock.

In addition to the diseases and physical sufferings of the men, there was an intense mental strain. Good and honorable men compelled to endure such treatment, feeling the symptoms of sickness, seeing their comrades dying all around them, and wondering what the future held in store, was enough to try the souls of the strongest of men. As night came on, and the camp became fairly quiet, and the friendly moon shone down upon them, a comrade's mind would go back to the home folks in "God's country." The only pleasure they could enjoy was the hallowed memory of home, mother, wife and children. This was a veritable paradise. This forced separation was almost more than the mind could

CALIFORNIA GOLD RUSH AND CIVIL WAR 89 

endure. "How long will this last?" "Will I ever see them again?" These were questions uppermost in their minds. 

Finally, about midnight, the comrade in spite of his physical suffering and mental anguish falls asleep, only to be awakened at daylight by swarms of flies that infest the place. During these trying times the Bible was eagerly sought and read by those fortunate enough to possess one, and its message furnished comfort, cheer and hope to many a weary soul.

Should any prisoners escape, as they frequently did, they were run down by blood hounds in a most unmerciful manner. Scurvy, a vile disease, caused by lack of vegetable food, was very prevalent. Vermin galore abounded in all the southern prisons. Besides this notorious Andersonville prison in Georgia, there was the Libby prison at Richmond, Virginia, almost as bad, and the Salisbury prison in North Carolina. Mr. Richardson, correspondent of the New York Tribune, says the death rate at Salisbury prison was 13 per cent per month. Mr. Glazier, before mentioned, asserts that the deaths at Andersonville totaled 13,000. In view of the awful carnage of the battle and the sufferings of these "earthly hells" is it any wonder that General 

Sherman said, "War is hell." 

The following poem taken from "The Capture,

Prison, Pen and Escape" is said to represent a true incident and was written by G. H. Hallister of Litchfield, Connecticut.

No blanket round his wasted limbs, Under the rainy sky he slept; 

While pointing his envenomed shaft, Around him Death, the urcher, crept;

He dreamed of hunger, and held out 

His hand to clutch a little bread, That a white Angel with a torch,

Among the living and the dead, Seemed bearing, smiling as he went;

The vision waked him, as he spied The post boy followed by a crowd 

Of famished prisoners, who cried

For letters-letters from their friends. 

Crawling upon his hands and knees 

He hears his own name called, and lo! 

A letter from his wife he sees! 

Gasping for breath, he shrieked aloud, And, lost in nature's blind eclipse,

Faltering amid the suppliant crowd, Caught it and pressed it to his lips.

A guard who followed, red and wroth, And flourishing a rusty brand, Reviled him with a taunting oath, And snatched the letter from his hand, "First pay the postage, whining wretch!" 

Despair had made the prisoner brave, "Then give me back my money, sir! 

I am a captive-not a slave! 

You took my money and my clothes; 

Take my life too-but let me know 

How Mary and the children are, And I will bless you 'ere I go."

The very moonlight through his hands As he stood supplicating, shone,

CALIFORNIA GOLD RUSH AND CIVIL WAR 91 

And his sharp features shaped themselves Into a prayer, and such a tone 

Of anguish there was in his cry,

For wife and children, that the guard- Thinking upon his own-passed by, And left him swooning on the sword. 

Beyond the "dead line" fell his head—

The eager sentry knew his mark, And with a crash the bullet sped 

Into his brain and all was dark! 

But when they turned his livid cheek 

Up toward the light, the pale lips smiled, Kissing a picture fair and meek 

That held in either hand a child.

As awful as the Civil War was, it was necessary to perpetuate the American idea of liberty and freedom, conceived by our forefathers in the eighteenth century; and those who sacrificed their lives that the Union might survive, and crush the tyrannical power of slavery, have reason to be honored today by those who enjoy the fruit of their labors.

A product of these stirring times was the writing of our patriotic songs, "Marching Through Georgia" and "Tenting Tonight on the Old Camp Ground."

These, with others, have given inspiration and fervor to our people from that time on, and add greatly to the meaning of our annual Memorial Day services. True patriotism, based on righteousness, is an asset to any nation. 

Finally after four years of bloody conflict, the cause of freedom was won, and as a fitting tribute to the brave soldiers, the Grand Army of the Republic tri-

umphantly marched down Pennsylvania Avenue in Washington, past the capitol, to be viewed by the President, and an appreciative public. Gradually, Iowa and 

Dallas county men were discharged at various places, and returned to resume their former occupations. 

The first G. A. R. post in Iowa was the one organized in Davenport on July 24, 1866. In due time other posts were organized throughout the state. Post No. 

26 at Perry was called the Redfield Post, in honor of Col. James Redfield who valiantly died in service as before mentioned. Colonel Mills G. A. R. Post in 

Adel was organized in June, 1881. 

The first annual state encampment was held at Keokuk in 1875 and these have continued annually since that time. Today there are only a comparatively few veterans remaining in Iowa. During the first eight months of 1937, 86 of these died and in a short time. all will be gone. The last veteran in Adel died a few months ago, and only two still survive in Perry.

The following Dallas county men took part in this great war. Those who were officers are designated with a *, but many are of low rank, even musicians and wagoners being included in the original list.

This list was believed to be a complete list of Dallas county men, but several omissions have just been added, and it is very probable that there are yet others overlooked. Moreover, it must be borne in mind that many old soldiers of our acquaintance, were at that time, from other places, and have come among us since 1865.

SECTION TWO THE CONSTRUCTION PERIOD

CHAPTER RAILROAD BUILDING 

1868-1882 Expansion and Prosperity-First Train-race to the Missouri 

River Narrow Gauge Railroad-Milwaukee Main Line- 

Angus Boom-Granger Line.

We have now passed the pioneer stage—the railroads have come-a new era has begun. For the next 

30 years we shall consider a great construction period. 

Railroads are built; towns spring up; the population rapidly increases; farms are developed; business expands. In many ways it was the best period of our history; the poor man had a chance to get started; bright futures were ahead, and everybody could have something to do. Dallas county had already proven itself to be a fertile and productive country, capable of producing much more than its own needs, but due to inadequate means of transportation had developed very little beyond its crude beginning. 

The first train in Iowa to carry passengers was run from Davenport to Walcott on August 28, 1855.

Exactly eleven years later the first train rolled into Des Moines. This line, paralleling the Des Moines river, came up from Keokuk, and was called the Des 

Moines Valley railroad.

During the fall of 1865 great cargoes of railroad equipment were unloaded from steamboats at Omaha and work on the Union Pacific railroad was begun. 

Its completion across the prairies, and on to Cali- 101

fornia by 1869 was one of the outstanding achievements of American history. At this time (1865) the 

Burlington was built slightly beyond Ottumwa; the 

Rock Island had reached Kellogg, and the Northwestern was running trains to Boone. All were anxious to reach Omaha first and connect with the Union Pacific. 

The Northwestern won the race on January 22, 1867.

The others did not arrive until more than two years later.

The main line of the Rock Island through the southern part of the county was the first railroad to enter Dallas county; this being done in 1868-69 in its race for the Missouri river. This road, at first being called the Mississippi & Missouri River Railroad, was begun at Davenport in May, 1854, and completed that year to Iowa City, a distance of 54½ miles. Iowa City was then the capital, and this place remained the western terminus for several years. Gradually it was built westward as occasion required, until the last wide gap from Des Moines to Council Bluffs was built. 

The next railroad to cross the county, about the same time, was the Des Moines & Fort Dodge line.

This was an extension of the Des Moines Valley road, and was truly a pioneer north and south road. This road, unlike most roads of that time, made a diagonal bee line across the county instead of the crooked, winding course of many others.

It may be a surprise to many to know that Adel went along for nearly 30 years without a railroad, but such is a fact. Both of the county's railroads missed 

Adel, and there was no prospect of Adel being reached

RAILROAD BUILDING 

103 by rail. The big companies were too busy building main lines to consider any spurs at that time. But Adel was the county seat; besides it was the oldest and largest town in the county (815 in 1875). They could not hold their prestige and business, and possibly the county seat, too, without a railroad. Their situation was both humiliating and embarrassing-they must have a railroad.

Finally the business men of Adel decided to build a railroad of their own. A company was organized, and on April 12, 1878, incorporated as the Des Moines, Adel & Western Railroad Co. The officers were: 

President.. 

Vice President. 

Treasurer Secretary. 

.Dr. T. J. Caldwell J. W. Russell .L. Lambert 

. A. C. Hotchkiss

The proposed railroad was to run from Waukee to Adel, a distance of 6½ miles. Adel people set to work with great enthusiasm, both in raising the capital and doing the grading. Ralph E. Joy, well known ex-county official, states that when a boy he drove a team on a scraper for 50 cents a day and dinner. Many others living today took an active part. By winter the road was completed at least to the river; but to build a bridge across the river was too much at first. In addition to the inconvenience of crossing the river for train service there were other disadvantages as well. 

In the first place it was a narrow gauge road; i. e., the rails were much closer together than a standard guage line. Narrow gauges range from three feet to three

and a half feet in width, while a standard gauge throughout our country and most foreign lands have a width of four feet and 8½ inches. At first there was only a "dinky" engine and one boxcar. This car carried passengers, freight and all, with the passengers sitting on seats along the wall; yet the people of Adel were jubilant and grateful for any kind of rail service. An engine house was built for the locomotive, but the car had to stand out.

The next task was to raise more money in view of bridging the river. On January 31, 1879, a contract was let to Jonathan Peppard to build a bridge and approaches for $4,740, and to be completed within 90 days. On Saturday, April 26th, all was in readiness. for the first train to cross the river and really arrive at the station. The entire population turned out; children gathered at the school house and marched to the depot, headed by the Adel Cornet Band. Stores were closed and the business section was deserted. An interesting program, in charge of Coel Noel, was given, including a talk by Benjamin Greene, who concluded by eulogizing Dr. Caldwell and presenting him with a beautiful gold headed cane, bearing the inscription, "Presented to J. T. Caldwell by his friends, April, 1879." Professor Phillips then spoke in an effective and appropriate manner, and ended by presenting Mr. Greene with a cane also; being an exact counterpart of the one given Dr. Caldwell.

From the start the promoters had intentions of building westward as it became possible, and during the next few years the line was extended through Redfield,

RAILROAD BUILDING 

105 Panora, Herndon, Jefferson and on to the north as far as Fonda, 113 miles from Des Moines. As the line grew, more equipment was added and better service maintained. The company after some years had financial troubles and Polk & Hubbell, Des Moines capitalists, acquired the controlling interest. They leased the line to the Wabash company for a term of years, after which it was managed by the Des Moines men. During the early days of the road the little engine was often jokingly called the "teakettle."

The county's next line of railroad was the main line of the Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul through the northern edge of the county in 1881. At that time the 

Milwaukee was the largest railroad system in the world, and when it became known that they were to build a main line through Perry the little town of a trifle over 1,000 people received "the thrill that comes but once in a lifetime.”

The construction work in this vicinity began on June 10, 1881. The right-of-way through Perry was purchased by public spirited citizens and donated to the railroad company. In September rumors were afloat about making Perry a division station, but no definite action was taken until November 18th, when a railroad meeting was held in Union Hall. At this time a committee consisting of D. J. Pattee, W. L. Warren and 

H. H. Cardell was appointed to confer with the Milwaukee officials to ascertain what would be required to locate the shops here.

Not until the following February did the company announce their decision. They agreed to locate the

division here if suitable ground would be donated. A committee got busy at once to raise money, and in only a week's time reported that the Waldo tract of 20 acres northeast of town had been contracted. The bridge across the Des Moines river was completed on 

March 3d and the next day the first train reached

Perry, being able to go as far as Coon Rapids, and on 

June 30th the line was completed to Council Bluffs.

Between 1880 and 1885 Perry enjoyed its greatest boom. It became the home of many railroad people; many new business men came, including G. R. Melick and the Diddy brothers, as well as scores of families engaged in other pursuits. With its roundhouse, shops, switching trains, and shrieking whistles it presented the appearance of a bustling little city. The population jumped from 999 in 1880, to 2,573 in 1885. Perry was "sitting on top of the world."

At the same time that Perry was booming, the town of Angus, only a few miles up the railroad was at its height as a coal mining town. About nine big mining companies were in operation and it was said to be the largest mining town in the state. The population suddenly jumped to between five and seven thousandso they say, but official census figures only show 1,673 in 1885. Possibly between census taking times it may have been much larger than the recorded figure. 

In 1885 a steady decline began. Five years later there were only 704 people, and in 1895 the number had dwindled to 473. Houses were torn down or moved to nearby farmsteads, and to Perry, where scores of homes were either wholly or in part built of the salvaged

RAILROAD BUILDING 

107 material. Angus optimism once hoped to outclass Des 

Moines and become an important railroad center, but today it is only a flag station on the railroad, hardly able to maintain one store.

The next line of railroad to be built was the line running partially through the eastern edge of the county and serving Granger, Madrid and Boone. This was the year after the Milwaukee was built and when the new line wished to build across the Milwaukee, the officials promptly refused permission, but after a time of negotiation, terms favorable to the Milwaukee were agreed upon and permission given. Prior to the coming of the Milwaukee, Madrid citizens had been wishing for a railroad, and in one of the railroad meetings that they frequently held, one of the citizens arose to speak saying in substance, "We have been wanting a railroad, now we are to have two." A wooden trestle of unusual height was built over the Des Moines river, and this bridge became known as “high bridge."

This line was built by the St. Louis, Des Moines and 

Northern Railway Co. On February 8, 1895, it was sold under foreclosure to G. M. Dodge, F. M. Hubbell and F. C. Hubbell, who conveyed the property to the 

Des Moines Northern and Western Railway Co., which was the same line as ran through Adel and Redfield. On 

May 1, 1899, the property and franchise of both lines were sold to the Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul Railroad Co., and has so remained ever since.

With the completion of this road, Dallas county had four lines of railroad, two east and west lines, and two north and south, and all were built within a period

of 14 years. These railroads meant wonders to the development of the county and the prosperity of its citizens. About 1875 a proposed railroad survey was made east and west across the county passing through Minburn. This company was called the "Des Moines. & Western Midland Railroad," but never got beyond the surveying stage. Another road was surveyed and some grading done south of Angus on a proposed line to St. Louis. This was during the palmy days of Angus' mining boom, but the railroad died before its birth.

CHAPTER  

1847-1900 

Original Plat-State Road-Stage Coaches-Crude Postal Service Ferry-Early Courthouses-Third Courthouse Gregg School-Churches-Soldiers' Reunion-Dr. Henderson. 

The early history of Adel is one of unusual interest, due to the fact that its existence dates back to 184790 years ago. The town experienced many things that 

Perry cannot boast of. Adel is not only the oldest town in the county, but it existed more than 20 years before Perry was even thought of. The original town of Adel (Penouch then) was begun on land originally owned by John Miller. Among the earliest residents were J. C. Corbell, Geo. Warden, Wm. Ellis, Ira Sherman and Benjamin Greene, the two latter being pioneer merchants. Mr. Green's store was opposite the northeast corner of the square.

The original town plat extended from the river to High street, and from Grove street on the north to Green street on the south. The names of the streets from Grove street south to Greene street were Rapids, Court, Main, Prairie, and Ferry street. From the river westward they were Benton, Em, Plum, Race, Vine, and Walnut. The names of the north and south streets have since been changed to numbers, so that now the courthouse is bounded by Main, Eighth, Court and Ninth streets. To the north were out-lots. By 1875 109

two additions had been annexed, Rice's on the west and 

Greene's on the south.

The first road in Dallas county was a portion of the old state road running from Davenport to Council Bluffs. The second general assembly of the state legislature appointed John Wright, Jesse Richman and John Wyckoff to locate this road through Dallas county. This appointment was on January 12, 1849, and on June 29th they proceeded with their task, choosing a route running through Adel and Redfield. The distance across the county was 25 miles and 8 chains. 

For a long time this was the only road upon which any time or money was spent.

This old road is famous for at least two reasons. 

The gold seekers trekked over this route, and for years it was the course of one of the most important stage routes in Iowa, and extended from the on-coming railroads to Council Bluffs. Many interesting stories of the old stage days make an interesting subject for the youngsters of today. Mr. Elmer Diddy of Perry, now 

82, delights to tell of the stage coach when he was a boy, and his father, Levi Diddy, was the stage agent. 

He says, "You could never tell just when it would come, but when it was about a half mile away the driver would blow a horn." Adel was a full-fledged station, with a large double shed capable of holding two complete outfits, horses and all. While passengers and mail were unloading, and others taking their places, skilled hands were quickly changing the horses. As soon as all was ready, the driver, seated on his high seat and exposed to all kinds of weather, would crack

his whip and away they would go. This did very well for those times, when weather and roads were good, but many were the woes of a stage driver when conditions were bad. Only the hardiest kind of a person would qualify for this work, and the same was true of the horses.

In those days there was quite a net-work of stage routes. Several traversed Dallas county. The line from 

Boone to Council Bluffs passed Mr. Clarke's stage house at Pilot Lake in Lincoln township. The stage which stopped at the Seth Dayton brick house in 

Beaver township ran from Des Moines to Fort Dodge and was known as the "half way house." Another line is said to have run up from Des Moines through Des Moines township to old Xenia and probably on to Boone, and another line ran from Des Moines to Jefferson in an early time over the "diagonal road" west of the present site of Perry. The old Stebbins house, which set on a slant, near Perry, was built on the diagonal road of that early route. 

In 1847 the county commissioners duly appointed Thomas Butler as Lot Fund Agent, whose duty it was to sell the surveyed lots of Penouch to anyone wishing to buy. On October 6th he sold nine of these lots, but the demand was so poor and the price so low that they were, for a time, withdrawn from the market. Only a few more sold during the next few years. Upon lot 

5 in block 8, J. C. Corbell built the first house in 

Penouch. It was a log house, and made and used for the double purpose of a dwelling house and a post office. 

Judge Burns tells the following story of this primi-

tive post office: "S. R. Scovell was deputy postmaster in 1848, under Squire Corbell, chief. In June of that year we called at the post office at Penouch for a magazine we were taking at that time. We found the deputy postmaster sprawled on the dirt floor of the office, with his leonine head pillowed on a rick of 'Session Laws,' fast asleep. Intone, the deputy got up, yawning and scratching his head, and began to look through his pillow, in which the uncalled for mail was interlaid, for the magazine asked for. It could not be found, and the deputy remarked that, 'it must be here somewhere, for I have certainly read it. May be possible it has blown through these turnal cracks, for its powerful windy here sometimes. I'll look and see.'

The deputy went out doors, and, on looking around in the tall grass, found it some 30 feet away, where, sure enough it had been blown." Until the mail and stage route was begun it was a part of the postmaster's duties to go to Fort Des Moines and carry the mail to Penouch. 

On July 7, 1851, the board of commissioners fixed the price of the outlots at various prices ranging in value from $10 to $30, and that one lot, No. 15, be granted to the M. E. Church at Congress price.

On March 6, 1850, the commissioners granted a license to Horatio Morrison to run a ferry across the river where the state road ran. This permit was for five years and the cost was $2.00 annually. The rates established were as follows: 

Four-horse team and wagon. 

Two-horse team and wagon. 

One horse and man. .50c .35c 15c

Footman 

Cattle per head. 

Sheep and swine.. 5c 5c 3c The first burying ground was a short distance south of town, but not proving satisfactory, a change was made to a place a mile and half west of town, but after a few years it, too, was abandoned. Then the present Oak Dale cemetery was begun north of town. 

The Dallas County News was established by Capt. 

Amos Dilley in November, 1872. He was succeeded by J. M. Landis, S. H. Greene, Noel & Newton, J. B. 

White, Wm. Roberts, J. W. Russell, S. M. Leach and finally to A. C. Hutchkiss. For many years Mr. Hutchkiss ably edited the paper, which was a credit to Dallas county. "The New Era" was begun by John McLaury in 1878. This was followed by the Democrat published by T. R. North and later by Sam Carrell, and finally published by A. W. Vaughn under the name of 

Dallas County Record.

Old court records show that the first district court was held at the house of William Miller, and that there was no case on the docket. The jury room was out in the open air under the foliage of nearby trees. This historic session in charge of Judge James Charlton of Iowa City, ordered that the eagle side of an American 

25 cent piece be used temporarily, as the county seal until a proper seal could be secured. 

The center of attraction in Adel has always been the courthouse, and every citizen of the county is more or less interested in what is done there. For several generations the people have paid their taxes,

secured their marriage licenses, heard court, recorded their deeds, etc., at the courthouse; therefore a detailed account of its development should interest the whole population.

Necessarily the history of the courthouse goes back to the beginning of the county, and the first building was in keeping with the times and circumstances, as we shall presently see. On July 8, 1848, Buel Lathrop purchased lot 8 in block 9 in the town of Penouch. 

Upon this lot he built a double log cabin which he soon afterwards sold to the county for a temporary courthouse. This cabin was made of hewn cottonwood logs, and roofed with clapboards of the same material. 

This roofing material of course, did not carry any guarantee against leakage, and the architectural design was not elaborate, yet within these crude walls important business of the county was satisfactorily transacted. 

The only two judges who presided over terms of district court in this place were Hon. James P. Charlton and 

Hon. William McKay.

In 1853 a new courthouse was built on lot 4, block 17 across the street and south from the square. This building was a great improvement over the first, being a one-story frame structure, 40 feet long and 20 feet wide. Judges who served in this location were Hon. 

William McKay, Hon. Chas. J. McFarland and Hon. 

William M. Stone.

From the beginning of the town a square in the center had been reserved for a courthouse, but apparently there was no inclination to build until a permanent and commodious structure was needed and could be

afforded. At an election in April 2, 1855, a vote was taken on levying a tax for building a new courthouse, but the measure was lost by 99 to 179 votes. Two years later the population had become greatly increased and the need of larger quarters became more evident, so another vote was taken. This time the proposition carried by a good majority; there being 

401 votes for and 240 against it. In June of 1857 the contract for building was let to C. Rodenback for $9,980 and he erected the building the following year. 

This third courthouse was considered a magnificent structure. It was built of brick and was two stories high. The lower story was divided into five offices and the upper was equipped for a courtroom. The dimensions of the building was about 42 by 64 feet, and the total cost complete was around $20,000. 

By 1874 the office space was inadequate to meet the need and another building was erected at the southwest corner of the square. This building of brick construction, was one story high, and built in the shape of the letter L. The offices of treasurer, clerk and recorder were moved to the new quarters. Many years later, when these offices began to have a shabby appearance, they were nick-named the county "stables". 

For nearly a half century this third courthouse was used, and scores of the "old timers" can recall memories, both good and bad, of those days, as the judge handed down his decision on legal and civil matters. 

For years Judge Burns was a familiar character around Adel and the courthouse. Others of this period were Judges Wilkerson, McHenry, Nichols and Gamble.

ALLAS COUNT 

18 58 0.5.1 

J. M. ROLAND W. L. MITCHELL RALPH E. JOY THE OLD COURTHOUSE

About 1853 Cole Noel opened up a general store. 

As early as 1868 Adel could boast of a three-story brick store building housing the large and elegant Risser store. Although nearly 70 years old this building still adorns Adel's business district, and is in a good state of preservation. The lower floor is occupied by the 

Cozad and Forrester drug store. The oldest brick building in Adel is a small one-story building used by 

J. E. Godown as a barber shop and is about a half block west of Ninth and Main streets. This building is said to be about 80 years old.

The first hotel was a small frame building, which later became a part of the Hill house. For a long time it was the only hotel in town and was operated by R. 

R. Bailey. During these times it was a stage station on the line of the Western Stage Company, operating from Davenport to Council Bluffs. In 1855 the "Plank 

House" was built by Jacob Frush, and occupied the site later used by the Forrester House. The "Plank 

House" derived its name from the fact that it was built of planks which was an unusual way of building. 

To this new place the stage station was moved where it remained until the line was discontinued about the time of the completion of the Rock Island railroad through the southern part of the country. In 1855 there were not more than twenty-five houses in town, and only three stores; besides Mr. Noel's, there were those of Wm. Ellis, and E. D. Smith. But after about this time the town grew more rapidly. 

An important matter of Dallas county history is the creation of the "Gregg Fund" and the "Gregg

Seminary". Years ago, Thomas D. Gregg, wealthy land owner of the Adel vicinity, deeded 160 acres of his land to Dallas county. The purpose of this gift was to establish within the county, a school teaching higher branches than were taught in the common schools.

The deed was dated September 15, 1866, and at the next session of the board of supervisors they accepted the donation in the name of the county, and pledged the people of the county to faithfully carry out the desires of Mr. Gregg. In January 1867 the Gregg Normal 

School Association was formed and incorporated, and the business entrusted to a board of twelve trustees consisting of the state superintendent, county superintendent and chairman of the board of supervisors. 

The others chosen were Benjamin Greene, J. R. Reed, T. J. Caldwell, John Maulsby, F. S. Graham, William

Campbell, Cole Noel, L. M. Holt and E. Willard. At the same time J. W. Redfield, J. R. Reed and T. J.

Caldwell were commissioned to effect a sale of the land. 

In due time negotiations were made with the Adel independent school district to build an additional story to their proposed two-story building; $5,000 was paid the Adel independent district by this plan and the Gregg interests assumed one-third interest in the building and grounds. The proceeds of the land apparently lacked $400 of reaching the required amount, but this was made up by an appropriation from the swamp land fund. 

Mr. Gregg died in 1876, leaving a will that donated additional property to the county, which when sold,

amounted to $9,837.55 in cash. The district court, in 

May, 1889, ordered that the Gregg fund be turned over to a board of trustees. Those appointed were J. W. 

Russell, Lemuel Warford and T. A. Thornburg. At this time the fund had grown to $12,424.50. 

The first school house in Adel was a one-story frame building in the west part of town on Main street. After a few years this structure was removed and a twostory brick building was erected on the same site. This school was used until 1869 when the combined Adel and Gregg school was built at the present location. 

This school building was 45 by 90 feet. Among the first teachers of the town were James Reed and Miss Anderson.

This is the Had a person been on the streets of Adel 80 years ago they would have seen an unusual sight-they might have imagined they were in old Mexico for up the street comes a vehicle drawn by an ox. town dray; but business is not so rushing and the old ox seems to meet every need. Sam Loomis, the proud owner, had broken him remarkably well, and when he sold him he brought the fine sum of $100. 

Adel's first bank was operated by members of the Van Meter family in a small room on the south side of the square. They were followed by F. S. Graham and later Leroy Lambert; and finally Wm. Brenton became owner. The Adel State Bank was organized by 

S. M. Leach in 1882, and occupied a fine two-story brick building at the southwest corner of the square. 

The Dallas County Savings Bank was organized in

1893 and was housed in a fine brick building at the southeast corner of the square. 

Adel's secret societies, or lodges, were organized as

The first elders were Samuel Miller, Wm. Davidson and Leroy Lambert, and the well known Judge Burns was one of its influential members. The early meeting were held at private homes, the schoolhouse and the old courthouse. Their first building was erected in 1868, and in 1890 an addition was built on the west.

The Methodist church was organized as a mission in 1850, and included all of Dallas county and parts of Polk and Greene as well. Preaching services were held in six different places, and the first pastor received a salary of $152. In 1862 the first building was erected. This was replaced by a new and better structure which was dedicated by Rev. W. B. Ridgeway on September 12, 1886. The first board of trustees were I. J. Cole, Lewis Jolly, George Noel, James 

L. Snyder, Pierson Young, Samuel Witham and Wil liam R. Rigg.

Wm. Roberts was Sunday school superintendent for many years.

The Presbyterian church was organized July 6, 1856, by Rev. Thompson Bird with eleven charter members.

Until they were able to build they met in the schoolhouse, courthouse and in the Methodist church. In 

1868 they erected a 32 by 48 brick church on a lot donated by S. J. Garroutte one block south of the southwest corner of the square. It was dedicated by 

Rev. T. O. Rice on September 6, 1868. The first elders were E. D. Smith, F. S. Graham and H. H. Moffatt.

A company of militia-Company A of the First Regiment, was organized in Adel in May, 1878. They had an armory over Houghton's drug store, and regularly drilled one hour each week. Among a full list of noncommissioned officers was Capt. Amos Dilley. 

The military spirit seems to have been running high in 1878. Following the formation of the militia in 

May the old soldiers met for their first reunion on

August 17th, when a large crowd gathered amid great interest and enthusiasm. It was decided to have another grand reunion of two days during the time of the county fair in September. Everything was done to make it a success. About 150 were registered and it was estimated that that many more failed to do so. 

The following officers were chosen for the ensuing year: Colonel..... 

Lieutenant Colonel. Major.... Adjutant.
