D. J. Pattee

Amos Dilley .J. R. Joy 

.T. R. North .A. H. Chase .T. J. Caldwell

The town of Adel was incorporated in 1856. During the March term of court William Ellis and 47 others presented a petition praying for a town organization, proving to the satisfaction of the court that there were 300 inhabitants in the town, and that one fourth of the legal voters were subscribed to the petition. At an election, ten days later, the proposition carried, and articles of incorporation were drawn by Jeremiah Perkins and E. D. Smith. In August, 1867, the town gave up its first charter, and re-organized under the new state law regarding town corporations. 

No doubt the best known gristmill of the county during the 19th century was the flour mill at Adel. 

This mill was originally built in 1857 by J. H. Strong and H. H. Moffatt at a cost of $20,000. Like many enterprises, it had a rather up and down experience, and was managed at various times by Messrs Hill, Farlow, North, Noumger and Van Meter. In 1889 the company was organized with L. Swearingen, president and J. W. Russell, secretary. At this time extensive improvements were made, including steam power, and the capacity was materially increased. 

No early history of Adel would be complete without mention of the eccentric Dr. Henderson, who came in the spring of 1848. He was highly educated and master of a dozen languages, but displayed many peculiar eccentricities in all that he did or said. If called on a case where the patient was most sure to die, Judge Burns says that after the customary procedure he

would likely say: "Well, sir, you're sick, very sick, sir, and if you have any prayers to make be about it, sir, at once. There is no use of me nauseating your stomach with a prescription that can do no good; you must die, sir, and if you have a will to make, make it. Don't be deluded by a fallacious hope that you will live, or even grow better; you will do neither. "Still, sir, if you have money to spare foolishly, I am willing to watch by your bedside and smooth your pathway to the grave as best I can, sir. I will charge you $20 for this interview, but, sir, if the money is forthcoming now $10 will pay the bill. I await your answer, sir." A pause, the money is paid, the doctor looks it over carefully and says, "Do you wish my professional services longer?" "Gu-ess not," is the response, and the doctor moves carefully out, and the patient, sure enough, dies, as the doctor had predicted. 

Despite his ancestral and educational advantages he failed to make good, and carelessly drifted down into a life of sin. In less than a year he left, only to do worse in the next place, and become a direlict on the shores of time.

The most serious fire that Adel suffered years ago was in October, 1895, when the McLaughlin block was burned. The loss, consisting of opera house, stores and offices amounted to $30,000. Adel could boast of paving about 15 years before Perry enjoyed such improvements. About 1895, brick paving was put in all around the square, and for a short distance down each street leading therefrom.

Among the leading business men of these times were: 

Victor Sweely.

CHAPTER  

1869-1900 

Early Settlers-Harvey and John Willis-Churches-Cemeteries-Old Mill-Banking-Potts Lake-Fair Grounds -Produce-Saloons-Vigilants-Newspapers- Big Fire 

It is 1868; the county boasts of four towns-Xenia, Redfield, Wiscotta and Adel; the latter having a population of about 600, and the county probably 8,000. 

Most of the county was settled only along the larger streams. Vast stretches of open prairie, even though purchased by investors, was not settled. There was no Perry, altho Adel had existed for over 20 years. 

Nestled along the Raccoon timberline was a string of early settlers. A few were some distance out on the prairie, but not many. On the present site of 

Perry was the farms of two brothers; Harvey and John 

Willis. The present Willis Avenue was the line between the two farms. Not far to the northwest were the homesteads of Cornelius and George McKean, while to the southwest lived F. Wilson Hawk, Dr. Mowrer, John Roberts, Joseph Phillips, Lewis Thornburg, and

Patrick Graney. There were also settlers west of the river, and along Beaver creek. In the vicinity of the present Hall bridge Haratio Hall had 40 acres of timber. Here he erected the first steam sawmill in 

Dallas county. Before the bridge was built a ford was at this point called Hall's ford. 126

One day the news came that a railroad was being surveyed through the country, between Des Moines and 

Ft. Dodge. Straight as a bee line it came across the prairie, usually within sight of the Raccoon river timber. Fortunately for the Willis brothers their land extended out further from the river than the others and the survey went directly across their land. Of course there must be new towns created, and the Willis' saw their opportunity to secure a townsite upon their land. This being satisfactory with the railroad officials the boys proceeded to survey and plat the proposed town during the spring of 1869, giving five acres and 32 lots to the company, and with the understanding that the name of the town be Perry, in honor of Col. 

Perry of Keokuk, one of the officials of the railroad.

The first train to run over this line was on July 4, 1869. Doubtless railroad enthusiasm exceeded national patriotism along the line from Waukee to Perry that year. The first engines burned cordwood, and great piles were stored at Perry ready for use. Grass fires sometimes threatened to burn the supplies, and the railroad men used to speculate on how many sticks it would take to run from Perry to Ft. Dodge.

The original town extended as far east as Eighth street, which was Willis' east line. The northern limits was 'Edna street. This has since been changed to 

Dewey avenue, probably during Admiral Dewey's popularity in 1898. The southern edge was Estella street, which West Fourth street was the western limits, and the whole area consisted of 220 acres. With the one exception the streets were named then just as they are

HARVEY WILLIS DR. SAMUEL PANGBURN

now. Lucinda street got its name from Harvey Willis' daughter, Lucinda; while John named two of his streets after his daughters; Eveleyn and Estella. The streets were surveyed to a width of 70 feet, while the avenues were allowed 100 feet. It will be noticed that the width of Otley avenue changes at Eighth street; the eastern portion being street width. Otley avenue was named for Col. Otley who lived on Evergreen Hill, at Grove and Pine streets.

It is said to have been Harvey Willis' original idea to make First avenue the main business street, but some of the earliest business houses were on the north side of Otley avenue about a block east of the depot. No sooner had Perry started until D. J. Pattee, who was running a small store at Alton P. O., moved his store to the new town, locating on the north side of Willis avenue, opposite the "triangle". This store burned 

February 7, 1870.

The first house built in Perry was that of Benjamin Campbell. He had started to build one at Alton, but being unable to get a clear title to the land he moved the lumber to Perry and built there. His son, Harry, was the first child to be born in Perry. Mr. Campbell was a carpenter, and did a great deal of early day building, including stage houses at Beaver and Pilot lake and Judge Thornburg's famous 14-room house.

It used to be commonly said in early times that half of the town was Thornburgs and Willises.

The first stock yards were where Estella street crosses the railroad tracks. For about 15 years these were the only stockyards, but after the Milwaukee

WM. MCLUEN 

ELMER DIDDY

was built, yards were built on their road, and before long the first ones were discontinued. Many years later the M. & St. L. company built small stock yards near the south end of Grove street, but these proving unprofitable were soon abandoned.

Concerning the price of lots Wm. McLuen says, “In the early days of Perry much uncertainty existed in the minds of the residents as to its future. City lots could be obtained north of Willis avenue in exchange for a brass watch or an old gun. Transactions in real estate were frequently conducted so loosely that two or three persons would hold deeds for the same lot, and the disputes were settled by the casting of lots of those who held deeds."

Much of the ground upon which Perry was built was low and swampy, and in wet seasons ponds were here and there over much of Perry's area. M. E. Fagen states that in the early seventies he came to Perry on the train, and when he alighted, the depot was nearly surrounded by water. Mrs. E. F. Edmondson remembers when water lilies used to grow where Sixth and Warford streets intersect. It is said that in early times there were two ponds on Second street, one where the St. James hotel is and the other where the Gamble building stands.

The first schoolhouse was a small frame building at Second and Warford streets, and Jesse Macy and . Miss J. Perkins were the first teachers. After a few years the growing town found itself in need of larger quarters. To help meet the need Harvey Millis donated a half block of ground at Second and Pattee

streets upon which to build a new building. Some believed this location was out too far, but nevertheless a building was erected the following year (1876). 

This school house was the first brick building in Perry. 

In later years this school was known as the Washington school. In dimensions, it was 50 by 60 feet, two stories high, and cost $11,500. Later two wings were added, one on the north, and one on the south, and each two stories high.

After the remarkable boom of the early '80's, due to the coming of the Milwaukee railroad, two more schools were built. First the Otley school at Fifth and Evelyn, and named from Col. Otley, and then the 

Webster school at Fifth and North streets. These schools were nearly alike, and in earlier times were usually called the "north ward" and "south ward" schools.

Perry churches date back to the very beginning of the town. The Baptists first organized in the spring of 1869 at a point four miles south of Perry, and met in a schoolhouse. This was known as the Spring 

Valley Baptist church, and E. R. Swain was pastor. 

In 1870 the church was moved to Perry and the name changed to First Baptist church. They purchased the triangular property surrounded by Railroad street, Third street and Otley avenue. The building was a typical frame structure at the west end, while a parsonage was on the northeast corner. The first seats were home-made and of native material. For the first ten years Elder A. E. Simons was pastor. About 1878 

29 members were dismissed to start a new rural church

in Peoples township in Boone county. Pastors up to 

1900 were: 

Rev. Lampkin 

Rev. Wilson 

Rev. A. E. Simons .1880 .1881 ..1882 

Rev. Morton Parsons....1890 

Rev. Richard Venting....1891

Rev. D. L. Clouse.......1886 

Rev. George Morphy....1894 

Rev. J. P. Coffman. ...1897 

Rev. A. F. Sharpnack....1887 

Rev. W. H. Darwood....1889 

Rev. O. D. Sonners......1899

The Methodist church was organized in Perry on October 17, 1869, but did not build their first building until 1874. It was dedicated in November of that year and cost $3,100. In the early '70's five preaching places were served from Perry by the local pastor. 

They were Pleasant Plain, Maple Grove, Buffalo Grove,

Minburn and a schoolhouse five miles west of that town. Stirring revival meetings were characteristic of Methodism in those times. In one year's time, under the ministry of Rev. Wm. C. Smith, a young pastor, 200 souls were saved in the whole circuit. 

Among his converts were Dr. P. A. Mowrer and wife, and Dr. Samuel Pangborn and wife. Reverend

Smith received $300 for his first year's work, and $400 for the second. In 1891 the first structure was replaced by a much larger and better building. Both churches were on the same site, at Third and Warford streets. The early pastors were: 

Rev. I. F. Miller... .1869 

Rev. H. B. Kees... ..1869 

Rev. Israel Mershon.....1870

Rev. Ebenezer Brady....1875 

Rev. Samuel Jones ...1877 

Rev. William Chambers..1880 

Rev. Miles A. Wright....1881

Rev. Asahel Thornbrue . .1883 

Rev. William McGuire...1884

Rev. John Horswell 

.....1885

Rev. John St. Clair......1891 

Rev. Joseph Wright. ..1892 

Rev. Chas. L. Nye.......1894 

Rev. Elmer McDade ...1898

The first Catholic mass was celebrated in Perry in 1871 at a private home. This continued until they erected their first church in 1874. During the first 27 years there were only three priests serving the Perry church; Fathers Toner, Harney and Murphy.

The Christian church was organized in 1871. Services were held in various places, but part of the time in the new Baptist church. In 1876, when the high school was new, they held revival services there under the direction of J. B. Vawter. The first church building was erected in 1881, at the corner of Second and 

Lucinda streets. On Christmas day, 1898, they dedicated a splendid new edifice at Third and Lucinda streets, just a block from the old location. Early pastors were: 

Rev. Snyder .1871 

Rev. E. C. Ames.. 

Rev. Sutton 1873 

Rev. C. H. Stearns. 

Rev. Painter 

Rev. J. D. Andrews. 

Rev. Derry 

Rev. Morris 

Rev. Bert Johnson .1880 ...1889 

Rev. C. C. Morrison.

Rev. J. B. Rolison.......1899 

.1898 ...

The Presbyterian church began in a small way as early as 1869 under the preaching of Rev. H. H. Kellogg, a home missionary, but no formal organization was effected until August 17, 1880. There were 14 charter members, and J. D. Miller and D. Gould were elders. Their first church was erected at Fourth and 

Pattee, and dedicated Sunday, February 1, 1891. The

cost was about $3,300. The following year a manse was built next door to the church. 

On December 13, 1883, eighteen persons met in Goff's hall and organized the Congregational church. 

In 1888 they built their first church building at Third and Otley avenue. The Unitarian church, probably the only one in the county was built in 1894 at the corner of Third and Lucinda, at a cost of $2,000. Its opening date was in the fall of that year. What the churches and Christian influences have contributed to make Perry a worth-while town cannot be over-estimated. Questionable practices were restrained, temperence promoted and sterling character instilled within the hearts of most of its citizens. While individuals differed in their religous beliefs they had respect for their neighbor's views, being mindful of the original American idea of religious freedom. 

Many towns throughout the country had a town square in the midst of the business district, but Perry possessed something unique-the "triangle". This was a triangular piece of ground bounded by Willis avenue, Second street and Railroad street. Around this triangle was a hitching rack, constructed of a heavy chain run through large holes in the tops of posts. Here was the favorite place for the farmers to hitch their teams while in town, and it was no unusual sight to see several loads of hay in the line, which farmers had brought in to sell. Sometimes, for a Fourth of July celebration, a merry-go-round occupied the center of this convenient location. In an early day John Willis donated this piece of ground to the city.

Nearly every family had a barn at the rear of their lot in which they kept the family cow, and those who could afford it kept a driving horse. For visiting, attending funerals and Sunday afternoon driving the horse and buggy was a treasured asset. With the prevalence of cows and horses, livery barns and horsedrawn drays and delivery wagons there was a good demand for the farmer's hay and grain right at home. 

Should a person wish to go to any place off the railroad the service of the livery was at their disposal, where a horse or team, either with or without a driver, could be hired by the day or hour. The livery service, although antiquated now, was an essential feature of those times.

For many years local burials were in the Mowrer and Crocker cemeteries. By 1876 there had arisen quite a question about a new cemetery more suitable to Perry's need. The first step taken was when George 

Pease, at the suggestion of Nathaniel Dugan, appeared before the town council asking for a new cemetery. 

Considerable controversy followed, some opposed such a move, one man wished to operate a private cemetery, others said it would be foolish to buy more than five acres. 

The land desired was owned by a Mr. Miller, and he was unwilling to sell any of his farm. Finally the city took legal steps to condemn the land for burial purposes, and ten acres were acquired at $40 an acre. 

At that time many believed the ten acres would be sufficient for a hundred years. The first burial was of 

Mrs. C. M. Cross on March 2, 1878. Some time later

10 acres more were added on the north side, and the southwestern portion became the Catholic section. 

The first mill was a real credit to Perry, and the largest in the county. It was built in the summer of 

1875 by William Otis and Selby Bros. at a cost of $12,000. It was a three-story building 40 by 70 feet, and stood on the site of the present Perry mill. It had a basement and fireproof engine room. It originally had three runs of stone, but in the fall of 1878 was equipped with the roller system, a new method of flour making. Mr. Otis was a wealthy man and erected a large, magnificent house at Third and Dewey avenue, which still stands, although now in bad repair. In 

1882 they sold out to Kenworthy Bros. In 1885 A. J. 

Capen became a partner, but in 1891 he sold his interest to his brother, L. E. Capen. In 1894 Kenworthy sold his share to A. J. Harvey and the firm became Capen & Harvey. About 1895 the mill burned down, and the ground remained vacant until Mr. Holdefer built his present mill in 1914.

After this mill burned Perry almost secured what later became the great Quaker Oats Co. For many years Heath Bros. operated an oat meal mill just east of the Union station in Des Moines. The Wabash railroad company condemned the property, and they were forced to vacate. At this time they came to Perry to establish a mill here if conditions were favorable, and they took an option on the old mill site. They also went to Fort Dodge and took an option on some land there. One brother insisted on locating in Fort

Dodge, but the other favored Perry. They came back

to Perry, and on the depot platform, in the presence of Mr. Otis flipped "heads or tails" for choice of towns.

Fort Dodge won, and Perry lost what was the foundation of the Quaker Oats Co. in later years. 

THE OTIS MILL IN PERRY 

The first banking in Perry was a private institution conducted by George Blakeslee in 1873. Having no opposition he did not hesitate to exact 2 per cent a month for his loans. Soon David Pattee and O. Mosher opened the Citizen's Bank, west of the triangle. At once Blakeslee inserted a small ad in the paper, "Plenty of money at 12 per cent per annum." But in spite of the drastic reduction, business began to leave him.

Blakeslee then formed a National bank (August 14, 1883) with a capital of $50,000, which was located at Second and Willis avenue. Associated with him were 

Silas Moody, David Blue, A. W. Otis, and Howard

Rouse was the cashier. Things did not go so well, and finally the comptroller of currency investigated the methods of business and Mr. Blakeslee wisely but reluctantly decided to sell out. So in 1885 his interest was sold to D. J. Pattee and O. Mosher, and the 

Blakeslee family moved to Nebraska. "Blakeslee was ugly about executing the details of the transfer, and when Mr. Pattee tendered him some $70,000 in certified checks and currency he demanded it all in gold. Mr. Pattee, went to Des Moines and got the gold and then refused to let it stay in the bank over night. Blakeslee begged, but to no avail."

At various times a number of brickyards operated in the vicinity of Perry, the first being that of Cornelius McKean on his farm just northwest of the present Alton schoolhouse in 1882. Mr. McKean sold the southeast five acres of his farm to Mr. McBride, and he, too, began a brickyard. Both yards, although not large, did a fair business and some buildings in Perry today were made of those local bricks. By 1890 both yards were abandoned. About 1895, three brothers, Ed, Fred and Lester Cooper, began a brickyard where the Milwaukee crosses the Raccoon river, but this plant never reached very large proportions. 

The first bridge across the river near Perry was the Bennett bridge which was built about 1878. This name has since been changed to the Wolf bridge. Mr.

Bennett in early times ran a ferry at this place. The next bridge was the Hall bridge built in 1882. It was named from Horatio Hall who lived nearby. About 

1885 the Shively bridge, four miles south of town, was built, and named from John Shively of that neighborhood. With the changing of the road plans the Graney bridge was erected about a mile upstream some time later. The new route took most of the travel from the 

Shively bridge, and about 1895 it was taken down, moved, and used in the Garroutte bridge west of Dallas Center. The Graney bridge was entirely washed out by high waters, and about 1910 the present substantial structure was built. 

A well known early day landmark was the Potts lake a mile east of Perry, which covered parts of the James, Russell, Whaley and Connor farms, but mostly on the W. S. Russell place. About 1895 an attempt was made to drain it. A contract was entered into with a Mr. McClary to do the big job, which involved a guarantee to really drain the lake, but the job when completed was only a partial success. Considerable trouble and litigation followed for several years, and finally the district court cancelled the contract. In time a second attempt proved successful, except in unusually wet times. The water is carried to the northeast and flows into a small creek that empties into 

Beaver creek. George Orbin, Perry man, recalls that when the big lake was tapped and water run off that an abundance of fish, and snakes, too, were left grounded.

Before drain tile came into use much draining was accomplished by means of open ditches. Ephriam Long, who lived northwest of town, made a regular business of draining ponds and swamps. His mode of ditching seems strange indeed to people of today. With a four or six-yoke team of oxen and a gigantic plow he opened up the tough prairie and let the water out, in places which only required a shallow ditch.

When Perry was five years old many of the citizens wished to incorporate the town. In December, 1874, a petition was presented to Judge Mitchell at Adel asking for an election to see if the majority of citizens favored incorporation. Accompanying the petition were affidavits showing that the village had a population of 

540. The judge appointed A. G. Webster, L. D. Gam ble, G. H. Rowley, Ed. Lunt and P. C. Rude as commissioners to hold an election. On February 27, 1875, the election was held. Although the weather may have been cold, the election was one of the "warmest" ever held in Perry. Of the 120 votes cast, 63 were in favor of incorporation, while 57 were opposed to such a move. 

At the next election J. H. Conley was elected mayor. 

He was a blacksmith and a man of excellent reputation.

An interesting story is told of Mayor Conley. Three drunken young men were arrested for disorderly conduct. There was no office, no jail, nor any place to put them so he took them to the only place he hadthe parlor of his own home. They became so noisy that the family could not sleep, so he had to let them go-unpunished.

The Perry District Fair Association was formed in 1886 with Fred Knell as president and Steele Kenworthy vice president. The association purchased 40 acres of ground three-quarters of a mile from the triangle. This tract laid between the following streets. 

Willis avenue, Iowa street, Twelfth street and Sixteenth street. The entrance was at Twelfth and Otley avenue. 

The Art Hall was a round building on the hill just south of where the hospital now stands. The amphitheater, seating about 1,000 persons, was on the southern slope of said hill, and on the northern side of the race track. The west and much of the south side of the grounds was enclosed by a high board fence, which made part of the shelter for horses and stock. 

It had an up and down experience; financial problems perplexed the officers, and after about 15 years, the fairs were discontinued. However, there were some very good fairs and much interest. During the best times $3,000 were annually given in premiums, equally divided between races and farm products. 

People of today may be much surprised to know the extent of Perry's shipment back in the early days of the town. According to Mr. Jennings, the agent of the Des Moines and Fort Dodge railroad, their road shipped the following number of cars from Perry in 

1878: Corn, 664; hogs, 153; oats, 90; cattle, 30; flax, 13; potatoes, 10; butter and eggs, 12; wheat, 9. Making a total of 981 cars. Two things will be readily noticed: first, Perry was in a great corn country, and second, the railroad was doing a flourishing business. 

However it should be borne in mind, that car capacities

then were considerably less than now. It is true that the new broken prairies produced enormous crops of corn. For years Perry possessed great long strings of corn cribs, in several places, where speculators held for better prices. 

Four years before this time, September 18, 1874, the 

Perry produce market quoted the following prices: 

Wheat, 68 cents; rye, 69 cents; oats, 35 cents; barley, 50 cents; corn, 50 cents; potatoes, 60 cents to 75 cents; beans, $1.25; onions, $1.00; butter, 18 cents; cheese, 18 cents; eggs, 10c; flour (best), $1.45.

The following Perry lodges were organized in the year indicated: Masonic, 1871; A. O. U. W., 1877; 

Good Templars, 1878; Eastern Stars, 1893; Rebekahs, 1895; M. W. A., 1883.

Perry once had a Chinese merchant, whose name was Ar Shong. His store was at First and Willis avenue, and he truly had a general store for his wares included dry goods, clothing, groceries, dishes, chickens, feed, wood and most anything a person might wish to buy. 

He is said to have been a member of the Presbyterian church, and for a time did a flourishing business. 

Some people have thought that Perry never had a saloon, but such is not the case; however, saloons never got a very good start in Perry, for its citizens were not the type to support such enterprises. In 1873, a saloon was in operation where the Stokley lumber yard is located. Things drifted along for some time until a climax was reached. One night in this place one drunken man almost scalped another with his jack-

knife. This incident was the beginning of the end of this notorious place. 

Captain Haskins, the constable, Lee Thornburg, and

Nute Beeson, decided to completely wipe this place out of existence. Entering the place late one night they proceeded in "Carrie Nation style" but using a sledge hammer instead of a hatchet. When they were through, the fixtures, beer kegs, bottles, and everything was a jumbled mass of ruins. The proprietor went to 

Des Moines and engaged lawyers to bring suit against the three men. At this point nearly every business man in Perry rallied to the support of the defendants, and soon $360 was subscribed and given to the saloon keeper, who seeing the attitude of Perry citizens, disappeared from town without leaving a forwarding address. 

Within a few days after this man's departure, a black headed Dutchman from Grand Junction declared that he was not afraid to come down to Perry, and that he would run a saloon here without interference. 

He came, repaired the old saloon, and arranged to open on a Saturday. To introduce himself, as well as his beer, he announced that he would give away all the beer the men could drink on that day. His place was crowded, and many men who had never been drunk before were drunk that night.

Undaunted, the same men attacked the place again. 

From Hain's blacksmith shop they secured some heavy iron pieces, with which they battered the door down. 

Inside were a dozen men lying on the floor, dead drunk.

Without further delay the crusaders retreated into the darkness along the railroad track to await devel-

opments. Shortly the angry saloon keeper came out and fired several shots into the darkness. The three men then began throwing rocks through the demolished door, and the liquor dealer in turn kept shooting into the darkness. He finally was forced to give up and run from the place. His business career in Perry lasted only one day, and he left poorer in purse, but richer in experience.

A few years later, a man by the name of Otto Manger opened a saloon outside the city limits that was known as the "Blue Goose." This was on West Willis and east of Frog Creek. This place seems to have run for some time and was the cause of more trouble and sorrow than any place ever in Perry, but this, too, was finally driven out by force, as were the others. Another man, Austin Dunlap, ran a joint for a short time on the west side of the triangle.

It was back in 1882 that an epidemic of smallpox struck Perry. A railroad man brought it here about 

Christmas time in 1881. Soon it spread to many people, and through January and February the whole town was quarantined, and practically isolated. Some railroad men lived in cabooses at the extreme east end of the yards. Some merchants, with limited capital, became bankrupt because of poor business during its three months course. Even though the disease was bad, no one in Perry died, but some deaths occurred. elsewhere.

A striking example of the work of the vigilants is recorded in the case of Dr. Slocum, a Perry druggist and money lender. Mr. Slocum acted as agent for east-

ern interests in the lending of money to local people. 

The temptation to cheat and steal overcame Slocum and matters grew from bad to worse. Large sums were involved and some lost their property. All the time the evidence against Slocum was becoming more clear. 

The vigilant committee took up the matter. The man was notified that he must make amends, but this he refused to do. About midnight, one night in Novembear, 1878, ten men, with their coats turned wrong side out and faces blackened, called at the Slocum home at Fifth and Evelyn streets. When he opened the door they grabbed him, clad only in his under clothes.

He was forced to lie in a spring wagon, and was driven rapidly away, followed by the others on horseback. 

The whole procedure was designed to frighten him as much as possible, but they were incensed to the point of really doing away with him, their intentions. being to put him under the ice of Beaver creek. He begged for mercy and promised to make restitution, so they threw him into a barb wire fence and let him go.

Some time later he arrived at the David Hastie home. 

Bloody, cold and angry, he asked for a gun, with which to seek vengeance, but was persuaded to stay until morning, when he was taken to town.

After Mr. Slocum's seizure, Captain Haskins, the marshall, was called, who went to rescue Slocum. Overtaking the crowd, a skirmish followed resulting in one of the vigilantes falling from his horse and breaking his leg. He was arrested by Mr. Haskins and taken to town. When the disguise was removed everybody knew 

Mr. Tom Gardiner, a respected farmer east of the town.

The next morning Justice Weber released him, and it was the concensus of opinion that the vigilants were justified in their harsh treatment. In a short time, Mr. Slocum left Perry, and later died in Chicago. 

The Perry fire department had its beginning back in 1878, when Fred Knell was instrumental in its formation. In February, 1879, a meeting was held in the old blacksmith shop of F. M. Hain where the

Perry fire department was duly organized with a membership of 15. A. W. Otis was the first chief. The department's property then consisted of a hand engine and 300 feet of hose. Four big wells were built in different parts of town to furnish an abundant water supply. In 1880 a city hall and fire department building was erected on the city's lots on First avenue. The city council abandoned the place in August, 1904, leaving it for the fire department alone. In 1887 the city council made a ruling that the membership of the department should always consist of 36 members. The same year the old hand engine was replaced by a new Watterus steamer, and the hose was increased to 900 feet. 

In the '70's Conley & Payton manufactured plows and mining tools. According to an old paper in 1874, they were "compelled by their increasing business to substitute steam for horse power." About 1882, Mr.

Moody began the operation of a creamery, which continued for many years; until the Van Camp condensory was built in 1908. The business grew steadily until two large buildings were needed to meet the requirements. 

This firm was one of Perry's reliable institutions and

a credit to the town. Their location was on West Willis avenue, just west of Frog creek.

The Perry Nurseries were established in 1872 by Geo. H. Barton, but were later sold to Frank Edmondson who gradually built up an extensive business. The grounds and office were located on West Park street and this nursery had the distinction of being the originator of the "free replacement" system. Their salesmen covered nearly the whole state, and a flourishing business was enjoyed.

The first real hotel was the magnificent three-story Stewart House at 1313-15 Second street. It was built by John R. Stewart in 1885. It was a marvel for those times, and the pride of the town. With a pressed brick front, electric bells, tile floor and a charge of $2.00 it was in a class far above most hotels of that time. Prior to this there was the old Perry House, which was more of a boarding house than a hotel. In time the St. James 

Hotel was built near Second and Otley. Others were the Deland House near the old roundhouse; the Norton 

House, near the Milwaukee station and the Markress 

House at Sixth and Rawson. 

For many years there were no sidewalks in Perry; dirt paths run from house to house, and even from store to store. The first sidewalks of board construction, were laid in 1884. Later, many "sandwich sidewalks" were used. They were simply beds of cinders sandwiched between two by fours. Not until 1897, when Allen Breed was mayor, did cement begin to be used for this purpose.

The Perry waterworks have always been at the present location. At first the waterworks and light plant were housed in the same building. A city water franchise was issued on August 4, 1891, and the light ordinance on October 10, 1891. Bonds to the amount of $9,500 were sold for the construction of these improvements. The erection of the stand pipe and the beginning of city water service began that fall, but the first electric lights were not vailable until the next year.

In July, 1894, a terrific explosion completely wrecked the entire plant. One man, Henry C. Cook, was killed, and his body badily mangled. Overheated boilers were believed to have caused the disaster. Mayor Breed hurriedly went to Omaha to secure a new pump and water service was resumed in a few days, but the light service was disrupted for several weeks. In a few years the city floated $35,000 in bonds for an improved and enlarged plant.

In 1896, an outside syndicate built an electric light plant at Second and Otley avenue. This plant was directly in the path of Second street, and at that time would be considered the south end of the street. A. L. 

Hindert was the local manager. In a short time the business was purchased by Dodson & Swearingen of 

Perry. For many years before electrical appliances became common the service was for lights only, and there was no current on the line during the day time. 

A whistle in late afternoon indicated when the current was turned on and lights could be used.
