a tiny and unique newspaper called the "Perry Locamotive." While having eight pages they were only three by four inches in size. Among the items in the issue of July 2, 1884, was the following: "David Pattee has commenced to build the largest residence in town. 

It is 28x60, with an ell-addition; it will be frame and brick veneered."

The first newspaper in Perry was the Perry Chief, begun by Ed Lunt and J. W. Jones in September, 1874. 

In 1879, the paper was purchased by A. C. Newton of Adel, who had just sold his paper, the Dallas County News, to Mr. A. C. Hotchkiss. At this time it had a circulation of about 800. Newton disposed of the paper early in 1881. By June it had changed hands twice when it was purchased by F. M. Hoeye for less than $1,000. Under Mr. Hoeye's management improvements were made and the paper took on new life. Within 18 months the circulation was nearly doubled. Up to 1884, the paper was printed on a Washington hand press, but at that time a cylinder press and steam power were added. About this time Mr. Hoeye, purchased the business of a local competitor, the Perry Pilot, and the subscription lists of the Dallas Center Globe and the Woodward Times. These, he says, were "bad bargains" and meant much financial loss, consequently, in 

December, 1887, he sold out to W. H. Gallup.

Two years later it was again sold to C. M. Pinkerton and I. K. Wilson, who later sold to Allen and A. M. 

Harvey. In the spring of 1894, the Harvey's began the publication of the Daily Chief. About this time, Hoeye was again in the newspaper business in Perry publish-

ing the Reporter, and in 1895, a partnership was formed between he and Allen Harvey, and the two papers consolidated.

This partnership proved to be a satisfactory one. 

A linotype was added at a cost of $3,600. The circulation of the Chief Reporter reached 2,000 and that of the 

Daily nearly 1,000. At the time of the Spanish-American war, Harvey enlisted in the army and the daily edition was discontinued. Up until this time the daily had always been a morning paper, but when it was resumed it was made an evening edition and has remained so ever since. The Chief was first published from the second floor of an old building at First and Willis. During the next 20 years various places were used, but when the Daily Chief was born, it occupied the Gamble building just west of the well known Gamble block. From here they moved to the Opera House block in 1902 and to the present location in 1928. 

No doubt the greatest disaster of Dallas county history was Perry's big fire in 1898. Sunday, November 

20th, was a beautiful autumn day, but in the evening it began to rain, and during the night it grew colder and began snowing, accompanied by a high wind from the northwest. At 9:30 p. m. about the time the people were returning from the church services, the first alarm was sounded. This blaze was at the rear of the Oxford restaurant, and was soon put out. At 10:30, a second alarm was given. Little did the citizens realize then what was before them. This time the fire was at John 

Mitchell's livery barn where Daw's drug store now is, at Second and Lucinda.

That the fire was of incendiary origin there can be but little doubt, for during the early stages of the conflagration, blazes began at several places, and at the rear of the Ammerman grocery, two men were seen setting fire. At any rate the one at the most unfortunate location of all, soon got beyond control. With a high northern wind, the fire not only followed down the west side of the street but jumped across to the east side taking everything as it went on both sides clear to the next cross street-Warford.

Realizing the grave danger, and their inability to cope with the situation, the firemen sent for help from Madrid. They responded promptly and made the run on the railroad in 20 minutes arriving in time to do considerable good. They also sent to Des Moines for help and 20 firemen came, making a remarkable run of 34 miles in 40 minutes, but their arrival was too late to do any good, and they returned at 4:00 a. m. 

During the height of the fire, burning firebrands were carried by the wind like paper, setting at least a dozen other fires that were promptly extinguished. Charred pieces were later found a mile or so into the country. 

Several people were injured, but no fatalities occurred. 

The next issue of the Chief Reporter carried the big head line: "Fortunes Swept Away." Among the firms completely ruined were: Mitchell's Livery Barn, Sani 

Restaurant, Leonard's Meat Market, Gough's Tailoring Shop, Oliver's Millinery Emporium, Trouth & Miller (hardware), The Armory, Grand Leader (department), Walker's Restaurant, Fenner & Johnson (shoes), Pierce's Millinery, Ringheims (dry goods),

Star Clothing House (McCammons), Gilberts Hard ware, Heaton's (dry goods), Wild and Rall (shoes), Bailey & Robinson (clothing), E. J. Dooley, (drugs), Roland & Dowling (groceries), Gamble Furniture Co.,

Perry Advertiser, Dr. Trouth, Dr. Kellogg, Dr. Foltz, 

Shortley & Harpall (attorneys), Cardell & Giddings (attorneys). Families living in apartment above were as follows: Allen Breed, T. J. Gilbert, Frank Cowden, John Foltz, Will Schroyer, Perry Williams, F. H. Reid, Miss Mary Pierce, E. W. Preston, J. B. Wallis, Harry 

Smith, Matt Bibbins, J. S. Draper, Mrs. M. E. Walker,

Walter Cardell. 

Messages of sympathy came in from all parts of the state, which helped to cheer the down-hearted citizens. 

The loss was estimated at $300,000. The charred carcasses of 15 horses lay in the livery barn ruins, one of which was valued at $1,000. Several inches of snow covered the ground the next day, and the people, for the moment, were much dejected. But most folks soon rallied from the shock of the catastrophe and began making plans for rebuilding. In a way it was a good thing, for many old and faulty buildings were replaced with structures much more durable. The Breed block, three stories high and nearly a block long was erected the next year, as well as the Gamble block and several others. The Breed block for many years has been the pride of Perry, and would be a credit to a city of twice the size. 

Forty year ago the firm of Shotwell and Davis was well known through the country. They dealt in produce, but especially butter and eggs; they built, south

of town and along the Rock Island track, the "duck pond." This was an artificial pond, made by excavation; and nearby was a well and windmill for the purpose of pumping water into the pond during dry times. 

This pond became a favorite skating place during winter months, and continued so for many years after the firm and windmill were gone. Another local skating place was the "roundhouse pond," a natural body of water not far from the roundhouse. Both have since been drained, and are no more.

For many years before the advent of artificial ice, an ice house stood an S. Third street. An abundant supply of natural ice was annually harvested from Raccoon river and packed in sawdust. About 1894, a Y. M. C. A. was organized here, with rooms in the 

Diddy block on Willis avenue. Will Tarr was secretary and Marion Diddy treasurer. Others taking an active part were M. C. Hayward, H. E. Jones, John 

Roberts and Frank Edmondson.

About 1895, "Billy" Sunday, who was just beginning his career as an evangelist, held a tent meeting in Perry. The meetings, lasting a month, were at 

Third and Bateman streets, and proved quite successful, with a goodly number of converts. Mr. Sunday and his singer, Mr. Oliver, were entertained at the 

G. R. Melick home while in Perry.

In 1893, Mr. John Law built the fine bank building at Second and Warford streets, and began the Citizens State Bank. Mr. Walker became associated with him in the bank, and on March 6, 1898, they sold out to S. S. Dilenbeck and Son, who moved here from Arthur,

Iowa. Before this building was entirely completed, Leonard French moved into the best office rooms on the second floor, and is still in the same rooms after 

44 years.

The older residents vividly recall the condition of Perry's mud streets, especially in the spring when the frost was going out. On one occasion, a loaded dray "got stuck" in the mud opposite the Bailey block. After futile attempts to pull out, the horses were unhitched and the dray and its load left until the next day. That night the ground froze and it was necessary to chop through the frost to get out. 

After the Civil War, Cornelius McKean, who was a captain in the army, kept 300 stands of arms in an upstairs room at his home in the Alton vicinity. These were the weapons of the home guards held in readiness should they be needed. At a later time a militia of 100 men had their headquarters in the Armory in the business section of Perry.

About 1900, while the country still possessed many military ideas as a result of the Spanish-American war, an encampment was held in Perry. Eight hundred soldiers, coming from various places of Iowa, gathered on the Pattee grounds west of town, for a week's encampment and drill. It was a big week for Perry. 

People came from far and near to see the soldiers march in dress parade, and to witness sham battles, and see the tent city of the "army post." Large contracts were made with local concerns for provisions, and a great deal of interest was taken in military matters. 

Perry merchants of this period aside from those

already mentioned were: L. D. Gamble, clothing and furniture; W. H. Chandler, implements; W. H. Heaton, dry goods; Melick Bros., groceries; Diddy Bros., general store; Harvey Willis, stock buyer, and Frank 

Heaton, grain buyer.

Early doctors in Perry were Samuel Pangburn, P. A. 

Mower, Theo. F. Johnson, Arthur Ross, A. W. Trout, R. E. Aiken, I. N. Paul, as well as some others. Attorneys of the nineteenth century were: H. A. Hoyt, Shortley & Harpell, Cardell & Giddings and Edmond Nickols.

In 1896, after the Otis mill had burned, John Shively started a combined saw and gristmill at the south edge of town. In addition to the regular custom sawing, he manufactured pickets for picket fences, which were very common at that time. About 1902, this mill, too, burned down, and for 12 years Perry had no mill of any kind.

During the '90s, great long cribs of corn were being stored in Perry awaiting higher prices. There had been a bumper crop in 1895 and corn was very cheap. 

Among those engaged in the corn business was Marsh Ammerman. In 1896, he took a trip to Mexico. His friends thought it was a pleasure trip, but it proved to be of a business nature, for he entered into a contract of tremendous proportions. The Mexican government was expecting war with Spain and the purchase of abundant corn supplies was a part of their preparation. They contracted with Mr. Ammerman for 5,000,000 bushels of shelled corn to be delivered within eight months. He now had not only an outlet

for his own corn but for all the surplus corn in the country as well. We can scarcely conceive how much corn 5,000,000 bushels would be. It is said that Mr. 

Ammerman sent down about 40,000 bushels, but no more was sent, and the contract was never fully carried out. The Mexicans would not carry out their part of the contract, so Mr. Ammerman was not obligated to proceed further with his part.

CHAPTER THE  

1850-1900 

Produce Shipments-Edwin Conger-Desota Boom- Change of Names. 

The early day towns of Xenia and Wiscotta have been described in a preceding chapter. Perry and Adel are especially featured in special chapters, but now we shall consider the other towns, whose history is both interesting and important.

DEXTER 

This town, located in the southwestern corner of the county, was begun when the Rock Island road was built through the county in 1868. The town was surveyed and platted by M. J. Marshall and A. Kimball. 

No lots were deeded to the railroad company, and the first one was sold to Hunter Brothers for $100. 

The merchants were Cheeseman & Ellis, who opened a store in a one-story plank shanty soon after the town was platted. Trains did not begin running until August, and all the earlier houses were built of lumber hauled from Des Moines by teams. The first grain shipped from Dexter was wheat, at a time when corn had to be brought from farther east to use there. J. J. Young built the first hotel, which was long known as the Dexter House, but later became the Johnson House. 

The first newspaper was the Dexter Herald, established in 1871 by J. J. Davies. In 1881 the name was changed 159

to the Dexter Sentinel. The town was incorporated 

December 13, 1870, and G. K. Rockwood was the first mayor. Mr. Carruthers was the first postmaster, in 1868. 

The first school was held in a one-room log house. 

In 1870 a two-story frame building, costing $5,500, was built, which served its purpose for many years. 

One of the first teachers was Joseph McLain. In 1878 a stock company was formed of business men and farmers for the purpose of establishing a normal school. 

A three-story brick building was erected at a cost of $6,000, and for many years it enjoyed a splendid patronage. Attempts were made to secure funds from the Gregg fund to enlarge the school, but this could not be accomplished. In 1896 it was consolidated with the public school district, and in 1905 was torn down to make room for a new and better school building. 

The Presbyterian church was organized November 8, 1868, and in 1871 a frame church building was erected. The United Presbyterian church was organized September 20, 1879, with J. C. Allen and C. C. 

Graham ruling elders. Later a small church and parsonage was built and entirely paid for. The Methodist church was organized in August, 1869, with 40 members, and in 1872 a fine building costing $3,500 was erected. Rev. James Lisle was the first pastor. 

The German Lutheran church was organized in 1871 by Rev. F. Doescher. This church conducted a Lutheran school, which was begun in 1875, and the building was used for both school and church purposes. For a time there was a Universalist church in Dexter, but

its existence did not last more than a few years. Probably this was the only church of this faith ever to exist in Dallas county. 

Early physicians were A. W. Vance, E. J. Smith, E. F. Linton and Robert Evans. The State Bank of 

Dexter was established in 1875 by G. G. Pierce at

Dallas and Marshall streets. Other business firms were: 

S. D. Clark, groceries; Downey & Conger, dry goods; 

J. G. Stanley, drugs; J. H. Aten & Bro., hardware; 

J. F. Watts, furniture; J. B. Vorse, implements; J. T. 

Van Orman, lumber; F. A. Small, grain buyer; Homan & Richter, meat market; C. Wolffinger, saloon. 

According to Mr. B. Batley, the railroad agent, the

Rock Island shipped 905 cars of farm products from Dexter in 1878, including 143 cars of wheat and two of broom corn. Butter shipments amounted to 98,500 pounds.

Linked with the history of Dexter is the remarkable career of Major Edwin Conger. From local farmer to U. S. Minister to China is the record of this distinguished man. In 1868 he settled on a farm near Dexter. In time he became county treasurer, then state treasurer, and finally was elected to Congress. In 

Washington he became well acquainted with high officials, and was appointed by President Harrison as minister to Brazil, later by McKinley to China, and even later to Mexico. At home and abroad he won the confidence and respect of all people. Perhaps he was Dallas county's most illustrious citizen.

REDFIELD 

This town was named from Col. James Redfield, Civil War veteran and Dallas county pioneer. The town was begun by the Cavenaugh Brothers in 1850, and was named New Ireland. In a few years they sold their holdings, including the townsite to Colonel Redfield. At the August term of court in 1860 a petition was presented asking that the name of the town be changed from New Ireland to Redfield. In view of the circumstances the judge, Henry Thornburg, granted the request. The town was surveyed and platted by 

O. D. Smalley, then county surveyor. The first stores were operated by Thomas Campbell and Macy B. Maulsby.

Not until 1879 did the town have a railroad. In that year the narrow gauge road, built to Adel the year before, was extended westward through Redfield, and on to Panora. The post office, begun in an early day, was first called McKay P. O. This was moved to 

Wiscotta about 1858 and called by that name. When 

Redfield took its present name the post office, too, did likewise. The early schoolhouse was built in 1867 at a cost of $3,200. It was 30 by 40 feet, and two stories high. Two of the first teachers were Ida Twichell and 

Maggie Mitchell.

The Christian church was the first in town, being organized in 1862 by J. C. White. In 1865 they built a meeting house, mostly of native lumber, and the material for seats was hauled from Des Moines. The 

Methodists were few in number at first, and usually met

in the Christian church. In 1885 they built their own place of worship and Rev. T. A. Lampman became pastor.

Business interests in 1879 were as follows: Scott & 

Maulsby, general store; S. S. Harmon & Son, general store; John Puffer, groceries; Dr. R. H. Rust, drugs; 

Macy B. Maulsby, physician; Ira Smith, attorney; 

Mahlon Thomas, furniture, and R. M. Finicum, meat market. There were two hotels-the Indiana Hotel and the Iowa House. A Masonic lodge was begun at the early date of June 8, 1860. The name of Maulsby has always been closely associated with Redfield. The "Maulsby Store" was begun by Irwin Maulsby on August 8, 1857. For 36 years this store continued under the same management. In 1893 Mr. Maulsby retired, but his son, W. E. Maulsby, continued on. 

DESOTA 

This is another town that was begun when the Rock Island railroad went through in 1868. It was named from Mr. Desota, one of the railroad officials. The land upon which the town was built was formerly owned by Thomas Hemphill and J. H. and H. G.

Van Meter. These men together gave about 100 acres to the railroad company for the purpose of starting a town. On March 27, 1868, Capt. A. J. Lyons, county surveyor, completed platting the town. There were nearly 300 lots in the original plat, and the town sprung up as if by magic. Most of the lots were quickly sold at prices ranging from $25 to $500 each. The very first one sold for $250 and was bought by a Mr. Jones.

The first building in town was built by A. J. Lyon and William Hemphill for a real estate office. It was only 10 by 12 feet in size, but in this place most of the town was sold by the above firm. During the first season about 30 houses were erected. The first brick building was erected by Robert Hillyer for a grocery store.

So confident were the railroad officials that Desota would make a flourishing town that the company purchased 160 acres adjoining the town. This was surveyed and sold in three and five-acre tracts. The depot was built unusually large in anticipation of a large town in the near future. For four years the town was a division station on the Rock Island, and saloons and gambling houses flourished in characteristic style. The first railroad agent was Alex Smart, who, at a later date became agent of the Milwaukee at Perry. He was followed by E. Conger, who continued for over 

21 years. The first postmaster was A. A. Collins.

Early business interests were: J. W. Blackman, general store; Siegel & Gotstein, general store; S. Wright, groceries; Dillon & Son, groceries; Payton & Mitchell, hardware; E. M. Giles, drugs; W. H. Smith, furniture; 

C. B. Campbell, doctor; P. Jones, butcher; Isaac Koch, implements; R. F. Lewis, lumber and grain, and A. A. 

Nolan, attorney.

Desota quickly became an important stock market. 

The first carload of hogs were sold by G. W. Miller to H. G. Van Meter in 1868. Mr. Miller received 7 cents per pound in Desota and Mr. Van Meter secured 

8 cents in Chicago. In a few years there were several

stock buyers, one of which was Peter Fish, who, for a time, averaged a carload of stock a day throughout the year. A wide territory was covered, extending nearly as far away as Perry.

The pendulum of vice in the wide open town swung too far, resulting in an important temperance move in 1878. This movement was under the leadership of 

John W. Harden of Des Moines. In a comparatively short time over 500 people had joined the Desota Tem perance Reform Club. J. S. DeMotte was president and 

J. F. Perry, secretary. In 1879 Desota was one of six towns in the county without saloons. The Masonic lodge was chartered on June 2, 1869. The Odd Fellows were organized in 1879. The Desota school building was built in 1870 at a cost of $5,000. It was a twostory brick building 30 by 60 feet. Mrs. Collins, the postmaster's wife, was the first school teacher in the little frame building used at first.

The first church organization was the Methodist in 1868, but not until 1872 did they build their first meeting house. This was of brick construction, 34 by 60, and cost $6,000. The first pastor was Rev. J. E. Darby. 

A Presbyterian church was organized in 1869, and the next year they built a church 30 to 40 feet, but reverses came; death and emigration so reduced their number that they were unable to continue beyond 1877. Reverend Bayliss was the first pastor. 

The Christian church was organized November 26, 1870, by Elder A. J. Hobbs. Quite contrary to the usual course, this church was built and furnished and all paid for before there was an organization made. There

were over 40 charter members. In June, 1871, one 

Sunday evening, shortly before meeting time a hurricane came up suddenly, and blew the church over. It was rebuilt again in the same style and dedicated the following November, just a year from the first completion.

VAN METER 

This town was platted in 1869, and was first called Tracy, but afterward was renamed Van Meter in honor of J. R. Van Meter, a local pioneer settler. The town was begun on land owned by W. H. Wilson, who had built a house before the town started. Mr. Wilson became the first postmaster, and E. D. Smith kept the first store. For years before the town started, Jacob 

Van Meter operated a flour mill in the vicinity and did a flourishing business.

A petition for incorporation was granted June 1, 1877. J. R. Van Meter, T. E. Moore, G. C. Briggs, 

W. H. Jennings and B. F. Goar were duly appointed as commissioners on corporation elections. The first schoolhouse was a two-story frame structure of two rooms, and Tim Adams, J. F. Curren and Miss Hoover were the first teachers. About 1895, a modern sixroom brick building was erected. 

Chas. McCoy built the first hotel. For many years it was run by Graham Richard and was known as the Graham House. The first physician and druggist was 

Dr. C. M. Dodge, followed by Drs. Bunce, Woodruff and Smith. Before bridges were built a ferry was

maintained at "Coon Point" east of the mill. Joel 

Patton was one of the pioneer ferrymen. 

There were two early day churches-the Methodist and United Brethren, the latter is said to have been an especially strong force for good. Rev. S. E. Dennis was one of the early pastors, but John De Moss was first. Nathan Moore was a charter member, and he and his wife were the second couple married in Dallas county. Prominent families of this church were those of Eli Golden, the Trindles, Potter, Jennings, Hester, Holroyd, Switzer, Neideigh, McNair, Stump, Clayton, Neal, Shambaugh, Gutshall and Drs. Summers and Allcott.

The first elevator was built by Miles White. General stores were run by C. W. Bogue, W. H. Jennings and 

Goar Bros., M. A. Tuttle and J. B. Rubinson, operated drug stores; meat markets were conducted by M. F. 

Clayton and Welch & Hunter. In the early days of

Van Meter, the town was a great shipping point for cord wood and railroad ties. B. F. Goar employed a large number of men for years, cutting wood, and great ricks used to be piled up along the tracks between the depot and elevator.

There was considerable coal mining in the Van Meter vicinity and saloons flourished for a number of years. 

Dispensers of "wet goods" were Messrs. Drew, Donovan and Hockett. When the first Methodist church was abandoned it was bought by Hockett and converted into a saloon which was an unusual occurrence, and no doubt prompted much comment.

The I. O. O. F. lodge was organized in 1871, and the 

Rebekah lodge in 1874. The Masonic lodge was organized first in Desota in 1869, and later moved to Van Meter. The first bank was organized on March 

6, 1899, with a capital of $10,000, and was called the Van Meter Bank. It was owned by members of the 

Goar family. Prior to this, they had served the town fairly well in connection with their mercantile establishment.

BOONEVILLE 

This small town was not begun until 1871, about three years after the railroad was built through the country. Owing to the nearness to Des Moines and other towns it never had much chance of growing to any size. The land upon which the town was built was formerly owned by Mrs. Susannah Boone, wife of 

William D. Boone, one of the earliest settlers and it is from these people that the town derived its name. 

The first house was built by George Baldwin. On this site, W. H. Baldwin later ran a store and was postmaster. J. Winter conducted a general store and Mr. 

Jones a hotel. S. H. Benson was a stock dealer and

Dr. Tidball and Dr. Morgan cared for the physical welfare of the community. There was a methodist church and an Odd Fellows lodge which was chartered 

October 14, 1874. Like Van Meter, it prospered in early times through the wood business, and even became incorporated, but never went forward very far. For years it has only been a mere village void of any comparative importance.

WAUKEE 

Waukee was begun in 1869 when the Des Moines & 

Fort Dodge railroad was built. L. A. Grant owned the land upon which the town was started, and built the first house near where the water tower now stands. 

W. J. Johnson ran the first store and was the first postmaster. The first birth was that of Minnie Parker, November 25, 1869. The first death was that of Mrs. 

A. Tyler on January 26, 1870. It is thought that John 

Atkins and Dora Taylor were the first couple married, the ceremony being performed by C. F. Clark, justice of the peace.

The town was incorporated in 1878, and the first officers were as follows: Mayor, C. F. Howe; recorder, W. E. Humphrey; assessor, J. E. Huston; marshal,

Samuel Shearer; councilmen, C. C. Tyler, Patrick Ho gan, A. T. Blackman, W. Wood, Taylor Bate, C. F.

Clark. The first schoolhouse was a two-story frame building, 26x40 feet and cost $2,300. This building filled the need throughout the balance of the nineteenth century.

The cemetery, one-half mile south of town, was begun by a joint stock company and contained only two acres. 

Additional ground was added later to meet the need. 

In an early time, Waukee had a library association that had quite an extensive circulation of books. The 

I. O. O. F. lodge was organized March 14, 1875, and the K. of P. March 21, 1893. Being surrounded by a fertile and productive country, the town soon became an important point for the shipping of stock and grain.

At an early date the Episcopalians built a church, but in a few years the numbers became so reduced that the membership was disbanded, and the building sold to the M. E. Church denomination. The Presbyterian church was organized April 24, 1870, by Rev. 

William Campbell, with 21 charter members. The first regular minister was Rev. George Pierson, who remained for five years. Their building in the southwestern part of town, was 28x40 feet in size, and cost $1,600. The lot on which the church stood was donated by General L. A. Grant, early owner of the town site. 

The Catholics and Christian 

Christian denomination had churches, and the Friends had a church four miles northeast of town.

For many years the Wragg Nurseries was the principal industry of the town. John Wragg, the founder, began on his land south of town in 1863. By superior stock and honest dealing he established an enviable reputation that not only extended throughout Iowa and the nation, but to numerous foreign countries as well. In time the expanding business made it necessary to secure ground within the town limits upon which to erect large, commodious buildings and storage cellars. 

Upon John Wragg's retirement, the business was conducted by N. C. Wragg, the youngest son, under the name of J. Wragg & Sons. The junior member was born in the business, and became a recognized authority upon horticultural matters.

DALLAS CENTER 

This town was named thus because it was near the

center of the county, although it is four miles from that point. It was one of the new towns begun when the Fort Dodge railroad was built through the country in 1869. The land on which the town is located was formerly owned by Percival & Hatton, and R. D. 

Kellogg. The survey of the town was made by Francis Pelton, and the plat was recorded in June, 1869.

None of the land or lots were deeded to the railroad company, but it was entirely a private enterprise of Percival & Hatton. Within a few years the Huber & 

Vandercock addition was annexed on the east. 

Three streets on the west were named Kellogg avenue, Percival avenue and Hatton avenue. Beyond that, they were numbered; east and west streets were named Laurel, Maple, Ash, Walnut, Sycamore, Cherry,

Vine, and Linden streets. On the west edge of the town a block was reserved for a park which was called Mound park. It was once hoped that the county seat would be located in Dallas Center and this park was held in readiness for the courthouse when such a change was made. 

The first store was run by O. N. Steele, in a shanty east of the track, and on the open prairie, in June, 1869. 

In a short time T. R. North followed with a stock of groceries and hardware, and also J. E. Fleischman, with dry goods and mixed stock. Other early day business interests were: Melick & Fox, general store; I. G. 

Morgan, drugs; Townsend & Cartwright, hardware and furniture; Martin & Woodward, hardware and implements; O. Mosher, bank; E. J. Stevens, shoes; 

Clarey Bros., meat; Jed Brockway, jewelry; Schamel

& Laughran, grain dealers; W. H. Brenton, stock buyer; J. S. Easton, lumber; W. S. Jameson, livery barn; 

Mrs. A. Perkins, Dallas Center House. Three saloons were run by Henry Saunders, J. Freund and P. Mc 

Grath. Doctor Criley was the leading physician, and 

J. M. Hoopes, O. Mosher and J. L. Loring were attorneys. A gristmill was owned by R. Roberts and a planing mill by C. Rathburn & Son.

The town's first permanent newspaper, the Dallas 

Center Globe, first appeared on December 31, 1875, published by Samuel Sloane. Prior to this J. W. Jones of the Perry Chief published the Dallas Center Mail for a short time. For many years, L. J. Loring ably edited the Dallas Center Local. About 1897, he sold out to E. A. Emmert who started the Dallas Center 

Times. J. L. Loring, one of the early pioneers was a man of unusual ability. He surveyed the new railroad through Dallas Center, wading through swamps and driving stakes in the black mud. Besides being editor and surveyor, he was an attorney, merchant, insurance agent and notary.

A two-story brick building of four rooms was erected for school purposes in 1874 at a cost of $10,000. For some years before this building was abandoned, a frame building across the street was used as a supplementary primary room. Dallas Center citizens have taken great pride in their school system, and have spared no expense in keeping abreast of the times. 

Dallas Center lodges were organized as follows: I. O. 

O. F., October 5, 1872; A. F. & A. M., July, 1876; 

M. W. A., December, 1894.

Closely associated with Dallas Center history is the name of Brenton. The senior member of the family, W. H., successfully engaged in stock raising. His three well known children were Charles, Clyde and Mrs. D. D. McColl. In 1873, he organized a private bank in Dallas Center which was the beginning of the well known Brenton banking interests in central 

Iowa. Hon. John Fox was also one of the local distinguished citizens. He was a Civil War veteran, large land owner, stock feeder and member of the state legislature. He helped to organize the Farmers' 

Mutual Insurance Co. of Dallas County on February 

5, 1895, and was secretary for four years.

The Presbyterian church was organized in April, 1869, by Rev. William Campbell. In 1873, under the direction of Rev. Geo. R. Smith, a suitable house of worship, 34 by 50 feet, was erected. Prior to 1873 the Methodist church belonged to the Waukee circuit. 

The church was incorporated in 1873, a parsonage built in 1876, and a chapel built on a leased lot in 1877, at a cost of about $650. 

The Yorker or Old Order River Brethren were organized near Dallas Center in April, 1877. Martin

Nissly was the first minister. These people believe in simplicity of the gospel, righteous living and uniform apparel for their members. They have no meeting house but have their services in their homes. On special occasions when large crowds are expected, the meetings are held in any out building large enough to accommodate the crowd. At the present time, Peter 

Kellar is pastor, and John Kellar is bishop. In 1886,

the Progressive Brethren church was organized by Rev. 

Noah Flora with 12 members. The same year they erected a church building at a cost of $1,200. In 1907 it had about 100 members, a good Sunday school and an Endeavor society. 

The German Baptist Brethren church was organized in 1875, one and a half miles east of town. They erected a frame church building at a cost of $1,500 and have a cemetery close by. Their pastors serve without a stated salary, and engage in secular work. 

Dallas Center has long been known as a town of modern improvements and thrifty citizens. Perhaps no town in the state represents more wealth per capita than Dallas Center.

LINDEN 

Linden was started in 1878 when the narrow gauge railroad was extended westward from Adel. The name of both Linden and Linn county came from the well known native tree by that name. The land upon which the town was built was formerly owned by Elisha Evans and Matt Burnham. The first house was built by James Lord, and Lew Manor and Wm. Smith were pioneer merchants. Dr. Payne was the first physician. Dr. Mallory was also an early well-known doctor. 

The first schoolhouse, a frame building, burned down about 1892, and a substantial brick building was built in its place at a cost of $4,000. The first church was the Methodist, whose building was moved in from the country. The Friends (Quaker) church was or-

ganized in 1882 in a schoolhouse on the farm of Rev.

A. W. Holloway. The following year a new church house was built. The Christian church was organized in the spring of 1892 by Rev. A. C. Burnham. There were about 30 charter members, and a meeting house was built the following summer. The dedication service was conducted by Rev. D. M. Helfinstein, who was the president of Palmer College. Reverend Burnham re mained for seven years.

The Masons and Odd Fellows organized lodges many years ago. Several men at different times have published newspapers, but the newspaper business was never very lucrative in Linden. The first bank, The 

Farmers' and Merchants' Bank, was begun in 1890. 

In time it passed into the hands of Geo. Messenger, who organized the Linden Savings Bank.

MINBURN 

This town sprung up with the advent of the Des Moines & Fort Dodge railroad and was laid out on land owned by J. B. Hill and D. F. Rogers. The first house was built by F. H. Baker who was the first merchant and postmaster. Among the early day business interests were: John Warford, general store; Wm. 

Seidler, drugs; Torry & Gardiner, general store; Brayton & Bro., hardware; H. W. Gribben, grain dealer;

Mat Crawford, stock buyer; John Oklund, livery; 

M. M. Maurer, saloon; Marcus Gill, Gill House; E. R. 

Aiken, physician; Osborne Bros., lumber.

At one time Minburn had a flourishing steam sawmill, as well as a planing mill. This mill was operated by

Osborne Bros. Minburn's shipments of grain, stock, butter and eggs were unusually heavy at an early date. 

In the '70's they had two frame school buildings of one room each. In 1878 the principal, S. J. Lauder, received $42.50 per month, and the assistant, Sophia 

Burnett, received $30.

The Methodist church was first organized at a schoolhouse near Minburn in 1859. When the town was begun the place of meeting was moved to Minburn. 

In 1877 a fine frame structure, 30 by 48, was built at a cost of $1,400, and was dedicated the following February. The Presbyterian church was organized in a log schoolhouse in the country in 1869. The seven charter members were: William Luellan, Eliza Luellan, Lizzie Luellan, Ruth Rogers, J. M. Porterfield, Clara 

Porterfield and Wm. McFarland. A building was erected in 1871. It was a frame structure 28 by 42 feet, and cost $2,000. The church, being handicapped because of insufficient membership, never seemed to prosper. Eight years later the new church had never been dedicated, and it seems that it never reached a very high tide during its existence. A Minburn newspaper, the Minburn Criterion, was published by R. E. Zerwekh. 

GRANGER 

Before the town of Granger was started their existed a small inland town two miles to the north by the name of Kelsey. When the Polk & Hubbell narrow guage railroad was built they found this village not easily accessible for their railroad purposes, therefore they

made a proposition to a number of farmers that if they would donate $1,000 they would locate a depot on section 12. "Enterprising men like Abe Elefritz, C. F. Fitzsimmons, C. F. Hotchkiss, A. D. Hotchkiss, James Fraser, E. E. White, C. F. Holmes and others interested themselves in securing this sum and the work of locating the depot commenced." 

Mr. Siberling of Ohio, laid out the new town. Francis 

Geneser built a frame building and began a general store. Soon this store was burned, and Mr. Geneser erected a brick building. For several years he was the only merchant in town. J. A. Vail was the second merchant. Soon other merchants and businessmen found opportune places in the new town. 

The United Brethren church was begun about 1892, being an outgrowth of a country church in Grant township, which was organized by "Father" Jacobs, as he was affectionately called. The Christian church was organized in 1894 by F. H. Lemon. Some of the charter members were J. G. Ricke and wife, L. Swing and wife,

K. Means and wife and A. J. Elefritz and wife. Their church building was erected in 1895 at a cost of $1,500. 

The Catholics also had a church organization. The 

Odd Fellows, Rebekahs and Woodmen lodges were also begun in the town. 

BOUTON 

When the Milwaukee main line was built there was no station between Perry and Woodward. Farmers about midway between were very desirous of a new station, and at a mass meeting held in a schoolhouse

they appointed Martin O'Mallay to interview Superintendent Campbell of the railroad company, and secure, if possible, a station.

The result was a promise to build a depot if the people would subscribe and buy for the company six acres of land. This was done and the company put in a side track, but made no move toward a depot. "Instead of using the six acres they only used three, so the unused portion was taken back by the grantors." 

Finally the matter of the depot was taken before the railroad commissioners, who ruled that the company should build the depot, so this was done about the fall of 1883. 

D. D. Bryan opened the first store, but later sold out to John O'Malley. Bryan was also the first postmaster; the United State post office being established on March 20, 1886. Prior to the erection of the depot the place was only a flag station known as “Alligan.” 

The town was named Bouton in honor of Mr. Bouton, from whom the land was purchased. 

J. C. Bryan platted the town and gave land for a town park. In 1885 Allen Breed of Perry built a grain elevator and put Wm. Wolfe in charge. Mr. Wolfe built the first house in the new town and later went into business for himself. J. P. O'Malley and J. R. 

Swearingen had the first lumber yard and S. S. Delenbeck & Son organized the first bank. In 1898 the Bouton Columbian Club was organized as a stock company. A two-story building was erected, with the lower floor leased to Thomsen & Lobes, who conducted a

general store. The upper floor was used as a town hall and for meetings of a public nature. 

WOODWARD 

This town was begun when the Milwaukee road was built in 1881. The old town of Xenia was close to the survey, but not being a suitable townsite for a railroad town, a new town was begun a mile or so farther west, and called Colton. Because there was another town in the state by that name it was changed to Woodward in remembrance of the beautiful Woodward Gardens in California. The land was formerly owned by Henry Hutsinpillar, J. R. Cole and Dr. Sauks,

Al Hughes had the first dwelling house, and Craft, Parks & Leaming operated the first general store. These men moved their store from Xenia when the new town started; in fact most of Xenia's business interests moved to Woodward, including the well known Dr. Brookings.

Other early day business firms were: Thornley & 

McCracken, clothing; Dr. James Potter, drugs; J. W. 

Winkler, grain and stock buyer; Burr Lee, hotel; Mr. 

Wilmer, lumber; Dan Couch, furniture; John Calvin, meat market; Wm. Wade, furniture, and J. Barrows, newspaper. S. O. Conger was the first banker (1883) and for nearly 20 years his State Bank was the only bank in town. The Woodward Enterprise was begun by A. F. Ingalls in 1887. 

Edith Potter was the first child born, and the first death was that of Robert McQuiston. The first wedding-a double one-was that of W. D. Hall and Laura

Nance; and David Preston and Ada Herdman. The first public school was begun in May, 1882, being held in the Miller building, and taught by Mrs. Enos 

Rhoades. In 1883 a brick school was built which met the need for 24 years.

The Woodward M. E. church really had its beginning in Xenia in the early '50s. When Woodward was started the church was moved and re-organized, and Rev. Wm. Stevenson became the first pastor. Closely associated with this church is the M. E. Robbins Chapel six miles southeast of town. This chapel was built in 

1898, and both churches are served by the same minister. The first M. E. Sunday school superintendent in Woodward was A. M. McColl's father. There was also a Baptist and Christian church, each contributing their part to the spiritual needs of the community. 

The Baptist building was built in 1892. J. R. Cole donated the ground for the Christian church, and Mrs. 

J. W. Winkler and Mrs. John McCracken secured most of the subscriptions for the building. 

The I. O. O. F. lodge was organized in 1882, the 

Rebekahs in 1896 and the Masons in an early time. 

The first sidewalks were merely a string of boards anchored in the mud. When Anthony McColl became mayor he was instrumental in changing to cement walks.

DAWSON 

When the Milwaukee main line was built they established a siding and grain house at this place (1881). 

Soon after this the Chicago Coal Co. discovered what

seemed to be a good coal area in this vicinity, so they proceeded to not only develop mines, but a new town as well. Miners and their families came and houses sprung up thick and fast. Dawson became a hustling little town, but soon the supply of coal suddenly gave out and the miners left. Merchants and others were by this time located there, so they continued on without the support of the mines. 

The land at this point was formerly owned by W. E. 

Tolle. The flag station was at first called "Undine." 

In 1884 it was changed to Dawson. A post office was established in 1887 and M. T. Finley was the first postmaster, but not until 1889 was the depot built. The first house was built by Milo Lee who bought grain for 

Allen Breed. The first doctor was Dr. J. T. Coveny. 

General stores were run by Shepard & Co., and Finley & Tolle, and Swiler Bros. had a hardware store.

In 1889 a company known as the Dawson Town & Gas Co. endeavored to restore prosperity to the little town, following the discovery of excellent brick clay along the river. They built a large brick and tile plant, but a few years of operation proved it to be a loosing venture, and the property went into the receivership of the Union National Bank of Omaha.

A schoolhouse was already built on the present site before the town was started. In 1895 an additional frame building was erected. Some of the early teachers were George Coleman, Tilly Gillands, Eliza Ridnour and C. W. Danks. The first church, the United Brethren, was organized in 1884 but not until 1896 was their first meeting house erected. The Methodists held serv-

ices occasionally in the schoolhouse. The only lodge of early times was the Woodmen society, which was organized in December, 1890. 

High Bridge was once a flourishing town in Des Moines township. Its life depended upon the mining of that section, and when the mines were exhausted the town naturally died out. The depot was moved away four years ago, and the last store quit in 1936.

Scandia was once a mining and tile yard town of about 1,000 people. Its height was reached about 1912, when it had three general stores, mines and tile yard. 

The coal supply ran out; the clay for tile did not prove satisfactory, and today nothing but ruins remain. The tile yard equipment was purchased by the Stokley Company of Des Moines and moved to Brownsville, Texas, about 1920.

CHAPTER GENERAL PROGRESS 

1868-1900 

Agricultural Improvements-Orton Bros. Circus-County Farm -Coal Mining-Temperance Question-Grasshoppers— 

Bank Robbery-Murder-Spanish 

American War

The advent of railroads within the county greatly increased the influx of settlers, and in a short time the entire country was settled by enterprising citizens. 

The changes that took place during the next 30 years were remarkable indeed. Expansion everywhere, and along all lines, made it possible for the rank and file of the people to prosper, secure homes, buy farms, engage in business and enjoy the fruit of their labors. 

Changing modes of agriculture and adequate means of transportation made it possible for the farmer not only to produce all that was necessary for local needs but to raise an abundant surplus for the eastern industrial centers.

Corn was no longer tended with a one-horse shovel plow, but two-horse cultivators were used, first of the walking type, and by the close of the century the riding style. The farmers also learned to use a "bang board" and one man husk on one side of the wagon only. The virgin prairie, when broken, produced wonderful crops, and the country abounded in corn galore. 

The early reapers were continually being improved, but when the "binders" came into use the farmers were 183

jubilant, for one of the hottest and most laborious jobs of the farm was forever eliminated. Later came the bundle carrier, which was only another step in the trend of labor saving devices. 

Crude threshing machines gradually developed into efficient handling of grain. The old horsepower threshers were run by four to six horses driven around a circle by a driver mounted on a platform in the center, with a whip to help the lazy ones along. The grain was run into half bushel measures and counted and poured by an attendant, into a wagon. A man on each side of the machine "cut bands" while another "fed" the thresher. Horsepower machines continued up to about 

1895, although steam threshers were coming into use as early as 1885. The first steam rigs were not portable, but had to be pulled about by horses. 

Check row corn planters, discs, adjustable harrows and corn shellers were wonderful improvements in farming. Woven wire meant much to the hog raising industry. Wooden pumps replaced the old oaken buckets in the open well. Thrifty farmers began to go to market in buggies and carriages instead of open lumber wagons. Houses were built with cellars underneath, in which the vegetables, apples and canned fruit was kept in winter, and milk, cream and butter in summer. 

Schoolhouses were established every two miles through the country. Fields were fenced, roads were graded, more and better buildings were erected, orchards and groves were planted, country churches were built, some drainage of low land was undertaken, towns were born, others expanded, business increased. Things were mov-

GENERAL PROGRESS 

185 ing, prospects were bright, opportunities were plentiful and all who were industrious or ambitious need not hesitate to launch forth in the work of his choosing. 

Fresh milk for the townspeople came from country dairies only. The dairy man would load his milk into cans, and usually with a one-horse conveyance make the rounds through town. A hand bell was rung to announce his coming, and the housewife would come out with some kind of container to get her milk, which was drawn from a faucet in a ten gallon can. Milk stations and the pooling of milk did not begin until many years later.

A notable dairy at Perry was the Moore dairy, which was begun by John H. Moore in 1882. Later it was operated by his son, Fred, who named it the Spring Brook Dairy. For 30 years steady service was maintained, with a price range of 5 to 64 cents. Mr. Moore at times had as many as 50 cows.

For many years after the first railroads came there were no grain elevators, but grain houses served the purpose. Small grain and shelled corn were sacked in grain sacks. The grain dealer had hundreds of grain sacks and when a farmer wished to sell his wheat, etc., he secured from the dealer sufficient sacks for his need and hauled it to market. After unloading the sacks onto a platform it was wheeled into the grain house, and when a car was loaded the same proceedure was followed again. Robert Ginn had an early day grain house in Perry just east of where the mill is now. 

George Heaton erected the first elevator on the present

site of the Stokley elevator. Allen Breed seems to have been the first grain dealer on the Milwaukee line.

The older residents of today recall with keen pleasure the "good old days" of those times. There seems to have been more general satisfaction and contentment then with these medium advantages than there is today with our latest inventions, luxuries, social conditions and future uncertainties. There was a consistent effort to live within one's means.

The agricultural college at Ames was doing much that proved to be practical, and gradually the effects were seen in Dallas county. Improved strains of livestock, grains, poultry and nursery stock were being developed, as well as, better methods of carrying on farm activities.

In 1885, the Des Moines & Fort Dodge line running through Waukee and Perry was sold to the large Rock Island system. This change was undoubtedly for the best for our citizens. In July, 1896, the Minneapolis & St. Louis railroad began running trains through Perry over the Rock Island tracks. On January 1, 1900, the

M. & St. L. bought out the local Rock Island interests and then the Rock Island began running some trains over the M. & St. L. tracks; an arrangement that has continued until the present time. Mr. Tom Beatty of 

Perry was in the employ of all three of the companies, having begun in 1876.

A well known Dallas county enterprise was the Orton Bros. circus of Adel. Begun about 1866 by Hiram 

Orton, the circus enjoyed a good patronage throughout the major portion of the nation for about 50 years.

GENERAL PROGRESS 

187 Associated with the circus, more or less during its time, were four sons: Lester, Den, R. Z. and Miles.

The latter is said to have been the best horseback rider of his time.

This was a wagon circus, a type not uncommon in the nineteenth century, and was in operation before Barnum was known outside of New York. Ralph E. Joy of Adel, recalls as a boy of his family, being close neighbors to the Ortons, both families living on West Main street. He says that Ortons had a large round building on the south side of the street in which they did their winter practicing, and that he was thoroughly familiar with the family and the work of the circus. 

The Ortons owned a farm east of Adel and on their land a small flag station was established on the railroad which was named Ortonville. Thomas Mann, the father of Miram Mann, present Adel postmaster, played in the orchestra of the circus. Some idea of the hazards of an overland circus can be secured from the following item taken from the Desota Exponent in 

May, 1898. "As Orton Bros. show was traveling from 

Earlham to Van Meter Tuesday night, they met with quite an accident by one of the teams running off the bridge near Fon Van Meter's, killing both the horses and hurting the driver. The night was dark and stormy and it was almost impossible for the driver to keep the road."

For a good many years, the county had no jail; apparently there was not sufficient need to justify the maintenance of one. What few "jail birds" there were, were taken to Des Moines. On October 8, 1861, the

county voted on the proposition of building a jail. Two hundred and six votes were favoring the move, while 427 were opposed. Nothing further was done for ten years. At that time there was talk of installing one on the second floor of the courthouse, but this was finally decided as impractical. In 1871, the board of supervisors appropriated $5,000, or such part of the sum as was necessary, to build a two-story brick building across the street north from the courthouse. This was built the following summer. 

The initial purchase of land for the establishment of a county farm was made on July 9, 1869, when the county purchased 160 acres of Cyrus Hemphill for about $4,000. This location is not far from the geographic center of the county and three miles north of Adel. A few years later, 40 adjoining acres were purchased.

Upon this land a house, 30 by 45 feet, and containing 13 rooms, was built as well as, a good barn and other outbuildings. As time went on more acres and buildings were added. At the close of the century, Jap 

Reynolds was the steward. During his administration a new addition costing $10,000, was built, also a new barn at a cost of $5,500. Other improvements included cement walks, waterworks, and electric light plant. 

In an exhibition of soils at the Centennial Exhibition in Philadelphia in 1876, Dallas county was adjudged the banner county of the banner state. The sample of soil was taken by Professor Fox, former state geologist from the Raccoon rived valley near the Flinn farm in Boone township. The sample was placed in a large

GENERAL PROGRESS 

189 glass tube six feet in length and about 10 inches in diameter. The top four feet were of a rich black soil, and beneath was a two-foot layer of yellow clay. This was placed in Agricultural Hall with other like samples, and over all competition received the first premium. 

In 1879, a history of Dallas county was published by the Union Historical Society of Des Moines. This compilation of early historical facts has meant much to the people of today. Scores of people could have narrated interesting and important matters, but to gather them together in book form for preservation, was the work of an enterprising historian. Without this history the present one would have been short of many of its best and most important items. 

Sixty years ago, considerable coal was being mined in the southern part of the county. Many of these mines were near the surface and some went straight into the sides of hills, and river banks. There were many small mines in Van Meter, Union and Linn townships but operated in a small local way, however, there were a few large mines. In 1879, the Chicago & Van 

Meter Coal Co. operated the largest coal mine in the county. It was only a short distance from Van Meter and along the river. The shaft was 257 feet deep, 50 men were employed and the average daily output was 

1,000 bushel or 36 tons. 

Lump coal, at the mine, sold for 9 cents a bushel, and nut coal at 8 cents. The Rock Island took two flats, or 24 tons, a day for their use and the balance was sold locally. This mine was equipped with steam power, and L. J. Platt was president of the company. The

only other mine of much importance was the Pioneer Coal Shaft in Des Moines township. It was owned by 

Frank West of New York, and operated by horsepower.

The shaft was 40 feet deep, and the first vein two feet thick. Twenty feet beneath was another vein three or four feet deep, but it was too far from a railroad to be worked very extensively. 

Various kinds of building stone were being quarried in Adams and Van Meter townships. At one time the 

Rock Island built a "spur" line to the Bear Creek stone quarry in Adams township. Twenty men were employed here, some of this stone was used in the new capitol building at Des Moines, and shipments were made to various parts of the state. 

The temperance question has always been a big problem, for liquor is almost as old as man himself. 

Among the early settlers a few were given to drinking and subsequent ruin. Judge Burns tells the following interesting story of an early liquor dealer: An Anderson Kelly, an old man full of years and full of whiskey, too, ran a whiskey shop in Penouch. His source of supply was in Fort Des Moines. In mid winter, his supply ran out and he set out with a barrel mounted on a tree fork sled drawn by a yoke of oxen. He arrived at Jim Campbell's, secured his whiskey, and the next morning started for home. He had scarcely reached the open prairie, west of the fort, when a characteristic pioneer blizzard blew in from the northwest. The farther he went the worse the storm became. 

Progress became very slow and difficult. Finally the

GENERAL PROGRESS 

191 weary oxen stopped, turned their backs to the wind and refused to proceed further. 

Kelley, with a log chain hook, knocked the bung from the bunghole, and with a blue-stem sucked his fill from the precious fluid. Grabbing hold of the tail of the "near" ox, he compelled them to go with his ox goad, leaving the oxen to choose their own course. 

Finally they reached the home of a settler, where they remained overnight. Not until ten days later did the weather and trail conditions permit him to return for his barrel of whiskey. During this time there was not a drop of whiskey at Penouch for his thirsty patrons.

Upon reaching the barrel, he discovered that it had leaked fearfully, during his absence, from some cause, and he was not slow in accusing old John Wright of being responsible for the leakage.

In April, 1855, the people were privileged to vote on the "Prohibitory Law." There were 233 votes in favor of the proposed law and 164 opposed to the same. 

The whiskey crowd were not satisfied, and two years later Benjamin Bennett and 180 others petitioned the district court to submit a vote to the people on a license plan, accordingly on the first Monday in April, 1857, an election was held, but the results were against the license plan by a good majority, and the temperance principles of the county were maintained. 

The temperance cause grew steadily. The W. C. 

T. U. and Good Templar lodges and other temperance clubs were formed in many communities, but despite the noble efforts of good people, bootleggers and unscrupulous merchants would not hesitate to violate the

law, for some men would sell the shirt off their own back to secure the accursed thing, and other would stoop to anything for money. 

As a whole, the people of Dallas county and of the state of Iowa have not favored the liquor traffic, but it has been an almost constant source of trouble and agitation. Our better state officials have well expressed themselves when they caused to be inscribed on the walls of the state capitol these words, "Nothing is politically right that is morally wrong."

Many prosperous farmers became large land owners, in some cases acquiring one or more thousand acres, but few places in this section of the country were known as ranches, however, there was at one time quite a famous ranch of 2,000 acres in Boone township owned by James Calanan. This was along the Raccoon river and was a feeding ground for improved breeds of cattle.

As the result of a general depression in 1894, an army of discontented and unemployed men began a march to Washington to register their grievances. During the summer, General Kelley with several hundred men passed through Van Meter township a foot. Near the home of Eli Golden on the north side of the Raccoon river, they went into camp. While here they were visited by many people from far and near, and were given large quantities of provisions. The presence of so great a body of men caused some alarm in the minds of local property owners, but their misgivings soon vanished as they proceeded on their way to Des Moines in a peaceable manner. After a few weeks

GENERAL PROGRESS 

193 sojourn in Des Moines, they took passage down the river on rafts, toward their destination. 

John Henry Shively of Perry, a veteran miller of the old type, invented a device for keeping the grooves in the old mill stones. The inventor named it "diamond bur dresser," for which he was offered $10,000 but refused. About this time (1878) the roller process of flour making came into use and all chance of realizing anything was forever gone. 

An agricultural move, called The Farmers' Institute, was begun in 1898. Its purpose was to promote more interest in better farming. Seed corn selection, livestock improvement, public roads, drainage and rotation of crops were some of the subjects under consideration. 

The first meeting was held in Breed's Opera House in 

Perry, February 17 and 18, 1898. John Cownie and

Henry Wallace of Des Moines were the principal speakers. An organization was formed in which Josiah 

Petty was president and L. L. Feather, secretary. Later meetings were held in a different community each time and such men as M. J. Wraggs, W. W. Philipps, John 

Kent, M. J. Graham, John Fox, Wm. Danner, C. R.

Brenton and Geo. White, took an active part. They secured the Milwaukee "corn special" and Professor Holden of Ames to stop at Adel for exhibition and instruction.

High water and floods along the Raccoon river have always been a rather common occurrence. While the lower river bottoms were very fertile and capable of producing excellent crops, there was always danger of floods either damaging or ruining these crops, should

the floods occur in crop season. David Hall of Perry states that in September of 1875, the river was exceedingly high. At this time, the water surrounded the house of Wm. Patterson, a neighbor. Mr. Patterson chopped a notch in the corner of his house at the crest of the water, two and a half feet above the ground. This farm was later purchased by the Hall famly. As recently as 1935, Harry Hall, a brother, wrecked the old house, and they state that the water crest since then has never equalled the record of 1875. 

In 1867, most of western Iowa had some unusual visitors. Myriads of grasshoppers flew in from the west.

These troublesome insects originated in the Rocky 

Mountain regions, and were sometimes called "Rocky 

Mountain locusts." Before their arrival here in August, they had ravaged large sections of Kansas. Traveling at a great height, their numbers were so great that they would obscure the sun like a cloud. On a sunny day, their wings had a bright shining appearance, resembling snow flakes. Upon alighting, they would cover the ground and in a short time would devour everything green. Usually they would remain for weeks with apparently no thought of further migration, but when they decided to move on, they would rise as by a signal and fly away.

Their arrival in 1867 was too late to affect the earlier crops, but not too late to deposit millions of eggs in the soil for hatching the next year. In many places, the crops were completely ruined in 1868. Of this year, Mrs. Coleman Thornburg of Perry, says in her autobiography, "The grasshoppers have taken all the wheat

GENERAL PROGRESS 

195 in Dallas county except about two acres of ours, and four acres belonging to another man." At this time the plague extended as far east as Polk, Warren, Clark and Ringgold counties.

Another visitation of these unwelcome guests came in the '70s, being far more widespread and general than ever before or since, but centering more to the northwest. Beginning in 1873, they gradually grew worse with each passing year. In 1875, 32 counties of Minnesota had been laid waste. They spread into 

Dakota and Nebraska. By the close of the summer of 1876, the situation was disheartening indeed. In hopes of devising some effective method of combat, the governors of Minnesota, Iowa, Dakota, Nebraska, Kansas and Missouri met in a conference at Omaha in October. Some noted entomologists were invited to attend, but little that was new was brought forth; every known remedy had been tried. At the close of the conference, the governor of Dakota suggested a day of prayer, in appeal to one higher and of greater power than they. 

The suggestion was received lightly by some, but very favorably by Governor Pillsbury of Minnesota. That winter, the Minnesota legislature appropriated money for relief, and 6,000 people were fed and clothed by the state.

With the approach of spring, many ministers were asking for a day of prayer and on April 9, 1877, the governor of Minnesota, J. S. Pillsbury, issued a proclamation asking the people to humbly fast and pray for deliverance from the awful plague. A part of this historic document follows:

"I do hereby, in recognition of our dependence upon the power and wisdom of Almighty God, appoint Thursday, the 26th day of April instant, to be observed for such purpose throughout the State; and I invite the people on the day thus set apart, to withdraw from their ordinary pursuits, and in their homes and places of worship with contrite hearts, to beseech the mercy of God for the sins of the past, and his blessing upon the worthier aims of the future."

Infidels ridiculed, but it made a profound impression upon most people, who faithfully carried it out. 

Governor Pillsbury said, "I never saw a stiller day in

Minneapolis." The next day the weather turned unseasonably warm and remained so for several days. 

Grasshoppers exceeding anything yet known hatched out, but at this stage it suddenly turned cold, freezing the moist ground, and the grasshoppers, too. In later years the governor once said, "We have never seen any grasshoppers since."

While it is a generally admitted fact that crime has greatly increased during recent years it must not be thought that there was but little trouble in earlier times. It may have differed in many respects, but the principles were the same. Horse thievery, bank robberies, and even murder, were sometimes the news of the day.

In 1895, Adel was the scene of a wild, desperate bank robbery. On the morning of March 6th, two men entered the Adel State Bank. The first man carried a sack, followed by another with a shotgun, who demanded of Cashier S. M. Leach, "Fill that sack." Leach re-

GENERAL PROGRESS 

197 fused, and was immediately shot, although not seriously.

Sheriff Joseph Payne happened to be nearby and fired two shots at them before the men left the bank. They grabbed a sack containing $260 and came out shooting at everybody in sight. J. M. Byers was shot in the hand, Postmaster Barr in the head and wrist, Cecil

Decker, in the head and arm and J. L. Simcoke in the neck. Windows in Miss Carouttes store and Verne

Russell's restaurant were broken by stray shots. 

Running to their team and buggy, they hurriedly drove away, going in a westernly direction. A posse was soon in hot pursuit. Three men, Den Snyder, Charlie Warford and Tom Reynolds, kept within sight of them. Eight miles from town they left the team, one hid in Widow Oneal's barn, the other, taking to the timber. Unable to cross the river this man promptly surrendered.

Hundreds of shots were fired into the barn but the robber refused to surrender. The barn was then fired, and heat soon drove him out, but at once he fell riddled with bullets. The captured man proved to be C. W. 

Crawford of Patterson, in Madison county. His slain partner was Orlando Wilkins, who was the desperate one of the two. A great crowd gathered from far and near and it was with the greatest difficulty that the officers prevented a lynching. Dr. Caldwell, the president of the bank, was at the time in the south, and first knew of the affair when he read of it in the Atlanta 

Constitution. Young Crawford was tried, convicted, and sentenced to Fort Madison for 20 years. The body of Wilkins was crudely buried in the local cemetery.

Late in the summer of 1876, an unfortunate circumstance occurred in the murder of a man named Woods. This man, in company with a Jasper Mason, was traveling, in a wagon, from Atlantic to Des Moines. 

On the way they partook freely of "booze" and engaged in a prolonged and heated discussion of the respective merits of the then presidential candidates, Hayes and Tilden. Near Desota, tragedy and fate overtook them. 

Exactly what happened no one knew but Mason. He claimed that they got into a drunken row, and that Woods assaulted him with a knife. In self defense, he says, he shot Woods. The team and wagon belonged to Woods, but after the shooting, Mason traveled on alone, leaving Woods lying on the ground in a dying condition. The injured man was later carried to the 

Fon Van Meter home and Dr. Smith was called. Four days later, he died without making any statement of the facts of the case. 

Suspicious of intentional foul play, the doctor and others sought to apprehend Mason, who they found at Prairie City. He declared that he had purchased the team of Woods and felt no compunction in taking them away. Mason was tried in district court in Adel in October, being defended by Attorney T. R. North.

He received a life sentence to the Anamosa penitentiary. A peculiar feature of the law at that time was that the defendant in a criminal prosecution was not permitted to testify in his own defense. During the coming years, Mason made repeated efforts for pardon, but to no avail, because of the feeling in Dallas county. 

Feeling at Desota ran high, and it was very difficult

GENERAL PROGRESS 

199 for the officers to prevent a lynching. Many years later there was a growing feeling that a man convicted entirely upon circumstantial evidence was hardly given justice, and in April, 1907, he received his pardon from Governor Cummins, after 31 years incarceration in the Anamosa prison.

Early in 1898, a condition developed between our country and Spain that soon led to declared war. 

For sometime Spain's intolerable oppression of Cuba had greatly annoyed our government, but when the 

Spaniards blew up our battleship, Maine, while on a friendly visit in Havana harbor, in February, a climax was reached which resulted in war being declared against Spain, on April 21st, and President McKinley called for 125,000 volunteers. 

On April 25th, 40 men left Perry on the Rock Island to join the troops mobilized in Des Moines. It was a memorable day in Perry. The militia gathered at the 

Armory, and with 5,000 people present, marched down Second street to the depot where they entrained at 

9:18 a. m. School was dismissed until 10:00 o'clock, flags were flying and the band playing. Pathetic goodbye's were said as the boys left for the uncertain future. In Des Moines they became a part of Company 

B, 52d Iowa Infantry. Their camp called Camp Mc 

Kinley, was located on the fair grounds. At first, they lived in the horse barns, but later in the cattle barns.

On May 28th, they left for the south going via Chicago and Louisville, Kentucky. Wm. Cunningham was detained at Camp Tanner, Springfield, Illinois, because of sickness, and in three weeks died. Chickamauga

Park, Georgia, was as far as they ever got. At one time they were assigned for detached service in Porto

GENERAL PROGRESS 

201 Rico, but someway were never moved further. On 

August 28th they left for Des Moines, arriving a few days later.

When they left Georgia, Frank Munn, was too sick to leave and had to remain in the hospital. In less than a week he, too, died. Upon arrival in Des Moines, they were granted a short furlough, but after this were held in quarantine for a time, and in late fall were discharged from the service.

SECTION THREE THE MODERN PERIOD

CHAPTER 10  

1900-1938 

County Seat Controversy-New Court House-Adel Mill— Riverside Park-The Gregg Fund. 

With the turn of the century, there was considerable mention of the twentieth century. A great deal of pride was taken in up-to-date inventions, methods and conveniences. People marveled over the progress made since the days of the pioneers. They would not have thought it possible that the next 40 years would bring about even greater changes than the last 40. Automobiles, radio, airplanes, television, electric refrigeration and air conditioning were not even thought of. 

The twentieth century was due to bring about a wonderful fulfillment of Bible prophecy, "Many shall run to and fro, and knowledge shall be increased." (Dan. 12:4.)

For many years, the question of proper location for the county seat was never raised. Adel naturally grew into the place, and everybody was satisfied, but when two lines of railroad had been built through the county and neither one reached Adel, the town of Dallas Center became confident that a change could be made. 

Besides having a railroad they were about a mile closer to the geographic center for the county than Adel. For about 30 years the controversy was intermittently kept up, and Adel was never certain of keeping the coveted courthouse. Adel citizens and their advocates shrewdly kept the matter from coming to 205

a vote. When the Milwaukee boom came to Perry, and that town became the metropolis, they bid strong for the courthouse too. The old courthouse, built in 

1857, was getting old and a new one was needed, but where it should be built was the county's most difficult problem.

Even Waukee aspired to capitol honors, and in this case a vote was taken, resulting in defeat for Waukee. 

The question caused the most spirited contest ever experienced in the history of the county. Adel was on the defensive, and her rivals on the aggressive. Candidates for county office felt the effects of the issue. 

Newspapers voiced the sentiments of their constituency, and sometimes resorted to sarcastic discussions. Some pretty shrewd moves were made on both sides and every advantage was taken of technicalities and mistakes. 

Even rivalry between competing railroads was brought to bear upon the question.

Although the aspirants for the county seat were unable to effect a change in location, yet they were successful in defeating all propositions to vote a tax for a new courthouse, although it was generally believed that a new and larger one was needed. For 20 years conflicting factions kept the whole matter deadlocked. 

In 1898, a vote was taken on a proposed $65,000 courthouse on the old site; 1,605 votes were in favor and 2,281 against the proposition. During the next two years there was a decided change in the minds of many people. They realized that the old courthouse was out of date, and inadequate to meet the need, besides it was no longer considered a safe place for

valuable documents and records from a fire standpoint, and furthermore, they began to realize that their bickerings were getting them nowhere. 

In 1900 the matter was again voted upon, this time for an $85,000 structure. The tide had turned-3,338 votes were favorable, and 2,049 opposed the special tax. Adel itself voted 16 to 486 in favor of the project. 

It was a great victory for Adel, and her citizens were jubilant indeed. Plans were immediately made for a big celebration the following Friday. Old animosities were apparently forgotten, bells were rung, bonfires lighted and speeches made with torchlight processions galore. A. C. Hotchkiss, editor of the Republican, hurriedly wrote an expressive poem of unusual length, to relieve the exuberance of his feelings. 

The board of supervisors, consisting of John Shively, M. W. Gribbens and Levi Hockett, and C. R. Lyon, clerk of the board, let the contract for the new courthouse to James Rawson & Sons of Iowa City, for $74,300. This did not include plumbing, heating and furnishings, and their work was to be completed by April 1, 1902. 

The building, 84 by 126 feet in size, was constructed of the best quality of Bedford stone, some of the blocks in the main wall weighing as much as three and a half tons. The floor of the halls and lobbies were of tile, and marble wainscoating graced the walls of the central portion of the building. Including the basement, there were three complete floors, all conveniently furnished for the county offices and court room. The building with its pretty white stone, red roof, artistic archi-

tectural design, and tower rising to a height of 128 feet, presents a very pleasing appearance in its setting of a gently sloping bluegrass lawn-a building which is a credit to those who planned it, and a source of pride to Dallas county citizens.

The dedication day in September, 1902, was another memorable day for Adel and the county. A tent, seating 1,200, was erected just east of the Christian church and used for the dedication service. The Jefferson band furnished splendid music and Hon. Joseph A.

Dyer of Des Moines, delivered the dedicatory address. 

A part of the day's program was the public inspection of the entire building by an appreciative citizenry. 

The building was electrically lighted throughout and heated by hot water from a nearby heating plant. 

After the letting of the original contract, numerous changes and additions were made as shown by the
