One of the last attractive finishing touches was the big clock installed in the tower. This cost $1,000, and was purchased by Adel people with D. A. Blanchard, pioneed merchant, contributing $500. On November 

25th, the leading attorneys of the county hung a picture of distinguished Iowans in the court room. Of the seven placed on exhibition, one was of a Dallas county man-Hon. Edwin H. Conger.

Soon after the completion of the courthouse, two other matters arose to disturb the peace of the people. 

It seems that the Perry people had agreed to support the courthouse tax upon the condition that a new jail would be erected in Perry. This was not done, and considerable feeling was created. Legal steps were taken by Perry citizens to carry out their wishes, but to no avail. Besides, it was contended that the county board had exceeded their authority in expending more than the $85,000 originally voted upon. The board admitted that they had technically erred, but claimed that the end justified the means. The court sustained the board, but many felt it would have been better to have asked the voters for an expression on the additional money invested. In a few years, this turbulent state of affairs had passed into history, and permanent peace came to abide. The question of county seat location has never came up since, and probably never will.

Perhaps no county official was ever better known over a long period of the time than Ralph E. Joy. 

On January 1, 1937, he retired after a 49-year period of faithful service, the last 25 years as clerk of court

and the preceding 24 years as deputy clerk. Mr. Joy has demonstrated that courtesy and consideration, as well as, efficiency is what the public likes to see in public officials. Coming with his parents to Adel in 

1867, he has seen the town and county develop through the years, and takes a great deal of pride in Dallas county institutions.

During the days when the courthouse question was being agitated, the complaint was frequently made that Adel had no first class hotel. To overcome this objection, the Adel Improvement Co. was formed to build an up-to-date hotel; $10,500 was raised in subscribed stock and donations, and the Arlington Hotel was built at the northwest corner of the square. Dr. Caldwell was president and J. W. Russell secretary of the company. After many years of successful operation, the 

Arlington is still Adel's leading transient home. 

In 1903, T. R. North gave to Adel, the advantages of a modern opera house, built at a cost of $14,500. Its present name is the Rialto. About 1900, the Greely 

Brothers started a creamery. Directly it was taken over by the McRae Brothers, who for a while made it pay, but it had to be discontinued. Among Adel's larger fires was the one on November, 1905, when Heston's drug store, the Adel furniture store and A. M. 

Riggs' general store were burned. The loss was approximately $50,000.

The well-known Adel mill made a remarkable growth under the management of Macy & Hancock. With water power, they operated very ecoomically. When the water supply was insufficient, they used steam

power. Farmers brought their wheat to grind from a distance of 30 miles. Three grades of flour were made Sovereign, Ambrosia and Ruth-and these were sold throughout the state. The daily capacity was about 

100 barrels. Unfortunately, this thriving mill burned down on August 22, 1914. The loss was estimated at $25,000. Prior to this time, Mr. Macy, had begun a light plant in connection with the mill. This was Adel's first light plant, and the rate, due to water power and double purpose machinery, was much cheaper than that of most towns.

After the fire, the milling was discontinued. Rebuilding was for a light plant only. Mr. Macy made a deal with a Des Moines electric company whereby he could sell any surplus electricity he might have when water was abundant, or he could buy when a local shortage might occur. During the first year, they sold more than they bought. The company expanded until the towns of Dallas Center, Desota, and Van Meter were being supplied from Adel. About 1922, Mr. Macy sold out to the Iowa Light and Power Co. of Des Moines.

On Easter Sunday, 1902, the Christian church burned down, but immediately plans were begun for the erection of a new and better building, which was dedicated the following December, by Evangelist B. B. 

Burton, who held a series of meetings at that time. 

The church property was valued at $8,000. Mr. J. B. 

White was Sunday school superintendent for over 30 years, retiring in 1905. About 10 years ago a magnificent new brick church was built on Ninth street, and the old building was converted into an oil station.

The present pastor is Reverend Sippell. The Methodists also erected a fine new brick building in 1923, and the present pastor is Rev. W. G. Warren. The 

Catholics have a church on West Main street, and Reverend O'Neil is the pastor. 

Adel's original water supply came from an artesian well near the mill, and was pumped by their machinery to a standpipe in the center of the town. In 1907, a modern sewerage system was installed. The Farmers' 

Mutual Telephone Co., operating in that portion of the county had over 1,300 farmers connected by phone in one rural system in 1907, and John Fox was president of the company. The Mutual still continues, but about 1922, the Northwestern Bell Telephone Co. bought some local interests and have developed into the best telephone service of the present time, with local exchanges at Dallas Center, Granger, Van Meter and Perry.

In January, 1935, the Adel State Bank suspended business and since that time the Dallas County State Bank is the only banking institution in Adel. This is one of the Brenton Bros. banks and O. D. Ellsworth is cashier. The Brenton interests are also found in Dallas 

Center, Waukee, Granger, Woodward and Perry.

Adel has had numerous small factories at various times, but one really assumed large proportions for some years. This was a company that manufactured husking gloves and mittens, and grew until a large two-story brick building was needed to accommodate the business. A few years ago the building was abandoned and has since been converted into an apartment

house. This was south of the railroad tracks not far from the depot.

By 1914, Adel had outgrown the old schoolhouse built many years ago and a new modern structure was built. About two years later, another smaller building was built. These two modern buildings adequately took care of Adel's school needs. There are now 538 pupils, 20 teachers and Donald Weir is superintendent. In 1906 a commercial club was organized to promote the general welfare of the town and to induce outside enterprises to locate here. A. C. Hotchkiss was chosen president and J. B. White, secretary.

Benjamin Greene, one of the county's prominent pioneers, passed away in June, 1902, at the ripe old age of 83. He came to Adel about 1850. "He was a member of the house in the Fourth General Assembly, which convened at Iowa City in December, 1852, when this district was comprised of the counties of 

Jasper, Polk, Dallas, Guthrie, Greene, Boone, Story, Marshall, Hardin, Risley, Yell, Fox, Pocahontas, Humbolt, Wright, Franklin, Cerro Gordo, Hancock, Kossuth, Palo Alto, Emmett, Bancroft, Winnebago and 

Worth. Four of these counties are not known in Iowa today." He was also a member of the Sixth General 

Assembly, four years later. He was one of the leading promoters of Adel's first railroad, giving two years of his time and several thousand dollars in money, donating it all.

The ground just north of town and along the mill slough which was used for so many years as a fair ground, was purchased by the city in April, 1903, to

be converted into a park. The purchase price was $3,200 and several adjoining lots were also purchased, bringing the total outlay up to over $5,000. John 

Shannon deeded one acre, and streets within this area were vacated for park purposes.

Willows were planted on the edge of the water, the grounds were surveyed and landscaped, a substantial fence erected and the grounds named, "Riverside Park." This has become a popular place for many public gatherings such as Farm Bureau picnics, 4-H achievement shows, etc. Just northeast of these grounds a covered bridge for many years spanned the mill slough.

Adel was fortunate for a city of its size to secure government city mail delivery which was begun on 

May 1, 1930. The first carriers were Fred Wood and

Vernon McCleary. Adel's postmasters as far as can now be ascertained were as follows: 

J. C. Corbell, 1847 

Stephen K. Scovell 

Ira Sherman 

W. C. Thomas

James Robinson 

N. B. Nickols

G. A. Atwood S. B. Hemstead 

N. B. Nichols

A. C. Hotchkiss, Sept., 1904

Mary Ikenberry, May, 1933

Among the leading permanent business interests of the present time are: Buckman's, hardware; Deford

Bros., furniture; Fowler's, drugs; Cozad & Forrester, drugs; Heynen's, general store; S. F. Clampitt, groceries; Sloan Lumber Co. and elevator; Eclipse Lumber Co.; Mack's cafe; Buckley's, shoes and clothing; 

A. E. Skinner, hardware and harness; Sanders & 

Fortier, groceries and meats; Clyde Coulter, variety store; John Blanchard, hardware and implements; Lon 

B. Smith, groceries, and M. E. Williams, clothing.

Chain stores operating in Adel at the present time are: 

Atlantic & Pacific Tea Co., Gambles Store, United

Food Market, State Liquor Store. 

The historic Gregg fund, which is now 70 years old, has steadily grown through the years. Regardless of differing opinions it is generally conceded that it has been entrusted to competent and shrewd men who have made a remarkable record of increase. On January 1, 1938, the principal had reached the splendid sum of $154,484.75. The funds have been invested mostly in good farm mortgages.

In 1935, the county tried to collect taxes from the fund, contending that under the circumstances it should not be exempt, but when carried to the supreme court the decision was against the county. The present trustees are Vern Russell of Adel, Anthony McColl of 

Woodward, and Harry Wifvat of Perry. The old

Gregg school in conjunction with the Adel public schools was discontinued when the change was made to the new buildings. What the outcome may be would be difficult to say. Times and conditions have so changed since Mr. Gregg created this fund that some do not believe such a school is necessary. Others say

that this amount is not sufficient to build and endow an institution of this kind.

In the summer of 1919, the Ashton addition was annexed to the western edge of Adel thereby making room for the steady and gradual expansion of the city. Adel now has a very suitable library, which is very beneficial to those attending the public school. 

It was begun about nine years ago in the old Presbyterian church.

Since paving in the larger towns has become prevalent, Adel has not failed to keep abreast of the times, so that today pavement is not only found around the square, but on the principal streets as well. 

In early times Benjamin Greene conducted a nursery, but was followed in later years by M. J. Graham, two miles east of town. After many years of successful operation Mr. Graham is still operating the nursery, despite the multiplied number, and keen competition in nursery stock.

O. C. Wright of Adel operated the first bus line in 

Iowa. His first venture was between Adel and Des 

Moines in March, 1921. After developing quite a line, he sold out to the Cedar Rapids Transfer Co. in 1930. About a year later, service was extended westward to Omaha. Today there are two lines through

Adel: The Union Pacific stages and Burlington trail ways. The Adel sales barn was built early in 1936 by 

S. J. Nusam, who is a competent auctioneer, and former sheriff. The sales barn, at its convenient location, at the northeast edge of town, is the weekly scene of large crowds of people, buying, selling and trucking.

In 1934, while workmen were digging gravel at the southeast corner of town, they found a piece of engine boiler about seven feet beneath the surface, of the gravel. How it got there was a mystery to everybody, but Mr. Spring recalls that many years ago, a steam sawmill blew up in an explosion, and some of the fragments were blown clear into town. Probably some was blown into the ground. 

Scott Snyder, upon leaving the Perry Chief in 1925, came to Adel to buy the Dallas County News of A. C. 

Hotchkiss. A few years later, he bought the Dallas 

County Record of Victor Sweeley's widow and merged it with the News. Since then, there has been but one newspaper, which serves the community in an admirable way, and is one of the official county papers. 

Of the 26 men who have been governors of Iowa, Dallas county has had the honor of furnishing one of them-Hon. Geo. W. Clarke of Adel. In 1878 he came to Adel and opened a law office; a few years later he formed a partnership with J. B. White under the firm name of White & Clark. In 1900 he was elected to the state legislature. So well was he liked by both republicans and democrats that his re-election continued until he became governor of Iowa on the republican ticket in 1913. His manner and efficiency won for him a second term, and Dallas county can well be proud of one who filled his place so well.

Gov. GEORGE W. CLARK

CHAPTER 11  

1900-1937 In 1900, Perry had an official population of 3,986. 

The town made a steady growth and expansion from the very start, never once failing to show a material increase in population from one decade to another. As ⚫ early as 1880, when only 11 years old, it exceeded by 10 the population of Adel, and has ever since remained the metropolis of the county. During the 30 years before 1900, the county population rose from 12,019 to 23,058, but during the next 30 years only reached a level of 25,493, which is 117 less than it was in 1915. 

During the first decade of the present century, Perry had a series of memorable street fairs, or carnivals. 

These were held in the fall on Second street, which was roped off, for about four days. Merchants placed pretty artistic booths at the edge of the street in front of their stores. In these were eating stands, concessions and advertising. At intersecting streets were tent shows, merry-go-rounds, etc. Motion pictures were just beginning then, and first appeared in tent shows I called the "electric theatre." Great crowds turned out and enjoyed the festive spirit of the occasion. Those who recall these carnivals will probably never forget the showers of confetti, the "coney island, corn crist" and the electric treatre. 

Before Perry had a real park, there was a small tract of ground at Fourth and Park streets, called 219

McColl's Park. Here were numerous large maple trees and considerable grass-an ideal picnic ground. 

It was at this location that the first chautauqua was held in Perry about 1905. The Redpath chautauquas were proving very popular over the country, and Perry was no exception. Noted lecturers and talented musicians appearing in the new and novel way drew great crowds, and returns for the next year were assured a year in advance. For a good many years they continued, even in smaller places, but after 15 years or so, they seemed to have run their course and gradually disappeared from lack of support. 

In 1910, C. Durant Jones, who was an aggressive temperance worker and prohibitionist, launched what was at first called the Iowa Temperance Chautauqua.

The first year's chautauqua, although small in size, proved very successful in spreading temperance reform, besides yielding some degree of profit. The next year the name was changed to the Jones Chautauqua System, although the policies remained mostly the same. 

Five tents and sessions were simultaneously in operation, reaching 65 towns in all parts of Iowa. The schedule was carefully planned from Perry and things moved along like clockwork. The talent was obtained from 

Pennsylvania to California, and proved very interesting and instructive. In most places the sessions were decidedly successful. In a few years, the business had grown until several complete circuits, independent of each other, were in operation over an area of 12 states, but the magnitude of the enterprise and proportionate expenses connected with declining chautauqua activ-

ities and interest soon plunged the business into bankruptcy.

There have been numerous bad railroad wrecks in which Perry people were involved. The worst in the history of the Iowa division was near Rhodes, Iowa, on August 6, 1902. At this time a Milwaukee work train collided with a regular train resulting in 14 deaths, including engineer Fred Braman and S. D. 

Markress. Thirty others were injured, most of whom were laborers on the work train. 

On June 23, 1920, a Rock Island freight train was partially wrecked between Perry and Angus. The contents of several cars of grain and hogs were violently dumped in the right-of-way. About a year later (July 30, 1921), a southbound M. & St. L. passenger train plowed into a Milwaukee freight which was crossing the track at Perry. It was about daylight on a foggy morning and the fog evidently hindered. visibility. At this particular time, Roy Paiste was returning from work at the roundhouse riding a bicycle on the opposite side of the Milwaukee freight, but in the path of the crash. He was probably killed instantly. 

In a Milwaukee wreck at Portmouth, Engineer Pendy of Perry was killed on March 6, 1924.

In the days of the old roundhouse, the railroad owned an unused tract of ground nearby that became known as the "showgrounds," due to the fact that all the circuses coming to Perry erected their tents there. 

Ringling Bros., Wallaces and Hagenbeck's have all showed on this tract. In later years, the Tri-County fair grounds were used for circus purposes. For a

number of years, the Yankee Robinson circus, whose winter headquarters were east of Granger, used to show in Perry for their annual spring opening. Until the circus moved from the state, Perry looked forward to the spring opening as a regular occurrence. R. M. 

Harvey, held a prominent place with the Wallace- Hagenbeck circus, and was instrumental in having tons of their advertising matter printed by the Chief company. For many years they have done a great deal of circus printing. 

In 1903, more room was needed for Perry's growing school population and the Willard school was built at Seventh and Pattee streets, at a cost of $15,000. By this time the original school building, which later became known as the high school, was getting old and inadequate, and a feeling prevailed that a new building, and location, too, should be considered. The principal problem proved to be the location, rather than the building. When the present location was suggested, some people in the western portion of the city strongly opposed the cite, but after a warm controversy the eastern place was decided upon. This property was formerly the George Heaton place, being a double block between Tenth and Twelfth streets, and south of Willis avenue.

The people had voted $32,000 for the purpose, and the building was erected in 1909. On February 22, 1910, the school was dedicated and thrown open for public inspection. At this time a commercial course was added to the curriculum of the school, and four years later a normal course was added. The first class

to graduate from the high school was only seven in number, in 1883. In comparison with that, is a maximum of 86 in 1935.

Within ten years, four new schools were built in 

Perry, the third and fourth being Webster and Lincoln schools, to replace the old Webster and Otley schools. 

These schools, built at the same time, 1913, are exactly alike, and were up to the minute in modern construction and conveniences. The Lincoln was built on the site of the old Otley building, but the other location was moved two block farther west, and the old Webster building was stuccoed and transformed into an apartment house.

The old Washington building was used for various purposes until April 30, 1917, when it was sold at public auction. What to do with this excellent piece of ground, so well located, became a municipal question. 

There seemed to be no probability of it again being used for school purposes, therefore, many favored converting it into a downtown park, but this proposal was halted when it became known that the donor, Harvey Willis, gave it to the city for school purposes only.

Today it is kept mowed in summer, and is occasionally used by a carnival or for a tent meeting. With the building up of the west end, as a result of the new roundhouse location, a grade school was needed in that section, too, so in 1922, the Roosevelt school was built at West Eighth and Willis avenue. The complete property was valued at $85,000. The school system now consisted of a high school and four grade schoolsone in each quarter of the city.

No sooner had this building been erected until the school board was beginning to realize that the high school would soon be too small. Consolidated schools were in vogue-country children were attending high school as a regular thing, whereas in times past only a few would consider a high school education. The 

Perry Independent School District was extended into the country, in some places, as far as two miles. For several years, the high school was much crowded, as the number of pupils increased annually. Different plans were considered to remedy the situation but finally a government P.W.A. proposition was taken advantage of to enlarge the present building. On 

November 4, 1935, the people of Perry voted on the proposed proposition, which resulted in 885 in favor, and 182 opposed to the same. The original amount planned on was $117,000, of which the government would donate 45 per cent or a limit of $52,650. 

Late in the winter of 1936, work was begun on the two additions, one on the north end, but the larger, containing gymnasium, on the south end. By the opening of school in September the new part was occupied, although not completely finished. In September, 1931, floodlights were installed on the athletic field permitting the playing of football at night. The Perry Parent

Teachers' Association was formed at the high school in May, 1913, following a Mothers' Day program.

Mrs. L. A. French was the first president, and Mrs. 

H. L. Thomas, secretary. The purpose primarily was to create and maintain better cooperation between the parents and the teachers in the various phases of

school life. Meetings are held on the fourth Friday of each school month. The purchase of playground apparatus, health, milk for the undernourished, dental work, and sale of Christmas seals are some of the activities taken up by the group. At present Mrs. A. W. 

Nicholson is president.

There are now 54 regular teachers employed in the Perry public schools. The salaries paid them range from $840 to $2,250, with the superintendent getting $3.300. The personnel of the school officials are as 

Superintendent.. 

Secretary of Board. Treasurer..

President of Board. 

J. S. Vanderlinden 

.L. A. French 

W. O. Hunter .Harry Wifvat 

Other board members are: M. E. Jenkins, George 

Fenner, Mrs. Chas. Swift and Mrs. H. M. Cole.

In September, 1920, the Catholic people opened the new Parochial school at Fifth and Lucinda streets, which is in charge of the Sisters of St. Francis. The building is modern in every respect and the school offers an entire four-year high school course. 

The first exclusive light plant was built at Second and Otley, but across what is now Second street. This was in 1896, and residents on the south end of that street had to go up town via First or Third streets.

After the new light plant was built at another location in 1916, the old building was torn down, but solid concrete bases, etc., remained. Agitation was then begun about opening up Second street, making it one

continuous street from end to end. A portion of the 

Allgood building on Railroad street, which extended too far to the east was removed, and finally in 1920, Second street was really opened to general traffic, after a 24-year blockade.

After the removal of the old Baptist church (1904), from the triangular area bounded by Third, Railroad and Otley avenue, the property was sold to parties who enclosed the whole outer edge for the operation of a "10-cent feed shed." For many years this shed was a popular place for the farmers. They would rather pay ten cents to put their team or horse under the shed with a little hay and have their belongings safe from theft than to tie outside in all kinds of weather. After automobiles replaced "old Dobbin" the same sheds, then dilapidated, were used as an automobile salvage place. Later the old sheds were removed, the grounds put in presentable condition and an excellent filling station erected. 

One improvement that went a long ways toward changing Perry from a town to a city was the laying of pavement on the principal streets. During the summer and fall of 1910 not only the business district but Willis avenue, Otley avenue, Warford street and First avenue were paved. The pavement on Willis avenue was of creosoted wooden blocks. In a few years water penetrated the tarred surface and the swollen blocks heaved up in places from a few inches to a foot high. For a number of years these conditions presented quite a problem for the city council. The next general move toward city street paving was about

10 years later when Third, Fourth and Sixth streets were paved. At various times other areas and short connections were paved, until the primary streets were all paved, and the rest were surfaced with gravel, so that at any season traffic can freely move all over town. 

The Perry Library Association was formed in 1894, and Wm. Tarr became librarian. At one time there were 

1,000 books, and when the new library was begun 350 of the books were added to the 1,050 new ones purchased. At the regular city election in March, 1902, the people voted favorable about the building of a city library. At that time, Andrew Carnegie, wealthy steel magnate, was encouraging the starting of libraries in small cities by contributing freely of his means for this purpose. Mr. Carnegie donated $10,000 for a library in Perry, and the structure was erected upon the "triangle" in the center of the business district which was owned by the city. The new building greatly enhanced this section of town and filled up an old vacant space. The formal opening was held 

December 19, 1904, at which time Governor Cummins gave an appropriate address. Miss Flora Bailey was chosen as the first librarian, and after a third of a century, she is still holding the same place, to the general satisfaction of all.

In 1907, a superior court was begun in Perry in the basement of the library. Wm. Fahey was the first judge and Adrian Cross the clerk. A special decree of the Iowa legislature, backed by a vote of the local people, made such a court possible in some counties, and according to law, only the vote of the people of

Perry could abolish it. Judge Fahey, was succeeded by 

John Shortley in 1908, who continued until his death in 1913. Judge R. S. Barr was then appointed, followed by W. W. Cardell in 1915. Judge Cardell retired in 1920 and the court was automatically suspended, but not technically abolished.

The "Booster Edition" of the Daily Chief was published in the spring of 1910 to commemorate the thirtyfifth anniversary of the incorporation of the town. It contained 44 pages and was brimful of interesting sketches, history, advertising, pictures of individuals, stores, etc., all designed to stimulate interest in Perry and show up the manifold advantages of the fair town. 

R. M. Harvey was the general manager and Scott 

Snyder, the managing editor. The personnel of the merchants, and the methods of business have changed greatly since then. Today a copy of this paper is a cherished relic in the estimation of anyone fortunate enough to possess one. Much of the information used in this history was gleaned from its pages and from another large special edition of the same paper published September 19, 1928, the occasion being the sixtieth anniversary of the founding of the town. At this time the Chief moved into their elegant new home, fully equipped in modern style. 

On a stormy night in 1904, a member of the fire department, half jokingly, said the department ought to erect a new building for their home. The idea appealed to others and soon a definite move was afoot looking toward a new home. Money was raised in various ways, and work begun in earnest. The corner-

stone was laid on April 18, 1905, with attorney Wm. 

Fahey giving an address. The amount of $5,500 was spent on the project, not including much labor donated by members. Later, the second story rooms were finished for living quarters and Mr. Hollis moved in, in 1915 to become the steward. 

The first of Perry's three fire trucks carried 1,000 feet of hose and a pump capable of shooting 750 gallons of water a minute. Another similar, although smaller truck, was purchased besides a hook and ladder truck. 

Many of the fires now are put out with chemicals only, which have several advantages over water. Since the advent of high speed trucks, the firemen began the practice of running to rural fires, where they sometimes could do very effective work. Early in 1937, a move was begun toward the purchase of a truck especially for fires on farms and in nearby small towns. Little by little the money came in from farmers and land owners until the purchase of a special truck was made possible. On Farmers' Day (June 9th), the new truck was displayed for the first time in the farmers' parade. 

Arrangements were made to keep the truck at the city fire station.

Each fireman has a telephone and is able to know immediately after the sounding of the alarm, just where the fire is. The firemen have always received $1.00 each, for every time they respond, whether the fire be big or little. Harry Timeon is now chief. The firemen, through the years have fought many serious fires, chief of which are shown on following page.

The Otis Mill, about 1895.. $ 35,000 

The Big Fire, Nov. 20, 1898... 300,000 

Clements block, Dec. 25, 1906. . . . . 50,000 

Dignan's store, May 14, 1907... 25,000 

Osmundson factory, Oct. 13, 1914.. 15,000 

Globe Co., Jan. 27, 1915.. 130,000

$955,000

At the time the Inter-Urban was building into Perry, David Pattee owned a tract of land lying just west of the new line and south of Willis avenue. This he decided to donate to the city for a park, which was gladly accepted and named Pattee Park. The formal opening came in April, 1906. Trees were set, a bridge built across Frog creek and the stream improved. Of course, it takes time to develop a beautiful park, but eventually it became a very nice place a credit to any town. In 1921, a swimming pool was built, which has since been enlarged and improved. Hedge fence, brick gateways, electric lights, picnic tables, mowed grass, flowerbeds, tennis grounds, benches and children equipment all combine to make it an ideal city park. 

The president of the park board is A. V. Airhart. 

For years there has been a Commercial Club of businessmen whose object was to induce new business enterprises to come to Perry, and to help and encourage those who were already here. As far back as 1900

such a club existed under the leadership of Allen Harvey. It was during his time that the Monday noon meetings were begun.

During the years, many new enterprises were drawn to Perry, such as the De Laura Auto Co., the Osmundson Spade Factory, A. S. Kibby and the washing machine industry, the Van Camp Condensory, Shorthill 

Steel and Iron Works, and the Interurban railway. The 

Club was also instrumental in the first gravel roads, the first city paving, the expansion and relocating of the Milwaukee shops and the development of the Perry 

Town Lot and Improvement Co. In 1916, the club took the more modern name of Bureau of Commerce and Roscoe Zerwekh became secretary. The Grimes 

Canning Co., Marks Hat Co., At Last Washer Co., 

Progressive Foundry, Hausserman Packing Co. and

Tri-County Fair were largely the result of the bureau's endeavors. Later the name was changed to Community 

Club. Today N. P. Black is at the head of the organization.

Early in 1912, B. C. Dilenbeck decided to open up a new addition to the town. The large Shorthill plant, then under construction, apparently assured employment for many people, and his east end addition would be a very suitable matter. The main objection raised by people was its distance, of a mile or more, from down town but this was overcome by the prospects of a street car line. On July 14, 1912, a franchise was granted to B. C. Dilenbeck to build a car line. The line was never built, but a carload of ties was piled upon the ground ready for use.

The first addition consisted of 22 acres west of Sixteenth street and 40 acres east of Sixteenth. The second, of 20 acres, was south of Willis avenue, while a third, of 82 acres, was further out. The ground was surveyed and platted, and annexed to the city under the name of Dilenbeck addition. Streets were graded, and sewer and sidewalks were constructed. Sixty teams were busy most of the summer doing various kinds of work.

Nearly 50 new houses were built, lumber sheds were erected and lumber purchased by the carload. In 

September, an auction sale was held to sell the properties to the public. The houses, in all stages of construction, were scattered over much of the area, but all on laid out streets with sidewalks, sewer, young trees, etc. Many houses and lots were sold, but many were not.

About this time the Perry Town Lot and Improvement Co. was incorporated at $100,000, with Mr. 

Dilenbeck holding most of the stock. An attempt was made to gradually sell off the unsold properties, but after the passing of the boom, little progress was made toward further improvements. Today a good deal of this area is settled as far east as Eighteenth street, while some has reverted back to farm land. 

The Shorthill proposition proved to be a "lemon" in the phraseology of one writer. It was a stock company attempting to operate on a large scale-$250,000 was their goal, and many local people, having faith in the enterprise, bought stock. A large shed of enormous size, and with extremely heavy concrete

foundation, was erected. This was about as far as they got when the company went into receivership, and the attractive "bubble" burst. Some years later the property of the defunct company was purchased by Chas. Haussermen, and upon this site the packing house was built.

Another important addition to the city was Highview. This comprised the 40 acres formerly used by the old fair association between Twelfth and Sixteenth streets. In the spring of 1905, this land was surveyed and platted, and lots sold at public auction. Slowly new houses were built and today it is an important part of the city, and the hospital is within this area. 

After the discontinuance of the old fairground, there was no suitable place for baseball, horse races, etc., so a group of business men leased some land on the western edge of Perry of D. J. Pattee for such purposes. This tract was north of Willis avenue, about where the Roosevelt school is now located. A race track (without grading) was laid out and a ball park arranged. George Gardiner was chosen manager, and for a number of years events of this nature, including 

Fourth of July celebrations, carnivals, etc., were held there.

When the new Milwaukee shops were built adjoining this land on the north, the Pattees saw an opportunity to plat the ground, and dispose of the property. This land, of approximately 40 acres. was annexed to the city of Perry on August 19, 1913, under the name of

Pattee Riverview Heights. Being high ground and close to the new shops, the lots were in good demand,

and in a few years much change had taken place. The 

Roosevelt school was built within this area. 

About this time C. Durant Jones opened up a small addition south of Iowa street, and the McLuen interests annexed McLuen addition south of Park street and east of the M. & St. L. railroad. Other minor additions were Langfords, Winegars, Fairview, Foster & Heaton's, Park, Willis' first and second, Gillinands, Somerville's, Millers', Norris', Robinson's, and Kronkites'. 

The Perry Normal College was one of Perry's flourishing institutions at the beginning of the present century. It was founded by H. C. Well in 1892 as a normal school only. Early in 1895, Wm. Tarr was employed as an instructor, and before the year was over he was in full charge. For the next ten years, Mr. Tarr worked with untiring energy to build up one of the best schools of that time. The motto that he always endeavored to carry out was "The good of the student, first, last, and always." The school occupied the entire second floor of the Diddy building on Willis avenue, but the summer schools were so largely attended that he secured the use of the high school. As many as 250 sometimes attended and Mr. 

Tarr had the cooperation and endorsement of prominent business men. Among the instructors of Mr. 

Tarr's time were: Prof. L. E. Blezck, and Carolyn Forgrave. ། ཀ In 1905, Mr. Tarr sold out to E. L. Meek, who conducted the school for one year, then he sold out to C. Durant Jones. After the beginning stage a com-

mercial course was added which remained to the end. 

After the building of the new high school and the commencement of normal and commercial courses there, the college could no longer survive. Among the later teachers were: E. E. Woods, F. M. Fazel, Mrs. Mignonette Donellson and Mrs. Anna Springer, all well known now in Perry and vicinity. Many people today, with pleasure and gratitude, look back to the P. N. C. days.

The Kings Daughters' Hospital is one of Perry's worthwhile institutions. Built in 1913, at a cost of $25,000, the credit belongs to the Kings Daughters, a group of local women organized in 1904. A year later plans were made for the erection of a hospital when sufficient funds could be raised. Eight years were required to reach their goal, which gave to Perry a first class hospital service. With three floors and basement, ample room was provided for office, operating room, kitchen, laundry, and 35 beds for patients, a few of which are maintained by the county. The hospital enjoys a large patronage and has become an essential part of the city. Mrs. Pierce was the first president in 1904 and the virtual founder of the society. Miss Victoria Carlson of Chicago, was the first superintendent and Mrs. John Sarrett is the present superintendent.

For many years, a gang of criminals, whose headquarters were near Angus, menaced a wide scope of the country. The name of Burn's gang was applied to them because of their leader, Tom Burns. Burns was a man of unusual ability and his manner among people

was most misleading as to the true state of his heart. 

On different occasions, arrests were made of members of the gang, but finally the leader, himself, was caught stealing from the Robinson clothing store in Perry.

His conviction and sentence to the penitentiary in 1905 broke up the gang and no further trouble was experienced from this source.

Rural free delivery was first made from the Perry post office on July 15, 1900. At this time, two routes were begun, with B. B. Hopkins and W. B. Taylor as carriers. Later two more routes were added, and for a time even a fifth, but with the advent of automobiles and good roads, the routes have been gradually lengthened in miles, and reduced in number, so that in 1937 there were only two. Carriers of these routes are Oscar Daniels and Carl Johnson.

City delivery was begun on March 2, 1908, with three regular carriers: Charles Jones, Marvin Cole, and Fred Melick. After 30 years, two of the number are still on the local force. Mr. Melick has made an unusually excellent record of 30 years steadily employed at the same job, while Mr. Jones has for a long time been assistant postmaster.

Through the influence of Congressman Hull and the growing postal receipts, Perry was permitted to have a new government post office building. A site at Third and Warford streets was purchased at approximately $6,000 and upon this ideal location the government spent $63,000 for the building and $5,000 for fixtures. 

Built of Bedford stone pressed brick and tile roof, the building presents a very pleasing appearance. It was

formally opened on February 15, 1915, when 3,000 proud citizens filed through the building to inspect 

Uncle Sam's new quarters. 

A first class post office must sell at least $40,000 worth of stamps annually and it is very unusual for a place the size of Perry to reach that figure. A small box in one corner of David Pattee's store was large enough for Uncles Sam's business in the early days of the town. Calvin Carver, who was postmaster just before Mr. Pattee, received about $30 a year for his services. This amount represented a certain per cent of the postage stamps sold. 

Perry postmasters through the years have been as follows: Calvin Carver, (Alton) 1867; D. J. Pattee, 1869; Lindsy Willis, 1881; Dewitt West, 1886; L. B. 

Thornburg, 1890; Zac Dunlap, 1894; Frank Hoeye, 1898; H. A. Nash, 1914; G. C. Parsons, 1923; W. T. 

Stockton, 1929; Frank Aubrey, 1935.

As early as about 1913 or 1914 when aviation was still in its infancy, Perry had a local aviator in the person of Ralph McMillan. One evening his plane fell in Frog creek, but he escaped unharmed. He soon left 

Perry, but continued flying, until he was killed in a crash at St. Francis, Kansas, on September 2, 1916.

Many times planes came to Perry to carry passengers on short flights. At first a few minutes ride would cost as much as $10, but the price gradually came down to $1. On one occasion a plane crashed on the Earl 

Freestone farm carrying two Perry men, Oscar Connor and Carl Johnson, to their death (June 5, 1927). At

one time there was talk of building an airport near Perry.

In one of the nation's major aerial disasters Bert 

McLaughlin of Perry, was killed. Mr. McLaughlin, owner of the Square Deal Grocery, in company with two of his friends, Ewald Berglund of Boone, and 

Marcellus Zinsmaster of Des Moines, had taken a trip to Los Angeles. On the return trip the plane crashed in the mountains about 20 miles from Salt Lake City (February 23, 1934). Several days were required to find the wreckage, but when found the entire number of eight people were dead; probably killed instantly, as their bodies were badly mangled.

For several years Perry had an unusual grocery service. Bert Schaffer, on May 20, 1922, began the operation of a traveling grocery. By motor power he traversed the city streets with quite a stock of groceries that the busy housewife could purchase, when needed, without leaving home. Probably the first community grocery store in the residential districts was one on 

West Willis avenue, as long ago as 1916. Since then they have gradually increased until today there are at least a dozen such stores. A few have been of considerable size, and of a permanent nature, such as the Sixth Street Grocery. This was originally opened up by 

George Overton, prior to 1921, near the present location. That fall he built the present store, and in a year or two sold out to Norman Wang, who successfully conducted the business until recently, when he sold out to H. W. Lindholm of Ogden.

In 1878 some believed the new cemetery would be of sufficient size for all time. Later that much more was purchased on the south side, but still more land was needed by 1922. Twenty acres were purchased just across the road to the east. A fine fence was erected and a Charles City firm landscaped the grounds. It was not considered another cemetery, but an addition to the old Violet Hill cemetery. The first burial here was a 16-year-old girl, Harriet Critchfield, on May 29, 1923. Since then a large portion of the space is well taken up. The name of Violet Hill was suggested by 

Mrs. Ben Moore in 1907.

Among the historic revival meetings of Perry were the Harper tabernacle meetings which began on February 24, 1924, and closed on April 6th. The Protestant churches united in bringing Evangelist A. K. Harper of Fairfield, Iowa, to Perry for these memorable meetings. A large board tabernacle was erected at Second and Warford, where the Union block had burned a few years before. After a short time an addition was built along the entire west side. This made a building covering the whole area of a quarter block. Tar paper covered the outside, and eight coal stoves heated the place inside. A room to one side of the platform was used as living quarters for the janitor and his wife, who were Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Collins. To the other side of the platform was an inquiry room, where penitent sinners might be instructed in spiritual matters. 

Immense crowds attended and many professed salvation.

As a result of these meetings a men's gospel team was soon organized, which met in various churches on Sunday afternoon, besides occasionally going out to other towns to hold meetings. Under the leadership of Mr. Harper a move was launched to close Sunday movies. While revival fires were still burning, and the people awakened to the demoralizing effects of evil, a vote was taken (May 1st), resulting in victory for the Christian forces. The city officials faithfully carried out the desires of the people to the consternation of the theatre owners.

Three years later a reaction came. Many ardent advocates of the first move became lukewarm. Opposers of reform contended that Perry was loosing business because of the situation, and the city council was petitioned to hold a referendum election. On November 

18, 1927, the people went to the polls again. The church people put up a systematic fight, but the trend of the times prevailed-Sunday movies were again unrestricted.

One of the greatest news stories of Iowa history was begun near Denison, Iowa, on February 3, 1931.

John Smith of Perry was engaged in the manufacture of a stock and poultry insecticide called "Disintone."

His business had made a remarkable growth in a few years' time, and he had recently ran for governor of 

Iowa on the Farmer-Labor ticket. On the evening of 

February 3d he left Perry with a truckload of Disintone for the western part of the state. Near Denison the truck was ditched and took fire. A passing motorist came to the scene and rescued the unfortunate driver

from the burning truck, who was burned beyond recognition. The body was taken to Perry, and buried in the usual way.

A short time before this he had taken out a large amount of life insurance, and his wife tried to collect $10,000 on one of his policies. The insurance company immediately became suspicious, and refused payment. 

As time went on, the more suspicious they became, and finally on March 12th the body was exhumed, and it was proved beyond question that it was not the body of John Smith.

Immediately a state-wide search began for Smith, and the case baffled officers for several months. In 

St. Paul, Minnesota, he bought a new car; on April 11th he married Miss Pauline Shaw in Kansas. Later his wife received a tip to meet him at a certain time at 

Black's Corner. Officers accompanied her to this place, and although they recognized him, failed to capture the mysterious criminal. For weeks nothing further was heard of him. Not only was the matter the subject of conversation throughout Iowa, but newspapers as far away as California devoted whole columns to the story. During some of the high spots of the case newspaper reporters came to Perry from Omaha, St. 

Paul and other cities. 

One morning in June, the 23d, a farmer near Garner, Iowa, found a man bound hand and foot with wire, along the roadside. The man said that he had been robbed or kidnapped, but it proved to be Smith. He was returned to Perry, and then to Adel, where he pleaded insanity. Judge W. S. Cooper committed

him to the department for criminally insane at the 

Anamosa penitentiary. Six years later (May 17, 1937) he was declared sane by the Iowa Sanity Commission, and returned to Sheriff Knee at Adel.

The Grand Opera House was built by R. M. and 

Allen Harvey in 1903. This was an up-to-date theatre, with a seating capacity of 750. The management has changed many times since then, and about three years ago it was entirely rebuilt and re-opened in elaborate style, with the new name of Perry Theatre.

During the winter of 1931-32 many farmers in South Dakota were entirely out of feed for their stock. Government relief hadn't yet begun to function. Perry farmers had a big crop of corn, and it was only worth from 10 to 25 cents a bushel, so local farmers decided to lend a helping hand to their needy neighbors, and give them a carload of corn. (Some people were burning it for fuel that winter.) The Milwaukee railroad agreed to haul it free, and Denniston & Partridge donated their services in loading it. On February 20th a car, containing 1,352 bushels of shelled corn, left Perry, which undoubtedly was a God-send in Dakota, and worked no hardship here. At another time, a shipment of corn, or its equivalent in money, was sent to famine sufferers in Arkansas. 

For many years F. A. Wildman was the principal auctioneer for many miles around Perry, but he was finally succeeded by L. A. Conway, who later was joined by his brother, Ray. In the fall of 1932 they began having regular weekly sales of livestock and all farm products in the main building of the defunct Tri-

County Fair. These sales proved to be very popular, and was a decided passing from the old custom of farm to farm sales. For a couple years prior to this, they had conducted a similar sale at West Third and Pattee streets.

In 1934 the Conways purchased lots at Eighteenth and Willis and erected a large and convenient sales barn, and have very successfully operated there since that time. During the drouth of 1934 southern Iowa farmers were obliged to sell their stock because of insufficient feed, while northern Iowa farmers had a plenty, consequently it was to the mutual advantage of both for one to sell to the other, and the Perry sales barn was made a common meeting place. Hundreds of head of stock are often sold in one day, with the sale continuing sometimes until 10:00° p. m. 

During 1919 there was quite a feeling that Perry should have another fairgrounds, and it was believed that such an undertaking would pay, besides being a good thing for the city. Various sites were considered, but finally a portion of the old Fred Moore dairy farm (38. 91 acres) lying between the Interurban tracks and the southern edge of the city was purchased. Prominent business men and farmers promoted the project and 400 stockholders were enrolled. W. H. Pattee was chosen president and Roscoe Zerwekh, secretary.

Twenty-one other men constituted a board of directors. 

The organization was named the Tri-County Fair Association, and was designed to include Dallas, Boone and Greene counties.

In the spring of 1920 work began with a rush, and was hurriedly carried forward to be ready for fair that year. About $55,000 was spent for buildings and improvements. A half-mile race track was built. 

The large amphitheatre and adjoining bleachers would seat 2,600 people. Other buildings included art hall. stock building, swine and sheep pavilion, speed barn and a few smaller buildings. Pearson & Son built the amphitheatre and the National Wood Works Co. the other structures. 

The first fair was held September 13th to 16th, and was a great success. Great crowds attended and $2,000 was given in prizes. Besides those already mentioned, E. D. Carter, H. C. Modlin and Chas. Etnire had an active part in the management at various times. The fair continued for about 13 years, the first half of the period being very successful, but the last half showing constant decline in attendance, interest and support. 

The whole project was undertaken during the time of extremely high prices. The land, wages and material were at prices that in normal times would seem prohibitive. Consequently financial troubles overtook the organization, and proved to be the principal cause of its dissolution.

Reminiscent with the Tri-County Fair was the many rodeos held there during this time. Wild, longhorn 

Brahma cattle and experienced cowboys came from the western ranges to display their skill to a thrilled crowd of spectators. During the time of their visit it was no uncommon thing for some of these wild cattle to run through city streets, over peoples gardens, and

into adjacent farmlands, jumping over fences like jackrabbits, to the consternation of the local people, and the aggravation of the cowboys. 

After the fairs were discontinued Mr. Kronkite converted one of the large stock pavilions into an elaborate dance hall and named the place Betty Lou Gardens. Time brought about various changes and today it is operated as a skating rink. About three years ago an out of town wrecking company purchased the amphitheatre and race barn, which were torn down and the lumber sold. Very little use is now made of this ideal piece of ground. 

In 1923 a wave of unusual activity was sweeping the country. The Ku Klux Klan, a secret organization, was at its height during 1923 and 1924. Throughout most of the nation thousands of people were joining this society, which advocated loyal Americanism, law enforcement, restricted foreign immigration and discrimination against Catholics, Jews and Negroes. 

Strict secrecy, masked faces, white robes and the burning of fiery crosses characterized their meetings. 

The move reached Perry when an organizer gave an open air lecture on the Washington school grounds during the summer of 1923. Many worthy citizens responded to the appeal, and soon a large membership was made up at Perry, augmented by many from nearby towns. A big mass meeting and celebration was held at the fairgrounds on the evening of September 24th. Klan members came from distant parts of the state and one of the largest crowds ever assembled in Perry came from far and near, curious to see

and hear more about the strange group. One feature of the occasion was an excellent display of fireworks, followed by the burning of an immense wooden cross. 

A similar meeting was held at the same place the following May.

In warm weather their private meetings were often held in the open fields of their rural members. The whole move created a breach between Catholics and Protestants, often to the disadvantage of each. Some unpopular policies were advocated and carried out, politics were agitated at election times; many members dropped out and in a few years the vital life of the society had mostly expired, although it still continues to the present time. 

At the time of Perry's second boom (1912) there was an influx of colored people. With the removal of the old roundhouse, property around there, and especially to the north, depreciated much in value, and this section offered them the most advantageous place to locate. There was also a small group who settled near the new shops, far removed from their brethren. 

Mr. Henry Lewis, one of the best known and respected of their number, states that there are now about 150 colored people in Perry, with about 250 at Moran, Zookspur and Highbridge, making the county population about 500.

For many years Perry has been a rather important popcorn center. A few years ago a peak of 160 acres was raised around Perry in one year. R. E. Fitzgerald & Son were not only the originators of the local industry but the principa' growers and dealers as well.

Beginning before the World War times they have seen the wholesale price range from $2.50 per hundredweight in depression times to about $15 in war times. Perry popcorn has been shipped from coast to coast, and in many states of the Union. Many varieties have come and gone, with varying degrees of approval, but the old reliable commercial variety is the Japanese hulless.

The pioneers raised the White Rice, but the Jap hulless variety entirely replaced that kind 20 years ago. For a few years the large yellow Argentine corn was very popular.

The first popcorn stand in Perry was operated by 

Wm. George, a boy who had recently come from Greece, being unable to speak the English language, and with less than $25 in his pocket. This pioneer stand was in a small booth at Second and Warford, just north of the present J. C. Penney store. The corn was popped in a large wire popper suspended by wire over an open gasoline torch flame. This was in 1900, and the foreign boy made good. Eight years later he opened up a first class confectionery store, called the Olympia 

Candy Store. For many years it was one of Perry's recognized retail stores. Instead of wasting his spare time he applied himself to the study of English and 

American ways, and for two winters went to school here. Various people have run street corner popcorn stands, but Vern Council exceeds them all for duration of time, having begun in 1918, at Second and Willis, he has continued at the same location up to the present time. At one time there were four of these outside stands, but the present trend is toward inside places.

Among the best known Perry doctors were the following who came here in the year indicated. P. A. 

Mowrer, 1855; Samuel Pangburn, ..; Theo. F. Johnson, 1877; A. J. Ross, 1877; R. E. Doidge, 1883; A. W. 

Trout, 1884; R. E. Aiken, 1884; I. N. Paul, 1885;

Keith Diddy, 1926; J. H. Jewell, 1927; P. W. Beckman, 1928; R. M. Conley, 1932; L. A. Utterback, 1931; J. M. Margolin, 1933; C. H. Bennet, 1935, and 

H. E. Haymond, 1936.

During the prosperous mining days of Angus Dr. 

Johnson had 175 regular patients there whom he served for one dollar each per month whether they were sick or not. Today both he and Dr. Ross, who came to 

Perry the same year, (1877) are residents of Perry, although retired.

For many years the basement of the library was used for a city hall, but needing more suitable quarters a move was made to the Bryan building at First and 

Willis. For six years the city rented the first floor of Mr. Bryan, and then in Mayor Kelly's term there came an opportunity to purchase the whole threestory building at the bargain price of $15,000. Purchase of the building was sanctioned by vote of the people. Besides providing an ideal location for the city offices, there is much revenue derived from the rental of the upper floors.

An interesting matter for those who love the beauties of nature, is the distinction given a cottonwood tree on the George Mullen farm south of town. The local 

Garden Club had taken part in a state tree contest of the Federated Garden Clubs of Iowa. In their convention at Ames in January, 1934, first award was given to this outstanding tree, which had a base circumference of 22 feet and 11 inches, and a height of over 125 feet. A. M. Thornburg of Perry states that his uncle, John Henry Thornburg, back in 1862 went fishing in Raccoon river. He carried his catch home on a small cottonwood pole, and afterward stuck it in the grounds. Seventy-two years later the "stick" had become the magnificent tree described above. 

A few years ago primary road No. 46 was established from Perry to Manilla, and to shorten the distance, a short-cut was made west of town, beginning at the end of "Sand hill" pavement, and extending westward about a mile and a half, to where it joined the old road. This change eliminated a sharp curve and a steep hill. The change also made necessary the building of a new bridge not far above the Wolf bridge. 

The state put in one of their characteristic first-class

bridges at a great cost, and the new bridge has been named the Spaulding bridge.

It is not often that Perry is the scene of a large convention, but in June, 1935, there were two, only a few days apart. First the Veterans of Foreign Wars gathered from a large area for a three-day convention. 

Parades of veterans, and competing groups of boys from various cities, dressed in bright, gay uniforms, marched, with peppy step, through the downtown streets, to the pleasure and admiration of throngs of people. A few days later a state convention of the 

Christian church met in Perry, and 1,500 delegates attended during the course of the meeting. 

For many years T. R. Phillips was Perry's principal undertaker, with his services in connection with local furniture stores, except the last few years, when he conducted the Philipps Funeral Home at the old David Pattee home. He continued in business until his sudden death on December 31, 1928. Associated with

Mr. Philipps at the last was Ben Workman, who with 

Mrs. Philipps continued to run the funeral home for some time, but soon Mr. Workman established the 

Workman Funeral Home in the old McCammon residence at First and Lucinda. Mr. Workman has been very popular and enjoyed an extensive patronage, conducting as many as 115 funerals in a single year. The home has been greatly enlarged and all modern conveniences are embodied in the service. 

The first exclusive funeral home in Perry was opened by C. D. Bessemer in June, 1921. This was down town on Willis avenue, where he remained for five years

before moving to his present ideal location in the old 

Bert Holmes property at Fourth and Willis. He, too, has enlarged and improved his quarters, so that today he has the largest seating capacity of any like home in the country. Recently he has installed a pipe organ, and was the first mortician in the county to purchase a modern funeral limousine.

Funerals, like everything else, have changed much through the years. The first funeral hearse in Perry was made for Dave Willis, the driver, by Carpenter & Son. The passing from horse-drawn conveyances and slow processions occurred in Perry in 1915, when 

Lawrence Tiernan and Ernest Lester purchased a Rock 

Falls motor hearse in Sterling, Illinois, and were soon followed by Leslie Morgan of the Gamble Furniture 

Store. Passing mention should be made of Dave Willis, who for 22 years was town marshal. He also had the first livery barn, the first bus, and used to run a street sprinkler. 

The magnificent Pattee hotel was built by Harry and Will Pattee as a memorial to their father, D. J. 

Pattee, who had died the year before. It was on or near this site where Mr. Pattee conducted his store when Perry was begun. The three-story hotel contains 

74 rooms, all modern in every respect. The lobby was patterned after the La Salle Hotel in Chicago. The 

Commercial Club gave an elaborate opening banquet on May 29, 1913, which was attended by 400 people.

Two hundred and twenty-three were served in the dining room, and the rest on tables in the lobby. John

Carmoody acted as toastmaster, and T. Fred Henry's band furnished music. 

About 35 years ago Frank Edmondson, the nursery man, planted a 15-acre apple orchard south of town. 

Later the place was sold to John Strait, and many people will recall buying apples there. Another commercial orchard was that owned by M. E. Fagen, west of Perry. In 1926 he sold 3,000 bushels which brought him $3,500.

There have been numerous bad windstorms in Perry at various times, but perhaps the worst was on August 8, 1924. On this occasion a strip through the northern part of town, and extending to the east, was the scene of much damage. Most streets were impassable because of trees and branches strewn therein. On the 

Robert Hastie acreage a small barn was literally scattered in pieces over an adjoining field. At the F. N. 

Bartlett farm the government thermometer shelter was wrecked and inside doors of their house were torn off. At the Walter Schell farm a threshing machine was blown over. Throngs of people were out until nightfall to see the damage.

Most of the events of history center around the persons of men and women who carry out the drama of life, therefore the passing of these characters from the scene of action constitute one of the interesting features of history.
