December 30, 1937

The Perry Country Club was organized on March 9, 1916, in the Elks parlors, with 20 charter members. 

H. M. Pattee was the first president. By April there were 41 members and work on a new club house was

begun at once. This building was of frame material, 60 by 90 feet in size, and was formally opened on July 10, 1916. The location of this clubhouse was three miles north of Perry, along Beaver creek, and on 40 acres of land purchased of A. D. Moore. After a little more than seven years of use the clubhouse burned down on October 22, 1923. The first building had cost about $2,850, but the second one was much larger and better, and cost around $9,000. Pearson & Son secured the contract, and it was built as near fireproof as possible, much of the material coming from the Adel Clay Products Co. The new building was opened for use in June, 1924. In 1925 the Perry Tennis Club was taken in to share the advantages and privileges of the spacious grounds. The site commands a wonderful view of the rolling grounds, beautiful timber and adjacent field. There is now a large membership, and 

Blake Willis is president. 

About ten years ago Dr. John Jewell located in

Perry, having come from Coon Rapids. Later he was joined by his two brothers, Harrison and Thorlow, and the three doctors opened the Jewell Clinic at Third and 

Willis, on Nevember 1, 1934. This building was formerly a frame rooming house, but they put on a brick veneer, which made a very fine appearing building. 

A great deal of interest is taken in the Three-Quarter Century Club, which is sponsored by the Perry Chief. 

Out of respect and honor for the men and women, who a generation ago were the backbone of the community, this club was designed to bring its members

together once each year for a social time of fellowship. 

The plan, in its local form, was conceived by Editor 

G. E. Whitehead and Rev. Peter Jacobs, pastor of the Congregational church, in 1931. The only qualifications of membership are to be 75 years old and express a desire to join. There are no dues or fee, nor charge for the elegant dinner served on "banquet day."

The old folks are the guests of honor and all expense is borne by the sponsors.

The first annual gathering was held in the Congregational church on May 14, 1931, with 90 members present. It has since been an annual affair, being held in various churches. The average attendance is now about 130. The oldest man and woman are reverently called the "king and queen". The present king is Albert 

Miller, aged 95, and the queen is Mrs. Barbara Burrington who will be 100 in March. The old people are made to feel that their lives and presence are appreciated.

Since the turn of the century numerous new churches have been organized in Perry. One of these is the 

Christian Science church, whose small services, in private homes, date back to 1907. It was organized on December 17, 1913, with 14 members. and Mrs.

L. M. Rice was the first practitioner. They met in the old Unitarian church, and then in the Jones college building. In 1932 they built a fine pressed brick church at Fifth and Otley at a cost of $15.000. Dedication was on September 8, 1932. There are now 51 members and Wallace Shaffer is reader.

The Assembly of God had its inception in 1906, when it was begun as a Holiness Mission. The meetings for ten years were held in various places, but finally the old Unitarian church was rented. This continued until 1929 when the church property, including parsonage, was purchased. At this time the building was enlarged and remodeled. Mrs. Clara McKean was the first leader, during the Holiness days, followed by R. E. Fitzgerald, who continued for about 12 years. 

By 1910 the society was transformed to a Pentecostal body, whose distinctive message is the baptism of the Holy Ghost, accompanied by speaking in tongues. 

Amid much misunderstanding and persecution the work has steadily grown, until there are now over 90 members. Rev. Fred Freeland is the present pastor. 

In 1917 the church became affiliated with the General Council of the Assemblies of God, an international missionary movement, whose American division is authentically said to be the fastest growing church in the United States. Ten gospel workers and preachers are now on the field from the Perry church, and eight young people are in Bible schools in various stages of preparation.

In the early days of the church a very unusual thing occurred. Miss Prudence Van Gilder fell under the power of God during one of the services. Failing to regain her composure, she was carried to her home at Fourth and Pattee, where she remained in that condition for 28 days. Many folks came to see her and there was much comment over the state. The Daily 

Chief of May 24, 1907, says, "Miss Prudence Van

Gilder lies at her home in a trance—the girl went into a trance when she fell unconscious on the floor of a branch mission conducted by Mrs. Scott Ladd of Des 

Moines." Two days later the same paper states, "Prudence Van Gilder spoke for the first time today in four weeks. Her first words were 'Jesus is coming soon.' 

At intervals she would talk saying a few sentences.

What she said no one could tell." During this time she had wonderful visions of both Heaven and hell, and stated that she was conscious all the time, but was unable to speak or move. The unusual experience resulted in her conversion.

Another church was the Latter Days Saints, who first met in the Diddy building about 1910. On March 

5, 1913, they dedicated a frame meeting house on 

North Fifth street. Reverend Hand was the first pastor, but the present incumbent is E. C. Kibby, who has served for a good many years. There is also a colored Baptist and colored Methodist church, both of which were begun in the early '20's. For several years 

Lutheran services have been regularly held in the Presbyterian church, and Rev. R. T. Mueller is the pastor.

When Jack Bruce was mayor in 1919 and 1920 the mayor's salary was boosted to $100 a month, but the money did not seem to be forthcoming. Some time later Mr. Bruce stated that if it was paid that he would give $100 to every church who held regular services. The back salary was paid, and Mr. Bruce was as good as his word.

In 1904, after a third of a century of service, the old Baptist church was torn down and a new one built at Fourth and Lucinda. This structure was of cement block construction, which was quite prevalent at that time. In 1924 the building was enlarged, remodeled and stuccoed, and finished with an entirely different arrangement. A fine large parsonage was later built next door to the church. Under the ministry of Rev. A. D. Mohr the church was brought into accord with that division of the church known as Fundamentalists, rather than the trend of Modernism. The later pastors of the church have been: Rev. W. H. Bird, 1901; Rev. W. J. 

Bartlett, 1903; Rev. Chas. McMann, 1904; Rev. J. E. 

Treloar, 1906; Rev. H. J. Moore, 1910; Rev. D. O. 

Slyter, 1911; Rev. Leland, 1913; Rev. T. O. Searcy, 1916; Rev. F. Yearned, 1924; Rev. L. R. Bobbitt, 1929; Rev. A. D. Mohr, 1931, and Rev. M. S. Hansen, 1935. 

The Methodist church that was built in 1891 was struck and destroyed by lightning on the evening of May 12, 1908. The people, at mid-week service, had left the building only a short time before the fiery bolt came. Work on a new church was immediately begun, and the new building was ready for dedication on March 21, 1909. This building, with its large tall stone pillars and green tile dome was quite an asset to the city of Perry. The gift of a fine pipe organ contributed much to the popularity of the church. 

Under the eight years ministry of Rev. Chas. M.

Edmondson, who came in 1922, an annex costing about $50,000 was built on the south side. This section is

used for Sunday school rooms, special meetings, gymnasium and various public gatherings. Mrs. G. R. 

Melick was Sunday school superintendent for 25 years.

Rev. A. W. Armstrong is a well known retired Methodist minister, having spent 43 years in the ministry. 

For about 60 years he has regularly attended the conventions of the Des Moines conference, and has been chosen as their historian. He has kindly contributed matter for this work. 

Pastors since 1900 are: Rev. Albert H. Collins, 1901; 

Rev. Jas. H. Senseney, 1905; Rev. Thos. E. Thurreson, 1908; Rev. Amos E. Griffith, 1912; Rev. Jas. M. Williams, 1913; Rev. Alfred A. Bishop, 1917; Rev. Chas. 

M. Edmundson, 1922; Rev. Frederick E. Edwards, 1930; Rev. Arthur Atack, 1932, and Rev. Ulysses S. 

Smith in 1936.

The present sturdy Catholic church has stood for 36 years, and apparently is about as good as ever. This magnificent edifice, 46 by 106 feet in size, and costing more than $25,000, was dedicated on August 10, 1902. 

The walls are of limestone, the shingles are slate, and a steeple rises to a height of 96 feet. On the summit is a gold gilt cross. Rev. James Cleary was pastor for about 35 years, coming her in 1898. The present pastor is Rev. Wm. A. Coughlin.

The Christian church has used their present structure at Third and Lucinda for nearly 40 years. During this time it has been altered and enlarged, and is now in very good condition. The more recent pastors have been: Rev. Roy Caldwell, 1901; Rev. J. M. Hoffman, Rev. Ralph Callaway, 1904; Rev. Chas. Coakwell;

Rev. R. H. Ingram, 1908; Rev. C. H. Strawn, 1910; 

Rev. Harry Howard, 1912; Rev. Lavack, 1915; Rev. 

Wm. Knotts; Rev. C. E. Chambers, 1917; Rev. Glen 

Crawford, 1923; Rev. B. A. Gann, 1924, and Rev. 

Warren Hile in 1929.

Members of the Presbyterian church put up a fine new modern brick building on the site of the old church in 1917, under the ministry of Rev. S. A. Saxe. Unfortunately a fire broke out in the church on February 18, 1923, which seriously damaged the building, but was soon repaired. Rev. Walter T. Martin is the present pastor. The old Congregational church at Third and 

Otley, was abandoned in 1905, and a new frame structure was built at Fourth and Warford. A fine parsonage is next door to the east. Rev. J. Irvine Walker is the present minister. Perry's major churches are all within the area of a few blocks. There are three on one corner-Third and Lucinda. There is not, and never has been, a single church west of the M. & St. L. tracks, and considerable of the east end is nearly a mile from a church. 

Thirty years ago, Harry Gill, a Chicago horse buyer, was making regular trips to Perry to buy horses.

Mr. Gill was an expert in his line, and in a few minutes' time would make his bid on a horse, which he usually bought at a satisfactory price. One account of his trips here is taken from the Chief of April 4, 1907.

At this time he bought 39 head, at an average price of $140.

About 1905 Perry installed a modern sewerage system, which emptied, through a septic tank, into the

Raccoon river on the Mowrer farm. A few years ago the city built a fine new modern annex to the city pumping station. About the same time an extension of 20 feet was added to the height of the standpipe, which made a total height of 120 feet. A conical roof was also put on at this time. 

The city of Perry received considerable unfavorable publicity in 1934 because of a condition arising in the high school. Some trouble arose between the superintendent and one of the teachers, resulting in the teacher being suspended by the superintendent. The school board was divided about the propriety of the same, and much contention followed. The citizenry, too, was drawn into the controversy, and some public meetings were held in the high school auditorium where feelings were sometimes running too high for comfort. 

The majority of the people seemed to be opposed to the superintendent, and that portion of the board that upheld him, consequently some board members resigned, and other were chosen in their stead. 

Finally on May 18th, after six weeks of bickering, the superintendent was officially discharged. Then a new trouble developed, for shortly before this the old board had re-hired him for a three-year term, at $3,000 per year. He demanded the salary, but the new board refused under the circumstances. Miss May Hill, county superintendent, upheld the local board in dismissing the superintendent, but later Agnes Samuelson, state superintendent, reversed their decision.

Later, on the grounds of this reversal, the discharged superintendent appealed to the federal court, and finally,

in the fall of 1937, when the case was about to come into the higher court, a settlement was made between the litigants, which was approved by the court. In this settlement the former superintendent received $2,000, but had the costs to pay. 

The trouble of 1934 smouldered along for a year, and broke out anew on May 1, 1935, when most of the high school students went on a strike. The principal and the teacher involved before had been discharged, but the student body demanded that they be retained. To gain their object they paraded the business streets, and otherwise spent their time instead of being in classes. This condition existed for three days before order was restored. The teacher was retained, but the principal left, having a better job elsewhere. The largest class to ever graduate from the school was the one which graduated soon after this unfortunate circumstance, there being a total of 86. The Boy Scout movement in Perry was begun on 

March 14, 1927. At first there were two troops, of 32 members each, and were in charge of Harry Heaton and George Gibson. A third has been started since, which is in charge of Gerald Heinzelman. Warren 

Daniels has since taken the place of Mr. Gibson. The boys are instructed and trained in many interesting and useful matters, and make their headquarters at the Methodist annex. Boys who have reached the age of 12 are eligible.

One of the marvels of modern times is the radio. 

Its rapid development, on a universal scale, is almost beyond belief. As early as 1901 Marconi invented

the principles of wireless telegraphy by code. By the time of the World War it was being used quite extensively, but the government checked its private use during the war times. After the war great strides were made in wireless telegraphy and radio by means of the Morse alphabet, but the first public broadcasting of the human voice over the air was at Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, in November, 1920, from station KFKD. 

Lee Weise, a Perry boy, at the age of 12, became interested in radios. Little by little he developed a receiving set of his own, and on February 6, 1922, was astonished and thrilled to hear music over the air, as clear as though it had been in the next room. 

So zealous was he in radio work that he soon had a radio sending station of his own, which was licensed as station 9BYC. At first he used the code system, and before long had the pleasure of talking to people in many parts of the world. At one time he gave advice to a boy in New Zealand on how to fix a bad clutch in his Buick car. At another time he was heard by Station WNP (Wireless North Pole) on the steamer Boudoin, then frozen in the ice off the coast of Greenland. He received thousands of post cards from other amateur radio fans in all parts of the country. 

A matter of special interest now, is the fact that at one time people were willing to come to one common place to listen to a radio. Early in 1924, Col. Sutherland Dows, of the Iowa Railway & Light Company, gave the city of Perry a radio for public use. This fine General Electric radio, costing about $500, was installed by the city in the basement of the library,

and the public was invited to come and listen to it on Tuesday and Friday nights. David Bonine had charge of its operation and care, but in about a year radios were becoming so common that its further use in this way was not practical. 

At first all radio reception was by means of "ear phones" which fitted over the ears of each individual who listened, but it was not long until "loud speakers," by means of a horn came into use, which enabled everybody nearby to hear. About 1922 the first purchased radios were coming out, and by 1930, or before, most people were daily listeners to radios in their own homes. Morton Lyons, Chas. Paiste and

Earl Hastie are said to have purchased the first ones in Perry.

The newspaper plant and business of the Perry 

Chief was purchased by James Rhodes of Newton,

April 19, 1926. The previous owners were R. M. 

Harvey and Herbert Adams. Mr. Rhodes is publisher of the Newton Daily News, and also has newspapers at Carroll and Missouri Valley. G. E. Whitehead came to Perry from Newton to become resident publisher of the Chief on June 1, 1926. The Rhodes management has made a decided success in many ways. In keeping with the forward march of the company a new home for the paper was built, and ready for occupancy by August 20, 1928. The building, at Second and Pattee, is a half block long, and arranged in the most convenient style. With the new building came the installation of a mammoth duplex press, which is indeed a marvel in rapid and efficient

printing. On September 1, 1928, a large 44-page special anniversary edition was published, which abounded in pictures and historic matter of Perry and vicinity. During 1937 they began the publication of a weekly paper called the Chief-Advertiser. The daily paper now has a circulation of over 3,000, and the weekly over 1,700.

For some years R. E. Zerwekh published the Advertiser, first as a weekly, then a semi-weekly, and finally, for a short time as a daily, but it was not long after this time that it was absorbed by the Chief interests. C. Durant Jones, for a time, published the 

Free Press, but about 1924 he sold out to Carl Sexauer, who changed the name to Journal Press, and later, in 1935, the plant and paper was purchased by Harry Green, who again changed the name to Perry Press, and so it exists today.

BREED BLOCK, PERRY

CHAPTER 12  Chain Stores From Livery Barn to Garage-Merchants of Yesterday-Perry's Retail Sales Record. 

In this chapter we shall endeavor to give a brief, impartial account of Perry business firms of the twentieth century, who continued long enough to be of a permanent nature; first those which are now extinct, followed by those of today. In some cases the old firms sold out to their successors, and today are known by another name. Before proceeding further, let it be understood that some of our best citizens were compelled to quit their business on account of circumstances they were unable to help, viz., the chain store system. Men of limited means were utterly unable to compete with big corporations, backed by eastern capital. A combination of circumstances unite to work against the local retail dealer, and little by little most of them are forced to retreat. 

On December 6, 1913, George Arnold started a clothing store on a small scale. The business proved successful and soon he found himself in charge of an increasing and flourishing business. 

For years he occupied a room at the corner of Second and Lucinda, but the last few years of his business career was at a location a few doors south. About a year ago he closed out his stock and retired from business, after about twenty-three years in the retail trade. 273

For nearly forty years the name of Miller Brothers was a household word in Perry. The two brothers, Will and Edwin, came to Perry in the fall of 1897, and opened a hardware store in the Odd Fellows building. About ten years later they moved to a location in the twelve hundred block on the west side. of Second street. In earlier times they sold implements, buggies and harness, as well as hardware. 

Heating and plumbing were also included in their line, but these, too, were dropped when they moved to smaller quarters on Willis avenue a few years before they retired from business. 

The E. A. Lester livery barn was begun in Perry about 1892, and for years was the leading livery of the town. After automobiles replaced horses on the road he converted his barn at First and Lucinda into a garage. On June 28, 1922, the garage was ruined. by fire, and 32 cars were burned. Afterward the place was sold to the Jenkins & Batterson Company. 

The Aubry livery barn had a similar experience when it, too, was changed to a garage to meet the need of the times (1913). The garage, with Studebaker sales and gasoline service, was disposed of only recently after the junior member of the firm had been appointed postmaster.

R. H. Culbertson came to Perry in 1884, and began to manufacture cigars. For over thirty years his goods were known in central Iowa. With a force of from four to fifteen men, his average annual output was 150,000. One of the most popular brands was

the "Bullfrog." To the front of his factory he conducted a retail store known as the Smoke House. 

Heaton's Chicago Store was established in 1879 by W. H. Heaton. Dry goods, shoes and men's furnishings were their principal lines. After the death of 

Mr. Heaton in 1920 his son, Harry, continued in the business until March, 1933, when the stock was closed out. Probably no other Perry store operated, under the same name, for so long a period of time. 

For about 35 years J. W. Pefley conducted a unique shop called the Perry Novelty Works. Guns, locks, bicycles and most everything were repaired, and saws, scissors, etc., were sharpened. He came to 

Perry in 1894. In later years his shop was to the rear of his house, just west of the Perry mill. 

The Helvetia Cafe was begun early in the present century by Alfred E. Anderson. Being close to the 

Grand theatre and maintaining all night service it became a very popular place. In later years only a lunch counter service was maintained, and the name changed to the "Excelso." After Mr. Anderson's death a few years ago the business was sold. 

The Oxford restaurant was begun in September, 1897, by Walker & Bradley. Later Mr. Walker retired and Harry Bradley run the place for many years. This was the first eating place in Perry to maintain all night service. Sometime after 1910 the business was discontinued.

Among those coming to Perry in the Milwaukee boom days was G. R. Melick, who engaged in the grocery business in 1882. His was a typical, yet up-

to-date, grocery store of the 19th century, and enjoyed an extensive trade. In 1908 he quit the grocery business to engage in the real estate business. He still continues in this work, but his advanced age retards his activities. 

Perry's pioneer photographer was L. M. Stoops, who established a gallery in 1888. Practically all the older homes in this community have photos bearing his imprinted name. Four-fifths of the pictures in the "Booster" edition of the Chief (1910) were taken by him, and the artist who prepared the cuts recognized unusual merit in Mr. Stoop's work. The following November he died suddenly in the Methodist church, at the age of 70.

Early in the spring of 1921 an energetic person from 

Omaha bought out the H. C. Donaker stock, north of the Grand theatre. On April 1st W. A. Frans opened the Fair store. After four years he moved to a location east of the library. The business grew steadily, and in 1929 he built a fine, new, strictly modern store at 1117-19 Second street. date of the new store was on July 30th.

The opening 

About a year or two ago he sold out and moved to Boone. 

Jack Bruce first conducted a meat market in Perry where the Chief building stands. His opening date was October 1, 1898. Later he moved to the south end of the same block. In 1910 he sold out to his brother, Harry, and for a time quit the retail business, and dealt in wholesale meats from his establishment west of the city. Sometime later he opened a store on Willis avenue west of the library, and on

October 1, 1922, opened his big grocery and produce house at Second and Otley. This place he called the "Snowball Market." In 1928 he had two branch stores in other parts of the city. After his death in 

1932 the business was conducted by his son Ralph. 

About a year ago the store was closed and young 

Mr. Bruce went to California. 9. 

Many years ago John Dignan opened a grocery store, and because of its double width he called it 

Dignan's Double Header. The first store burned on 

May 13, 1907, but was soon replaced by a much better building. For a time, about 1910, they conducted branch stores at Gardiner and Brough. After Mr. 

Dignan's death in 1926 the store was run by his son. 

Leo, until it was sold to the Schwartz grocery later in the same year. In Mr. Dignan's newspaper ad of 

March, 1910, he quoted the following prices: Flour, $1.45; bread, three loaves, 10 cents; five gallons gasoline, 70 cents; potatoes, per bushel, 55 cents. The price paid for produce in trade was: Eggs, 20 cents; butter, 24 cents, and cream, 25 cents;

For a considerable length of time, extending each way from 1910, an enterprising jewelry business was conducted by C. H. Winner. Artistic catalogues were sent out each fall, and the place was very popular. 

Memory of the Golden Rule store still lingers in the minds of many people. This full fledged variety store was begun by F. D. Richardson in the spring of 1905. When the Clements block burned in December, 1906, Mr. Richardson was compelled to seek a new location, which was a block and a half further

north. The golden rule idea was well carried out, and the store enjoyed the good will and patronage of the people. About 1929 Mr. Richardson closed out his stock and retired from business. 

Waters & Donahue opened a new furniture store at 1217 Second street in June, 1919. Despite keen competition they did a large volume of business during the twelve years of their existence. 

After L. M. Stoops' death in 1910 the photo gallery was sold to J. S. Scoles. For seventeen years Mr. 

Scoles continued, with his gallery in the Carter building. Omer Gray of Valley Junction bought the Scoles business in May, 1927, and moved to another location. 

About a year ago Gray sold out to the Edmondson studio.

After eight years in the employ of the Gamble 

Furniture Company T. R. Phillips opened a furniture store in the old Union block 1902. For three years he had a silent partner and the firm was known as Phillips & Company. Various partners came and went during the years, including George Birdsell, George O'Malley, Lawrence Tiernan, F. M. Hoeye and L. E. Sherman. When the Union block burned in 

1920 they moved to a location in the next block north, and on May 1, 1921, moved to a new building of their own on Warford street. In a few years the firm was dissolved and Mr. Phillips continued at undertaking from the old Pattee home, where he opened the Phillips Funeral Home in 1926. 

Forty years ago Perry's leading shoe store was 

Wild & Rall, who poetically advertised, "Wild & Rall,

Shoes for All." Later Mr. Rall acquired sole ownership, also of another store in Cedar Rapids, and the firm was incorporated as the Rall Shoe Company. 

When Mr. Rall moved to Cedar Rapids to manage his store there, he left Harry Markress in charge here. 

In 1920 Mr. Markress bought the local business, but retained the old firm name. A few months ago Mr. 

Markress died, but so far the store continues as heretofore. 

Charles Hausserman came to Perry in 1902 and engaged in the meat business. For nearly twenty years he operated the well known market. After his tragic death in 1921 his brother Carl came from a Montana homestead to buy the business of the estate. About five or six years ago Carl quit the store and left Perry.

The Feguson Market was begun by J. K. Ferguson in 1905 after he had worked three years for Charlie Hausserman. With the exception of a few years at the last, his thirty year business career was at 1018 

Second street. In 1927 he added a full line of groceries to his stock. Three years ago he sold out to the 

Dreyher Grocery.

Beginning in 1882, and quitting in 1932, is the half century record of the Diddy family in Perry mercantile circles. In the earlier year Marion Diddy opened a general store. In 1891 he was joined by his brother, John, and the firm name became M. L. Diddy & Bro. For thirty years, Elmer, a brother, had worked in the store. John retired, Marion died, and after the Diddy block burned, Elmer, about 1912, opened another store on Railroad street known as

E. J. Diddy & Son. In a short time they moved to the Odd Fellows building where they remained until retiring, July 20, 1932. 

The Lew Crist cigar store dates back to 1883. Up until 1918 he manufactured cigars that were well known throughout central Iowa. Among the various brands made by him, the "Ben Franklin" was perhaps the most popular. In the old days a smoke house or a cigar store usually displayed a fancy, painted wooden 

Indian in front of the store. That meant a cigar store just like the striped sign means a barber shop now. 

Gradually these went out of style, but Lew Crist still had one long after most Indians were gone, but even Lew's Indian came to an end. On April 19, 1919, it was accidentally knocked over and wrecked. Poor

Indian was so old that he went into many pieces, and had to be consigned to the "happy hunting ground." 

The Gamble Furniture store is another Perry institution begun in an early time. Levi D. Gamble first owned a grocery store, but after his store burned he opened a furniture store in 1882. Suffering great loss in the big fire of 1898 he retired from business, and Leslie Morgan, his son-in-law, continued on, with the old firm name. Mr. L. R. Burrington had been in their employ since 1900, and when Mr. Morgan died in 1928 the former purchased the business, and it has since been known as the Burrington Furniture store. 

An old time Perry firm was the N. Henness Company, who were succeeded by Buckley Bros. They were burned out in the Clement block fire of 1906,

but later opened in the new Wimmer & Williams block, with the dry goods department off Second street, and the grocery division on Willis avenue; in fact it was the same location as is now used by the Newberry store. About 1923 they closed out and retired. 

Prior to 1890 T. J. Gilbert opened a hardware store which he conducted for many years. About 1920 he sold out to G. C. Parsons, who in turn sold out to the 

Smith Hardware in 1923. On January 25, 1929, the store was gutted by fire and was never re-opened. 

The Perry Rendering Works was going strong in 1928; nine men were employed at the plant a mile or so west of town, and four trucks covered all the territory within a radius of 35 miles of Perry. Special telephones were installed in 24 towns of this area. The dead stock were converted into tankage and soap grease, beside the customary hides. V. R. 

Starling, formerly of Des Moines, started the plant in 1923. A few years ago fire destroyed the plant and it was not rebuilt. Since that time the Perry 

Rendering Service was begun by Dick Conaway, who hauls dead stock to Des Moines.

The Chas. A. Graves dry goods and ladies ready-towear store was in the Myrtle block for many years, but amid a changing world Mr. Graves sold out to the 

Connors company in 1923.

For forty-odd years O. F. Roddon conducted one of the leading clothing stores of the city. For many years he was one of Perry's three main clothing stores, the other two being Bailey & Robinson, and McCammons. After many years all three are still in operation,

by the younger generation, although somewhat different in form and name.

On the corner west of the library David Ainley ran a hardware store forty years ago. For nearly seventeen years his brother, Albert, had a jewelry stock in one corner of the store. In 1913 he (Albert) joined his son, Oscar, in forming the Ainley Book store. 

Forty-two years in the grocery business is the record of James A. Dowling. At the time of the big fire his firm was Roland & Dowling, later Dowling & Allgood, later Dowling & Wright. Mr. Dowling, Elmer 

Diddy and John Dignan were three veteran Perry grocers. 

Graham & Hylbak used to run a grocery store west of the library. Graham sold out to Hr. Arnold and the firm became Hylbak & Arnold. Later Arnold withdrew, and in a few years the store was discontinued. 

The present Sanitary meat market, with its newly installed counters, office, fixtures, pictures, up-to-date refrigeration and ornate front is the achievement of 

Harry Bruce, who began on the same corner 28 years ago. Through the years people have flocked there for good buys in meat. Only in the larger cities would a person find so good a market. On December 12, 1924, Mr. Bruce received national distinction for the largest item order ever sent in the United States, by the 

Cudahy Packing company. There were 868 saparate items in the shipment, which required a whole car for transportation.

The Denniston & Partridge company came to Perry in 1923, when they took over the interests of the Armour Grain company. About 1915 Armour's had bought out the Neola Elevator company, and William 

Tack came to be Armour's manager. Before the

Neola's time the lumber yard and elevator were entirely separate. The Neola people had bought the grain business of Allen Breed, and in 1913 rebuilt the elevator. In 1905 the lumber yard was west of 

Second street, and that year was purchased by H. C. 

Modlin & Company. They sold to the Hansen Lumber Company in 1910, and about a year later they moved across the street to the present location. In a few years the lumber yard and elevator came under the same management, and have continued so ever since.

The McDonald Drug Company succeeded the Gardiner Drug Company in October, 1924. Four years later they moved into the remodeled bank building at Second and Willis, and are still there. They are local representatives of the Walgreen drug system. 

After L. D. Blue left the shoe firm of Graney & Blue he opened a men's clothing store which he called the 

Fashion Shop. In 1924 he sold out to A. M. Thornburg, who retired a few years ago. 

W. H. McCammon opened his clothing store in 1891. 

About ten years later his brother, S. A., joined the firm and it became W. H. McCammon and Bro. For many years it was often called the "Star Clothing House" and much roadside advertising was done. In 1920,

his sons, Horace and Jean, entered the firm, and since their father's death in 1928, have had full charge. 

The Ringheim dry goods and ready-to-wear store was begun by two sisters, Hannah and Emily Ringheim of Nevada in 1903, and was managed by Mr. and 

Mrs. F. M. Livingston-Mrs. Livingston being a sister of the Ringheims. For years it was Perry's leading dry goods store. In 1918 E. J. Denny of St. Louis purchased the store, but has retained the old name up to the present. It is unusual for a store to run for 35 years by the name of people who never personally operated it.

Barton & Tutt came to Perry in 1915 and bought out the Rathburn & Cleveland stock, which had originally been the Ammerman grocery store. Mr. Barton died in 1929, and since the withdrawal of Mrs.

Barton the genial Ansel Tutt has operated alone. 

Before Perry's notable fire W. J. Edmondson and

Dennis Graney engaged in the shoe business. Board signs on county telephone poles told of the "E & G” shoe store. In 1904 the firm became Graney & Blue, and in 1911 Mr. Graney purchased the Blue interests, and since then it has been the Graney Shoe Company. 

Until recently the store was located in the Elk's building. 'Twas back in 1895 that F. H. Reid opened up a jewelry store, which for years was Perry's best. His store was in the old Union block at the corner of Second and Warford. Soon after his death in 1919 the store was purchased by Milliken & Major, who

have maintained the same high standard up to the present. 

The Carpenter Cleaning Shop has been in operation for about 25 years, although in different places. In 

November, 1927, at his home on Fifth street, he installed an Allinson continuous flow cleaning system at a cost of several thousand dollars.

The only real book store in the city is the Ainley book store, which was opened by Oscar Ainley and his father in 1913, when the Pattee hotel building was first available to tenants. Twenty-five years have gone; the father has passed on, but Oscar and the store are still at the same place. 

In March the Henry Miller & Son hardware store will reach their 45th anniversary in Perry. Miller & 

Trouth bought out the old W. H. Chandler stock in 

1893. In 1913 Mr. Miller built the large three story building which is now the home of Montgomery Ward & Company. Ten years later he sold this building and moved to the present quarters across the street. In time Mr. Trouth dropped out and Clarence Miller, a son, took his place. Since 1933, when the father was tragically killed, Clarence has conducted the business. 

The John Deere line of farm machinery is one of their strong features.

Thirty years ago M. C. Magee conducted a grocery store in the Breed block, which he later sold to the 

McLaughlin brothers. Wallie and Bert enjoyed a good trade, but about twenty years ago Wallace withdrew from the firm to move to Minnesota. On June 

18, 1918, Bert opened the Square Deal grocery, des-

tined to be one of the most successful groceries that Perry ever had. This store adopted the policies of self serve and cash and carry. So successful was the business that within ten years Mr. McLaughlin had established three other stores outside of Perry-two in Boone and one in Madrid. Since Mr. McLaughlin's tragic death in 1933 the store has been run by his son Robert. Twenty years of steady rushing business in the retail trade is a good record for these times. 

The Perry Granite Works was begun by C. D. Oldham in 1898, in a location on the west side of the triangle. In 1906 Barney Archer came into the employ of the firm. By 1924 he acquired virtual control of the business after holding half interest for several years. Mr. Archer has built up a fine business, having many satisfied customers within a radius of 40 miles of Perry. 

For many years Jerry Coakley operated a drug store on Willis avenue by the first National bank, but on August 2, 1919, the store was sold to Ray B. Smith. and Howard Baker. The next year they moved to a new store room a couple doors to the west. A couple years later Smith bought Baker's interests, and he has very successfully operated ever since. 

The McColl Lumber Company began operations in Perry in 1894, and was always known as a dependable and up-to-date firm. For a few years after Donald 

McColl's death in 1924 the business was in charge of Mrs. McColl, but on August 1, 1928, it passed to the 

Stokley Lumber Company who are the present owners. 

Another small elevator, which was built by W. R.

Grant, soon after the interurban came, is also owned by the company. 

The Perry Lumber Company, at First and Willis, is owned by A. D. Fransham, who came to Perry in 

1920, buying out the Markress Lumber Company.

Since that time Mr. Fransham has made extensive changes and improvements. Prior to the Markress ownership the yard was operated by Spahn & Rose, with Wimmer & Williams preceding them. 

E. P. Dooley, veteran Perry druggist, has been in the drug business at the same location for 42 years. 

He has seen many druggists come and go, but he continues on through the years. At first he was associated with Mr. Mott, and the firm was called Mott & Dooley. 

After suffering loss in the big fire of 1898 he resolved to build on the same site, and his new building was ready for occupancy by the following September. This store is known as the Rexall store.

John Daw had his first drug store experience in the employ of E. P. Dooley. Later he purchased the John 

O'Malley drug store in the Clements block, but unfortunately was soon burned out when the block burned in December, 1906. He then bought the John Mitchell building at Second and Lucinda, where he has been ever since.

The Van Fossen Music House was begun by E. C. 

Van Fossen at his home in a small way on East Evelyn street. About ten years ago he moved to a downtown location at Third and Evelyn. He sells radios and electric refrigerators as well as musical supplies.

Ellis the Cleaner has steadily been serving the people

of Perry and vicinity since 1918. His excellent work and convenient location make it a favorite place for many people.

The Capen Hardware Company dates back to 1902, when A. J. Copen purchased the Steele hardware stock, east of the library. For many years before, Mr. Capen had conducted a harness shop, and for a long time had a harness department in the hardware store. In 

1906 he moved to the present location west of the library, and his son Harry became an active partner. 

Since the father's death in 1913 Harry has had full charge.

In 1922 W. A. Murray and son, Clarence, built a shop on First avenue, and began the Murray Tire 

Service. Later, when the Perry Oil Company operated from there, they enlarged their quarters and inaugurated a one-stop service. About ten years ago Clarence became sole owner, and now makes a specialty of tires. 

Clyde Edmondson of the Edmondson Studio entered the field in the spring of 1923, with a partner, Mr. 

Darmer. In a short time the partner withdrew, and 

Mr. Edmondson, assisted by his wife, has seen much expansion and improvement in his business. Since taking over the Omer Gray studio they are the only photoprapher in town. 

In 1918 Fred Develing was on a trip to the west coast, but being a jeweler of considerable experience, he stopped to work for F. H. Reid for a while, and has been here ever since. Like nearly everybody else he was favorably impressed with Perry, and when Mr. Reid died two years later he opened a shop of his

own on Warford street, where he has continued ever since. 

Among the firms burned out in the big fire was the Bailey & Robinson clothing company. After the fire the firm was incorporated as the Robinson Clothing Company, although Mr. Bailey still retained an interest. In 1913 the interests were divided-Mr. Robinson opened the "Woolen Mills" in the Pattee hotel building and Floyd Bailey took over the original store on Second street, where he still remains. 

W. B. Robinson was joined by his son Palmer in 

1922, two years before the father's death. The firm still continues, but a year ago moved to the room formerly used by the Fashion Shop.

In 1906 U. G. Putman began an eating establishment east of the library. In 1911 he was joined by 

Richard Fouch, and the business grew. In 1925 Mr.

Putman died, and since then the place has been operated by Mr. Fouch and his son, Everett.

The Jones Electric Company has been in operation continuously since 1908, when Arthur Jones established the business. Since his death in 1930 his son C. E. has continued on.

The Kemble-Smith Floral Company, whose head quarters and greenhouses are in Boone, opened a place of business here in 1924. In addition to the local retail trade, the community wholesales to 32 towns from the Perry branch. Arthur Airhart is the local manager. 

The McCreary Bros. hardware store was begun in 

Perry in February, 1923. In addition to hardware they handle the International line of farm machinery,

having a separate warehouse for such on West Willis avenue. Nine years ago they opened a branch store in Adel, which is still in operation. 

Fred Ling came to Perry soon after the Milwaukee went through, and has spent practically all his time in hotel and restaurant work. For twelve years he operated the Stewart House. He was in his present location, just north of the Milwaukee station, for 21 years, and "Ma" Ling's cooking has been very popular among the railroad boys.

The Sunbeam Dairy was begun by Frank Daniels on December 11, 1929. Some years before this Bradley Thomas had operated the Sanitary Dairy from the same place, but it was absorbed by the Perry Dairy. 

The Sunbeam is the largest milk distributor of the city, averaging about 700 quarts daily. A few years ago they installed an ice cream service, which has had a remarkable patronage, with annual sales exceeding that of the milk. About 20 flavors are sold, most of which are 29 cents a quart, $1 per gallon and $4.50 for five gallons.

The Perry Dairy was begun a good many years ago by Al Barker, who had a Second street location and enjoyed a large trade. In 1927 the Perry Milk Products Company bought the business, and has since operated it from the condensory. 

W. A. Petty built an ice house on S. Third street for natural ice many years ago. About 1907 this place burned down, but was rebuilt with cement blocks. In 

November, 1911, Jack Paul bought the business and immediately remodeled and enlarged the place, putting

in equipment for the making of artificial ice, which was begun on the 12th of the following March. The capacity was 75 tons. On January 1, 1919, Mr. Paul began to sell his entire output to the Reynold Bros., who delivered it over the city to the consumers. During this time ice cream was made extensively and a good business was done, with little or no competition. 

On December 21, 1923, Mr. Paul sold out to the

Hutchinson Ice Cream Company of Des Moines. Ice was no longer made, and the Reynolds began an ice business on the Interurban tracks, shipping it in from 

Des Moines.

The Perry Paint & Glass Company was begun by 

A. L. Henning in 1929. A fine trade has been gradually built up with the Pittsburg paint. The store is at 1516 First avenue. The Mid-West Seed Company is a very promising firm in Perry, having been begun by Clarence Gottschalk a few years ago. They enjoy a fine local trade and a wide wholesale trade. 

The Perry Iron & Metal Company operate a junk yard just south of the Perry Mill. Besides ordinary junk they buy furs in season and salvaged parts of automobiles are sold. Isadore Rubinson is the proprietor, and has done a large volume of business during the many years of his business in Perry. During 1937 they shipped 33 cars of old iron. Most of this iron eventually went to Japan for the manufacture of armaments to be used in their war of aggression in China. 

Lones, the florist, began in a small way at his home at Seventh and Pattee in 1924. Today he has a fine

group of modern greenhouses and enjoys a good local trade. 

Henry Nobling came to Perry from Kewaunee, Ill., in 1910, and opened a blacksmith shop in the alley north of the present Pattee hotel. On May 13, 1919, he opened a garage in keeping with the trend of the times. This place was west of the library, and Mr. 

Nobling has seen a steady growth each year, so that now he occupies a double space on Railroad street, one of which is used for batteries, tires and accessories. 

Seaton's I. G. A. store was opened by Paul Seaton on July 5, 1934, and enjoys a good trade. This store was originally begun by Keith Woods about a year and a half before this time. This is a home-owned store, but is federated with a large system of stores through the Independent Grocers Alliance. The Hines Shoe 

Store is owned by E. O. Hines who opened on December 1, 1931. Their plan, too, is one of cooperation through the Central Shoe Company. 

Demer's Coast to Coast store handles hardware, auto accessories, paints, etc. This store was opened in October, 1933, and is owned by A. W., Fred and 

Henry Demers, who came from Storm Lake. This is not a chain store as some suppose, but one of a federated system cooperating together. Camp's readyto-wear store was opened by Carl Camp on March 2, 1930, in the Taylor block on Willis avenue.

For many years B. F. French conducted a China store at 1110 Second street. Sometime after his death on December 5, 1923, the store was sold to the Dil-

lon Company, who later moved to the Masonic building at Second and Willis.

About twenty years ago C. R. Hoagland opened a grocery store just west of the First National bank. 

Some years later his son, Harry, took over the business and commenced the Harry's Cash and Carry store. A change in location, a block to the east, was made later, and here the store has since made a remarkable success.

About ten years ago E. L. Orr took over the garage at First and Warford, and began the Economy Chevrolet Company. Full garage service was maintained and many new Chevrolets were sold. Mr. Orr sold out to the Northey Chevrolet Company on October 22, 1936. In 1897 Chas. Timeon opened a cabinet shop for the manufacture of store fixtures and similar prod- Mr. Timeon died in March, 1920, and his son 

Harry continued with the business, which is now past the forty year mark.

It would be difficult to give a full account of all the baking ventures in Perry, but a few are worthy of mention. The Perry Bakery, owned by R. D. Kauf man, took over the Bradley Bakery on February 1, 1928. Mr. Kaufman soon built up a large trade, and had a daily capacity of 10,000 loaves of bread. A long truck route was run to other towns, and much bakery goods were sold. Within the last year the bakery was sold to Benjamin Lapham. For about twelve years J. P. Fox ran the Home Bakery, which enjoyed a fine trade.

Other firms of importance are: Perry Dress Club; 

Chocolate Shop; Sam's Place, Pete Sumas, shoe shop; 

Reynolds' Cafe; Wilson's Cafe; Swan Furniture Company; Electric Sales and Service; Iowa Hatchery; 

Randolph Hatchery and Produce; Trimble Furniture 

Company; Ranes' Cafe, and Laborde Radio Shop.

Automobiles, good roads and chain stores have built up the larger towns at the expense of the smaller ones. 

Distance is not the barrier it once was and besides, many people enjoy the trip of ten to thirty miles to a larger town to mingle with the crowds. 

Extensive newspaper advertising also contribute a lot to that end. 

In the edition of November 1, 1937, the Perry

Chief carried a half page ad, in which it was pointed out that Perry food merchants, during the first six

months of 1937 sold more groceries and meats than any other city of its size in the whole state of Iowamore than one city of 15,000 population, more than two cities of 11,000 population, and more than any city in Iowa of less than 10,000, with the exception of one.

A week later the same paper stated that the Perry retail sales had increased $1,114,000 during the last two years.

The reason given for these remarkable records were the fact that Perry had excellent stores and exceptionally low prices, coupled with extensive advertising through local newspapers, principally the Chief, with a circulation of over 3,000, and their weekly paper, the Chief-Advertiser, with a circulation of over 1,700. The above figures were gleaned from sales tax reports.

At the opening of the twentieth century there were three banks in Perry; the First National, of which 

D. J. Pattee was the head; the Citizens State Bank, headed by S. S. Dilenback, and the Perry Savings & 

Exchange Bank, mostly owned by Allen Breed. In 

1906 the Peoples Savings Bank was organized with J. P. O'Malley as president. This bank was in the new Wimmer & Williams block, and later became the Peoples National Bank. Sometime later they took over the quarters of the Savings & Exchange

Bank, remodeled the rooms, and changed the name to Perry National Bank.

In 1913 J. C. Bryan built what is now the Municipal building, and for a time operated a fifth bank at that location. In October, 1915, the Dilenbecks sold the

Citizens Bank to M. M. Heptonstall, who changed the name to Security Savings Bank. After about eight years he sold to Chas. Graves and his brother. The 

Peoples Trust & Savings Bank was begun by E. D. 

Carter on March 2, 1927, in what is now known as the Carter block, after the former bank had moved a block further north. The Pattees relinquished their claim in the First National on December 23, 1923, and for two or three years Mr. Grimes was in charge. 

In 1926 it was taken over by the Brenton interests, with E. R. Burkett as local manager, and has remained so since that time.

In 1925 three of the four existing banks closed their doors. The Security Savings on January 23rd; the Perry National on January 24th, and the Peoples 

Trust & Savings Bank on September 21st. This left only one bank-the First National. For over two years this was the only bank in a city of nearly 6,000. 

However, on November 3, 1927, the Perry State Bank was begun at Second and Warford, with J. C. Bryan as president and N. P. Black, cashier. Since that time the two banks have had a steady growth and substantial existence.

CHAPTER 13 SMALL TOWNS AND RURAL COMMUNITIES 

1900-1938 

Census Chart-Consolidated Schools-Bad Fires-Criminal Episodes Brick and Tile Industry-Federal Housing Project-The Lott Indian Affair— The State Farm. 

Dallas county is primarily an agricultural section, and the major part of the population is found in rural districts, and small towns. No longer are the country people regarded as "hayseeds," for with practically all of the modern conveniences and advantages, they are often envied by their city friends. It is often said, during these depression times, that the farmer is the most independent of all classes, for he has first chance on the products of the soil and farm. 

A comparison of Dallas county census figures is a matter of much interest to thoughtful readers. The figures given here were taken from government census books at the state library, which do not show population of unincorporated towns. Until such time, the population is included in the township figure. It will not be possible to mention all the changes in schools and churches and merchants that have taken place in each community since 1900, but something of interest from each town shall be attempted. 297
