SMALL TOWNS--RURAL COMMUNITIES 299 WAUKEE 

Waukee has always been noted for its nurseries, and today the town is still on the nursery map. The 

Waukee Nurseries, in charge of G. E. Davis, is the outgrowth of an industry started many years ago.

In 1901 a modern four-room brick school building was erected at a cost of $6,000. Waukee has the distinction of having the first consolidated school district in the county—a change that was effected in 1916. 

In December, 1901, Waukee suffered a serious fire loss. The fire started in the hayloft of the Spencer 

Smith livery barn. With no fire protection whatever, except the "bucket brigade" it was impossible to stop the fire until eight buildings had been destroyed. Seven of the horses in the livery barn were burned. The following firms suffered loss as indicated. Smith livery barn, $2,000; Brenton Bros. lumber yard, $10,000; 

J. H. Carter, implements, $2,500; T. F. Howe, groceries, $2,000; L. L. Storer, restaurant, $1,500; Advocate, printing office, $500; John Straw, shoeshop, $750, and A. J. Bassler, furniture, $1,500. Total loss, $20,750.

Waukee suffered another large fire lost only recently, when the grain elevator of H. Dallman & Son burned (January 31, 1938). Nearly 20,000 bushels of grain, mostly oats, were lost, and the loss was estimated at $12,000. The fire burned slowly for several days afterward.

During the summer of 1907 the various churches held union revival services under the direction of 

Reverend Birch, noted evangelist of Winona Lake,

Indiana. Much interest was aroused and many people were converted. As a result of these meetings the Young 

Men's Club was organized, whose purpose was to foster good reading, proper recreation and spirituality. 

Various newspapers have had short careers in times past, one of which was the Waukee American, published by C. Durant Jones before he came to Perry. 

The following people now have offices in Waukee: 

Mayor, G. E. Davis; postmaster, Mrs. Lou Davis; superintendent of schools, Chester J. Hartman; Methodist pastor, Rev. C. W. Hohanshelt; Christian pastor, Reverend Balenger and Catholic pastor, Reverend O'Neil.

DALLAS CENTER 

Dallas Center replaced their 29-year-old schoolhouse with a fine new brick building in 1903, at a cost of $13,000. Like other phases of Dallas Center life, the citizens have taken much interest and pride in keeping the school up to date, and it is believed that no town of its size excels them in school facilities. Elmer 

Foster is the present superintendent.

Pastors of churches in Dallas Center and adjacent rural districts are as follows: Church of the Brethren, 

Rev. D. D. Flushware; Methodist, Rev. W. W. Bentgson; Presbyterian, Rev. C. E. Durdine; First Brethren, Rev. Wm. Gray; Dunkard Brethren, Rev. Roscoe 

Royer; Brethren in Christ, Rev. Christian Nisley, and the River Brethren, Rev. John Kellar.

The Farmers' Mutual Fire and Lightning Associa tion, formed in 1895, has continued to grow through the

SMALL TOWNS—RURAL COMMUNITIES 301 

years, until now the amount of insurance in force is about $10,000,000. An office and secretary is maintained in Dallas Center, and satisfactory service is rendered at a minimum cost. At the present time 

Alonzo Copeland is president; J. F. Summerson, vice president, and H. S. Fox is secretary and treasurer, a position he has held for 26 years. There is a director in each of the 16 townships.

Dallas Center is known far and near as the original home of the Brenton banking interest, which is now one of the largest in the state of Iowa. Besides the home bank at Dallas Center and the big bank in Des 

Moines, they have local banks at Perry, Woodward, Granger, Adel and Waukee. 

Only recently, on November 19, 1937, the fire demon visited Dallas Center, and left a fine half block in smoking ruins. The fire, which started in the basement of the Schramm drug store caused a loss of approximately $50,000. Repairs were being made on the water tank, and the small supply of water that it contained was soon used, which indeed was a most unfortunate circumstance. In their predicament calls were sent to the firemen in Perry, Adel, Granger, Grimes and Waukee, and all promptly responded, but were unable to stop it until the following business places were lost. Schramm drug store; United States 

Post Office, Brenton State Bank, Helfenstein & Royer, grocery, and the Masonic Hall. The only newspaper, the Dallas Center Times, has been published for about 

40 years.

MINBURN 

Perhaps the greatest change that has come to Minburn is the re-arrangement of the road system into, and through the town. The main road of former days is a most insignificant one now, since the primary road comes in the "back door" instead of in front as before.

Minburn once had a small newspaper, called the Minburn Criterion, which was edited by R. E. Zerwekh. 

About 20 years ago the depot burned down, and a fine brick station was built in its place. The Minburn 

Brick & Tile Co. was organized in 1880 by Hill Bros. 

The plant, west of town, was operated at intervals up to about 1916. In 1893 fire destroyed the property, but it was rebuilt the next year. 

A distressing incident occurred in Minburn a few years ago (July 23, 1931), when Virgil Untied, the night marshal, was ruthlessly shot down by an unidentified bandit, while faithfully doing his duty. The bandits had robbed several business houses, and Mr. 

Untied, between three and four in the morning, was trying to apprehend them, when he was fatally shot while in the shadow of the elevator. Mr. Untied was 

33 years old. The criminals escaped, and are still at large.

The Christian church was first built in 1897, and Rev. Vernon Harrington was the first pastor. In 

1919 it was moved a little further east, and enlarged to its present size. The present Methodist church was built in 1901.

SMALL TOWNS-RURAL COMMUNITIES 

303 The Minburn co-operative store was in operation for many years. In 1909, after an existence of 20 years, it was purchased by E. J. Rowe, who ran it for another 

20 years, but changing road and business conditions compelled Mr. Rowe to quit, to enter the gas business on the same site. During the better days this store did a large volume of business, selling nearly everything, including furniture and implements. 

Everett Shaw, one of the leading merchants, has the distinction of being the first one in the county to open a locker refrigerating system. His opening date, with 204 lockers, was on June 28, 1937. Now there are others in Perry, Dallas Center and Adel. Minburn affairs are largely in the hands of the following people: 

Mayor, Oscar Beaver; postmaster (acting), Mrs. 

Lillian Wicks; superintendent of schools, J. S. Duncan;

M. E. pastor, Rev. Eugene Alexander, and the Christian pastor, Rev. Hesper Kennedy.

REDFIELD 

Redfield, being one of the oldest towns of the county, is an appropriate location for an old settlers' reunion, and for many years such gatherings have been very successfully carried out. These joyous occasions are looked forward to with much pleasure by those who laid the foundation of our fair county. 

It seems that but few towns escape a serious fire at some time or other, and Redfield was no exception to the rule. At one o'clock one Sunday morning in March, 1900, Dr. Thornburg discovered smoke issuing from the Reynolds building. Before it was checked an entire

row of frame buildings, on Thomas street, between First and Second streets was laid waste. The bucket system and a few chemical extinguishers were no match for fire raging in a row of frame buildings. Only by heroic efforts were nearby properties saved. 

The Redfield Clipper of that time listed the following losses: J. L. Bandy building and stock, the Clipper plant, Redfield Harness and Saddlery Co., Harper's meat market, Jeffries Bros., F. F. Stiles grocery, Betz & Spillers grocery, Peppard block and C. B. Walsh, drugs. The Redfield Review was started in May, 1904, by a stock company. Many different ones have managed it since then, but it still conveys the news to the local citizens. Prior to the commencement of the 

Review, C. G. Brown had edited the Clipper, from 1889 to 1904. 

At the present time Redfield is the center of brick and tile activities in this part of the country, although some years ago Adel had this distinction. An account of the brick and tile industry in Dallas county is a long and changable story. Perhaps no man is better qualified to give a history of local clay products than Halver R. Straight of Adel, who now operates a plant east of Redfield.

He states that in 1907 he, with his brothers, Guy H. and Lee S., bought the McKissick plant at Adel. The 

Straights enlarged the plant to three times its former capacity, and ran it until January 15, 1926, when they sold out to a New York corporation, who formed a merger of 31 plants in Iowa, Missouri, Kansas and

Oklahoma. They also bought a plant at Van Meter.

SMALL TOWNS-RURAL COMMUNITIES 305 

This gigantic combine was called the United Clay Products Corporation. The company was managed from Kansas City, and Iowa sales were made from 

Des Moines.

After two years the company went into bankruptcy. 

A re-organization was effected and the name of United 

Brick & Tile Co. was used. Mr. Straight built a new plant east of Redfield, which he called the Adel Clay Products Co. West of Redfield is the plant of the Redfield Brick & Tile Co., which is owned by Robert

Goodman. These three plants are the only ones now operating in Dallas county, and are about equal in production, each one putting out from 20,000 to 

30,000 tons annually.

When the Straights ran the Adel plant it was called the Adel Clay Products Co., and in 1910 they began using the trade name of "Irontile", a name which they reserved when they sold out, and one that they still use. After Straights sold out to the big company they bought the Desota yard at auction, and moved it to Redfield in starting the new plant there. Like many other enterprises, Mr. Straight says that when they began in 1907 there were 286 plants in Iowa, and today there are only 27.

Prior to Straight's time W. L. McKissick had been engaged in brick and tile making for many years, dating as far back at 1878, when he had a plant between Desota and Van Meter. Through the years many changes were made. At different times he was associated with 

R. M. Kerns, A. C. Hubbard and E. D. Blackman.

In March, 1904, he established the present plant in Adel, which he held until its sale to Straights in 1907. 

Redfield joined the ranks of consolidated schools on March 26, 1920. Business interests in Redfield at the present time include: C. A. Diddy, drugs; Don 

Towne, groceries; E. E. Reynolds, general store; Harry 

Kohl, hardware; Dalby Lumber Co.; Des Moines 

Elevator Co.; Scott & Son, general store; L. H. Deford, furniture. Positions of public trust are: Postmaster, Hattie Bandy; mayor, George Ford; superintendent of schools, Spencer Frink; Methodist pastor, Reverend 

Stroud; Christian pastor, Rev. Wilbur Leatherman; physician, Dr. Aherns.

GRANGER 

The town of Granger is an enterprising place of 300 people on the eastern edge of the county. For a good many years the Yankee Robinson circus maintained winter headquarters east of town, and along the interurban tracks.

An outstanding church of this section is the Assumption Catholic church, whose magnificent building was erected in 1901, at a cost of $14,000. Its members take pride in keeping everything in A-1 condition. The church was built under the direction of the pastor- 

Father J. J. Moran, who was born in Germany, and educated in Rome, Italy. The bell in the cupola was a gift of M. Costello, and a sad coincidence is that it was first used at his funeral.

As a result of the depression a great cry was raised about the problem of making a living. Various people

SMALL TOWNS-RURAL COMMUNITIES 307 

in different parts of the county conceived many ideas as to better means of subsistence. One of these was a plan suggested by Rev. L. G. Ligutti of Granger. His idea advocated a colony of families living in modern houses, with a few acres of ground, upon which to make a partial living. This plan appealed to the federal government, and they decided to give this plan a trial in the Granger vicinity. Accordingly, a tract of land was purchased northwest of Granger, and about 50 modern homes were built, on tracts of land ranging from two to five acres in size. These were to be sold on long range terms to those who wished to buy. On 

December 15, 1935, they were ready for occupancy, and a goodly number moved in-mostly those who worked in coal mines of that region. What the outcome will be is yet to be seen.

In the horse and buggy days the main road from 

Perry to Des Moines went through Minburn, Dallas

Center and Waukee. When Camp Dodge was built and pavement extended out that far, people were drawn to that route, which passed through Moran and Granger, but when primary roads number 6 and 7 were paved everybody took one of these roads, either through 

Dallas Center and Grimes, or through Adel.

The people of Granger built a fine new school about 1915, and about two years ago the Catholics built a parochial school just south of their church edifice. 

Officials in Granger are as follows: Mayor, E. E. Fitzsimmons; postmaster, Leonard A. Moran; superintendent of schools, A. I. Hosfelt; United Brethren pastor,

Reverend Hampshire; Catholic pastor, Rev. L. G. 

Ligutti; Chirstian church, no pastor. 

VAN METER 

The town of Van Meter has recently received a great deal of publicity through the baseball achievements of Robert Feller. Having made somewhat of a record as an amateur ballplayer he was asked by the Cleveland ball club to come to the eastern city to demonstrate his ability. He made a great hit, and immediately sprang into the limelight. At this time he was only 16, and a junior in high school. He is about to start on his second season as a professional ball player, at a big salary. So popular has he become that "Bob Feller" candy bars are now being sold.

Van Meter seems to be noted for its boys, for in addition to the Bob Feller story, Junior Clayton, last fall, received the national distinction of being one of the six healthiest boys in the United States. This decision was the result of a health contest in 4-H circles. 

The Van Meter consolidated school district was formed July 15, 1921. This was the last one to be consolidated in Dallas county. Since that time the trend has been in a different course.

The only government river gauge in Dallas county is at Van Meter, and is in charge of B. W. Landers, local restaurant man. Brenton Bros. operate a lumber yard, Dr. A. G. Felter is the local physician, while 

Paul D. Fisher runs the general store. Others of distinction are: Postmaster, Mrs. Glenn Miller; mayor, John Jungman; superintendent of schools, O. E. Lester;

SMALL TOWNS-RURAL COMMUNITIES 309 

United Brethren pastor, Rev. E. A. Elliott, and the 

Methodist pastor, Reverend Fix.

DEXTER The people of Dexter built their fine new school building in 1905. W. D. Clampitt is in charge of the school system at the present time. Two churches are in operation; the Methodist, with Rev. J. H. Freedline as pastor, and the German Lutheran, whose pastor is 

Rev. Kurt Daib. L. S. Drew is mayor, and George A. 

Crane is postmaster. A newspaper, the Dexter Journal, conveys the local news and advertising to the people of that section. 

In 1903 the Fort Des Moines Canning Co. opened a canning factory in Dexter. About $36,000 was spent in buildings and equipment, resulting in one of the best factories in the state. In 1906 they shipped over 

30 carloads of canned goods. The payroll amounted to $1,000 a week during the canning season. At that time the principal brands were Ft. Des Moines and 

Dexter Prairie Queen. Considerable change has taken place since then and today it is operated by the Dexter Cannery, Inc. Their annual production now runs from 

50,000 to 100,000 cases of canned goods. 

The Excelsior Thresher Tooth Co. was once a very promising concern, having a paid up capital of $37,000. 

Unfortunately the factory burned down in 1907. The 

Dexter Company, makers of the famous "Dexter" washing machines, was begun in Dexter many years ago, but about 1912 the company moved to Fairfield, Iowa.

Drew's Candy Shop, which was begun about eight years ago, has quite a record in the manufacture of homemade chocolate candy. Thousands of pounds are annually shipped to all parts of the United States, and some to foreign countries. The Chapler & Osborn 

Hospital and Clinic is far ahead of those usually found in towns the size of Dexter.

Great excitement prevailed in the neighborhood of 

Dexfield Park on July 24, 1933. A desperate gang of bandits, known as the Barrows gang, had left a trail of crime and death through Arkansas and Missouri. A great search was being made for them. A tip was received by officers that they were in hiding north of Dexter. Thirty officers closed in on them, and one of the bandits was mortally wounded, but the others made their escape. 

Marvin, or "Buck" Barrows was brought to the

Kings Daughters Hospital in Perry, where he died in a few days. His mother came from Dallas, Texas, and took his body home for burial. Shortly before the raid they had stolen the Edward Stoner car, and after the battle this car was left behind with 62 bullet holes in it. Two of the others, Clyde Barrows and Bonnie 

Parker, made their get-away in a car stolen from Valley Fellers. Within a few months both of these two desperate bandits had been run down by the law, proving that "crime doesn't pay."

DESOTA 

The town of Desota started off with a boom in 1868, with bare lots selling at exorbitant prices. The railroad

SMALL TOWNS RURAL COMMUNITIES 311 

company had in mind to make this an important point on their line, but things did not work out that way. 

Today the little town is struggling to hold its own, and is not as large as it was 60 years ago, however, cherished recollections of former days, when all was so promising, fill the memory of many old settlers. 

Times and conditions are continually changing-what is in demand today is discarded tomorrow. The little town is only one of thousands in the country that has no possible chance of growing in these modern times. 

The small business circle includes the C. A. Maricle grocery store, L. O. Benfields hardware and the Riley

Bros.' elevator. Positions of trust are held by the following: Mayor, Norman Beeson; postmaster, Mrs.

Fudge; superintendent of schools, S. C. Highbarger and 

Methodist pastor, Reverend Fix. 

WOODWARD 

Woodward has made a steady, gradual growth through the years, with a present population of close to 1,000. The leading church is the Methodist, whose fine brick building was built in 1907. Woodward has a fine hotel building on Main street, which was built in 1904, and contains 22 rooms. The Woodward Enterprise, which is still regularly appearing, was begun away back in 1887. The school building in the west part of town was built in 1907. Just west of the town is a beautiful well kept cemetery. The leading bank, owned by the Brentons, was begun in 1902. 

The Woodward Enterprise of June, 1907, gives an account of the finding of 20 or more skeletons, in one

grave, as men near Scandia were excavating gravel. 

It was a mystery to all to account for such a strange discovery. Many who examined them said they were those of Indians. To support this belief was the fact that stone pipes were found in the gravel. Some of the bones were those of children, and some of the skulls were well preserved, with teeth only slightly colored, but most of the skulls showed signs of being struck with a sharp instrument. The burial had been made many years ago, for over the grave was a large oak tree, estimated to be 60 or more years old. Some believed there had been a battle between the Indians and early settlers, while others thought some disease had suddenly taken the lives of many Indians. No one seemed to have any definite knowledge of the strange circumstance. 

J. M. Cave, of Linn township, offered what may be a solution of the problem. He states that in 1853 a trapper named Henry Lott lived near this place, and that a band of Indians abused his family. One child was driven down the river toward Fort Des Moines and was later found frozen in the ice. Besides, the fright caused Mrs. Lott's untimely death. Mr.

Lott and an elder son bitterly sought revenge. When an opportune time came he freely gave them plenty of "firewater," and when they were in a drunken stupor they "laid them out" with axes. Mr. Lott was a friend of the Cave family and afterwards told him the details of the tragic incident.

In many respects this is verified by a book on the early history of Webster City, written by Harriet

SMALL TOWNS—RURAL COMMUNITIES 313 

Bonebright-Closz in 1921. According to this interesting history, Lott seems to have made a practice of getting the Indians drunk in order to cheat them in trading.

Such practices often strained their relations, almost to the breaking point, consequently there was not a very good feeling toward Lott, and this accounts for their actions towards his family. The only point of difference is where this slaying took place, which still leaves it quite questionable. At any rate there seems to be no question but what the differences and trouble that existed between Lott and the Indians was one of the causes of the awful Spirit Lake massacre that came a few years later.

Twenty-five years ago Anthony McColl of Woodward was a member of the state board of control. At this time the state was ready to establish an epileptic colony somewhere in the state. Mr. McColl saw a chance to bring a big thing to Woodward, and through his influence succeeded in swinging the big institution to his town.

Work on the state farm was begun in 1915, but not until the fall of 1917 was the place opened to inmates. 

Although established for epileptics only, some years ago a change was made to include the feeble-minded, too. Year by year has seen expansion, until there is now 

1,175 acres of land, most of which is in field crops. 

There are 90 acres devoted to the growing of truck crops and fruit. A fine herd of Holstein dairy cattle, many of which are pure bred, are kept to provide the milk supply. Wm. Van Arkle is the efficient director of farming. The greater portion of the farm is in Boone

county-only 22 acres are in Dallas county. In October, 1937, there were 1,428 inmates, and a large number of employes. The total investment amounts to about two and a half million dollars. Dr. Chas. Irwin is the present superintendent. Woodward town officials and church pastors are as follows: Mayor, Russell Shaw; postmaster, Clyde Clonkey; superintendent of schools, C. J. Madden; M. E. pastor, Rev. Harry Coates, and the Christian pastor, Reverend Kaufman. 

BOUTON 

The town of Bouton has been very unfortunate in having several disastrous fires, so that today, most of what was once fine business buildings, lays in ruins. 

Consequently the town is hopelessly crippled in a commercial way. At one time it was an exceptionally good town for its size, and many of the business houses were substantial brick structures. 

Within a period of about 18 years five devastating fires had visited the business district of the town. The first, about 1912, burned four business houses on the north side of Main street. The second fire consumed the Opera House. The third destroyed the Dilenbeck bank and a drug store. The fourth, on October 27, 1927, burned a later Opera House on another site, and the last of the series razed a hardware store and garage. 

Present day business interests include Mr. Lass's grocery store, the Thornburg hatchery, Fred Schnoor, implements; Ray Tierney, elevator, and the Hansen 

Lumber Yard, managed by Ben Swisher. Harold 

Thornburg is mayor, Helen Lass, postmaster, and

SMALL TOWNS RURAL COMMUNITIES 315 

Manfred Moore is in charge of the schools. Two 

Lutheran churches are served by Rev. George Dirks and Reverend Muehler.

For many years the town staged an annual fall celebration called "Sour Kraut Days." A large per cent of the people in and around Bouton were Germans and a menu of kraut and wieners was much to their liking. 

These occasions brought great crowds for a general time of reunion and jollification.

DAWSON 

Dawson, like all small towns, has no doubt seen its best days, yet instilled within its citizens is that inherent pride of their own local community-an interest that will continue through thick and thin. The 

Witter tile yard was doing a large business during the first 15 years of the century. About the same time the 

Hutchinson Bros. coal mine was putting out 50 tons of coal daily, and the combined monthly payroll of the two places amounted to about $1,500. Today the 

Wignall mine west of town is doing a good local business.

The only church in Dawson is the United Brethren, which has adequately met the need for many years. On 

March 18, 1934, the church was completely destroyed by fire, which started soon after the people had left after the Sunday morning service. At this time Rev.

I. C. Fellars was pastor. In a short time a basement for a new church was built, but for two or three years it was not finished, but merely roofed over, and used in this way. Last fall a fine new brick structure was

built; some interior work is yet to be done, but it is expected that it will be ready for dedication on Easter Sunday. Rev. C. L. Windsor is the present pastor. 

There are now only two general stores, both of which have been in operation for many years. These are the 

Joe Roberts and W. W. Delahoyde stores; Leo Anderson runs a hardware store; Denniston & Partridge the elevator, and the Stokley firm, the lumber yard. For many years W. W. Delahoyde has been one of the leading beekeepers of the state. His honey, year after year, takes many prizes at the state fair. It has been humorously said of him, that he was the sweetest man in Iowa.

About two years ago the government discontinued the maintenance of a separate post office, and it was moved into the rear of Mr. Delahoyde's store, and he became postmaster. W. E. Ludington is mayor.

An addition to the schoolhouse is to be built in the spring, to provide more room for modern facilities, and increased numbers. It is a P.W.A. project, a plan whereby the federal government helps. The Dawson school was consolidated May 27, 1920, and has operated as such since that time. W. K. Armstrong is the school superintendent.

The Gardiner school was built and consolidated in 

1917. There was once a general store and elevator there, but both have been closed for many years, as well as the depot. It is only a flag station now, although there are several families and a filling station there. 

The Linden school was consolidated February 24, 1920. 

The present principal is Mr. Shelton. Grant Dunbar is mayor and H. C. West is postmaster. The two

SMALL TOWNS-RURAL COMMUNITIES 317 

churches, Friends and Methodists, are ministered to by Reverend Mortimer and Reverend Rowe respec tively.

Perhaps the worst cyclone to visit Dallas county was the one that struck the Moran vicinity on April 6, 1922.

At this time the roof was torn off of the Wm. Tiernan farmhouse, and the whole house was moved several feet. All the windows were smashed; Mrs. Tiernan was killed and other members of the family injured.

This house was a large one, only two years old, and built at a cost of $14,990. Thousands of dollars worth of damage was done in that area and the interurban was out of commission for a whole day. 

An unusual character of a generation ago was Wm. 

Wagner of Lincoln township, who was better known. as "Blind Billy." For 15 or 20 years he run a grocery and implement store across from the Ephesus schoolhouse. Despite the handicap of total blindness he could set up new machinery and drive a team of horses all through that section of the country to the astonishment of his friends and neighbors.

CHAPTER 14 PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 

1900-1938 

Packing House—Washing Machines-Milk Products-Plow and Shovel Works-The Milwaukee Railroad- Utility Companies. 

In 1919 Charles Hausserman, who had operated a meat market in Perry for years, conceived the idea of establishing a local packing plant. By enlisting the interest and co-operation of prominent businessmen and farmers a stock company was formed known as the Hausserman Packing Co. Mr. Hausserman was the largest stockholder, Con Graney was chosen president and Charles Cunningham, manager. The site of the old Shorthill steel plant, northeast of town, was secured, and during the winter and spring of 1920 

Pearson & Son erected the initial buildings. In June, the plant opened for business, with 30 employes and four salesmen.

Fifteen months after the opening of the place Mr. 

Hausserman was killed, while driving across the railroad tracks a short distance from the plant. After his death Roscoe Zerwekh became manager, but was succeeded by Ben Golden in 1923. Three years later the company was re-organized, and the name changed to the Perry Packing Co. Two Omaha men, E. F. 

Dougherty and B. P. Billings, with Mr. Golden owned all the stock. 318

PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 

319 Mr. Billings became president, and Mr. Golden was secretary and treasurer, as well as manager. For a few years the company made a remarkable growth. 

According to their figures, in 1927, there were 55 employes, 11 salesmen and payroll amounting to $95,000. From 300 to 400 hogs and 60 to 100 head of cattle were killed weekly. Two hundred items were sold and 200 cars were unloaded annually. Besides the various meat and by-products, such things as cheese, butter, oysters, fish, horseradish and canned goods were handled to swell the volume of business. 

Delivery over the greater part of the state was made by trucks. The featured brand of their products was the "Pros-Perry-ty" brand. But despite the apparent prosperity the management chose to close its doors on August 16, 1930. Many men were thrown out of work, and there was much concern about its re-opening. 

For four months all was quiet, but on December 15th the place was re-opened. It was now known as the 

Perry Packing & Provision Co. 

In the fall of 1933 the Arnold Bros. company of 

Chicago, purchased the plant. Rather than pay freight on livestock to Chicago they believed it best to kill them here and ship only the butchered carcasses to their Chicago headquarters. All fall was spent in remodeling the place, and after the outlay of much money they formally opened on February 11, 1934. 

Eight new refrigerator cars were put into service bearing the name, "Arnold Bros., Inc., Chicago, Ill.-Perry,

Iowa." This company has since operated the plant

giving employment to many people and maintained a market for local stock. 

The Osmundson Manufacturing Co. is one of Perry's stable institutions having gone through thick and thin for many years. In 1903 Henry Osmundson, a blacksmith in Slater, began to make the bar type of tiling spades. In a short time he decided to move to Perry and start a factory. Two little red frame buildings along the Milwaukee tracks were secured for his purpurpose. Associated with Mr. Osmundson was his son, W. H., who soon had half interest in the business. 

In 1914 the son became the sole owner. Prior to this time the company was making more tile spades than any factory in the country (25,000 in 1900). On October 13, 1914, a big fire destroyed the factory. Undaunted Mr. Osmundson began the erection of the first part of his present buildings. 

With declining use of tile spades, a shift was made to cultivator shovels, after the fire. He introduced the "corner off" type of shovel, which soon became popular and was used mostly by all implement manufacturers. A thousand shovels can be turned out in a single day. Each shovel goes through 14 different processes before reaching the finished state. A large air hammer weighing 30,000 pounds is used in pounding the steel pieces into shape; 27 styles are made, and they are sold in a trade territory covering 17 of the best corn states. Ninety percent of the towns in Iowa sell the Osmundson shovels. There are three buildings, the largest of which is 50 by 160 feet. The aim of Mr. 

Osmundson has been to give satisfaction, and he has

PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 321 

been amply repaid, for a satisfied patronage enables him to continue through depression, hard times and all. From 45 to 60 men are now employed. 

In April, 1918, a local canning factory was organized by Lee Reeves of Waterloo. Most of the stock was held by local men, and Mr. Reeves had had extensive experience in this line of work. This was at the time of the World War, and the production and conservation of foodstuffs was an important matter; naturally it appealed to the people. A 22-acre tract of ground along the Interurban tracks was purchased, and the buildings erected that year, ready for operation in the fall. The main building is 60 by 300 feet. The warehouse is two stories, and the corn shed is 40 by 200 feet. In 1920 it was consolidated with the Grimes company, and Mr. Reeves was retained as manager. 

The Grimes company was one of the major companies of the industry, having modern plants at Altoona, Pella and Rockwell City, besides those of Perry and Grimes. 

In normal times the company contracts for from 1,200 to 1,500 acres of sweet corn, which yields the farmers an income of from $40,000 to $50,000. During the rush season 150 to 200 people are employed, whose wages amount to from $20,000 to $30,000. The waste material is extensively used by the farmers, as feed for their stock, and the plant, in every way is a fine thing for everybody in and around Perry. However all has not been "smooth sailing." Drouth years, fires, over production, cheap prices, competition and inevitable financial troubles have harassed the company. During two seasons the plan did not run at all,

and on others the yield was greatly cut in quantity and quality. A few years ago the company was re-organized. Raymond Bender is the present manager.

An account of the washing machine industry in Perry is a long and interesting story. From his boyhood days A. S. Kibby was much interested in washing machines. After going to college in Ames, he, with two others, engaged in a small manufacturing business along another line. Unable to refrain from his washing machine ideas he built a miniature washer, withdrew from the firm and began to look for a location for the development of washers.

He came to Perry, and called on Miller Bros. They were so impressed that they put his machine in their window. Mr. Modlin, who was then head of the Commercial Club got the businessmen interested, and Mr. 

Kibby says, "Within 24 hours after I struck Perry, 20 Perry men had put up $250 each to start making washing machines." This was the day before Christmas in 1906. "Allen Harvey was running the Chief then, and he published a cartoon on the front page of the Christmas edition, showing a washing machine hanging on a Christmas tree marked as a gift to Perry."

Before long a factory was running in the old Shotwell & Davis building on West Second street. The merits of the machine were so evident that Mr. Kibby says they were soon "snowed under" with orders.

They soon outgrew these quarters and a new brick factory was built on Fourth street, north of the tracks. 

Things moved along fine; the machines became known all over the United States, the stockholders were sat-

PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 323 

isfied and the outlook was very promising. Mr. Kibby had managed the business for over two years when something unexpected occurred. He says, "One nice 

June day we woke up to find that one man had bought the controlling interest of the stock. That's when I went back to the old butter and egg plant with the idea for a new washing machine."

Mr. O'Malley, treasurer of the company, let him have $1,700 to get started with in a new venture. This was the beginning of two factories which continued for many years. The new company, which became known as the At Last Washer Co., made a fine record until 

1912, when many manufacturers became involved in litigation over royalty on dollies. Mr. Kibby was one with others, who fought such move. To win the victory, which they did, required four years of time, and $30,000 in money. "The setback was hardly overcome when the war came and we were unable to get the necessary materials to built enough washers to hold the thousands of dealers that were our customers," says Mr. Kibby. After the war the At Last company erected a fine new two-story building on First avenue, and his brother, Ernest, joined in the work. 

They developed a number of new practical improvements, including the thermos tub, which kept the water hot. But times were changing big companies, with an abundance of capital, were doing all they could to monopolize the industry, and Mr. Kibby, although with a fine machine, had insufficient capital to compete with them, although he continued until his death on December 26, 1929.

The Globe Manufacturing Co. was incorporated on 

January 21, 1907, with a capital stock of $25,000. 

J. C. Bryan was president, C. D. Oldham, secretary, and A. S. Kibby was one of the directors and active manager. The purpose was the manufacture of washing machines at a place already mentioned. In the spring of 1909 Mr. Bryan withdrew from the company, and B. C. Dilenbeck, who had acquired a large block of the stock, was made president and treasurer, and 

H. C. Modlin, vice president and secretary.

It was not long until the building had to be doubled in size to keep up with the demands for the "Quicker Yet" washers. The whole building now was 100 by 

100 feet in size, and including the basement there were three full floors. Completion of the annex was about 

December 1. 1909. An elegant office of spacious size was equipped to care for the rapidly growing business. 

Storage warehouses were established at Indianapolis, Indiana; Decatur, Illinois, and Milwaukee, Wisconsin, and at Davenport, Des Moines, Sioux City and Council Bluffs, Iowa. As early as 1910 they used as much as 

83 cars of raw material annually; 10,000 catalogues were printed, and patents were taken out in England, France, Germany and Japan.

Unfortunately this flourishing plant was destroyed by fire on January 27, 1915. The loss was estimated at $130,000, and less than one-third was covered by insurance. Despite the misfortune, the factory was rebuilt to the same size, and on the same site, and operations were resumed as before. The factory production reached its peak about 1919 or 1920. At that

PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 325 

time there were 60 workmen, whose minimum wages were 35 cents an hour, for a ten-hour day. Besides these, there were at least 25 others employed as salesmen and office help. The factory was organized and operated in the most modern way, with a foreman over each of several departments, with a general superintendent over all. For a long time S. H. Jones was the general superintendent. After some years changing conditions and circumstances resulted in the permanent closing of the whole institution.

Perry's prosperity has been largely due to the Milwaukee railroad company. The two major booms of the town were attributable to this company-the first in 1881, and the second in 1912. The occasion of the second was when the railroad company wished to enlarge their terminal, and double track the road. At first it was thought that the improvements would be in the east end, but suddenly it was announced that the company had purchased two farms west of town, and would build there.

During 1912 and 1913 new shops, roundhouse, coal chute and complete equipment was built on the newly acquired land. The roundhouse, of brick construction, ranked as class one, and was the largest on the Iowa division. Sixty-five engines were assigned for service at Perry, which meant that about 40 would pass through the shops every 24 hours. The coal chute had a capacity of 450 tons, and the estimated valuation of the company's property at Perry was close to three and a half million dollars.

When the double track was laid much larger rails were used to adequately carry the heavier engines that were coming into use. The road was straightened in the Des Moines river region, which considerably shortened the distance between Woodward and Madrid. 

A new bridge was built across the river about two miles north of the old one. This bridge is a remarkable feat of engineering; a striking example of twentieth century achievement. The length is 2,630 feet, and from the water level to the top of the rail is 146 feet. 

In the double track class it excells all others in some respects, but not in all. During the fall of 1913, when all was in readiness, a change was made to the new location. Gradually the buildings of the old place were removed, until it would never be known that shops were ever there except for the cinders that abound in that vicinity. This move was a decided step forward for the Milwaukee.

In 1928 there were 725 men and women employed by the Milwaukee in Perry, and the monthly payroll was approximately $85,000. Since then the depression and labor saving methods have reduced the number considerably. At the beginning of this year still larger engines, the type S-2, were put into use on the freight service. These mammoth engines with the tender, are 

110 feet, 14 inches long; 10 feet, 11½ inches wide and 16 feet high. They can carry 25 tons of coal and 

25,000 gallons of water, and when thus loaded the whole weight is 887,450 pounds, or over 443 tons. 

They are scheduled to run 60 miles an hour with a long freight train, with the same crew going much further than before.

PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 

327 In 1907 the Commercial Club learned that the big Van Camp company of Indianapolis, Indiana, was looking for a suitable location for the establishment of a milk condensory in the middle west. The members worked hard to hold the attention of the firm, and at the same time persuade the farmers to engage more in dairying, which was essential if the big plant came to Perry. Finally, the Van Camp officials chose Perry, and work on the building began in mid-winter, and was rushed in an unusual manner. On July 27, 1908, the place opened for business. 

The dairy industry of this section received a new impetus; production was greatly increased, and great quantities of milk came in on the Interurban, being picked up from platforms at every road crossing. Not only did they put up their own brand of milk, but car lots were canned for various wholesale grocery houses with their labels on. After about ten years the plant was taken over by the Waterloo Creamery Co. of 

Omaha, who ran it for several years. In March, 1922, two brothers, H. P. and H. J. Ryner, bought the busi ness and organized the Perry Milk Products Co. 

Under the Ryner management the plant steadily increased its volume of business. With 21,000 square feet of floor space there was ample room to install butter making equipment. A churn, costing $1,000 and capable of churning 1,000 pounds of butter at one time was added. This product known as "Spring Valley" butter, enjoys a wide distribution. Large contracts were entered into, sometimes with railroad companies, for use on their dining cars.

Of the 14 different products put out by the company one of them is Hi-Lactic milk feed. This is made by a process developed at Ames, in which the lactic acid. is developed to a super degree, and the skimmed milk condensed into a paste form. It is a scientific feed, especially suited to hogs and poultry. In one year 114 carloads were shipped into ten states, besides a large local consumption.

The engine room has a capacity of 450 steam horsepower, the brick smoke stack rises to a height of 125 feet, the annual fuel bill is $12,000, the city water bill reaches $2,000 and many Perry families depend on work here for a livelihood.

In 1918 F. M. Fazel began gardening in a rather small way, but each year increased his acreage and output. His original ground, hot-beds and warehouse were at Eighth and Jones avenue. Later he acquired about ten acres just south of town, upon which the old "duck pond" was located. He raised nearly everything, but at first made a specialty of strawberry plants, which he sold by contract to seed and nursery houses. 

His banner year was about a half million plants. Horseradish, for a long time, was one of his special features. 

This was ground and bottled in their local establishment, and a carload of bottles were used annually. 

The F. M. Fazel Co. became well known, and for a time Mr. Fazel was president of the Iowa Vegetable

Growers Association. In time he operated about 100 acres of additional ground, and raised some things on a large scale.

PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 329 

By the beginning of 1935 Mr. Fazel retired from the business, and his four sons, Roy, Lloyd, Dwight and Earl formed the Fazel Bros. company in February. 

All former activities were dropped, and they engaged in the hi-bred seed corn business. They contract with the farmers to raise the corn under their scientific care and supervision. In the fall the corn is delivered to their processing plant where it is sorted, graded and sacked. The 1936 crop sold at from $12 to $14 a bushel, but now it is only about $6. The 1937 acreage was 350 acres, but contracts now indicate that they may have 2,000 acres this year. In addition to their processing plant in the old Kibby washer building they conduct a retail store on Second street, where seeds, nursery stock, etc., are sold with the corn. The de-tasseling, and so forth of the growing corn, and the various processes necessary, require the employment of many men at some seasons of the year. 

When J. O. Holdefer first came to Perry the town had been without any milling service for over 15 years, and he concluded that here was a good opening, consequently he built a mill on the site of the old Otis mill. Proceeding cautiously, his first mill was only 

24 by 40 feet in size, with two stories. From this small beginning in 1914 he has continually enlarged as the growing business justified. Today the main section is three stories high, and ware rooms have been added until the total floor space is 6,000 square feet. All kinds of table cereals and poultry and stock feed are manufactured. The table products go under the "Perry Perfection" brand, while the poultry and

stock feeds are labeled "Pe-Em-Co" brand. Extensive custom grinding is done for farmers of a wide area. 

Electrical power is used, and the entire mill is a model of order and cleanliness. Mr. Holdefer made no mistake in coming to Perry.

The Des Moines Valley Produce Co. came to Perry seven years ago, locating in the building formerly used by the Globe Manufacturing Co. The first four years they merely bought poultry and eggs for the Priebe company; the eggs being graded and the poultry dressed in attractive style, but in 1935 the place was converted into a concentration plant, and all Priebe houses within a radius of 250 miles truck their poultry and eggs to Perry, from where they are shipped out in carlots to the eastern markets. They have cooler space to accommodate over five carloads of poultry, and three carloads of eggs. In 1936 they started a hatchery in connection with the business. Nine local men are employed the year around, and Joe Kidd is the manager. 

Henry Wiese came to Perry about 1905 and opened a horse shoeing shop. Later, when horseshoeing was on the wane he engaged in blacksmithing and acetylene welding. As a result of his welding experience he invented a new process of reclaiming worn out plow shares and cultivator shovels. In 1935, he and his son, Lee, engaged exclusively in the manufacture of these repair parts, under the firm name of New Process Plow 

Welding Co. Larger quarters were needed, so a move was made to a suitable factory building on Fifth street. A force of employes, sometimes numbering as many as twenty-five are required to keep up with

PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 331 

the demand, which is not confined to our country alone, for some are sent to Canada, and 3,000 a year are sent to Mexico. The annual production is from forty to fifty thousand, and the outlook is very promising. 

For many years the Sinclair company of Cedar Rapids bought hogs at the Milwaukee stock yards, with Walter Sund in charge. Later, the place was taken over by the Armour company, and they still are the local buyers. Prior to the Sinclair management J. I. Kelley was a local buyer for years. 

Some years ago local manufacturing firms co-operated together in showing their products in a downtown room, for a few days, where the public was invited to come and see the display of home products. This interesting collection made a very creditable showing for Perry and aroused much interest in local industries. 

Gas service in Perry dates back to 1905 when a company was formed of local men. A franchise was issued on April 5, 1905. For some years a water gas plant was used, but in 1913 it was changed to a complete coal gas works. The plant was built on 

Warford street west of the M. & St. L. tracks. In 

September, 1927, the Iowa Central Utilities Co. bought the plant of the Iowa Railway & Light Co., and greatly enlarged its capacity and business. In 

December, 1933, a change was made to natural gas, which is piped from southern fields. Not only is the gas used for cooking and small heaters, but many coal furnaces have been converted into gas heaters

for heating the whole house with no more work than that of adjusting a small device on the wall. The gas plant is no longer used, except in minor ways. At the present time there are 1,065 meters out, but some are not being used.

The Iowa Railway & Light Co. took over the local plant in 1912. In 1916 they erected a new plant on the Interurban railway. This is a large and strictly modern plant, having a concrete smokestock of 180 feet in height. A few years ago this remarkable stack was so damaged by lightning that about half of it had to be torn down and rebuilt. This company has greatly expanded their business in Perry and surrounding country. The plant is equipped with three large heavy duty six-cylinder oil engines of 800 horse power each.

Over forty persons are steadily employed the year 'round, caring for all the phases of the work. A downtown office with all appliances on hand, has been steadily maintained, rural lines have been built, new heat mains installed and excellent service given. Aside from Perry, the company operates in Dawson, Bouton, Gardiner and Woodward. 

On December 12, 1883, the Iowa Union Telephone and Telegraph Company of Davenport was granted a franchise to build a telephone exchange in Perry. 

Early in 1884 the first telephone system was placed in service with E. E. Thompson as manager. Due to the lack of patronage, the income was inadequate to meet fixed charges, and after about one year's operation the exchange was closed. During the next four years a few citizens of Perry received telephone

PERRY INDUSTRIES AND UTILITIES 

333 service on a line which was also used for long distance service.

The second attempt to establish a telephone system in Perry was in 1888 when a new exchange was placed in service under the management of Dr. Condon, a dentist. This exchange was operated for a year or so and it then followed the same procedure as its predecessor by changing to a long distance line. 

At one time during those early times the exchange was in the Rall shoe store, and the only two patrons were 

Walter Cardell, lawyer, and Shotwell & Davis, produce firm.

George N. Bandy then made a third and successful attempt to provide a telephone system for Perry in 

1895. On January 21, 1896, seventy customers were being served over the switchboard that he installed. 

A year before Bandy's time Fred Knell had become interested in telephone service and had secured a franchise. On February 3, 1896, Mr. Bandy sold three-tenths interest to eighteen individuals and firms, and together with them incorporated the Perry Telephone Company. Three years later the name of the company was changed to the Hawkeye Telephone Company.

In December, 1908, the Hawkeye company sold its Perry property to the Mutual Telephone Company of Des Moines, and the latter sold it to the Iowa Telephone Company on April 24, 1914. The name of the company was changed on January 1, 1921, to the

Northwestern Bell Telephone Company, and has remained so since that time. In 1911 the present tele-

phone building was built. Prior to this time the exchange had been across the street, on the second floor of the Breed building.

The business has grown through the years, and today there are 1,629 telephones running into the 

Perry exchange. Two hundred nineteen of these are in the country and the balance, 1,410, are in Perry homes and business houses. There are about 2,180 miles of wire encased in overhead and underground cables, and approximately 410 miles of aerial wire. 

This complete and modern equipment gives Perry a very excellent telephone service.

In order that Perry telephone users may be provided service twenty-four hours a day and seven days a week, thirteen telephone operators are employed by the company.

Others who are necessary to maintain good service bring the total number of employes up to eighteen people. In addition to the more than 

850 local circuits which terminate on the Perry switchboard, approximately twenty-four toll circuits are in use. These toll circuits provide telephone communication channels from Perry to other points in Iowa, the nation and most of the civilized world. Besides 

Perry, the company has exchanges at Adel, Dallas 

Center, Granger and Van Meter. Mr. B. L. Crane is the local manager.

CHAPTER 15 WEATHER RECORDS Climate Changing?-Weather Observers-Droughts-Winter of 1935-236-Unusual Records-Comparative Chart. 

The subject of weather and climate is one that interests us all, regardless of what our occupation is, and a brief review will probably not be amiss at this point. It is often argued by the older people that our climate is changing. "We don't have the deep snows and cold winters that we used to have," they sometimes say. The writer is inclined to believe, as Mr. 

Chas. D. Reed, Iowa weather director, contends, that if the people of today lived in poorly constructed houses, with common wood stoves, and traveled in open horse-drawn sleds and vehicles, and wore no overshoes-had no mail routes, telephones or radios, we would probably say that our winters were severe, too.

Even though there was a lessening of severity we can readily see where conditions under which we live make a lot of difference. As one man has said, "Winter is not so bad if a person is fixed for it." But is there a change? Old timers tell of hard winters; they also tell of "open" winters, when plowing could be done every month of the winter; but haven't we had both kinds recently?

The value of weather records now becomes apparent, as we make comparisons-comparisons based upon accurate, uniform government records. Believe it or not, but the lowest temperature ever recorded in 335

Iowa was not back in pioneer days, but in 1912. On 

January 12th of that year a record of 47 below zero was made at Washta, in Cherokee county, and it is doubtful if any winter has brought more snow than in 1928-29. Iowa Falls held the record that winter for Iowa with 72.1 inches, or a trifle over six feet. 

Perry had 55.2 inches and Des Moines, 51.2 inches.

But someone will say that there were no records in those days, but that is only partially right. Regular systematic records of temperature and precipitation for the entire state have been taken since 1873 Dubuque has records going back 87 years, Iowa City, 81 years; three other places over 70, and many over 

60 years. Naturally one who is exposed to the severities of a bad winter will recall it quite differently from one who remained inside. Another matter that would make considerable difference are the towns and groves all over the country, all tending to check the wind in its sweep across the prairies. 

Normally, January is the coldest month of the winter. Mr. Reed says, "The coldest Januarys in

Dallas county, arranged approximately in the order of severity, are: 1912, 1875, 1857, 1888, 1883, 1886, 1918 and 1887." It is a matter of history that the year of the Spirit Lake massacre (1857) was very severe.

The average temperature for January, 1912, at Waukee was 4.8 degress, as compared to a normal of 20.8 degrees. The minimum reading was 36 below on the 12th.

Mr. Reed also states that the coldest Februarys, approximately in the order of severity, were: 1936,

WEATHER RECORDS 

337 1875, 1899, 1885, 1905, 1843, 1838, 1823 and 1829. "You will understand," he says, "there were no actual records in Dallas county by which we quote actual figures prior to the establishment of the stations at Waukee and Desota, but from rather remote stations from which interpotations can be made, we can be fairly sure of our grounds in this connection." 

The first government weather records for Dallas county were made by James C. Michener, M.D., at 

Adel. These were for precipitation, and extended from 

December, 1875, to December, 1878. The oldest ex isting station within the county is at Waukee, which was begun on October 1, 1894, with John Wragg as observer. Most of the time this station has been in charge of Samuel F. Foft, but Barto Speer is the present observer. Another station was begun at Desota in November, 1897, with R. D. Minard in charge. This continued until Mr. Minard's removal from the county in January, 1904. The Weather Bureau's connection with the Van Meter river gauge began in 1919 with George Cotton as observer. B. W. 

Landers is the present observer, and records are kept throughout the entire year. 

The Perry station was begun on October 1, 1900, and has continued up to the present time. Observers have been: Dr. Theo. F. Johnson, October, 1900; J. A. 

Harvey, November, 1901; Ed S. Gray, July, 1910; 

S. J. Brumfield, February, 1912; Mrs. C. E. Anthony, August, 1917; F. N. Bartlett, February, 1920, and 

Eugene N. Hastie, January, 1925. The newest station is at Dexter where only rainfall and sky obser-

vations are maintained. This began July 27, 1935, with Wm. R. Blohm in charge.

In the spring of 1851 there was a historic flood throughout this portion of Iowa. An old mark on a tree in Union Park, Des Moines, shows the water to have been very high-about equal to that of May 

31, 1903. Another notable flood for Raccoon river was in 1875, mention of which was made in a preceding chapter.

Mr. Reed lists the summer drouths in the order of their severity as follows: 1886, 1894, 1930 and 1879.

He further adds, "You will observe that I omit 1934 and 1936 for the reasons that it was the excessive prolonged heat that used up the rain that fell in those years rather than the absence of rain." 

For many years the summer of 1894 was the one that people talked about in regard to severe droughts and crop failures. That year was indeed bad, and the corn crop was nearly a failure. The average rainfall for the entire state of Iowa was only .63 inches.

It is difficult to make an accurate comparison of seasons as a whole, for a season that may be a record breaker in one respect may be far from it in another, althugh hot weather and dry weather are closely related.

July of 1901 scored an all time heat record, and while crops were not good, they were much better than 1894 or 1934. On that notable month the Perry station had 19 consecutive days with a temperature of 100 or above, the highest being 110 on the 24th.

In the monthly report for that year, J. R. Sage, who

WEATHER RECORDS 

339 was then the state director, said, "The. figures recording sustained high temperatures for the longest period ever experienced in this section will stand for comparison in future years, and it may be hoped the new record will remain unbroken during the coming century." Thirty-seven years have passed, and so far it is unbroken.

Since that time there have been many years that have had drouth tendencies at times, but usually of a shorter duration, or at a time not so critical. The amount of sub-moisture, time of drought, length of its duration and degree of heat all contribute to make a difference in these dry times. The year of 1911 was quite dry with reduced crops, as was also 1913, but the next major drouth year was 1930. During the summer months of June, July and August the total rainfall at Perry was 4.68 inches with temperature readings of 100 or more on thirteen days. This year seemed to be the beginning of a series of droughts, extending through later years. The total rainfall for the entire year (at Perry) was 16.15 inches, which is less than half of the normal amount. Of the 103 stations in Iowa, Perry had the least precipitation of any. Oskaloosa had the most with 35.65 inches. 

The drought of 1930 extended right on into 1931.

Wells began going dry by the fall of '30, and for a year the rains were insufficient to help them. It could not be said that the drought was broken before the 

1st and 2nd of August, consequently crops that year, too, were far from good. The winter of 1930-31 was one of the kind that only comes "but once in a life-

time" for this section. Farmers actually plowed every month that winter-in February most farmers were busy raking and burning stalks and plowing-of course it was too early to sow oats. In September and November, 1931, the rains were unusually heavy, there being 8.29 inches in November alone (Perry station).

This not only spoiled much "down" corn but prevented the farmers from getting it out before winter. Lots of it was not gathered until the next spring. Perry people, in going to Des Moines the next spring saw farmers planting and husking corn the same day. 

The outstanding drought of American history came in 1934. This covered the major portion of the agricultural section of the United States, and was introduced by unprecedented dust storms in the spring. 

Much good Dallas county soil was blown into drifts at the edge of plowed fields. It was so dry in April that many of the oats never came up, but weeds predominated from the start. Many field were plowed up and put to corn, others were pastured and those that were harvested were almost worthless. One farmer got thirty bushels of very inferior oats from ten acres.

Running parallel with the drought was a plague of chinch bugs over most of southern Iowa and adjoining states. These bugs were bad as far north as the southern part of Dallas county. Small ditches, with creosote in the bottom, were mostly used to combat the destructive insects. The first heat wave came the last few days of May, when it was 105 on Decoration day. Strawberries, nearly ready to pick, were

WEATHER RECORDS 

341 dried up and rendered worthless. A promising crop was almost a total loss. Before relief came in the fall there were twenty-six days with a temperature of 100 or above. A new official record of 111 degrees was made for Dallas county on August 8th. Had the temperature been normal there would have been but little trouble for the total rainfall for the three summer months was 7.45 inches.

Corn, not only here but through many states, was the poorest of record. On sandy soil it was a complete loss. Even though there was but little corn, the farmers wanted to save what there was. In one case two men, working to one wagon could only get twentyfive hushels of inferior corn in a whole day. As a consequence corn mounted in price, reaching a peak of about 95 cents at Christmas time. Southern Iowa and Missouri were exceedingly short of corn, both old and new, so all that fall and winter hundreds of trucks were busily engaged in hauling corn from central and northern Iowa.

One of the ravages of the drought was the dying of many of the best shade and ornamental trees of the state, especially was this true of the white birch and some varieties of evergreens. The grass in the courthouse yard so died out that the ground had to be worked up and new grass seed sown for a new lawn. Northern Iowa, generally, had at least fair crops, while southern Iowa was hard hit. Dallas county was on the border line to the north of us conditions were much better.

Many farmers were unable to procure feed for their

cattle, and were forced to sell them at a great sacrifice in an effort to keep a few of the best ones for their private use. The government, during the fall, inaugurated a plan of buying famished cattle to relieve the farmers, and to provide means of buying feed. This was done by counties, and extended as far north as Dallas county. In the first purchase, at 

Adel, forty farmers sold 458 head. The following 

January 94 more were purchased. Of the whole number 94 were condemned as unfit for food, and the worst ones were taken to the right-of-way east of town, where they were shot and buried. Those suitable for food were processed at various packing plants and used for government relief work. One of the plants chosen for this work was the packing house in Perry. The total number purchased in the whole state was 23,073 head, 203 of which were condemned.

The price paid for this stock ranged from $2 for calves to a maximum of $20 for cows.

In 1935 crops here were from fair to good, but the next year another drought came to the county. Some sections were as bad as before, but Dallas county farmers fared some better than in 1934. Similar to 

1934 there were twenty-four days with a temperature of 100 or more, while the precipitation was considerably less-only 5.41 inches. The normal amount for the three summer months is 12.53 inches-33.47 inches for the entire year, ranging from 1.02 inches in 

December to 4.84 inches in May.

Conditions of a couple years had so reduced the surplus of corn that the price reached a peak of about

WEATHER RECORDS 

343 $1.25 in January and February. As chinch bugs accompanied the drought of 1934, so grasshoppers came with the drought of 1936. While not so serious here, they were a real menace in other sections of the state, and for the last two years they have gradually increased, so that the soil at the present time contains many eggs for the coming season.

It is rather strange that the worst winter in 117 years would come between these unusual summers, but one extreme often follows the other, and the winter of 1935-36 is one long to be remembered. The earliest weather records now known in Iowa were begun in Council Bluffs on October 22, 1819 by an army post surgeon. Many records in various parts of the state for a considerable number of years in succession were made by individuals, but there was but little done to organize or centralize the work until the 

Smithsonian Institute began in 1849. 

In this way, continuous, although broken, records have been maintained up until this notable winter.

The weather was near normal up until the 18th of January. On Saturday the temperature began falling; by nightfall it was three below zero, but there was nothing unusual about that. In a few days people expected a moderation, but a few days brought no change. Soon a week had passed, then two weeks, three weeks, and there was no abatement. This was destined to be a record-breaker. Everybody was talking about the winter, and its manifold complications. 

Exactly two weeks were in January, and when February was ushered in, many were the predictions of

prognosticators. Little did people realize on "ground hog" day that the worst was yet to come, but such was all too true. Not until February 21st did the subzero weather break. During this 35 day period there were only three days that the thermometer did not register zero or below.

During these two months the snowfall amounted to 26.9 inches, practically all coming in rather small amounts, every few days. With characteristic high winds the drifting was unusual. Snow plows on the highways would push the snow to one side; that only made a crest for deeper drifts the next time it snowed, and on and on it continued until most roads were blocked some for a few days, some for a week or two, and many minor roads remained so until spring. 

The high wind and low temperature made a very hard crust which was very hard to plow through. Sometimes it would take a powerful snow plow, with several men breaking the crust, several hours to go a quarter of a mile. By the end of this period there were many single track places where the snow would be piled to a depth of from seven to ten feet deep on each side. Snow plows worked day and night. The 

Interurban railroad was snowbound twice, once for four days. On one occasion about twenty men were taken out to shovel snow from the tracks. Drifting snow cut off their chance to return and at the close of the second day they were hauled in trucks from 

Moran to Perry after some very memorable experiences.

The worst single day was near the beginning, when,

WEATHER RECORDS 

345 with a sweeping wind and drifting snow, the temperature did not rise above 19 below zero all day. Those who were compelled to be out on such days, such as mail carriers, farmers, dairymen and many others, suffered much from frost-bites, and from the cutting and drifting snow. On one day no milk from local dairies reached Perry all day, and on several others, only a small amount. Church services and Sunday schools were almost abandoned, and one Sunday most, if not all, services were cancelled the day before. Because of extreme cold, blocked roads and coal shortage, the Perry high school classes were suspended for a total of twelve days.

Because of the extreme cold weather there was a decided increase in the demand for coal; this fact, coupled with the difficulty of getting to and from the mines, caused a real coal shortage. Some people had to resort to wood, some families doubled up with friends or neighbors; all had to curtail. In Des 

Moines a great many were lodged on the floors of the courthouse. Some churches threw open their doors to house those who were unable to obtain fuel. Those who had adequate shelter, food, clothing and fuel were fortunate.

During these trying, yet interesting times, when mail failed to come, the radio proved to be a great blessing to thousands of people who were more or less isolated. News of communities entirely out of fuel or food frequently came over the air. In some cases people were compelled to burn furniture and cut their shade trees to keep from freezing. Farmers, with

shovels, often would club together, and with a team and bobsled drive to town, often through fields, to get provisions.

There was some concern about floods resulting from the melting snow, should it melt too fast, but fortunately the water run off with but little damage. 

After the cold wave broke on February 21st the temperature never again reached zero. The bad winter was behind, but ahead was another hot, dry summer, with poor crops, despite the traditional idea that heavy snows assure good crops.

Since the establishment of the Perry weather station in 1900 some remarkable records have been made, some of which are here given. Unusually wet months have been: May, 1903, 11.41 inches; July, 1915, 11.47 inches, and the wettest of all in September, 1926, when the rains totaled 12.13 inches. Many farmers will recall the cool wet summer season of 1915. In 

May there was 8.75 inches of rain, and an average temperature of nearly four degrees below normal. In 

June the rain was less than normal, but the temperature was even a little more than four degrees below normal. The climax was reached in July when the temperature for the third successive month in growing season was below normal-4.6 degrees; and in addition to the coolness of the season the July rainfall was 11.47 eaches. This abundant rainfall came in seventeen of the thirty-one days of the month, and made it extremely difficult to harvest the small grain and hay. Many farmers pulled their binders with horses, and a gasoline engine to run the machinery.

WEATHER RECORDS 

347 Lee Wilson, Perry farmer, had forty acres of oats that he was unable to cut at all. 

As a result of this cool backward season corn growth was greatly retarded, resulting in frost-nipped corn in the fall. The combination of short crop, poor quality and America's entrance into the Warld war caused corn prices to soar. Late in July of 1917 local elevators were paying a record price of $2.00 a bushel.

The greatest single rain occurred on June 12-13, 1926. 'Twas on Saturday night that ominous clouds came over, leaving about two inches in the first installment. Later in the night the wind changed, bringing the clouds back and repeating the same thing. 

Three intermittent showers of about equal proportions had come by 1:00 a. m. Sunday forenoon the fourth and final shower came, bringing the total rainfall up to 7.57 inches in nineteen and one-half hours. Small streams were converted into miniature rivers, low ground was flooded and much damage was done to cultivated fields by washing and burying of crops. 

On this notable Saturday night William Rector of

Minburn was electrocuted on Second street in Perry as he touched a lamp post after one of the showers.

Extensive damage was done by lightning including the burning of barns on the Ed Mace and Dan Payton farms.

In the spring of 1889 oncoming crops were ahead of schedule and were very promising. Corn of good size was being plowed the second time, when, on May 31st, there came a killing frost which killed the corn

to the ground, but it grew up again from the roots and did fairly well. Another unusual spring was in 

1910. That year March was unusually fine and warm. 

Vegetation was two weeks ahead; by mid-April the fruit trees were in bloom; but in the midst of their slendor there came three successive days with freezing temperatures, the second morning with a reading of 21, April 17th. Needless to say, the fruit was killed and the leaves, too. For some time the trees presented a sorry looking spectacle with their blackened, drooping leaves. In time new leaves replaced the first ones, and nature pursued her course. 

It is a well know fact that Iowa has a very changeable winter climate one day will be like spring; perhaps the next will be below zero. A striking example of this occurred in February, 1930. On the morning of the 15th it was 29 below zero; nine days later the mercury reached 76, making a range of 105 degrees in nine days. One of the most unusual weather freaks was a temperature of 8 below zero in October, 1925. On the 29th the temperature dipped to 

7 below with the next morning exceeding that by one degree. Only on three occasions in thirty-eight years has the temperature ever reached zero in the month of November, and then never to exceed four below.

Two outstanding Marchs were in 1910 and 1912. 

The first having a temperature of over 14 degrees above normal and only a trace of precipitation, while the latter was about ten degrees below normal with an abundance of snow. Good sleighing continued throughout most of the month. Temperatures of

WEATHER RECORDS 

349 zero or below occurred on seven days. Another unusual March was in 1923, at least for a week or ten days about the middle of the month. Zero weather and deep snow prevailed. East Willis avenue in

Perry was entirely blocked for several days. The latest snow of record was on May 15, 1907, when enough came to make the ground white.

Thunder in mid-winter is indeed rare in this lattitude, but not an unknown occurrence. On Christmas day, 1916, thunder was heard, but the most unusual case of this kind came on December 30, 1936. 

Between five and seven o'clock in the morning a fierce electrical storm visited Dallas county, and the rainfall amounted to 1.03 inches.
