CHAPTER 16  

1900-1938 

Early Automobiles-Early Garages-License Plates-Develop ment of Road System-The World War-The Inter-urban 

Railway-Prohibition-The Depression-New Deal Policies Agricultural Improvements-Aviation- Old Age Pensions-Radio Development. 

The outstanding feature of 20th Century civilization is the automobile. Nothing has so revolutionized our customs, travel and industries as the automobile. 

About 1900 people were hearing of horseless carriages. Soon some were actually being seen; they were about like buggies. A small motor under the seat drove the rear wheels by means of a chain. The wheels were high with small hard rubber tires; the earlier types were steered by a lever rather than a steering wheel. 

Soon the wheels were made smaller and pneumatic tires were used. It was a long time before windshields were used, but the driver bravely faced the breeze with goggles on his eyes. A speed of 20 miles an hour was considered fast.

For a long time several objectionable matters faced every auto driver. In the first place the horses on the road were frightened to meet a vehicle without a horse or team. Usually the car driver would have to stop his car, get out and help get the horses by the strange contraption. Secondly the roads were not adapted to automobiles; rain and resultant muddy roads made it either difficult or impossible to travel. 351

The first car in Dallas county was a Currier, purchased by John Fitzgerald of Adel early in 1902. The first one in Perry was also a Currier, bought jointly by 

Lawrence Tiernan and George O'Malley. Other early makes of cars were Rambler, Sears, Haynes, Cadillac and others. Dr. M. G. Sloan of Dexter had one of the first cars in the county, a Cadillac. The second car in Perry was Dr. Doidge's Rambler. 

It is interesting to note that in 1910 the nation's cars were made in nearly every state in the Union. 

Michigan led the list with 49 factories, Illinois 39, and 

Indiana and Ohio 30 each. Iowa had seven, Kansas and Texas one each. Some years before this, even 

Perry had an automobile factory. It was where the 

Osmundson factory stands today. Two small frame buildings were erected by the DeLaura Auto Co., and two cars are said to have been assembled, when the company decided to leave Perry. 

In 1910 Allen Cox of Perry was running a garage at 915-919 Railroad street. This place contained 7,500 square feet of floor space, would accommodate 50 cars, and was claimed to be the largest garage in the state. 

They handled the Buick cars, and had sold 33 during 

1909. Rush Shortley was sales manager and Carlos 

Cox was foreman of the repair department. 

The other principal garage was the Rude Auto Co., whose location, at that time, was at Otley avenue and Railroad street. This company was formed in October, 1909, and the following spring were selling the Mitchell, Reo and Ford cars. At that time the three styles of 

Mitchells were selling at $1,000, $1,350 and $2,000.

Reo cars were priced at $500, $1,000 and $1,250 and the Fords were $950. In a few years this company moved to larger quarters at First and Warford, where for many years they enjoyed a large trade during the best days of Ford sales. In 1926 Rudes sold out to the LeCocq Motor Co., who secured the Ford agency. 

In a few years the agency passed to the Burkett 

Motor Co., and the Economy Chevrolet Co., owned by Elmer Orr, carried on in the old quarters. On 

October 22, 1936, the business was purchased by the 

Northey Chevrolet Co., who continue to the present time.

During the King's Daughters fair in 1909 three cars parked at the fire station did a rushing business taking people auto rides over the city for 25 cents each. Many amusing stories are told of people's early experiences with cars. One man got his car started on his farm, and for a time didn't know how to stop it. He had a rather exciting time dodging trees and buildings for a short time.

Automobiles hadn't gotten very far until it became evident that better roads must be had. As a means of accomplishing this the State Legislature in 1904 passed a law requiring all car owners to register, and conspicuously display a number, of Arabic numerals. The fee was $1.00. Three years later the fee was raised to $5.00. Up until 1922 the numbers were issued from 

Des Moines for the whole state, and there was nothing to indicate what part of the state a car was from. When county designations were first made the numbers were based on car registrations, and Dallas county was

number 30 for three years; in 1925 it was 31, and 32 in 1926. The next year the number was changed to 

22, and remained so for three years. 

Beginning with 1930 a double change was made.

First, the plates were reduced in size from 5½ by 16 inches to 5 by 13 inches, and Dallas county's number was changed to 25. The number and size of plates have remained the same since that time. When the plates were transferred to county supervision in 1922 they were issued from the treasurer's office. J. A. 

Sellards, who is in charge of this department, states that there were 6,999 cars, 973 trucks, 74 trailers and 

19 motorcycles registered in 1937. However, this is not a record number, for in 1929 there were over 

7,600 cars and over 1,200 trucks.

For many years the gasoline for cars was sold by garages from a pump in front of their place of business-it was merely a part of the garage service. In 

1917 separate filling stations began to be built. The first in Perry was the Standard station at First and Warford in July, 1917. The Standard company opened their first station in Adel about the same time. 

Competition is now keen in this line, with a surplus of stations everywhere. Perry has twenty-six, Minburn seven and Adel twenty. The principal brands in Dallas county at the present time are Standard, Phillips, Shell, Sinclair, Coryell, Deep Rock, Skelly,

Continental and Mid-Continent, and the retail price of regular, or medium grade, is 18 cents a gallon. 

The first gravel roads were about 1903. The first thirty-five miles leading into Perry came through

the active co-operation of the Commercial Club and the farmers. In those days the club paid $50 a mile in cash and the farmers donated the hauling. The first big job of graveling by trucks was about 1920, and extended from Perry to Adel, east to Woodward and north to the Boone county line. The early auto roads in the county were the White Pole road, east and west through Adel, the Panora Speedway, east and west through Dallas Center, and the Spirit Lake road through Perry that ran from Des Moines to the lakes. In those days the roads were marked in a great variety of ways on telephone poles; if there were no poles sometimes markings would be on fence posts. 

When the question of paved roads came before 

Dallas county voters, May 9, 1929, they decided in favor of pavement by a majority of 855 votes, but it was not until a year later, May 4th, that the first pavement was actually begun. This was two miles from Redfield, and the job when completed, extended across the county and on to Des Moines. Again the old road of the '49ers and stagecoach days looms into history. During the same summer paving was completed from Des Moines to Perry via Dallas Center and Gardiner. A few years later the gap between 

Black's Corner and the Guthrie county line was filled in, and from the said corner to Adel. 

In 1930 the Bergman law adopted the uniform primary system, and abolished township roads. The minor roads were called secondary roads. The White 

Pole road became known as No. 6, the Panora Speed-

way, No. 7, the Spirit Lake road, No. 17; the road running from Winterset to Ogden through Adel, No. 

169; and a road from Perry to Manilla, No. 46. Uniform steel plate markers were installed wherever necessary, and they were the same all over the state, except for number. Guessing the right way to go was eliminated. 

Within the county is 1,100 miles of public highways, and 700 miles of the secondary road are graveled. There is a county shed in each township where road maintainers are kept for that territory, and the state has three maintenance buildings, at Adel, Black's Corner and Perry. There is now available for the county use 2,000 50-foot rolls of snow fence. The road system of today is a wonderful improvement over that of thirty years ago. Some years ago the state highway commission, in routing the primary roads, put a new road diagonally across the William 

Moran 160 acre farm in Des Moines township.

Mr. Moran sued the commission for damages to his farm, and the district court allowed Mr. Moran $4,700. 

The case was appealed, but the Iowa supreme court upheld the former decision. 

A Dallas county man recalls his first overland trip in an auto in the following words: "I clearly remember the memorable morning of the day we were to drive to my uncle's place, 160 miles away. We were up bright and early; at six we started. The thrill of that beautiful summer morning was delightful. At noon we stopped along the roadside to eat our lunch and secure drinking water from a nearby farm house.

During the afternoon we got entirely off the trail unto the poorest kind of a road. So many corners were questionable about the proper course to pursue. Coming into a very hilly country the old Ford was sometimes unable to climb the hills, and all but the driver would get out and push. Such ordeals took much of the joy out of the trip, but finally at six in the evening we reached our destination. We were elated, and thought it a great thing to go so far in one day." 

Now most folks would easily make it in three hours, or one-fourth the time.

For many years the Fords were by far the most numerous of all makes. The earlier cars were of the open top style, having curtains to put on for the wet or cold weather. The lights were of carbon, and later called Prest-o-lite. Both had to be lit with a match like a gas jet. In time the winter tops came into use. These, with stationary tops, had isinglass slides that were pushed up and down. Windshields, electric lights, speedometers, starters, wipers, demountable rims and spare tires, anti-freeze solutions, shiftgears and radios are accessories that came through the years.

In Perry, two of the principal garages have been those of the McCarthy Brothers and Jenkins & Batterson's, each of whom have large establishments and maintain all night service. The McCarthy Brothers began in a small way in 1916, but did not build up their present fine new garage on Railroad street until 1920; The Jenkins & Batterson company began in 

Perry in 1921 at Third and Willis. Some time after

the Lester garage fire on June 22, 1922, they moved to that location at First and Lucinda where they have since remained.

Probably no event of the twentieth century history was more far reaching in its effects than the World war. The entire world is still feeling the effect of this awful conflict. It is not the purpose of this history to given an account of what led up to American participation, nor to give details of the same, neither to give a list of Dallas county men who took part. This information properly comes within the scope of American Legion history, one of which is being written by Thomas Finane of Perry.

Our country formally declared war against Germany in April, 1917. On June 5th, all men of military age in the United States were required to register. Nearly ten millions enrolled, over 500 of which were in Perry. During the spring and summer construction work on Camp Dodge went steadily forward. This cantonment extended for miles along the 

Interurban railway north of Des Moines. An enormous sum of money was spent. Inferior carpenters received as much as $7 for a short day. In September the first general draft was made, and many Dallas county men left their homes to begin a military career. Amid tears and hilarity they bade loved ones goodbye, not knowing what the future held in store for them. Most Iowa men were sent to Camp Dodge, where they were trained in the arts of war. 

To meet the immediate needs of war preparation a series of Liberty loans were carried out by the

government. The first, amounting to $2,000,000,000, was offered on May 14th. The second followed 

October 1, 1917; the third, April 6, 1918. The fourth, being the largest, asked for six billion dollars, and began on September 28, 1918. This was stopped when it had been exceeded by nearly one billion. The fifth, called the Victory loan, took place after the Armistice, in April and May of 1919. The total of all five amounted to $21,448,120,300. Dallas county and its various communities usually filled their quotas promptly.

Many war time measures were carried out. On November 1st letter postage was raised from two to three cents, where it has remained ever since. In December the government took charge of the railroads, and questionnaires were sent to all registered men. Each county classified their men into five classes, according to marriage, dependents, occupation, age, health, etc. 

Class One was taken first, with the other groups to follow in proper order. The American Red Cross promptly came into action and all were urged to join by paying $1 for membership. Another campaign, "Smokes for Soldiers," was carried out to provide the boys with cigarettes. The big tobacco companies were then sowing the seed of what they are reaping now. War savings stamps and Thrift stamps were issued by the government and were designed to meet the need of those who could not buy Liberty bonds in the larger amounts.

The wheat substitute plan was begun on February 1, 1918. Wheat was of prime importance then, and

to conserve the supply, was made a national duty. 

With every purchase of wheat flour a certain percentage of cornmeal, rye flour, barley flour, rice flour, etc., must be included. The sale of sugar, too, was carefully regulated; only so much was allotted to a family, depending upon the number therein. 

The second general draft was in February and many more men were called upon to leave home to face the unknown future. On March 31st, daylight saving time was made nation wide. Clocks were set up one hour in order to utilize more daylight in the morning, and lessen the need of artificial lighting at night. This was generally carried out the first year, but here it was so unsatisfactory that it was not undertaken the second year. The following September a second registration day was carried out. In preparing for the worst, additional men were desired to be in readiness if their help was necessary. The age limits were extended some each way from the original limits of twenty-one and forty-five. 

Camp Dodge became a military city with a steady population of several thousand. Warehouses, waterworks, electric lights, pavement and post office were permanent features. The work of the Y. M. C. A., Salvation Army and Knights of Columbus was extensively carried on from suitable headquarters. The mushroom growth of so large a place in so short a time was remarkable, and thousands of civilians came from all over Iowa to visit their boys and to see the "sights."

During these times many of the soldier boys came to 

Perry on the Interurban for brief visits. Every church had a standing rule that any solder who attended morning services would be taken to some home for Sunday dinner, and many responded to the generous offer.

Many abnormal conditions arose. A false patriotism filled the hearts of some people. Many merchants in Des Moines displayed posters bearing the words, "Whether right or wrong, our country first." Those who conscientiously objected to armed warfare were subjected to much scorn. One prominent minister, at a military funeral, said that all such objectors ought to be hung, and he'd like to help pull the rope.

Those who were pro-German, or anyone suspicioned of disloyalty were called "slackers," and their houses or property would likely be smeared with yellow paint. A hatred for the Germans was engendered among the people, and they were called "Huns" in derision.

During the fall and winter of 1917 an awful plague of Spanish influenza swept the country from the Atlantic to the Pacific. It began first in European army camps, and wherever it went, the death rate was very high. The number of deaths at Camp Dodge was very high and many Dallas county people died during a period of a few months. The disease baffled the skill of the best physicians, but was generally believed to be caused by a germinal infection. The malady was characterized by severe nervousness, causing headache and pain in the back and legs,

with a tendency to develop other complications, especially pneumonia. It is said that more people died of the awful pestilence than were killed in the World Local undertakers had more funerals by far war. that year than any year since. 

A striking example of the virulence of the disease is shown in the case of the Wilcox brothers of Perry. 

About the first of March, 1918, George Wilcox and family of Washington township moved to Minnesota. 

The children consisted of three grown sons and a young girl, and Mr. Wilcox had planned on extensive farming, but before they got settled one of the boys had the "flu." In a short time they all had it and before long all three of the boys lay cold in death. The family's goods were never unpacked, they returned to Perry with the remains of the three stalwart boys and they were buried together in Violet 

Hill cemetery.

On November 11, 1918, the country received the joyous news that an armistice had been signed, and that hostilities had ceased. An unusual celebration followed in nearly all communities of the country. 

Bells were rung, whistles shrieked, bonfires were built, parades were staged and excitement prevailed everywhere. Some of the most unusual pranks were carried out; it was like an overgrown Hallowe'en party. 

After the war, President Wilson went to Europe to represent our country in the formation of the treaty of Versailles, which was adopted and signed on June 

28, 1919.

The American Legion had its inception in Paris on February 15, 1919. At that time twenty young officers met and made plans for a national organization. The first convention in America was held in 

St. Louis in May, 1919. The first post in Iowa was at Spencer. The Floyd Foster Post No. 85, the first in Dallas county was organized in Perry on August 12, 1919. There were 260 charter members. The post was named in honor of the first Perry man to be killed in France, which occurred in October, 1918. 

Chas. Cornelius is the present post commander, and the present membership is about 130. 

Posts at Adel, Dexter, Dallas Center, Redfield and 

Woodward were formed in due time. Thomas Finane of Perry is one who barely escaped death while in service in France. At the Meuse-Argonne battlefield he was seriously shot by machine gun fire on September 26, 1918. The shin bone of a leg and an artery were fearfully mutilated. Today he is one of the active boys in Perry but still has to use a crutch to get about.

Among early twentieth century improvements were electric lines of railroads. For short lines, this type was said to have many advantages. In 1905 the Interurban railroad company began to build a line from 

Des Moines to Woodward and Perry. The grading work was in charge of Mr. Riley, a contractor of St. 

Louis. Various grading camps were erected, but one at the Henry Brady farm was typical of the rest. The moving of dirt was done by teams and

scrapers, elevators and horse-drawn wagons; and for long hauls, with dump cars on a narrow gauge track. 

At first Mr. Riley had Italian laborers from St. 

Louis, but their inability to work for a couple days after pay day caused him to "fire" them, and fill their places with Swedes from St. Paul, who seemed to do much better. Many of these men with their horses, etc., remained all winter living in tents. The building of this modern railroad was well received by Woodward, Granger and Perry, which gave them a service that only a few towns could boast of. Finally in November, 1906, the cars began running to Perry. 

The first day was Sunday and great crowds turned out to see Perry's new trolley cars. The brand new cars, fully equipped with all latest conveniences, were the source of much pride to everybody. 

For many years the automobiles were not at all common and the new line did a good business and became very popular. Twenty cars were run daily, which averaged about an hour and a half apart. A company plan that they hoped would be a success was the opening of Beaver Park at a location where their line crosses Beaver creek. The first year, 1907, they had a large crowd for a Fourth of July celebration, but beyond that the proposed park never got very far.

Early in 1910 a great deal of talk was heard about the appearance of Halley's comet, due in May. Should its tail touch the earth dreadful consequences would result, so they said. As the day drew near some people were much worried. It is said that a few peo-

ple didn't send their children to school on this particular day, and others hid in caves. The comet appeared on schedule time, but the omnipotent Creator held everything in its proper course. 

The evils of liquor date from the time of Noah. 

Four thousand years have not changed the relationship between King Alcohol and fallen man. Control and regulation of this gigantic monster has always been one of our chief problems. At the beginning of the twentieth century there were no saloons in Dallas county, but bootleggers and many drug stores sold "booze" illegally. There was much work continually being done to advance the cause of temperance and prohibition. Good Templar lodges, the 

Women's Christian Temperance Union, the Anti Saloon League and the Prohibition party were all potent factors toward temperance reform. Churches often threw open their doors for temperance and prohibition lecturers, and gradually a strong sentiment was built up against the liquor interests. J. A. Harvey, A. U. Coates, W. B. Ellis, Rev. J. C. Little, Dr. D. S. 

Grossman and C. Durant Jones were active temperance workers of Dallas county.

About 1906 C. Durant Jones, a militant prohibitionist, began the publication of a monthly paper called the Prohibition Journal. Mr. Jones fearlessly assailed the liquor crowd, and in return received missiles of eggs on his little printshop in Perry. He conducted a statewide campaign, much money was raised and used to send lecturers into the field, and despite the bold front of the enemy, the combined

forces of various organizations were gradually breaking down resistance.

When the war was in progress the government adopted war time prohibition, which was followed in 1920 by prohibition designed to be permanent. Going into effect on July 1st, it was humorously said of one saloonkeeper whose name was August, "The first of July is the last of August." The entire nation was closed up tight, saloons were converted to other purposes and breweries and distilleries stood idle. The nation's income was used for the welfare of the private citizen and his family. The acknowledged peak of prosperity came in prohibition times, but unfortunately many law enforcing officers were unfaithful in their work, some accepted bribes, others were intimidated by powerful underworld characters, some serious blunders were made, the public generally became indifferent and the churches were gradually drifting from former standards. "Eternal vigilance is the price of liberty." These penetrating influences, coupled with the fact that large well known groups of people were clamoring for beer, paved the way for a reaction that came soon after the advent of the "New Deal."

During prohibition days a beverage called "near beer" was permissible, but could not legally contain more than one-half of one per cent of alcohol. The 

New Deal was ushered in on a wet platform; their contention was that repeal of the Eighteenth amendment would help to bring back prosperity that had been lost during the depression. No sooner had the

Roosevelt term begun until steps were taken toward repeal. Beer, with an alcoholic content of 3.2 per cent, was legalized on April 7, 1933. 

On June 20th the state of Iowa voted on the repeal question. Dallas county as a whole was opposed to repeal, there being 2,934 for and 3,751 against the measure. The only precincts favoring repeal were 

Beaver, Boone, second and third wards in Perry, Grant, Spring Valley and Scandia, with Scandia leading in the wet list with 80% per cent of its people getting thirsty. Colfax township, on the other hand, rated a higher per cent of drys than any other, with only 22½ per cent wanting repeal. Iowa, as a whole, was about three to two in favor of repeal. 

On December 5th, after more than the required number of states had sanctioned repeal, the Eighteenth amendment to the National Constitution was officially annulled. The next few months a liquor set-up was planned for Iowa, the principal feature of which was the establishment of state liquor stores. 

A store was opened in Perry on August 30, 1934, with Lawrence Tiernan in charge. About two years later another was opened in Adel. An official report shows sales in the Perry store for the month of January, 1937, was $4,824.48, while Adel's sales amounted to $3,612.28. Permits to sell beer were also granted, and there are now eleven such places in Perry. 

During recent years a number of country resorts designed as amusement centers have been started. 

Principal among these were Dexfield Park, between 

Dexter and Redfield, Lake Robbins west of Moran

and the City club park west of Perry. Dexfield Park has been abandoned, but the others are well patronized by those who enjoy dancing and drinking. 

Water drainage has been a big matter in the county. 

Drain tile were first used around 1890, but were only in small sizes, many no more than three inches in size. 

Being so small they never proved very satisfactory. 

In time larger sizes were used with better results. The larger projects were eventually done by the county, and were called county drains. Today there are 108 such drains in the county, the largest of which was completed about 25 years ago. This was the matter of changing the course of Beaver Creek for a distance of 13 miles, extending from a point near Lake Robbins to the Polk county line.

A most unusual thing occurred on the night of 

October 28, 1929. In the early evening wild geese were heard at close range. This continued throughout the night, and some were shot by local people. 

This was an unusual occurrence, but when newspapers told of the same thing at Des Moines, Newton and Shenandoah it seemed much more so. For such a condition to exist over such a wide area, and on the same night would indicate that there must have been millions of them. Various reasons for the strange event were offered, but perhaps the most logical was that storms had driven them from their usual course. 

By 1930 many chain stores were in operation, and were having a telling effect on local retail merchants. 

Their capital and buying power enabled them to sell cheaper, while on the other hand the ordinary family

was compelled to buy where their dollar went the farthest. Despite the many arguments against the chain stores the trend continued that way, and no one seemed able to prevent it. Many will recall the scathing denunciations of Mr. Henderson of Shreveport,

Louisiana, against the chain system, which for some years, seemed to create considerable sentiment against centralized policies. 

Outstanding among the developments of the 20th

Century was a great international depression period. 

In our country it was precipitated by a financial crash in the stock market in the fall of 1929. Shortly, large numbers of men were "laid off" from their jobs in the east; gradually this condition moved westward until thousands of ordinary wage earners all over the country were out of work. The fulfillment of Zachariah 

8:10 had suddenly and unexpectedly come upon the people. 

Real estate values plunged to astonishing levels, farm products were only worth a fraction of former values, and wages were at a distressing rate. There was no reduction in taxes; interest on loans and mortgages continued, banks were closing daily throughout the country. Many people had purchased farms and real estate at very high prices during the prosperous days of the Coolidge administration, thinking that prices would at least remain fair, if not as high as they were then. How to meet the sudden and unexpected change almost drove some people frantic.

By the fall of 1932 corn was only worth from seven to ten cents a bushel, and some farmers were burning

it for fuel. Wages generally were $1.00 a day, although many worked for what ever they could get, sometimes no more than 50c a day. Hogs were as low as $2.50, chickens, 8c; eggs, 8c. Sausage, lard and hamburger could be bought for five cents a pound, and medium grade flour for 75c.

There had been financial panics before, but they would soon pass over, consequently the Hoover administration believed that this would too, but conditions only grew worse. At presidential election time in the fall of 1932 the simple slogan "We need a change" won a sweeping victory for the Democratic party. The state legislature on February 7, 1933, passed the mortgage moratorium bill, which postponed the payment of mortgage payments until January 1, 1935, hoping that by that time conditions would be better. That winter numerous farm sales were held in an attempt to satisfy mortgage holders and creditors. Amid these abnormal times some very unusual things were done. The 

Rosander sale near Perry on January 24, 1933, was typical of others throughout the state, however it was one of the first.

George Rosander's obligations to the First National 

Bank of Perry was a matter troubling both parties. 

Finally it was mutually agreed that a sale should be held in view of adjusting financial matters. Feelings among the farmers were considerably aroused against anyone trying to collect during this period of low prices. 

A crowd estimated at 1,500 gathered at the farm and mutually connived to bid in the property at a mere pittance. Cows sold for ten cents each, teams 70c

and corn one-half cent a bushel, with the total proceeds amounting to only $45.05, as compared to an estimated actual value of between $3,000 and $4,000. 

To say that this sale was unsatisfactory to the bank goes without saying, and finally other arrangements were made. This new and novel type of sale brought forth much newspaper comment. Two Perry men, at the time, were in Corpus Christi, Texas, when they heard newsboys yelling, "Read all about the big farm sale in Iowa." Eager for Iowa news they bought a copy, and were surprised to see that the big sale was at their home town.

The fall before a Farmer's Holiday movement was agitating the peace and order of the state. They hoped to persuade all farmers to refrain from selling their products until a certain satisfactory price could be realized, and all who would sell at current prices were liable to be waylaid while enroute to market by pickets, who would barricade the road, dump their goods and forbid them to proceed, sometimes resorting to violence. 

These tactics were not carried out in Dallas county, althought it was rumored that picketing was to begin at Perry, but it never was. On March 13, 1933, 3,000 farmers visited the state house to ask for farm aid.

In March, 1933, the New Deal took the national reigns of government. Immediately a bank holiday of about a week was carried out by government decree. 

Only those banks sufficiently strong to carry on were permitted to reopen; the weak and unsafe ones were weeded out. This move seemed to work wonders.

The closing of banks, often unexpected, was over, and conditions were somewhat stabilized. 

This was followed, on April 19, by going off the gold standard, and the deflation of our currency. In July the National Recovery Act was inaugurated, which was designed to shorten hours, increase wages and give employment to many more people; in other words sort of even up the work and wages. The rules imposed upon a complex business world were opposed by some, ignored by others, but accepted by people generally. 

To counteract its many advantages, there were many arguments against the program of the Blue Eagle, the ensign of the N. R. A. After a few months its popularity began to wane, and on the 28th of the following May the United States Supreme Court declared that it was unconstitutional. 

The next major move of the New Deal was the 

Agricultural Adjustment Administration, or the triple 

A. The portion pertaining to our section was the cornhog program, which was begun December 1, 1933. 

During the last two years there had apparently been an over production of corn and hogs. Henry Wallace, Secretary of Agriculture, engineered a program wherein the farmers contracted to reduce their corn acreage and pig crop in return for certain cash payments made by the government. A large per cent of the people availed themselves of this privilege in 1934, but when the drought of that year made a drastic and unexpected cut in the corn crop, resulting in their inability to feed pigs, they were not so enthusiastic about it the next year, however a goodly number continued on for the

second year. On January 7, 1936, the Supreme Court ruled that this too was unconstitutional. 

Early in 1934 the State Legislature passed a retail sales tax bill, which went into effect on April 1st. A 

2 per cent tax was imposed upon all retail sales, with a few exceptions, however small sales of less than 15c were exempt. Each dealer was required to have a license, conspicuously displayed, and remit his tax money quarterly to the Iowa Board of Assessment and 

Review in Des Moines.

The Public Works Administration, in its original form, was begun in the fall of 1933. This was a plan of the Federal government to give work to the unemployed on local construction projects. During the first month 13,689 Iowans were given work, and this policy of the government has continued, in various ways, since that time. During the first year or so the work was scarce and men plentiful, so the slowest methods were used to make the jobs last longer. Many men were employed in the slow laborious task of moving dirt in wheelbarrows on some road and bridge projects. Since this crude beginning this type of work has developed until many women are used in domestic duties, and men are used for clerical work as well as for manual labor.

The next governmental agricultural scheme for this section was the soil conservation program, which has been in operation the last two years. Under this plan the farmer is paid so much an acre in cash for land that he seeds to grass, and a much less amount for land already in grass, if it remains so.

Sometime after the war Congress passed a bonus bill, in which the soldiers were to be paid a regulated bonus in 1945. After the depression came many of the men were hard pressed for money and wished that they might collect ahead of time. During the summer of 1932 vast numbers of them went to Washington in an effort to collect the money. After several weeks of encampment not far from the Capitol they were forcibly expelled from the city. With the accession of the 

Roosevelt regime they hoped to fare better, but disappointment still awaited them. Finally, their persistence was rewarded, and on June 16, 1936, they received the coveted money.

In the realm of agriculture great changes have taken place since 1900. Then the farmers hay consisted of timothy, red clover, millet, cane and wild hay. Sudan and soybeans were unheard of. Alfalfa had never been raised here, and sweet clover was merely a roadside menace. Silos, grain elevators, and cylinder corn shellers with drag attachment had not come into use. 

The threshers were large, and run by steam engines. 

Blowers for stacking the straw were soon to appear. 

Baby chicks were hatched under old Biddy, but soon the home incubators came out. For many years they remained as a marvel of the 20th century. By 

1925 big hatcheries, doing custom hatching, were coming into use, and now a great many folks do nothing but simply buy the baby chicks. Then the old farm house had a small dark cellar. Now all new or improved homes have a basement, with plenty of light and are equipped with furnace, laundry stove and power

washer. Some who are on electric lines have electric lights and power; others have Delco lights.

Dairying is a matter that has received much attention in recent years. The type of cattle formerly raised were either beef or all-purpose type, but now many farmers specialize in dairy breeds, especially in the 

Holstein and Guernseys. When the Guernsey cattle were being introduced in Dallas county M. M. Heptonstall, president of the Security Savings Bank, at Perry was interested in this breed, and to induce people to raise Guernseys he gave away 1,000 quarts of the milk (April 19, 1922) to show people the superior quality of the milk. Today there are numerous herds of 

Guernseys, especially among those who retail milk. 

About 1920 testing cows for tuberculosis was becoming common. On August 1, 1934, the Iowa Department of Agriculture adopted a plan to aid farmers in freeing their herds of Bang's disease (bovine infectious abortion) by means of a blood test. Already many farmers' herds have been given the test. Should cows react to either the tubercular test or the blood test the government partly reimburses the farmer in addition to what he may realize at a packing house. Vaccination of hogs to prevent cholera has been quite successfully done for 

25 years.

The practice of putting milk in glass bottles began about 1912. A generation ago nearly every housewife baked her own bread, especially among the farmers.

But little bread was sold, and what was, was unwrapped. Only two or three years ago did sliced bread become available, and now practically all the bread is

sliced at the factory. Parallel with the chain stores came the big bread trucks from the cities to compete with local bakers. Now there are eight competing companies bringing bread into Perry. They are: Zinsmasters, Colonial, Ungles, Schultze, Betsy Ross, Peter 

Pan, Campbells and A. & P.

The cold packing of vegetables, fruits and meats became common at the time of the World War, and have continued so till the present. During horse and buggy days the farmer and his wife would go to town on Saturday afternoon to do the family's weekly trading, but now, they can go so quickly, that during warm weather, most of them prefer to go on Saturday night.

Farmers who burnt wood used to do all the work by hand, using an ax and an old fashioned buck saw. 

With the coming of gasoline engines progressive farmers resort to the speedy work of a buzz saw. Instead of setting milk in crocks and milk pans for the cream to raise and be skimmed with a skimmer, cream separators are now used, which probably date back to 1900. 

About the time of the World War tractors began to be used quite extensively. The Fordsons were among the first in the field. Since that time there has been a steady trend toward power farming. At first only plowing, discing and harrowing were done; later the two-row cultivation of corn was begun, and the last few years combines and corn pickers have come into quite extensive use.

Electric refrigeration began to be used a good deal ten years ago. Now a good per cent of the homes are equipped in this way; even rural homes can have this

convenience by means of a kerosene burning type. 

Refrigerated lockers, where meat, fruit and vegetables are kept in cold storage are not yet a year old in Dallas county. Everett Shaw of Minburn has the distinction of having the first one in the county. During the fall 

Guy Fiscel opened one in Perry, and later, plants were opened in Adel and Dallas Center.

As a result of automobiles, trucks and paved highways the railroads of the country have been gradually losing their business for many years. In many places secondary lines of railroads have been abandoned and the tracks torn up. None of this has occurred in Dallas county but just north of here, in Boone county, a branch line of the Minneapolis & St. Louis Company has recently met such a fate. The number of trains on all lines have been reduced from what they once were. Gas-electric passenger cars are now in common use, having the advantage of more economical operation. On the M. & St. L. railroad these cars were put into service on November 5, 1930, but the Milwaukee were operating them out of Perry some years before that.

Today it is almost impossible to raise choice fruits and vegetables without spraying, but in Grandfather's time his principal trouble was with the potato bug, squash beetle and cabbage worm. Insects, blight and fungus diseases are steadily growing worse, but modern science is not far behind in effective methods of combat. 

Paper boxes have almost entirely taken the place of the wooden ones formerly used for the shipment of all kinds of merchandise. Cellophane sacks are fast tak-

ing the place of common paper bags in the retail sale of many kinds of goods. 

In addition to these things are the following 20th 

Century changes and developments: painless dentistry, silos, road maintainers, Delco lights, the liming of soil, multiplied licenses and taxes, asphalt shingles, adding machines, bobbed hair, beauty parlors, road houses, night clubs, girls and women smoking cigarettes and drinking beer, rural electric lines, talking pictures, wirephotos, neon signs, traffic lights, electric fence, steel stanchions, milking machines, air conditioning and many other things. Surely Daniel's prophecy of increasing knowledge is fulfilled in a remarkable way. 

Should those who died, even in 1900, visit the earth today they would be startled at the change that has taken place.

In 1927 the Wallaces' Farmer of Des Moines conducted a state wide contest to choose the 16 best farmers of the state. There were certain rules, but the size of the farm was immaterial; successful profitable operation was the principal matter, and those who won were termed "Master Farmers". Among the number was E. L. Hill of Minburn. Although 63 years of age, he with the assistance of his two sons, Clarence and Howard, operated the 720 acre farm in a most successful manner, and won considerable distinction. 

A part of his farm program was the fattening and shipping of from ten to 15 carloads of cattle and hogs. 

Their beautiful farmstead is called "Hillburn Farm", and is noted for its abundance of beautiful evergreen trees, with nearly all varieties being represented. The

next year Henry Royer, of three miles east of Minburn, was accorded the same honor for that year. In October, 1929, this farm paper was consolidated with the Iowa Homestead. Many who recall the Des Moines Capitol will remember that it was merged with the Evening 

Tribune on February 15, 1927.

Air mail service from Chicago to Omaha was begun on July 1, 1925, and inasmuch as the route crosses the southern part of the county the occasion shall be worthy of comment here. In Des Moines the event was made the occasion of a great celebration. At noon a parade was made from the post office to the capitol, and back. The post office band gave a concert in the evening, when 10,000 people were gathered to see the first mail plane come in. Although due at 9 p. m. it did not arrive until 9:50. As the plane landed the band played the Iowa corn song, and James Carss stepped out at the head of the welcoming committee.

Mr. Carss was 94 years old, and had ridden the first train into Des Moines many years before. 

A few years later passenger service was begun over the same route, and on to the west coast. Today the 

United Air Lines have two types of planes through here; those carrying ten passengers stop at the Des 

Moines airport, but the larger ones capable of carrying 

14 passengers go on through to Omaha before stopping.

This rapid mode of travel makes a striking contrast with the slow rate of the '49ers on their way to California. Then it would take several months to go from here, but now travelers can cover the entire distance

of 2,633 miles from New York to San Francisco in less than 17 hours.

With the coming of paved roads came decidedly better bridges in many places. These were a part of the primary road system, and the cost was borne by the state, as ordered by the State Highway Commission. 

One of the first was built southwest of Redfield in 1930. 

The new Garroute bridge which is partly of cantilever design, was built prior to 1930. This is the highest and most expensive of any of the new bridges. The 

Rock Island viaduct was also built before 1930. 

The new bridge east of Adel on No. 6 was completed in the spring of 1932. Its opening on May 25th was made the occasion of quite a celebration. There was an address by former Governor Clarke, and Mrs. Ada 

Russell, the longest resident in Dallas county, had the honor of cutting the ribbon which let the traffic move over the new bridge. Mrs. Russell, daughter of Benjamin Greene, and mother of Burton Russell, was then 83 years old, and had lived in the county practically all her life, having come here with her parents in 1849, when she was only six months old. During the summer of 1935 the new and improved overhead bridge was built over the Milwaukee tracks west of Bouton, and the same summer a new bridge was built west of Perry on No. 46. The new bridge at Booneville was completed in 1936. This bridge is in two parts and cost $69,067.16. The new bridge north of Adel on No. 169 was opened during the summer of 1931. 

A most distressing feature of automobile travel on the public highways is the prevalence of accidents, and

loss of human life. About 1933 all drivers were required to secure a driver's license. This was designed to eliminate those who were not qualified to drive, but a few years later a strict driving test was required in the presence of a state patrolman. These strict rules and regulations no doubt help to reduce accidents, but despite all the precaution, accidents continue at an alarming rate. Below is a chart showing accidents and fatalities for Dallas county, as given by the Iowa 

Automobile Accident Report.

In each annual report, without exception, the second is responsible for more accidents than any other.

The Dallas County Farm Bureau was organized in the opera house in Dallas Center on January 26, 1918.

At the present time Marion Elson of Waukee is president, and Raymond Pollock is secretary. In 1937 there were 407 paid up members. The County Agent is jointly supported by the Federal government, the state and the county. The following men have served. in this capacity: Clarence Pickard, February, 1918; 

H. C. Codlin, August, 1918; Don Fish, Januuary, 1933;

Joe Beving, January, 1936, and Roger W. Leinbach, March, 1937.

The 4-H club work was begun in Dallas county over 15 years ago. The purpose is to train the members in the proper use of the head, the heart, the hand and health. There are now 15 groups of girls who range in age from 12 to 21 years. The total membership is now 170. In June each year there is a large state convention at Ames, to which each club can send a delegate. Besides the girls, there are also boys' clubs, who compete in pig and calf raising. 

Among the various practices growing out of the depression is the payment of old age pensions, to those who are in need, and over 60 years of age. Of 44,411 

Iowans who received checks on December 1, 1937, 502 went to Dallas county, and approximately 200 to Perry.

The average amount was $19.34. 

Another product of the depression was the proposed Townsend revolving pension plan. This plan, originated by Dr. Townsend of California, proposed to pay every man and woman over 60 years of age $200 month with the understanding that they retire from 2

further activity and spend the whole amount monthly. 

The contention was that the money in circulation would bring prosperity to all, and after once started would only revolve in regular course, bringing benefit to everybody. Great enthusiasm prevailed, and many local clubs were formed. The matter was seriously discussed pro and con, and in time it came before Congress, but endorsement there was not obtained. 

Despite the advantages of the plan many regarded it as visionary and impractical. 

From the beginning of the depression unemployment fluctuated more or less. In the fall of 1937 the Federal government took an official unemployment census, to ascertain the true condition in all sections. The results in Dallas county showed 527 unemployed, 176 on 

W. P. A. work, and 649 wanting work.

During the summer and fall of 1937 a plague of sleeping sickness among horses became serious in some sections. The 474 practicing veterinarians reported 

27,533 cases in the state, with a loss of 6,583 head.

While Dallas county fared better than some sections, yet a good many died here. The disease had perculiar characteristics, and developed rapidly in spite of all that could be done.

In 1907 Prof. R. F. Wood, who was then county superintendent of schools, published a second history of Dallas county. It had been nearly 30 years since the earlier one was published and the new history was a real asset to the county. This large volume, of unusual quality, also contained much Iowa history, and hundreds of biographical sketches.

For many years the Scandia Coal Company, owned by members of the Carney family, was the principal mining concern of the county. Their operation began soon after 1900. Mines 1, 2, 3 and 5 were in Dallas county, and during the best times their daily output was as much as 1,000 to 1,500 tons. Now their only mine in the county is the Zookspur mine which has been in operation for more than 25 years. Other large railroad mines of the present time are the Dallas Fuel

Company of Dallas, Norwood-White Company at Moran and the Schuler Coal Company at Waukee. 

About ten years ago the supervisors, and others, believed that the buildings at the county home were inadequate for the growing need. The public was invited to come and inspect conditions for themselves, and form their own opinions. Following this the people, by vote, sanctioned a $40,000 bond issuance for a new and modern addition. During the summer of 1929 a fine new brick structure was built. It is 50 by 100 feet in size, two stories high, and connected with the old building by a 50 foot hallway. This improvement gave the county an exceptionally good county home. 

For 21 years Mr. and Mrs. G. W. Harmon have been in charge of the home and farm. There are now 

536 acres of land, and on January 1, 1938, there were 65 inmates, about 20 of which are insane. Since Mr. 

Harmon's coming, Belgian horses have been specialized in, and usually about 35 head are kept on the place. 

About 50 head of pure-bred Holstein cattle are kept, about half of which are milk cows. About 250 head of hogs are marketed annually.

A few years ago the state fish and game commission established gave reserves here and there throughout the state. One of these, consisting of several sections, lay between Perry and Gardiner. The edge of the area was posted with metal signs forbidding trespassing and hunting, and it was believed that pheasants would become plentiful if given a chance, but so far the results have not come up to expectations. For years, shipments of game fish, from the state fish hatchery,

have been dumped in the Raccoon river to maintain the stock.

For many years a gradual change has been taking place within the Christian church. The dawn of the twentieth century seems to have been the threshhold of the Laodecean stage, into which the church would come, to complete the last of the seven prophetic periods of Revelation 2 and 3. About this time "higher criticism" was being heard in some quarters. Today we know the same thing, in more advanced stages, by the name of modernism. Its principal points of variance with the standards of the Bible, and our fathers, lie in the following matters: Divinity of 

Christ, eternal punishment, inspiration of the scriptures, evolution (Darwin's theory), the blood atonement, the supernatural, regeneration and pre-millennialism.

Most denominations are now rent asunder over the issues of modernism, and a great change has taken place in the standards and power of the church. The decay from within only paves the way for the steady advance of infidelity and communism that threatens the destruction of all our cherished American ideals. 

In a certain church the writer once saw a picture hanging on the wall-'twas a very simple picturea child kneeling in prayer before its mother. Below were the words, "This simple faith in God has made our country great." How true this is; and is it not reasonable to believe that the same things that made us great will also keep us great?

For about five years there was a great interest in consolidated schools. Many rural schools were elim-

inated and bus routes established to convey the children to and from the central point of a consolidated district. 

It was believed that such a course would be cheaper, besides have the advantage of better grading and instruction. After eight of these schools were established the trend turned to modernized local units and no more consolidated schools have been started in the last 17 years.

The county's consolidated schools and date of consolidation are here shown: Waukee 1916 Gardiner Linden Redfield 

Washington township.

Dawson Desota 

Van Meter 1917 February 24, 1920 March 26, 1920 April 6, 1920 . May 27, 1920

January 17, 1921 July 15, 1921 There are a total of 121 schoolhouses in the county. 

The teachers employed therein number 302, and of this number 88 are in the country. The school expense for the year 1935-1936 amounted to $410,937.70. At the close of this year 364 students were graduated from high school. The average attendance in rural schools was 898.3 and of town schools 4,261.6.

The Perry Shipping Association was begun in 1920. 

Thomas Turner was the prime mover in the matter and became the first manager. However a permanent organization was not effected until the following spring, when officers and directors. were chosen and by-laws formed. Life membership fees were fixed at $1.00, and eventually over 400 members came within the

organization. These extended, in some cases, to over 

20 miles from Perry, and a large volume of business was done, with much profit to the members. Not only did the members ship out their stock but some things were shipped in as well. Twine, apples, peaches and potatoes were some of the things brought in in carlots. 

During the best days of the organization from 125 to 150 carloads of stock were shipped annually, which netted the farmers from $165,000 to $200,000. Most shipments were to Chicago, but sometimes Des Moines held advantage over other points. A few shipments were made as far east as Cleveland and Pittsburgh. 

During this time an organization was formed at Minburn that operated for some years. Managers at Perry were as follows: Thos. Turner, 1920; Walter Schell, 1922; Scott Leonard, 1922; Elwood Jewell, 1929, and Ed. Reed, 1931. A few years ago this co-operative group ceased to operate.

The Credit Bureau of Dallas county was begun in 

May, 1930. Through this agency the merchants cooperate together in knowing the financial rating of the people. At first two offices were opened-one in Adel in charge of R. E. McNickols, and another in Perry.

In June, 1932, the two were consolidated, and the Adel office was discontinued. Mrs. Helen Randall is in charge of the office at Perry which serves the whole county in a very effective and accurate manner. Practically all adults are listed in the published reports which are available to members only. Six designations are given ranging from prompt to accounts given for collection. There are now over 1,200 such credit bureaus in the United States.

CHAPTER 17 COUNTY OFFICERS 

1847-1938 Government and civil order are essential for the well-being of any community, and in this respect Dallas county has had a very satisfactory record. Our officers, as a whole, have been trustworthy and capable, and a record of their names should be inscribed on our pages of history.

SUPERVISORS 

The first and most necessary of all were the county commissioners, who supervised the affairs of the new county. The earliest are here shown. 

April to August, 1847, Wm. Miller, Tristram Davis and Greenbury Coffin. 

August, 1847, to August, 1848, Wm. Miller, Noah Staggs and O. D. Smalley. 

August, 1848, to August, 1850, Wm. Miller, Tristram Davis and Wm. McCubbin. 

August, 1850, to August, 1851, Tristram Davis, Wm. McCubbin and J. C. Corbell.

In 1851 the board of Commissioners was abolished by an act of the state legislature, and for ten years the county affairs were managed by county judges, as follows: 

L. D. Burns.. .Aug., 1851 to Oct., 1859 

Henry Thornburg. . Oct., 1859, to Jan., 1861 

Jeremiah Perkins. . Jan., 1861, to Dec., 1861 389

Henry Thornburg resigned, and his place was taken by Perkins until October, when he was elected, but after this year the judge's office was confined to probate business.

The revised code of Iowa established a board of supervisors, with one from each civil township. At this time there were ten organized divisions which resulted in a board of ten members. This board held their first meeting on January 7, 1861, and consisted of the following members:

History does not tell us who followed these men, but the last large board, when there were 16 divisions,, in 1869-1870, were as follows: Benjamin Greene, Chairman; S. H. Vestal, L. D. Bullis, Chas. B. Snow, B. W. Thomas, L. S. Wells, B. F. Sincoak, David J.

In 1870 another change was made, in which the number was reduced to three; the first board to serve

COUNTY OFFICERS 

391 one, two and three years, after which one new member was to be elected each year. This arrangement always kept two old members on the board, and their election was to be at regular election time. This first board, elected on October 11, 1870, consisted of David Smart, three-year term; William Ellis, two-year term, and 

J. W. Redfield, one-year term. Mr. Ellis resigned, and on March 23, 1871, J. R. Van Meter, was appointed to take his place.

Inasmuch as the terms are for three years and the elections are biennial it is very difficult to know, and to show, just when each one began and quit. Sometimes a member is elected over a year before he begins to serve, and the records do not always make it clear when he is dropped from the list, however we shall do the best we can, and hope the reader will make allowance for possible and even probable, mistakes. DISTRICT JUDGES AND JUDICIAL DISTRICTS 

Hon. James P. Charlton, the first district judge who presided in Dallas county, belonged to the fourth judicial district, and was the only judge from that district who presided over a term of court in this county until the change. Originally, the fourth district was composed of the counties of Benton, Boone, Dallas, Iowa, Jasper, Johnson, Linn, Marshall, Polk, Poweshiek, Story and Tama, but in 1849 Washington county was annexed to it from first district, and Dallas, Boone, Jasper, Marshall, Polk and Story counties were detached, to form the new fifth judicial district, consequently Charlton was no longer the presiding judge here.

Dallas county remained in the fifth district about seven years, and during that time the following district judges presided over court in Adel: Hon. William 

McKay, of Polk county, presided over the second term

COUNTY OFFICERS 

397 of district court held in the county, June 4, 1849, as shown by the records. He was elected to that office 

April 2, 1849, and commissioned April 27th, just two years after the date of Hon. Charlton's commission.

Hon. McKay continued as presiding judge here until after the May term of court, 1854.

Hon. Phineas M. Casady of Polk county, was elected as his successor, April 3, 1854, qualified June 1st, and then resigned without serving even one term. Hon.

Chas. J. McFarland, of Boone county, was appointed in his place by Governor Hempstead, in May, 1854, and presided over the October term of court the same year. 

Hon. Wm. W. Williams, of Polk county, was declared elected as his successor on April 2, 1855; but this election was contested, and the contest was decided 

June 10, 1856, in favor of Hon. McFarland, who continued to preside over the district court in Dallas county until December, 1856, when a change in judicial districts was again made, and Dallas, Jasper, Marion and Polk counties were detached from the fifth to aid in forming the new eleventh district, and McFarland was no longer the presiding judge in Dallas county.

Hon. Wm. M. Stone, of Marion county, was elected the first judge of this new eleventh district April 6, 1857; and served in that capacity until June 1, 1859.

In accordance with Article V of the Constitution of 

1857, the 14 old judicial districts, formed under the constitution of 1846, were superceded by 11 new districts, the judges and district attorneys of which entered severally upon their duties January 1, 1859.

Under the change Dallas county again became a part of the fifth judicial district in connection with Adair, Guthrie, Madison, Polk and Warren counties.

Hon. John H. Gray was the first judge of this new district after the change under the constitution of 

1857. He was first elected October 12, 1858; was re-elected October 14, 1862, and served in that capacity with great acceptance for more than seven years, until October 14, 1865, when he died, highly esteemed and sadly lamented by all who knew him. 

Hon. Chas. C. Nourse was duly appointed his successor by Gov. Wm. M. Stone, October 16, 1865; and in less than a year resigned. Hon. Hugh Maxwell was appointed to fill the vacancy until October 9, 1866, when he was elected by the people, and was re-elected 

October 11, 1870, serving out his second full term, and occupying the position more than eight years. 

Hon. John Leonard was elected as his successor 

October 13, 1874, entering upon the duties of his office the first of January following, and faithfully served his four-year term in an acceptable manner. 

Hon. W. H. McHenry was elected as his successor 

October 8, 1878. 

Numerous changes have been made since that time, but at the present time Dallas county is in the fifth judicial district, which includes Madison, Marion, Adair, Guthrie, Dallas and Warren counties. The judges of this district are: 

Hon. Norman Hayes

Hon. W. S. Cooper.. 

Hon. E. W. Dingwell.

Knoxville Winterset . Adel

Having brought the reader down through the years, amid many changing scenes of life, we now leave him at the present, where he can see and know for himself. 

Following our retrospect of over 90 years many minds will turn to the future and wonder what awaits us.

Undoubtedly the laws of nature and the principles of cause and effect will continue on in their course through coming years, to accurately carry out the "blue print" of the omniscient and omnipotent Creator as revealed in the inspired Word.
